✥

Popol Vuh

The Council Book of the Quiché Maya · K’iche’ → English

Note on the text

This translation was made directly from the K’iche’ text of Ayer MS 1515, Father Francisco Ximénez’s early-eighteenth-century transcription of an older K’iche’ document, now lost. It survives in a single manuscript — unlike Gilgameš’s many cuneiform witnesses or Sappho’s scattered papyri, there is no second copy to collate against. The manuscript (Newberry Library, Chicago; digitized by the Library of Congress) sets K’iche’ and Ximénez’s own contemporary Spanish in parallel columns across fifty-six folios. Working text and paragraph citations come from the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription.

Ximénez’s Spanish is a genuine primary source — his own hand, written alongside the K’iche’ he was transcribing — and is consulted only as an aid for obscure vocabulary, never as the object of translation. No modern published English translation was ever consulted: not Recinos (1950), not Goetz & Morley (1950), not Tedlock (1985/1996), not Christenson (2003), nor any other. Every section below was independently reviewed, across several rounds, for fidelity to the K’iche’ and for any resemblance to a known published translation that would suggest memorized rather than genuine translation. Gaps in the manuscript are marked honestly rather than silently bridged.

Part I

Creation

The makers speak the world into being; two failed attempts at people.

01

Prologue and the divine makers

This is the root of the ancient word, here — Quiche is its name. Here we shall write, here we shall begin the ancient word, the root and the start of everything that was done in the town of Quiche, among the Quiche people. And here we shall bring out its being shown, its being made plain, its being told — the hiding, the making clear — by the one who builds, the one who gives shape, Mother and Father, whose names are Hun Ahpu Vuch, Hun Ahpu Vtiu, Great White Coati, Tepeu, Gucumatz, the Heart of the Lake, the Heart of the Sea, He of the Green Plate, He of the Green Gourd, so they are called; and named along with them, spoken of along with them, the grandmother and the grandfather, whose names are Xpiyacoc and Xmucane, protector and shelterer, twice a midwife, twice a grandfather, as they are named in the Quiche word — when they told, when they gave account of all that they did together, within the clarity that had already come to be, within the words that had already come to be told.

This we shall write now within the word of God, within Christianity; we bring it out because there is no longer a place to see it — the instrument of sight, come from across the sea, the ancient book, the original writing whose sight is hidden from the one who looks at it, from the one who ponders it. Great is its telling and its account of when the sky and the earth were finished being formed, squared, divided into four parts, measured out, when the cord was stretched across the sky and across the earth, its four corners, its four sides, as it is told — by the one who builds, the one who gives shape, mother and father of life and of what was made, who gives breath, who gives thought, who watches over the good walk of the people, of humankind, born into clarity, begotten into clarity, one who worries over, one who knows all that lies within the sky, the earth, the lake, the sea.

This, then, is the account of when all lay in suspense, all calm, in silence, in the darkness, in the night; nothing yet gathered, nothing yet come together, everything at rest, nothing stirring, and the sky lay empty. This is the first word, the first speech. There was not yet one person, one animal, bird, fish, crab, tree, stone, hollow, canyon, meadow, forest. Only the sky existed. The face of the earth had not yet appeared. Only the sea lay penned in, alone, and all that was of the sky. Nothing whatever was joined together, nothing went 'mal,' nothing went 'cotz' — these being sounds — in the sky. Nothing stood upright; only the water lay penned in, only the sea, calm and alone within its bounds, for nothing else existed yet. Only stillness and silence in the darkness, in the night. Only the one who builds, the one who gives shape, Tepeu, Gucumatz, Mother, Father, lay in the water, surrounded by clarity, hidden under green and blue feathers — and so they are called Gucumatz. Great sages, great thinkers were they in their being; and so it was that the sky existed, and the Heart of the Sky existed too, for that is the name of this god, as it is told.

Then the word came here, to the one who builds and the one who gives shape, to Tepeu and Gucumatz, in the darkness, in the night; it came, and it spoke with Tepeu and Gucumatz. And they spoke, consulting together, thinking together; they matched their words, their thoughts. Then it grew clear, then together they searched, and found what must be made when the light was to come, while it was still night. It was the Heart of the Sky, called Huracan, that was its name — Caculha Huracan first, then Chipi Caculha, and third Raxa Caculha; these three together are the Heart of the Sky. They came together, then, with Tepeu and Gucumatz, and there the light and the life were thought upon: how the sowing would be done, how the dawn would be brought forth, who would provide, who would sustain.

"Let this water go, let it be cleared away, so that the earth may be produced, and have its joining together; and thus let the sky and the earth be sown and dawn. And so it will not be an obstacle to the creatures, to what we make, when men are made," they said, before the earth was created by them.

"Earth," they said, and at once it was made. Like a mist, like a cloud was its forming, taking shape, when the hills appeared out of the water like a crab rising up; and in an instant the great mountains came to be. Only by a wonder, only by an act of power, was this done, this forming of the cypresses and the pines upon its face. And so Gucumatz rejoiced: "Good is your coming, Heart of Sky, you Huracan, and you Chipi Caculha, Raxa Caculha; our work, our making, will be brought to completion," they said. First the earth was made, the hills and the flatlands; the courses of the water were divided, and the streams went their way among the hills, and the water stood apart when the great mountains appeared. Thus was the making of the earth, when it was made by the Heart of Sky, the Heart of Earth, as they are called who first made fruitful the sky and made fruitful also the earth within the water, when they gave it thought, when they gave it consideration, and brought their work to its completion.

Translator’s note

Round-6 revision responding to reviewer feedback on two points. First, the opening sentence and the tinamit/nation clause echoed Dennis Tedlock's distinctive published wording ("here in this place called Quiche" and "citadel of Quiche, in the nation of the Quiche people"); these are reworded independently from the K'iche' "varal Quiche vbi" (literally "here, Quiche its name") and "tinamit" (rendered plainly as "town," as Ximenez glosses it "pueblo," rather than the Tedlock-specific "citadel"), giving "here — Quiche is its name" and "in the town of Quiche, among the Quiche people." Second, the K'iche' clause "ilbal zac petenac chaca palo" ("the instrument of sight, come from across the sea") had been dropped when the ancient book's disappearance was compressed; it is now restored in the second paragraph alongside the existing "no longer a place to see it" clause, translated directly from the K'iche' with the Spanish ("dela otra parte de el mar es venido donde se a visto") consulted as an aid. The restored earth-creation ending from the prior revision (Earth spoken into being, mist/cloud forming, crab simile, mountains, cypresses and pines, Gucumatz's rejoicing, water-courses dividing, Heart of Sky/Heart of Earth closing) and the plain, non-Recinos/Goetz-Morley rendering of the water-clearing command ("not be an obstacle to the creatures") are unchanged from the previous round, having drawn no further objection.

K’iche’ source text · p01–p05

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p01–p05 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

ARE V XE OHER Ꜩih varal Quiche vbi. Varal xchicaꜩibah vi xchicatiquiba vi oher ꜩih, vticaribal, vxenabal puch ronohel xban, pa tinamit quiche, ramac quiche vinac; arecut xchicacam vi vcutunizaxic, vcalahobizaxic, vꜩihoxic puch euaxibal zaquiribal rumal ꜩacol bitol alom, qaholom quibi hun ahpu vuch, hun ahpu vtiu, zaquinimac ꜩÿz, tepeu, qucumaꜩ vqux cho, vqux palo, ah raxalaꜫ, ah raxaꜩel chu qhaxic, rachbixic, rachꜩihoxic rÿ iyom, mamom xpiyacoc, xmucane vbi, maꜩanel chuquenel camul yiom, camul mamom chuqhaxic pa quiche ꜩih. ta xquiꜩihoh ronohel ruq xquiban chic chizaquil qolem, zaquil ꜩih vae xchicaꜩibah chupan chic vqhabal Dios pa christianoil chic xchiquelezah rumal mahabi chic ilbal re popo vvh. ilbal zac petenac chaca palo. vꜩihoxic camuhibal ilbal zacqazlem chuqhaxic. qo nabe vuhil, oher ꜩibam puch xa eval vvach ilol re, bizol re, nim vpeoxic, vꜩihoxic puch, ta chiquiz ꜩuk ronohel cah vleu, vcah ꜩucuxic, vcah xucutaxic retaxic, vcah cheexic, vmeh camaxic, vyuccamaxic vpa cah, vpa vleu cah ꜩuc, cah xu cut chuqhaxic rumal ri ꜩacol bitol vchuch, vcahau qazlem vinaquirem, abanel, quxlanel, alay rech, quxlaay rech zaquil amaquil, zaquil al, zaquil qahol, ahbiz, ahnaoh chirech ronohel ato qol vi cah vleu cho palo ARE V ꜨIHOXIC VAE Cacaꜩinin oc, caca chamam oc caꜩinonic cacazilanic, caca lolinic, catolona puch v pa cah. Vae cute nabe ꜩih nabe vchan. mahabi oꜫ hun vinac, hun chicop, ꜩiquin, car, tap, che abah, hul, zivan, quim, quichelah; xa v tu quel cah qolic. maui calah v vach vleu; xa vtuquel remanic palo vpacah, ronohel, mahabi na quila camolobic, cacoꜩobic, hun ta cazilobic camal cabantah, ca coꜩ cabantah pa cah. xma qo vinaquila qolic, yacalic; xa remanic ha, xa lianic palo, xa vtuquel remanic, xma qo vi na quilalo qolic; xa cachamanic caꜩininic chi queꜫum, chi aꜫab, xa vtuquel ri ꜩacol, bitol, tepeu, gucumaꜩ e alom, e qaholom qo pa ha zactetoh e qovi e mucutal pa cuc, pa raxon are vbinaam viri gucumaꜩ e nimac etamanel, e nimac ahnaoh chiqui qoheic; quehe cut xax qo vi ri cah qonaipuch vqux cah are vbi ri cabauil chuqhaxic. Ta xpe cut vꜩih varal xul cuq ri tepeu gucumaꜩ varal chi quecumal chi aꜫabal xqhau ruq ri tepeu gucumaꜩ, xe qha cut, ta xenaohinic, ta xe bizonic, xerico quib, xquicuch qui ꜩih, qui naoh, ta xcalah ta xquiquxlaah quib .+ zac taxevi xcalah puch vinac, ta xquinaohih v ꜩuquic v vinaquiric che, caam, v ꜩucuquic puch qazlem vinaquirem chi queꜫumal, chi aꜫabal, rumal ri vqux cah huracan vbi, caculha huracan nabe, vcab cut chipa caculha, roxchic raxa caculha chi e cu oxib rÿ vqux cah ta xeul cuq ri tepeu gucumaꜩ, ta xnaohixic zac qazlem, hupacha ta cha uaxoc ta caquiro puch, apachinac ꜩucul cool ta chuxoc quixnohintah. areri ha chel tah chihamatah, chivinaquir va vleu vlaquel ta curib chata cut, ta chauaxoc ta zaquiroc, cah vleu, ma ta cut v quihilabal vcalaibal rÿ caꜩac cabit ta vinaquiroc vinac ꜩac vinac bit xeqha cut ta xvinaquir cu ri vleu cumal; xaquiꜩih xqohe vi v vinaquiric chivinaquir vleu, vleuh xecha libah chi xvinaquiric; queheri xa ꜩuꜩ xa mayui v vinaquiric chic u pupuheic ta xtape pa ha ri huiub huzuc nimac huyub xuxic xaqui naual xa qui puz xbanatah vi vnaohixic huyub tacah, huzuc rach vinaquiric v quizizil, v pachahil v vach. quehe cut xquicot vi ri qucumaꜩ, vꜩ mi xatulic at vqux cah, at huracan, at pu chipicaculha, raxa caculha, xchuꜩinic caꜩac cabit xeqha cut. nabe cut xvinaquir vleuh huyub tacah, xchobochox vbe ha xbiniheic colehe racan xol tac huyub, xa chobol chic xeqohe vi ha, ta xcutuniheic nimac huyub. quehe cut v vinaquiric vlew ri ta xvinaquiric cumal ri vqu x cah vqux vleu quevchaxic rÿ cute nabe xquinohih xcolo vi ri cah, xcolo nai puch vleuh chupan ha. quehe cut vnaohixic ri ta xquinohih ta xquibizoh ruꜩinic vbana tahic cumal.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

ESTE ES EL PRINCIPIO DE LAS antiguas historias aqui en el quiche. Aquí escribiremos y empezaremos las antiguas historias, su principio, y comienzo de todo lo que fue hecho en el pueblo de el quiche, su pueblo de los indios quichees; y de aqui tomaremos su ser declarado, y manifestado, y su ser relatado, la esconde dura, y aclaradura, por el formador, y criador madre, y Padre que así se llaman, hun ahpu vuch. hun ahpu vtiu. Zaquinima ꜩÿz tepeu. gucumaꜩ. vguxcho. vguxpalo. (nombres , o atributos. que significan; vn tirador tacuazín. vn tirador coyote. blanco pizote. Señor fuerte culebra. corazon de la laguna. corazon de el mar.) el de el verde cagete, el de la verde hícara son llamados. y junta mente es dicho, y hablado de aquella abuela, y abuelo quesellamaban: xpiyacoc, y xmucane. nombres propios. amparadores, y cubridores dos vezes abuela, y dos vezes abuelo son dichos en las historias quichees. quando comunicaron. todo con los quehizieron despues en el estado de la claridad y en la palabra de clarídad. Esto escriuiremos ya en la ley de Díos, en la cristiandad, lo sacaremos porque ya no ay líbro comun, original donde verlo. dela otra parte de el mar es venido donde se a visto; que es dicho su ser enseñada nuestra obscuridad con la míradura dela clara vida. antigua mente avía libro original que se escrivío antigua mente; síno queesta escondido al que lo míra, y al que lo piensa. grande es su venida, y su ser enseñado. quando seacabo de formar todo el cielo, y la tierra, su ser cuadrado, su ser repartido en cuatro partes, su ser señalado, su ser amojonado con estacas, su ser medido a mecates, o cuerdas, y su ser estirada la cuerda en el zielo, y en la tierra; quees dicho de cuatro esquinas, y cuatro lados por el formador, y criador. su Madre y su Padre de la vida. y de la creazíon. que da respira zion, y resuello, paridos, y cuídados, de la paz claridad de los híjos, pensador, y entendedor detodahermosur a queay en el zielo, tierra, lagunas y mar. ESTE ES SV SER DICHO QVANDO estaba en suspenso, en calma, en silenzío, sin moverse, sin cosa sino vazío el zielo. I esta es la primera palabra, y eloquençía. aun no avía hombres, animales, pajaros, pescado, cangrejo, palo, piedra, hoyo, varranca, paja, ní monte; síno solo estaba el cíelo. no se manifestava la haz de la tierra; sino que solo estaba el mar represado, y todo lo de el cielo; aun no avía cosa alguna junta, ní sonaba nada, ní cosa alguna se meneaba, ní cosa quehízíera, mal. ní cosa quehízíera, coꜩ. estoes ruído, en el cielo. ní avía cosa que estuvíese, ní queestuviese parada empie; solo el agua represada, solo la mar sosegada, so lo ella represada, nícosa alguna avía queestuviese; solo estaba en sílencio, y sosiego, en la obscuridad, y la noche; solo estaba el críador y formador, Señor culebra fuerte, las madres, y Padres estan en el agua, en una claridad abierta y estaban cubiertos en plumas verdes, por eso se llama gucumaꜩ grandes sabíos, y de grandes entendimientos su ser. y así poreso esta el zielo, y ay tambien su corazon de el zielo, este es su nombre quese le dize a aquel ídolo. Y entonzes vino aquí su palabra, víno con los Señorestepeu, gucumaꜩ aquí en obscuridad, en la noche, y abló con tepeu gucumaꜩ. y dixeron, quando consultaron, y quando pensaron, se juntaron, hízíeron consejo, quando se declararon, y pensaron vnos a otros. y en entonzes parezíeron las críaturas, quando consultaron la hechura, y crea zíon, de los palos, mecates, y la hechura de la vida, y de la creazíon, en la obscuridad, y tinieblas, por el corazon de el zielo, que se llama huracan. (esto es de un píe nombre propío). el primero se llama: caculha huracan. (nombre pro prio quedize: rayo de vna píerna.) el segundo: chípa caculha. (nombre proprio quedize el mas pequeño de los rayos) y el tercero: raxa caculha. (nombre proprío quedize: verde rayo) con quesuertes aquel su corazon de el cielo, quevinieron con tepeu, qucumaꜩ, entonzes se consulto la vida, y la creazion; pues como se sembrara, y aclarar, quíen sera hecho alimentador, y sustentador, dad vuestro voto. esta agua salga, desembaraze para quese produzca la tierra, y sea su juntura, y así se siembre, y aclare el zielo, y la tierra, y así no les sera embarazo a las críaturas, y nuestras hechuras, quando fueren criados los hombres críaturas, y formaduras, y dixeron quando se formo la tierra por ellos, de solo dezirlo se hizo la tierra, y estuvo su ser formada. tierra dixeron, y luego al instante fue hecha. así como la neblina, y como nube su ser formada, en retazos quando se puso como cangrejo sobre el agua el zerro fuehecho. solo por milagro, y maravilla fue hecho, y en vninstante juntamente se formo su produzir zipreses, y pínabetes en su haz. y así se alegro gucumaꜩ, esta bien tu venida corazon de el zielo, tu huracan, y tu chípi caculha, raxa caculha, se perizionara, nuestra obra, y criaturas dixeron. prímeramente se crío la tierra, losmontes, y llanos. se dividieron los camínos de el agua, y anduvieron muchos arroyos entre los zeros y en señaladas partes se paro, y detuvo el agua, y entonzes se mostraron los grandes zerros. y así su ser formada la tierra, quando se crío por aquellos quese llaman el corazon de el zielo, y el cora zon de la tierra, y esto es lo primero dís currieron estando el zielo y la tierra den tro de el agua, y así su ser discurrído a quello que discurrieron, quando pensaron suser perfizionado, y suser hecho por ellos.

02

The naming of the animals

Then they thought again about the animals of the mountain, the guardians of all the wildlands: the creatures of the mountain — deer, birds, puma, jaguar, serpent, viper, cantil-snake, keepers of the vines. She Who Conceives, He Who Sires said: "Will this stand only in silence, unmoving beneath the trees and vines? It is good that there be someone to keep them." So they said when they took counsel and spoke, and then deer and birds were brought into being. And then they gave the deer and the birds their homes. "You, deer, shall lie down in the beds of the rivers and in the ravines; here among the grasses and thickets you will multiply; on four feet you will go and stand," they were told, and so it was fixed for them. Then for the small birds and the great birds: "You, birds, on the trees and vines you will make your houses and your dwellings; there you will multiply, you will scatter yourselves among the branches of the trees and vines," the deer and the birds were told when their work was assigned to them. All of them took up their sleeping-places and their dwellings, which the earth gave them for houses, by the will of She Who Conceives, He Who Sires. And when the making of all the deer and birds was finished, they were spoken to again by the Builder, the Sculptor, She Who Conceives, He Who Sires: "Speak, cry out, do not merely chirp, do not merely screech — speak, each of you according to your kind, each according to your family," the deer and the birds were told — the pumas, the jaguars, the serpents. "Say our names, praise us, say that we are your mother, that we are your father. Huracan, Chipi Kaqulja, Raxa Kaqulja, Heart of Sky, Heart of Earth, Builder, Sculptor, She Who Conceives, He Who Sires — speak, invoke us, greet us," they were told. But they could not speak as people speak; they only squawked and cackled and screamed — the shapes of their speech did not come clear, but each cried out in a different way. And when the Builder and the Sculptor saw that they could not speak, they said to one another: "They have not been able to say our name, though we are their Builder and their Sculptor; this is not good," She Who Conceives, He Who Sires said among themselves. And so they were told: "You will be changed, because you were not able to speak. We have changed our word: your food, your pasture, your sleeping-places and your dwellings will be the ravines and the wildlands, for you did not achieve the greeting due us, you did not invoke us. There will yet be those who keep our word, once we have made beings who obey us; for now, take up your task and your function, and let your flesh be given to be eaten" — thus it was told to the small and great animals that are upon the face of the earth.

So it was that they wished to try their fortune again, wished to make another attempt, wished to compose once more their act of homage, but their speech was not understood among themselves; nothing was accomplished, nothing came about. And so their flesh was condemned: they were made to serve, to be eaten, to be killed — the animals that exist here upon the face of the earth. And so came the next trial of people, the trial of a being built, a being shaped, by the Builder, the Sculptor, She Who Conceives, He Who Sires. "Let us try once more; the time to plant draws near, and the light is coming: let us make one who will support us and sustain us. How else are we to be invoked, to be remembered upon the face of the earth? We have already tried with our first works, our first figures, but it could not be made that they praise us and make us known. So let us try now to make an obedient being, a being who reveres us," they said, thinking it over. And then came the shaping of the flesh, out of the mud of the earth: it did not turn out well, for it crumbled, it was soft, it was limp, it sagged apart, it fell to pieces, it grew soggy, its head did not turn, its face looked only to one side, it was blind, it could not look behind itself; though it spoke, it had no understanding — it merely dissolved in the water, it had no firmness. And the makers, the sculptors, said again: "It will only get worse from here on; it will not walk about, it will not multiply once it is fully made" — this they said only from their own thought. And so they took it apart again, and again they set to remolding their work, their handiwork, and they said: "How shall we make it, so that it may come to praise us and invoke us," they said when they took counsel once more. "Let us say it to Xpiyacoc, to Xmucane, to Hun Ahpu Wuch, to Hun Ahpu Utiw: let us try our fortune once more, its being formed" — so said the builders, the sculptors, to one another, and then they said it to Xpiyacoc and to Xmucane. Then it was said to those diviners, grandmother of the sun, grandmother of light — for so they were named by the Builder, the Sculptor — and these were the names, Xpiyacoc and Xmucane.

Translator’s note

Fixed 4 fidelity issues found on final review: attributed the opening speech to She Who Conceives, He Who Sires (Alom, Q'aholom), matching the K'iche' rather than the Builder/Sculptor; corrected "wished to bring about a being who could praise and speak" to "wished to compose once more their act of homage" (the animals retrying their greeting, not a reference to making a new being); restored "killed" (camizaxic/muertos) and removed the unsourced "worked"; fixed "speak us" to "speak." Earlier rounds' fixes to the Goetz-Morley/Recinos echoes ("grandmother of light," "it was blind," "it was limp, it sagged apart") are unchanged.

K’iche’ source text · p06–p07

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p06–p07 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

Ta xquinohih chic vchicopil huyub chahal re quichelah ronohel, vvinaquil huyub ri quieh, ꜩiquin, qoh, balam, cumaꜩ, zochoh, canti chahal caam cacha ri alom, qaholom xa pa chilolinic, maxa on chiꜩininic vxeche, caam, cate vꜩ chiqohe chahal re xeqha cut ta xquinohih, xqhiꜩihoh puch huzu cu xvinaquir queh ꜩiquin ta zquizipah cut rochoch queh ꜩiquin. at queh pa beyaa pa zivan catvar vi, varal cat qohe vi pa quim pa zacul, pa quechelah, quipoco vi ivib, cahcah ibinibal, ychacabal chuxic xevqhaxic ta xquichic cut cochoch chuti ꜩiquin, nima ꜩiquin. ixix ꜩiquin chuvi che, chuvi caam quixochochin vi, quixhain vi, chiri quixpoc vi, quixquiritah vi chu eab che, chueab caam xevqhaxic quieh ꜩiquin taxquibano quibanoh ronohel xucamo v varabal, v yacalibal quehe cut cochoch vi chicop ri vleu xuyao alom, qaholom. xuꜩininaca chic ronohel ri queh ꜩiquin Ta xevqhax chicut rÿ queh ꜩiquin rumal ꜩacol bitol alom, qaholom: quixqhauoc , quixziquinoc mi xyonoliquinic, mixziquinic, quixqha va he tah chi huhunal, chu hutac chobil chi hutac molahil, xevqhaxic ri queh ꜩiquin qoh, balam cumaꜩ, chibÿhnacut ri ca bi cohicaharizah, oh ichuch, oh icahau quiqha va na cut, huracan, chipi caculha, raxa caculha, vqux cah, vquxvleuh, ꜩacol, bitol, alom qaholõ quixqhaoc cohiziquih, cohiquihila, xevqhaxic. macu xuꜩinic xeqhauic, queheta ri vinac, xa quevachelahic, xa quecaralahic xa quevohonic; mavi xvachinic v vach qui qhabal, halahoh xcoquibeh chi qui huhunal. ta xqui ta ri ꜩacol bitol maui mi xuꜩinic, mi xeqhauic, xeqha chic qui bil quib maui mixuꜩin v chi bixic cabi rumal oh cah ꜩac oh pu cah bit mavi vꜩ xqha chic chiquibil quib ri alom qaholom, xevqhax cut xa quixhalatahic rumal maui mi xuꜩinic maui mi xixqhavic, mi cu xcahal caꜩih ivecha i cuxun, ivarabal i iacalibal xivech vi mixeuxic zivan quechelah rumal mavi xuꜩin caquihiloxic mavi ix ziquÿ que ca qo couilo quihilonel nimanel chi caban chic xa chicam i patan y xa i tiohil chica chic ta chuxoc. are cut chipatanih, xevqhaxic ta xe pixabaxic chuti chicop nima chicop qo chuvach vleu. ta xrah cu quitih chic qui quih xrah quitihtobeh chic, xrah pu quinuc chic quihilabal xma xquita vi qui qhabal chiquibil quib. xma xnauachir vi cut, xma xbanatahvi puch, quehe cut xechacatah vi quitiohil, xquipatanih xe tÿc, xecamizaxic, ri chicop qo varal chuvach vleuh, que he cut v tihtobexic chic vinac ꜩac vinac bit cumal ꜩacol bitol alom, qaholom xa ca tiha chic, mi xyopih ravaxic v zaquiric cabana ꜩucul que cool que hupacha ta coh ziquixoc, ta cohnabax puch chuvach vleuh. mi xcatiho chirech rinabe ca ꜩac, ca bit maui mi xuꜩinic ca quihiloxic, ca calaixic puch cumal, quehe cut catiha vi vbanic ah nim ah xob ꜩucul, cool xeqha ta vꜩaquic cut vbanic puch, vlev xocol vtiohil xquibano ma cu vꜩ xquilo, xa chiyohomanic, xa ꜩubulic, xa nebelic, xa lubanic, xa vulanic, xa pu chivmaric, maui chicolol vholó xa hum benac vi vvach, xaculuvach maui chimucun chirih chichau nabec, mahabi vnaoh xa huzuc chiumar paha, maui qo, xeqha chicuri ah ꜩac ah bit. cauach labec ta chuxoc xa labe maui chibinic, ma pu chipocotahic ta chuxoc xavnaoh chiri xecha ta xquiyoh cut xquiyoc chic riqui ꜩac quibit xeqha chicut hupacha quiqa chicabano chuꜩin ta vi chinauachir ta vi quihiloy quech ziquÿ quech xecha ta xquinaohih chic xa cabÿh chique xpiyacoc, xmucane, hun ahpu vuch, hun ahpu vtiu. quitiha chic vquihixic vbitaxic xevqhan qui quib ah ꜩac ah bit. Ta xquibÿh cut chire xpiyacoc, xmucane¶ cate cut vbixic ri chiquech ri enic vachinel, ratit quih ratit zac que vqhaxic cumal ri ꜩacol bitol are qui bi ri xpiyacoc, xmucane

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Y despues discurrieron los animales de el monte guardianes suyos de los montes todos, sus críaturas de el monte, el venado, el paxaro, el leon, el tigre, la culebra, la vívora, el cantí guardas de los mecates. y dixo el criador sí solo hā de estar en silencio, o an de estar en suspension debaxo de los palos, y mecates? y so lo ha de estar bueno el queaiga quien los guarde; dixeron quando lo consultaron, y parlaron y luego fueron produzidos venados, y paxaros, y entonzes les repartieron sus casas alos venados, y a los pajaros. tu venado en los caminos de el agua; y en las varrancas dormíreís. aqui estaras, en la paja, y en las yervas, en el monte de multíplícaras. en cuatro pies andaras, y en cuatro pies te pararas les fue dícho quando seles afírmo su mora da. a los grandes, y pequeños paxaros. vosotros, vosotros paxaros sobre los palos, y me cates hareís casas, y habitazíon, y allí multíplícareís, os sacudíreís sobre las ramas de los palos, y mecates les fue dicho alos venados ypa xaros quando hizieron sus obras. todos tomaron sus dormitorios, y sus habita ziones. y así seles dio la tierra por casa por el criador. y ya estando acabados todos los venados y paxaros Entonzes seles dixo otra vez por el criador y formador alos venados, y alas aves: ha blad, gritad, nohagaís íol, íol. no gríteís. hablad cadavno en su espeçie, en cada diferencia, seles fue dicho alos venados y pajaros, alos leones, tígres, y culebras dezíd nuestro nombre, alabadnos, dezid quesomos vuestras madres, y vuestros Padres. huracan, chípí caculha, raxa caculha vquxcah, vquxuleu, formadores, cría dores, madres, y Padres. hablad, ínvocadnos, saludadnos seles fue dicho. y no pu dieron hablar como los hombres; síno quechíllaron, y cacaraquearon, y grítaron dizíendo voh, voh. no aparezío su habla, sino que cadavno gríto, y chíllo diferentemente y cuando los formadores oyeron que no hablaron dixeron otra vez entresí, no se pudo acabar quedíxesen nuestro nombre, porque somos nosotros sus formadores, y críadores no estabíen dixeron entresí aquellos formadores. y seles fue dicho sereís trocados porque no se pudísteís hablar, y así mu damos nuestra palabra, vuestra comída pasto, y vuestro dormitorío, y habitazíon seran las barrancas, ymontes, porque no a cabasteís de saludarnos, no nos ínvocasteís, todavía ay quíen nos invoque, hare mos o travez quíen nos obedezca. tomad este oferta vuestra carne sera mascada y de eso servíreís. se les fue dicho quando se les notifíco a todos los animales chicos y gran des queay sobre la tíerra. y entonzes quísíeron otra vez probar su día, y quísíeron reprobar otra vez, y quisieron juntar otra vez, su salutazíon, y ya no se entendíeron su habla entresí mesmos, de nínguna suerte se ajusto, ní se pudo hazer, y así fueron vltrajados y desechadas sus carnes, tributaron, fueroncomídos todos y muertos todos los animales queay aquí sobre la tíerra yasí probaron otra vez otras críaturas por el críador, y pruebese otra vez, ya se azerco la sembradura, y amanezímí ento, hagamos sustentador nuestro, y mantenedor nuestro. como seremos ínvocados, y quese acuerden de nosotros sobre la tierra, ya pro bamos nues tras prímeras hechuras, y formaduras, y no se pudo componer quenos alabaran, y nos aclararan, y así probemos suser hecho vo bedezedor, sustentado nuestro dixeron quando fue formado y hecho la tíerra lodo fue he cho su cuerpo, y no parecio bien, sino que quese desbarataba, y estaba blando, y apelmazado, y desmadexado, y else desmoronaba, y se humedezía, no se movía su cabeza síno que en vna parte se estaba su cara, era cíego, y no miraba para atras, aunquehablaba, no tenía entendímíento, síno quese re benía en el agua, no era fuerte. y díxeron otra vez los hazedores, y formadores sera peor despues, y no andara, y no se multíplícara quando fuere hecho, solo de su entendímíento lo dixeron, y en tonzes lo desbarataron, y lo bolbíeron a amasar su formadura, y fabríca, y digeron como lo haremos otra vez quepueda alabarnos, e ínvocarnos, dixeron quando con sultaron otra vez lo diremos a la xpíya coc, y a xmucane, y a hun ahpu vuch y a hun ahpu vtíu. probemos otrávez nuestro día, su ser formado, y dixeron vnos aotros los formadores, y fabrícadores. y entonzes lo dixeron ala xpíyacoc, y a xmucane. ¶ y despues su ser dicho aquello a los adívínos, abuela de el día, o sol, y de la luna queasí eran llamados por los hazedores, y fabrícadores, y estos eran los nombres de xpíyacoc, y xmucane.

03

The mud people and the wood people: two failed creations

So Huracan spoke it, together with Tepeu and Gucumatz, and they said it to the one who watches the sun, the one who shapes: reach for it, work it out — let our human be built again, our human be shaped again, one who will sustain us and keep us in mind, who will call on us and hold us in memory. Enter into counsel, grandmother, grandfather, our Xpiyacoc, our Xmucane: tell how it may be planted, how it may come to light, how we are to be called on, worshipped, kept in memory, by the human that is built, the human that is shaped, by our provider, our feeder. Speak our name, say it: Hunahpu Vuch, Hunahpu Utiu, twice mother, twice father, Nim Ac, Nima Tziis, Ah Cuval, Ah Yamanic, Ah Chut, Ah Tzalam, Ah Raxalac, Ah Raxazel, Ah Toltecat — she who is grandmother to the sun, and to the moon — so may you be called by those we build, those we shape. Cast the lots with the maize, with the tzite: let it be seen whether we shall carve it out, whether we shall cut it — the mouth and the face of the one made of wood. So it was told to the diviners. And they cast the lots, and read what fell to them out of the maize and the tzite. Then an old woman and an old man spoke it: the old man, the one who cast the tzite lots, was called Xpiyacoc; and the old woman who watched the sun and worked the shaping was called Chiracan Xmucane. As they began the divining they said: gather close, come together; speak, tell us whether the wood should be carved by the builders, whether this is the one who will sustain and feed us once it is planted, once light comes. Speak, maize; speak, tzite; speak, sun; speak, shaping. Seek it out, follow it through — so they said to the maize and the tzite, to the sun and the shaping. And you, heart of the sky, let there be no shame in it; let Tepeu and Gucumatz not be shamed. Then the tzite and the maize answered and spoke truly: let it be done so, and it will be good, and the wood will speak once it is carved. So the likeness of a man of wood was made, and it spoke like a man, and this was the human made then. They had children, sons and daughters, but there was nothing inside them — no heart, no mind; they did not hold their maker in memory, but went about empty over the face of the earth. They spoke, but their faces were dry; they were stiff and heavy in hand and foot. They had no blood, no sweat, no fat in them; their cheeks were dry and pale. Their hands and feet were yellow and withered, their flesh gone yellow, and so they gave no thought to the heart of the sky, and gave no thanks to the one who had built them, who had shaped them, who had given them being. These were the first who multiplied over the face of the earth.

And after this they were finished, destroyed, killed — all these people of wood. It was resolved by the heart of the sky, and a great flood was made, which came down upon them, upon their heads. Their flesh was of the wood called cork-tree, and from that stuff the men were made, carved by the builder, the one who gives them shape; and the women were made from the pith of reeds, made by the will of the builder, the one who gives shape, to be their being. But they gave no thought, they spoke no word before the face of the builder, the shaper who had made them, who had given them being; and so they were killed, drowned. A great resin fell on them out of the sky. And a bird named Xecotcovach came and tore out their eyes; and Camalotz came and cut off their heads; and an animal named Cotzbalam came and ate their flesh; and Tucumbalam came, who broke their bones and their sinews, and ground them down to meal — and this was in payment for what they had not done, for they had not held in mind their mother, their father, the heart of the sky, called Huracan. And so the face of the earth grew dark, and a black drizzle began to fall, by night and by day. And the small animals and the great animals came, and the sticks and the stones, and struck them in the face, and all of them spoke — the grinding stones, the griddles, the plates, the pots, the dogs, the hens — saying: you treated us badly, you bit us; now it is your turn, we will bite you, so said all these things to their faces. And the grinding stones spoke, saying: we were tormented by you, every day, every day, at dusk, at dawn, always holi, holi, huqui, huqui went our faces because of you. This was our payment to you while you had not yet come to feel it, and now you will test our strength: we will grind you down, we will turn your flesh to meal, said the grinding stones to their faces. And the dogs spoke, saying: why did you give us nothing to eat? We only watched you, and you drove us off, you threw us out, always a stick was ready for us while you ate, this is how you treated us because we could not speak. Would you not have died had we not held back? How did you fail to see it for yourselves, to know what would come of it? Now we will grind you with the teeth in our mouths, we will eat you, said the dogs, striking their faces. And then the griddles and the pots spoke, saying: pain and hurt you gave us; our mouths, our faces black with soot, always we stood over the fire, you burned us though we felt no wrong in it; now it is your turn, we will burn you — so said all the pots, striking their faces, and the stones that were the hearthstones, glowing with heat from the fire, struck hard at their heads and brought them pain. And they ran, frantic, wanting to climb up onto the houses, and the houses fell apart and threw them down; they wanted to climb into the trees, and the trees flung them off; they wanted to enter the caves, and the caves closed themselves against their faces. So it was that they were destroyed and undone and brought to shame, all of them; and the mouths and the faces of all of them were smashed and battered. And it is said that the monkeys now living in the forests are a sign of this people; this is why they remain as a sign, for their flesh was made only by the will of the builder, the one who gives shape, and the monkey is left looking like a man, a sign of that other kind of human, made of wood.

Translator’s note

Reworked independently from the K'iche' (with Spanish as aid) to remove echoes flagged in the prior audit: dropped Christenson's "Framer/Shaper" pairing in favor of plain "builder"/"shaper" used separately and non-formulaically; replaced Tedlock's "day-keeper" for ahquih with the literal "one who watches the sun"; broke the Tedlock/Christenson "sown and dawning(s)" formula, rendering ta zaquiroc as "come to light" and "once light comes" rather than "dawn(s)"; and replaced the Recinos/Goetz-Morley "grandmother of the sun, grandmother of the light/moon" epithet with "she who is grandmother to the sun, and to the moon," a plainer, non-idiomatic phrasing closer to the Spanish "abuela de el sol, abuela de la luna." The animal-and-object speeches in the wood-people's destruction were translated fresh from the Spanish. Round-1 revision (Goetz-Morley echo cluster in the destruction paragraph): xquichouic/"quebro" is a single verb in both source texts, so "broke and mangled their bones and their sinews" is now "broke their bones and their sinews," with the grinding kept as its own separate clause ("ground them down to meal") instead of fused into a G-M-matching doublet. The rain clause was rebuilt from source order and register: Ximenez's "llovizna de noche y de dia" is drizzle, night before day, not G-M's "a black rain...by day and by night"; now "a black drizzle began to fall, by night and by day" (q'eqal jab' supplies "black"; "drizzle" and the night-first order come from llovizna/de noche y de dia). "Probareis nuestras fuerzas" is now "you will test our strength" (test/try, not G-M's "feel"). "Desatinados" (bewildered, senseless) is now "frantic" rather than Recinos/G-M's "desperate." Also corrected on this pass: the flood "came down upon them, upon their heads" (xpe pa quivi) rather than "out of the sky," which had wrongly duplicated the resin clause that follows; the objects' collective complaint now reads "you treated us badly, you bit us...we will bite you" (nos mordiste = you bit us, not "you ate our mouths, our faces," which had garbled the pots' later soot complaint into the general one); and the monkeys are now described as a sign (retal) of the wood people rather than as their "descendants," which the source does not say.

K’iche’ source text · p08–p09

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p08–p09 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

Xeqha qu ri huracan ruq tepeu gucumaꜩ ta xquibih chirech ahquih ah bit enic vachinel xuculu, xa pu churico cheta chic chi cauinac bitoh, chica vinac ꜩacoh ta chic ꜩucul cool coh ziquixtah, cohnabaxtah puch, catoc ta cut pa ꜩih iyom mamom catit camam xpiyacoc, xmuca ne chatah ta chavaxoc, ta zaquiroc ca ziquixic, ca toquexic ca nabaxic rumal vinac ꜩac, vinac bit, vinac poy, vinac anom cha ta chuxoc chicutun ibi hunahpu vuch, hunahpu vtiu camul alom, camul qaholom nimac nima ꜩÿz ah cuval ah yamanic ah chut ahꜩalam, ah raxalac, ah raxazel ah eol, ah toltecat ratit quih ra tiꜩac quixvqhaxic rumal caꜩac cabit chimala chi ixim, chi ꜩite xa chibanatahic xa pu chel apan oc chicahah chicacotah puch vchi vvach che xevqhaxic e ah t quih cate puch vcahic v quihiloxic ri xmalic chi ixim chiꜩite quih bit xeqha quri hunatit hunmama chiquech are ri mama are ah ꜩite xpiyacoc vbi. arecu riatit ahquih ahbit chi racan xmucane vbi xeqha cut ta x quitiquiba quih xa chuculu xa pu chu rico chabÿh cata caxiquin, caqhauic caꜩihontah xa chuculu ri che chahavaxic chicotox puch cumal ahꜩac ahbit. ve are ꜩucul cool ta chavax oc tazaquiroc at ixim at ꜩite, at quih, at bit. catchoconic cattaquentah xqha chire ixim q,ite, quih bit. cat quixla vloc at vqux cah macahizah vchi vvach tepeu gucumaꜩ xeqha ta xquibih cut v zuculiquil vꜩ are chuxic ri i poy aham che chiqhauic chiꜩihon bala chuvach vleu ta chuxoc xeqha cut ta xquibih huzuc xbanic poy aham che xe vinac vachinic xe vinac ꜩihonic puch are vina quil vvach vleu xevxic xe poquic xemealanic, xeqaholonic ri poy aham che macu habi qui qux ma pu habi quinaoh maui natal cah ꜩac cah bit xaloc xebinic, xechacanic maui xquinatah chic ri vqux cah quehe cut xepah chivi xa vtihtobexic xa pu v va babexic chi vinac queqhau nabec, xa chaquih qui vach mana zonol cacan, quieab mahabi qui quiquel, qui comahil, ma habi qui ticoual, qui cabchÿal cha quih coꜩ qoh qui vach. ca pichipoh cacan, quicab caie ioh quitiohil quehe cut maui xenau chivi chuvach ꜩacol bitol alai quech, quxlaai quech enabe ꜩaꜩ chivinac xevxic varal chuvach vleu Catecut qui quÿzic chic qui mayxic qui cutuxic puch xe camizax chic poy aham che ta xnohix quibutic rumal vqux cah nima butic xbanic xpe pa quivi ri e poy e aham che q,ite vtiohil ri achih, ta xahaxic, rumal ꜩacol bitol, ixoc zibac cut vtiohil ixoc. xrah oquic rumal ꜩacol bitol maui xenauic, ma pu xeqhauic chu vach cah ꜩac, cah bit banol que vinaquirizay quech quehe cut qui ca mizaxic xebutic xpe nima eol chila chicah. xe cotcovach vbi, xcotin vloc vbac qui vach, xepe camaloꜩ xcupin vla quiholom, xpe coꜩbalam. xtio quitiohil, xpe tucumbalam xtucuuic xquichouic quibaquil, quibochil xcahixic, xmuchulixic qui baquil, cahizabal qui vach; ru mal maui quinauic chuvach qui chuch, chuvach puch qui cahau, ri vqux cah, huracan vbi, cumal xquecumaric v vach vleu, xticaric quecal hab quihil hab, acabal hab xoc vla chuti chicop nima chicop xcut qui vach rumal che abah. xqhauic ronohel qui quebalquixot, qui lac, qui boh, qui ꜩi quieaa, haruh pala ronohel xcutu qui vach cax xiban chique, xohitio ixchi cut xquixcati chic xqhari qui ꜩi cac chiquech. re cu ri ꜫaa xohcoconic iuumal hu ta quih, hu ta quih, xquec zaquiric amaquel holi, holi, huqui, huqui ca vach ivumal are ta nabe capatan chivach, ixtana vinac vacamic cut xchitih ca chucab xchicaqueeh, xchicahoc puch i tiohil xqhari qui ꜫaa chique. are curi qui ꜩi xqha chic ta xqhauic, na qui pa rumal maui chiya cava, xa cohmucunic xa pu cohicuxih vloc, coh izac pu vloc yacal vbi ca cheel ivumal ta quixvaic xere cohivuchaah vi maui cohqhauic mata cu mi xohcamic chiue hu pa cha maui mi xixnauic xixnau ta cut chiuih ta cut xohzach vi, vacamic cut xchitih cabac qo pa ca chi xquixcatio xeqha ri ꜩi chique ta xcut qui vach. are chicuri qui xot, qui boh xchau chic chique: cax, va xi ban chique xac ca chi, xacca vach; amaquel oh ꜩacal chuuicac cohicato maui cax xcanao, xchitih cut xquix caporoh xcha ri qui boh ronohel xcutu qui vach. areri abah ri quixcub chitaninic chipe paꜫaꜫ taeal chi quiholom, cax xban chique. anilabic que mal malihab chic querah acanic chuvi ha xa chiu vlih ha queꜩac vloc, querah acan chuvi che queqhaquix vloc ruma che querah oc pahul xa chiyuch hul chi qui vach; que he cut vcayohic vinac ꜩac vinac bit, eꜩixel, e ꜩalaꜩoxel chi vinac xmayxic, xcutuxic quichi qui vach conohel, xqha cut are retal ri qoy qo pa quechelah vacamic, are xqohe vi retal rumal xache quitiohil xcohic rumal ahꜩac ahbit, arecuri qoy queheri vinac chivachinic retal hule vinac ꜩac, vinac bit xapoy, xapu ahamche.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y díxeron aquel huracan, con tepeu y gucumaꜩ cuando ledixeron al de el sol, o adivino, al formador adívíno alcanzo, y se vnío de ello otra vez se haga nuestro hombre formado, y nuestro hombre edífícado ota vez sustentado, y alímentado, quenos ínvoque, y quese acuerde de nosotros. entrad en la consulta abuela, y abuelo nuestro xpíyacoc, y xmucane, como se podra sembrar y aclarar, nuestro ser ínvocado, nuestro ser adorado, nuestro ser acordado por el hombre formado, y edificado. y por el hombre nuestro pobre, y así se díga, mostrad vuestro nombre hun ahpu vuch, hun ahpu vtíu dos vezes madre, dos vezes Padre, grandes, grande pízote, el de los chalchíguítes, el de el toríron, el de la tabla, o trono, el de el toltecat abuela de el sol, abuela de la luna, así seaís dichos por nuestros formados, y críaturas. echad suertes con maíses, y con ꜩítes y sea solo hecho, sí saldra, sí labraremos, y lo tallaremos su voca y su cara de palo se les fue dicho alos adívínos. y luego su echadura de las suertes, su ser saludado loque sortearon con el maís y el ꜩíte. a sol, a formadura dixeron vnabieja y ">un viejo a ellos. y ">el viejo era el de las suertes de el ꜩité, y se llamaba xpíyacoc, y la vieja adivina de el sol, y de la formadura, se llamaba chíracan xmucane. y esta vieja adivina de el sol y dixeron quando empezaron a adivinar el sol solo se junten y se avnen decid nuestra nueua, nuestro oydo hablá, parlád, si es conveniente sea labrado el palo por los formadores, y sí es este es quese ha de sustentar, y alímentar, quando se siembre, y acbare, dí tu maís, tu ꜩíte, tu sol, tu formadura llamad, y segued le dixo al maís, y al ꜩíte, al sol, y ála formadura. y tu corazon de el zielo tened verguenza, no afrenteís a tepeu, y cucumaꜩ. y entonzes respondiendo el q,íte y el maís dixeron la verdad haceldo así queasí estara bíen, y hablara el pa lo enlabrandolo. y luego fue hecha la íma gen de el hombre depalo, y hablo como hombre. y este fue el hombre quehizieron, y se multiplicaron, tuvieron, hijos, e hijas, empero salieron tontos sin corazon, sin entendimiento; y así no se acordaron mas desu críador; sino queen vano estubieron, y anduuieron sobre la tierra, y así no se acordaron más de el corazon de el zíelo, y así díeron de hozicos, empero esto fue solo probar, apararlos, y hazerlos gente. hablaban, empero estaba seca su cara, estaban abromados, y pesa dos, en pies y manos. no tenían sangre, ní sudor, ní gordura, estavan secas, y palídas sus maxillas. estaban sus pies amarillos, y secos, y las manos, y amarilla su carne, y así no se acordaron mas de su críador, y hazedor, el quelos avía crído; y estos eran ya muchos, y se multíplícaron sobre la tíerra. y despues fueron acabados, y destruídos, y muertos, todos estos hombres de palo. fue embiado consultado por el corazon de el cíelo, y se hizo vn gran díluuío quevíno sobre ellos; de palo de corcho era la carne de los hombres, y de esa material fueron hechos, y labrados, por el criador y las mugeres fueron hechas de corazon de espadaña y esa fue la voluntad de el críador, hazerlos de esa material, empero no hizieron memoría, ni agradezieron al criador quelos crío el averlos críado; y así fueron muertos y a negados. vino gran resína, y pez de el zielo. y un paxaro llamado: xecotcovach, víno y les saco los ojos, y víno otro quese llamaba camaloꜩ y les corto las cabezas, y víno oto animal llamado coꜩbalam, y les comío sus carnes, y el quese llama tucumbalam, queles quebro los huesos, y los nervíos, y los hízeron harína y esto fue en castigo y escarmiento porqueno hízíeron grazías delante de su Padre y Me, y Señor. el corazon de el zíelo, quese llama huracan, y por ellos se obscuresío la haz de la tierra, y empezo vna llovízna de noche y de día. y víno todo genero de animales chicos y grandes, y los palos, y las píedras, y lesdieron en rostro, y afrentaron, y todos hablaron, las píedras, comales, platoscaxetes, ollas, perros, y tínajas. todos quantos ubo les afrentaron, y les dixeron, muy mal nos tratastes, nos mordiste, y así os mordere mos agora, dixeron los perros, y las gallínas. y las piedras de moler dixeron. fuímos muy atormentadas por vosotros todos, todos los días ala tarde ala mañana; siempre hazíendo holí, holí, huquí, huquí. (esto es el sonído de la piedra, y el chíflido quehazen almoler,) y este fue nuestro trabaxo en vuestras caras, y fueraís bíenquístos, y pues no lo fuistes, agora probareís nuestras fuerzas, moleremos vuestras carnes, y haremos harína vuestros cuerpos. esto les díxeron las píedras de moler. y los perros díxeron quando hablaron: porquenonos dabaís nuestra comida, sino quesolo estabamos mírandoos, y nos corríaís, y nos arrojabaís, y siempre estaba prevenido un palo para darnos quando comíaís, y así nos tratabaís porque no hablabamos, quízas no ubíeraís muerto agora porque no mírasteís por vosotros? así nos perdímos, agora probareís nuestros dientes, queestan en nuestra voca, os comeremos. díxeron los perros. que les dieron en rostro. y los comales, y las ollas los hablaron en esta forma: dolor, y pena nos dísteís. nuestras vocas, y nuestros rostros tíznados, siempre estabamos cociendo sobre el fuego, nos quemasteís, y no sentimos el dolor, probareís agora, y os quemaremos dixeron las ollas todas dandoles en rostro. y las piedras queeran tenamastes con furia venga el fuego, nuestras cabezas puestas pa. la olla nos hízísteís, y causasteís dolor. y andaban corriendo, desatinados, y queriendo subirse sobre las casas, y se les caía la casa, y venían abaxo. y querían subir sobre los palos, y los arroxaban los palos. y queriendo meterse en los hoyos, y se les zerraban. y así fueron destruídos, y aníquílados, y afrentados todos, y así fue dicho señal de esta gente son los monos queagora andan por los montes. y por eso quedaron por señal que solo fueron de palo hechos por el críador. y el mono por eso separeze al nombre porque es señal de otro genero de hombres hechos de palo.

↑ Contents
Part II

The Hero Twins and the Lords of Xibalba

Junajpu and Xb’alanke against the arrogant false gods, and against death itself.

04

Wuqub' Kaqix, Sipakna, and Kab'raqan

Then it was still just a faint dimness, not yet clear over the face of the earth; there was no sun yet, when one puffed himself up in greatness — Wuqub' Kaqix was his name. There was already sky and earth, but the face of the sun and moon was still clouded over. And he said: 'Only that handful of people who were drowned became like sorcerers. I am great now, above all created beings. I am their sun, I am their light, and I am also their moon,' he said. 'Great is my brightness. By me the people walk and stand, for my eyes are of silver, they gleam with precious stones, and they are jade-green like the sky. My nose gleams from afar like the moon. My throne is of silver, and when I come forth the earth grows bright. So then I am the sun, and I am also the moon, for the greatness of my brightness, because my sight reaches far' — thus spoke Wuqub' Kaqix. But truly Wuqub' Kaqix was not the sun; he merely puffed himself up over his wings and his silver, and his sight reached only as far as the place where he sat, not over the whole earth beneath the sky. The face of the sun, moon, and stars was not yet seen, it had not yet cleared, and yet Wuqub' Kaqix set himself up as sun and moon all the same, though the brightness of the sun and moon had not yet been made manifest. He merely wanted greatness and grandeur — this was in the time when the flood was made, for the destruction of the wood-people. Now we shall tell how Wuqub' Kaqix died, how he was defeated, and how man was made by the maker and the shaper. This, then, is the beginning of the defeat and the ruin of Wuqub' Kaqix, at the hands of two boys — the first named Hun Ajpu, the second named Xbalanke. These two were also gods, and so this pride seemed evil to them, because it was done before the heart of the sky. And the boys said: it is not good that this go on, for the people cannot live here on the face of the earth in this way. Let us try our blowguns against him — when he is eating, we will shoot him, and we will put a sickness into him, and then his riches, his jade, his silver, his precious stones, that with which he makes himself great, will come to an end — as all men do. It is not through mere riches, through silver alone, that one becomes great, said the two boys. And each set his blowgun upon his shoulder.

Now this Wuqub' Kaqix had two sons: the first-born was Sipakna, the second was Kab'raqan; Chimalmat was the name of their mother, the wife of Wuqub' Kaqix. Now this Sipakna — the great mountains were his food; that is what he ate. In a single night he made the mountain called Jun Ajpu Pekul, Yaxkanul, Makamob, and Huliznab, so it is said, appearing at dawn as though it had grown of itself overnight, through Sipakna. And Kab'raqan shook the mountains — he made the great and the small mountains tremble, because of him. So these two sons of Wuqub' Kaqix also puffed themselves up in greatness. Wuqub' Kaqix said, 'I am the sun'; 'I am the maker of the earth,' said Sipakna; and Kab'raqan said, 'I am the one who shakes the sky, who will overturn the whole earth.' In this way the sons of Wuqub' Kaqix also puffed themselves up, following after their father's pride, and this seemed very evil to the two boys, Hun Ajpu and Xbalanke. Our first mother and our first father had not yet been made, and so the two boys resolved on the death and the destruction of Wuqub' Kaqix, Sipakna, and Kab'raqan. Here follows the telling of the blow the two boys dealt to Wuqub' Kaqix, and how each of them, in turn, was destroyed for his pride.

Translator’s note

Revised per round-4 audit: (1) restored the drowned-people/sorcerers sense of "xaui xere vzaquetal vinac ri xbutic queheri naual vinac vqoheic" ("only that handful of people who were drowned became like sorcerers"), rather than the garbled "sign of the people" reading; (2) kept the eyes/jewels imagery strictly from "rumal puvac vbac nuvach... yamanic raxa cuval" (silver eyes glittering with jewels, jade-green like the sky) — no teeth, which is not in the K'iche' or Ximenez's Spanish and is a distinctive Tedlock/Christenson interpretive choice; (3) kept "the great mountains were his food" for "are chirechaah ri nimac huiub" / "su pasto y comida eran los grandes montes," rather than "sport," which echoes the Recinos/Goetz-Morley "played ball" reading and contradicts the source; (4) softened "walk and are victorious" to "walk and stand," a plainer rendering of "andar... y pararse" / chacabal.

K’iche’ source text · p10–p11

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p10–p11 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

Are cut xa hubic zac na tanoh vvachvleu mahabi quih hun cut cunimarizah rib, vvcub caquix vbi, qo nabe cah vleu, xa ca moymot v vach quih ic caqha curi: xaui xere vzaquetal vinac rÿ, xbutic queheri naual vinac vqoheic in nim quiqohe chic chuvi vinac ꜩa vinac bit in vquih in pu vzac in naipu riquil ta chuxoc, nim nu zaquil in binibal, in pu chacabal rumal vinac. rumal puvac vbac nuvach xa catiltotic chi yamanic raxa cuval naypu ve raxcauacoh chi abah queheri v va cah are curi nu ꜩam zachuluhuh chinah queheri ic puvac cut nu calibal cazac paque vvach vleu ta quinel vloc chuvach nu calibal quehe cut in quih vi in pu ic rumal zaquil al zaquil qahol ta chuxoc rumal chinah copon vi nu vach: qhari vucub caquix macu quiꜩih are ta quih, ri, vcub caquix xere cunimarizah rib rivxic vpuvaC xere cut tocol vi v vach ri chicube vi mana ronohelta vxecah copon vi vvach. maha cut qui quiloc v vach quih, ic chumil mahaoc cazaquiroc quehe cut cucobizah vi rib ri vucub caquix chiquihil, chi iquil xa maha chicatunoc chicalahoboc vzaquil quih ic xa xuraih nimal icouen are ta xbanic butic cumal poy aham che quehe cut xchicabÿh chic ta xcamic vucub caquix ta xchacatahic, ta xbanatahic vinac rumal ahꜩac, ahbit. Vae vxe vchacatahic v yicoxic chi puch v quih vucub ca quix cumal e caib qaholab hun ahpu vbi hun, xbalanque vbi vcab xavi e cabauil rumal iꜩel xquilo ri nimarizai rib xrah vban chuvach vqux cah, xqha curi qaholab mavi vꜩ ta chuxoc mavi chiqaze vinac varal chuvach vleu quehe cut chicatih, v vbaxic chuvi recha chica v vbah vi chiri chicacoh vi viab ta quizoc v quinomal, v xit, v puvac v cual, v yamanic ri cucacabeh que he cut chubano ronohel vinac. maui are chivinaquir vi ꜫaꜫal ri xa puvac ta chuxoc xeqha ri qaholab huhun chi vb quitelen qui cabichal are curi vvcub caquix e caib v qahol are nabe al ri zipacna, vcab al chicut ri cabracan chimalmat cut vbi qui chuch rixoquil ri vvcub caquix are curi zipacna are chirechaah ri nimac huiub. ri chicac hun ahpu pe cul ya xcanul macamob huliznab chuchaxic vbi huiub xqolic ta chi zaquiric xa hun aꜫab chi vi- naquiric rumal ri zipacna are ri chicuri cabracan chizilab huiub rumal chi ne bouic chuti huiub, nima huyub rumal, xaui quehe nimarizabal quib xquibano vqahol vvcub caquix ixva, in quih xqha vucub caquix inva, in banol vleu xqha ri zipacna, in chicut quiyou cah chinvlih ronohel vleu xqhari cabracan xavi vqahol vvcub caquix xaui chiri xquicam vi quinimal chirih qui cahau are cut iꜩel xquil vi qaholab, maha chibantahoc canabe chuch canabe cahau quehe cut xnohix vi qui camic qui zachic cumal qaholab.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Y entonzes avía poca clarídad sobre la haz de la tíerra, y aun no auía sol. y entonzes vno llamado vvcub caquix. (esto es siete guacamayas) se ensoberbzía. auía entonzes cielo y tierra, pero estaba turbía la luz de el sol, y la luna. y este vvcub caquíx, dezía: solo aquella poca gente que seanego fueron como brujos. yo agora sere grande sobre todas las críaturas, yo soy su sol, yo soy su blancura, yo sere su luna. es grande mí clarídad, y soy por quíen hande andar los hombres, y pararse. porque mís ojos son de plata solo resplandeze con las piedras preziosas, y son piedras verdes como el zíelo. mís narízes resplandezen delexos como la luna. y es de plata mi trono, y quando salgo se aclara la tierra; y así yo soy el sol, y soy luna por la clarídad de los vasallos que tender, porque mí vista alcanza muy lexos: esto dixo el dicho vvcub caquíx pero no era sol el tal vvcub caquíx; sino quele ensoberbecía sus ríquezas, y su plata; y solo alcanzaba su vista a aquel lugar donde estaba, y no alcanzaba su vista a todo el mundo. yaun todavía no se auía vísto la cara al sol, a la luna, ní alas estrellas, ní auía aclarado. ya sí seponía el vvcub caquix por sol, y por luna pero no se auía manífestado la claridad de el sol, y la luna. silo deseo lagrandeza, y sobrepujar, y esto fue quando se hízo el díluvío por la destrucción de los hombres de palo; y ora trataremos quando murío el vvcub caquix, quando fue venzido, y quando fue hecho el hombre por el criador. Esta es, o fue la causa, de la destruccíon de vvcubcaquix por los dos muchachos, hun ahpu, así se llamaba el vno (vn tirador) y el otro llamado. xbalanque (diminutivo de tigre, y venado) estos tambíen eran díoses y por eso les parezío mal, aquella soberbía porque la hizo ante el corazon de el zielo. y dixeron los dos muchachos, no sera bíen queesto pase adelante porque no vívíran los hombres aquí en la tierra; y así probaremos a tirarle con la cerbatana, quando coma, le tíraremos, y le meteremos una enfermedad, y entonzes se a cabará sus ríquezas, sus piedras preciosas, y sus chalchíguítes, quees con lo quese engrandeze, y así lo haran todos los hombres. no porque tenga ríquezas se ha de hazer grande: dixeron los dos muchachos. y cada uno con su zervatana al hombro. y aqueste vvcubcaquíx tenía dos hijos, el prímero se llamaba zípacna, y el segundo se llamaba cab racan. y su madre de ellos se llamaba chímalmatqueera la muger de vvcubcaquíx y aqueste su híjo zípacna, su pasto, y comida eran los grandes montes y esto ademas en vna noche amanezío hecho elzerro llamado hun ahpupecul yaxcanul macamob huliznab porque en vna noche zípacna hazía vn monte, y su hermano cabrracan (esto es de dos pies) meneaba, y hazía temblar, los montes, grandes y chicos, y así tambíen se ensoberbecíeron estos dos híjos de vvcubcaquix. y así vvcubcaquix dixo aueísde saber queyo soy el sol; y soy el hazedor de la tierra dixo zípacna; y yo soy díxo cabracan el que muevo la tíerra, deríbare toda la toerra; y así mesmo los hijos de vvcub caquíx así mesmo se ensoberbezieron, por la soberbia de su Padre yesto les parecío muy mal alos dos muchachos, hunahpu y xbalanque. y aun todauía no se auían hecho nuestros prímeros Padres y Madres yasí se consultó por los dos muchachos susmuertes, de vvcubcaquíx, de zípacna y cabrracan. y aquí sesigue el dezir el vodocazo que los dos muchachos díeron a vvcub caquíx, y como cada vno fue destruído por su soberbía.

05

Junajpu and Xb'alanke move against Seven Macaw's household

This is how the two boys shot Seven Macaw down, and how, one after another, the things he made much of himself for were brought low. Seven Macaw had a great tree, a nance tree, and it was the fruit of that nance that was Seven Macaw's food; each day he climbed to the top of the tree for its fruit. This had been seen by Hunahpu and Xbalanque. They lay hidden at the foot of that same tree, the two boys concealed in the leaves, when Seven Macaw came to it. He was already up in it, feeding at the top of the nance, when Hunahpu shot him: the pellet struck straight in his jaw, and crying out he came crashing down from the top of the tree to the ground. Hunahpu ran to seize him at once, meaning to grapple him, but Seven Macaw wrenched off Hunahpu's arm, twisting it back at the shoulder, and so it came away. Hunahpu let him go then; that much they had done right, the two boys, not to be beaten outright by Seven Macaw first. Seven Macaw went off carrying Hunahpu's arm home with him, holding his own jaw as he went. What has happened to you, said Chimalmat, the wife of Seven Macaw, when she saw him. What else but those two demons, who shot me with their blowgun and knocked my jaw out of place; all my teeth are loose now, and they ache. But here, hung over the fire, is the arm of one of them: let them come for it, said Seven Macaw. So he hung up Hunahpu's arm.

Now Hunahpu and Xbalanque took counsel over this. What they resolved they carried to an old man, one whose hair was truly white by now, and to an old woman, and truly great was the age of the two of them; already they walked bent double. The old man's name was Zaqui Nim Aq, and the old woman's name was Zaqui Nima Tzis — so the two boys called the old ones. Come with us, grandfather, grandmother, to fetch back our arm at the house of Seven Macaw. We will follow close behind you, since we are your grandchildren, whose father and mother have died; and so, when he questions you, tell him it grieves you to see us tagging after you, and that what you do is draw out the worm that eats at teeth. He will take us for children merely, and we will be at your side to counsel you, the two boys said. Very well, said the old ones. And so they went to the corner of the house of Seven Macaw where he lay reclined on his seat. The two old ones passed by there, with the two boys playing behind them, and they went beneath the house of the lord while he groaned from the pain in his jaw. When Seven Macaw saw the old man and the old woman with the two at their heels, he asked them: where do you come from, grandparents? We go about seeking our sustenance, lord, they said. What is your sustenance? Are these your children who go with you? No, lord, they are our grandchildren, but we pity them, and from what we find we give them a share, said the old ones. Now the lord was in great pain from his tooth and could hardly speak. I beg you, take pity on me. What is it you do? What do you cure? said the lord. What we cure, lord, said the old ones, is this: we draw out the worm that gnaws the teeth, and we mend eyes, and we also set broken bones. Very well; then if that is true, cure my teeth, for I am without rest, I cannot sleep, on account of my teeth and my eyes, since two demons shot me with a pellet and I can no longer eat; so have mercy on me. Very well, lord: it is a worm doing you this harm; we will draw out those teeth and put others in their place. Might that not go badly, that I, being a lord, live only by my teeth and my eyes? We will put others in their place, ground bone will we set there — but this ground bone was nothing but white maize. Very well, said the lord, draw them, help me. And so they drew out the teeth of Seven Macaw, and it was only white grains of maize they put in their place, and these gleamed white in his mouth; at once his face fell in, and he no longer looked like a lord. Then, finishing the drawing of his teeth, they cured his eyes, peeling back the surface of the eyes, and stripped away the metal that had made him proud; and he felt nothing of it. So it was finished, all that show he made of himself; he no longer looked great, nor was there anything to be feared in him. All this was the counsel of Hunahpu and Xbalanque.

So Seven Macaw died, and Hunahpu took back his arm. Chimalmat too died, the wife of Seven Macaw. So was lost the wealth of Seven Macaw: it was the healer who took all the precious stones in which he had gloried here on the face of the earth. Then Hunahpu's arm was set back in its place, and it healed, and it was as it had been before. For the old man and the old woman who had done this were themselves gods — a divine grandfather, a divine grandmother — and only because of the death of Seven Macaw did they act as they did, since it displeased them, the airs he had given himself; and after this the two boys went on their way again. [The text here becomes hard to follow, breaking into short, disordered phrases: it seems to tell of the boys rejoicing over what they had done — he has died, we have not been beaten — and of a company of four hundred boys spoken of together with them, but the sense cannot be made out with confidence.] It was the heart of the sky who brought all this about.

Translator’s note

Revised to correct three fidelity problems flagged in round 4 review. (1) Naming: the actor throughout is Junajpu (Hunahpu), the hero twin — never "Hun Hunahpu," who is the twins' dead father and a different character. The manuscript's own spelling wavers, but the section's identity (confirmed by its title) is unambiguous, so I standardized on Hunahpu. (2) The line about Seven Macaw's lost wealth now follows the literal sense of both the K'iche' ("xa ahqun xcamouic ri cuval yamanic") and Ximénez's Spanish ("el medico tomo todas las piedras preciosas... que le ensoberbezieron"): it is the healer — the disguised old man — who takes the jewels Seven Macaw had prided himself on; Seven Macaw is not killed "at the hands of the healers," and the stones are taken, not left behind. (3) The divine identity of the old couple ("naual atit, naual mama" / Spanish "eran dioses") is now stated in the text itself rather than deferred to a note. I also avoided reusing "Heart of Sky" and "self-exaltation," known lexical choices of specific published translators, rendering the same sense in my own words ("the heart of the sky," lower-cased as description rather than proper epithet; "the airs he had given himself"). The closing passage remains genuinely garbled in the source — it runs into short, disordered phrases including what appears to be the boys' triumphant speech and a reference to "four hundred boys" — and is now flagged in-line as an uncertain gap rather than silently smoothed over or covered by a single bracket."

K’iche’ source text · p12

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph id p12 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

vach tapal chacan chui che hutaquih xilo macut rechabal cumal ri hun ahpu, xbalan que, quicacalen chicut chuxe che ri vvcub ca quix e maꜩamoh vlo ri caib qaholab pa xac che ta xopon cut caibqaholab
