Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Este vvcubcaquíx tená vn palo, o arbol de nanses porque esta era slo sucomída y todos los días se subía al arbol de nanses a comer la fruta esto auían vísto hun ahpu, y xbalanque queera su comída, y se pusieron en espía los dos muchachos de baxo de el arbol escondidos entre las hojas de la íerua, y entonzes llego vvcub caquíc, y estando ya subido en el arbol entonzes hun ahpu le tíro vn vodocazo que fue derecho y le dío en la quíxada, y dando gritos cayo en el suelo y luego que hun ahpu vío caído a vvcubcaquíx fue a toda prísa fue agachado a cogerlo, y entonzes vvcub caquíx le cogío el brazo y avvcu ahunahpu, y selo arranco, y se learranco el brazo de la punta deel hombro y entonzes hun ahpu solto a vvcubcaquíx y así quedaron bien los dos muchachos, por quenoquedaron venzidos por vvcub caquíx y fuese a su casa llevando el brazo de hun ahpu, eíba teníendose las quixadas. quete a sucedido a vsted? dixo chimalmat asu marído vvcubcaquíx. quehadeser quedos demonios me tiraron conzerbatana, y me desquízíaron las quíxadas, todos los díen tes se me menean, y meduelen mucho; pero aquí traigo vnbrazo de vno de ellos colgaldo al humo sobre el fuego pa.que vengan por el los dos demoníos. dixo el vvcubcaquix. y entonzes colgo el brazo dehun ahpu. y entonzes hun ahpu, y xbalanque consultaron quedeuían hazer, y auíendolo consultado lo fueron a dezir a vn viejo queya estaba con la cabeza blanca y auna víeja, quedeuerdad ya era muy vieja, y tanta era la vegez de ambos que ya andaban corcobados. el viejo se llamaba: zaquínímac, y la víeja sellamaba zaquíníma zíz. vn grande pízote blanco. y les dixeron los dos muchachos al viejo y ala víeja acompañadnos para ír a traer nuestro brazo acasa de de vvcub caquíx nosotros yremos de tras de de vosotros como quesomos vuestros níetosquesehan muerto nuestro Padre y Madre y así en preguntándoos de zíd que andamos tras de vosotros; y quepasaís de sacar el gusano quesecome las muelas, y los díentes, y así como a muchachos nos vera vvcub caquíx, y nosotros te aconsejaremos esto dixeron los dos muchachos. estabíen dixeron los viejos y entonzes se fueron ala esquina dela casa de vvcubcaquix, el qual estaba recostado en su trono, y entonzes pasaron los dos víejos, y los dos muchachos jugando detras de ellos, y pasaron por de baxo la casa de vvcubcaquix. y estaba gritando de el dolor de la muela y víendolos vvcubcaquíx alos dos víejos y alos muchachos les pregunto, de adon de venís abuelos? nosotros Señorandamos buscando nuestro remedío dixeron como buscaís vuestro remedio? son hijos vuestros esos queos acompañan? no Señor son nuestrosníetos; sino que les tenemos lastíma Señor de lo quehallamos lesdamos vn pedazo de tortilla. dixeron los víejos. y en esto esta el Señormuy malo de la muela, y quea pura fuerza hablaba. yos os suplíco que me tengaís lastíma quees lo quehazeís? quees lo quecuraís? dixo el SeñorSor lo que cura- mos nosotros díxeron los víejos, es sacar elgusano de los díentes, y muelas y curamos los ojos, y tambíen curamos quebraduras de huesos. estabíen, y síasí es verdad curadme mis dientes, queestoy sín sosíego y no duermo, y tambíen me duelen los ojos, porque esto tuvo príncipío de que dos demoníos me dieron vn bodocazo, y así no puedocomer; yasí tenedme míserícordía. porque se me menean todos los díentes todos. esta bíen Señor gusano es el que lehaze daño sacare mos esos dientes, y le pondremos otros en su lugar. o quízas no sera bueno eso porque de esa suerte soy Señor y con eso solo como con los díentes. y con mís ojos. y dixeron ellos pondremos otros en lugar de esos, pondremos gueso molído, pero este gueso molído era solo maíz blanco. estabíen díxo el Señor sacadlos ayudadlos. y entonzes lesacaron los díentes a vvcub caquíx, y solo maís blanco fue lo que le pusieron en lugar dedientes; y estaban relumbrando los maíses en su voca, y luego se le cayo la cara, y nunca mas parecío Señory acabaron de sacar le todos sus dientes, y le quedo la voca moreteada y quando lecuraron los ojos a vvcubcaquíx ledesollaron las níñas delos ojos, y le quítaron toda laplata, y no lo síntío, y sacada quedomírando pero ya no era grande ní se ensoberbezía. y esto fue hecho por consejo de hunahpu, y de xbalanque, y se murío vvcubcaquíx, y entonces tomo subrazo hunahpu. ytambíen murío chimalmat, la mugger de vvcubcaquíx. y así seperdío su riqueza de vvcubcaquíx. y el medico tomo todas las píedras prezíosas que le ensoberbezíeron aquí en la tierra. y el Viejo y la vieja quelo hízíeron eran díoses y quando tomaron su brazo lo pusieron en su lugar y se revnío, y quedo bueno. y solo por la muerte de vvcubcaquíx quisieron hazerlo así, porque les parecío mal su soberbía; y despues se fueron otravez los dos muchachos. y esto fue hecho así por mandado deel corazon de el zíelo.

06

Sipakna and the four hundred youths

Now this is the record of what Sipakna did, the eldest son of Wuqub' Kaqix. "Mountains are my making," Sipakna would say of himself. This Sipakna was bathing at the edge of a river when the four hundred youths passed by, dragging a log to be the post of their house. Four hundred of them together had cut a great tree to serve as the mother-beam of their house of thatch. When Sipakna saw them he went to where the four hundred youths were, and asked them, "What is it you're doing, boys?" "Only that we cannot lift it," they answered, meaning the great beam. "Lift it, then, and I will carry it," said Sipakna. "Where must it go? What is it for, that you cut it?" "It is the mother-beam of our house," they answered. "Very well," said Sipakna, and taking hold of it he raised it onto his shoulder and carried it all the way to the door of the house of the four hundred youths.

Then they said to him, "Stay with us — have you a mother, a father?" "I have none," answered Sipakna. "Then tomorrow," said the four hundred youths, "we go again to bring another log for our house." "Very well," said Sipakna. And the four hundred youths took counsel among themselves and said, "What shall we do about this boy? Let us kill him, for what he has done is not right — he alone lifted the log. What we shall do is this: we will dig a great pit, and there we will cast him down into the hole. We will tell him, go down and dig out earth for us, since we cannot reach it, and when he is bent over in the hole we will drop a great log down upon him, and there he will die in the pit." So they dug a great pit, very deep. Then they called Sipakna and said to him, "We hold you dear — go down now and dig out more earth for us, since we cannot reach it." "Very well," he said, and went down into the pit. "Dig it well," they told him, "keep digging until you have made it very deep." "Very well," he said.

Then he began to dig, but the hole he made was to save himself, for he knew they meant to kill him; he dug a side-passage — a hole set at an angle, the width of an arm — where he could hide himself. "Is it dug already? Is it deep enough now?" the boys called down to him. "I am still digging — I will call up to you when it is done," he answered from below, in the pit. But he was not digging the floor of the pit for his own burial; he was digging the hole where he would hide. And he called up from below, though by then he had already made his escape: "Come and take away the earth that is dug out at the bottom of the pit, for truly I have made it deep. Do you not hear my voice? Yet I hear yours — it comes to me like one echo, then a second echo, from this hole with two turns in it," said Sipakna from within his pit; and there he crouched, calling out from time to time.

Then the four hundred youths dragged the great log and let it fall with a crash into the pit. "Let no one speak, let none of us say a word — only listen for his cry," they said, speaking this among themselves in secret, so that their faces would not betray them to one another. Then they let the log drop into the pit, and when it struck bottom Sipakna cried out once, and then fell silent; his cry was cut short. Then the four hundred youths rejoiced, saying, "How well this has turned out for us! He is dead. Had he lived, it would have gone badly for us, for he had already forced his way in among us, the four hundred youths." And rejoicing still, they said, "Now, for three days, let us make our maize-beer, and on the third day we four hundred will drink it." "Tomorrow we will look, and the day after we will look, to see whether the ants come out over the earth once it stinks and rots — then our hearts will be at ease, and we will drink without fear," they said. And Sipakna, down in his pit, heard everything the youths said.

On the second day the ants came swarming out in great numbers, going to and fro, and gathered beneath the log; some carried hair, others carried the nail-parings of Sipakna. And when the four hundred youths saw this they said, "The deceiver has finished him! Look, the ants have come, they are gathering, and they carry his hair and his nails — look what we have done." But Sipakna was alive; he himself had cut the hair from his head and had bitten off his own nails, and he gave these to the ants, so that the four hundred youths believed him dead. And on the third day the youths began their drinking, and all four hundred grew drunk. And while they were all drunk and no longer alert, Sipakna brought the house down upon their heads, and all were crushed — not one, not even two, escaped him: all were killed by Sipakna, the son of Wuqub' Kaqix.

Such was the death of the four hundred youths. And it is said that in the sky they took the place of the seven stars called the Pleiades, and among themselves they are named Motz, which means "heap," for it was in a heap that they died. But perhaps this too is only a tale. Now we shall tell how Sipakna himself was brought down, by those two boys, Jun Ajpu and Ixb'alanke.

Translator’s note

Corrected against the K'iche' directly. (1) Removed the invented "cries out to you" idiom: the K'iche' ("catarih cahoc") and Spanish ("hasta quelo hagas muy profundo") both just say to dig until very deep — no crying-out phrase exists in the source, so the earlier translator_note's Spanish quotation was fabricated and has been dropped along with the idiom. (2) The Motz gloss is now actually present in the translated text ("named Motz, which means 'heap,' for it was in a heap that they died"), matching K'iche' "moꜩ vbi... xa zacbal ꜩih lo" / Spanish "se llaman moꜩ. esto es monton, porque de monton fueron muertos." (3) Sipakna's echo speech now renders the K'iche' "hun elebal caib elebal" literally as "one echo, then a second echo" rather than flattening it. Naming was rechecked against the K'iche'/Spanish sense rather than any published edition: "Wuqub' Kaqix" (no hyphen), "Sipakna," and "Jun Ajpu, Ixb'alanke" (K'iche' "hun ahpu, xbalanque") follow the project's own modernized orthography, and the opening/closing frame sentences were reworded away from the "deeds of X, son of Y" / "maker of the mountains" phrasing shared by prior published translations, aiming instead at a plain, independent rendering of the literal sense even where it is less polished. Revision (round 1): fixed a sense-inverting error in paragraph 5 — K'iche' "mahabi chic hun caib xcolotah chiquech" / Spanish "ni vno, ni dos escaparon" means not even one or two escaped, but the prior draft read as though every youth escaped; reworded to "not one, not even two, escaped him." Also softened "that creature" to "the deceiver" for K'iche' "qaxtoq" / Spanish "demonio" (a devil/deceiver term, not a vague "creature"), and smoothed the Pleiades line to "in the sky they took the place of the seven stars" for a less awkward rendering of "en lugar de las siete cabrillas."

K’iche’ source text · p13

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph id p13 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y aquívan las obras de zípacna, el primer hijo de vvcubcaquíx. yo soy el hazedor de los montes dize zípacna. y este zípacna se estaba bañando en vn río quando pasaron los cuatrocientos muchachos que llevaban arrastran do vn palo para pílar de su casa, cuatrozien tos de montín, y cortaron, un gran palo para madre de su casa de paxa, y quando vío esto zipacna fue donde estaba los cuatrozíentos muchachos, y les pregunto. que es lo quehazeís muchachos? este palo respondieron queno lo podemos levantar, levantadlo díxo zípacna, lo llevare yo, adonde ha de ír? de que sírve, opara quelo aueís cortado? para madre de nuestracasa respondíeron ellos. estabien dixo zípacna, y tírando de el, y lo cargo, y lo lleuo hasta lapuerta de la casa de los cuatrozientos muchachos. y entonzes ellos le díxeron quedate consotros tíenes Madre. o Padre. no tengo, respondío zípacna. mañana díxeron los quatrozíentos muchachos bolberemos atraer otropalo para pilar de nuestra casa. enorabuena dígo zípacna. y entraron ellos enconsejo, los cuatroçíentos muchachos, y dixeron, queharemos con estemuchacho, matemoslo, porque no es bueno esto quehaze, el solo leuanto el palo. Lo que haremos sera: haremos vn gran hoyo y allí lo arrojaremos abajos en el hoyo ledíremos anda asacar tierra de aquel hoyo, y quando el este ínclínado en el hoyo le echaremos vnpalo grande, y allí moríra en el hoyo. y luego hízíeron vn gran hoyo, muy hondo, entonzes llamaron azípacna, y le dígeron: nosotros somos tus querídos, andad y cabad otropo en el hoyo porque no al canzamos. esta bíen dixo el, y baxo al hoyo, caba bíen le dígeron, hasta quelo hagas muy profundo, estabien dixo el. y entonzes empezo acabar, pero el hoyo quehizo fue para líbrarse, porquesupo. que lo querían matar, ycabo vn hoyo de lado, vn brazo hízo de hoyo, en donde se libro; ya esta cabado, ya ahondado ledíxeron los muchachos? aun estoy cabando, yo os auísare, y llamare quando ya este cabado, el hoyo dixo alla abaxo zípacna, en el hoyo; em pero no cababa el asíento de el hoyo, pa. su entierro; síno vn hoyo en quelíbrarse. y despues llamo de alla abaxo zípacna; pero ya estaba escapado quando llamo. venid tomad la tíerra queesta cabada enel asiento de el hoyo, porque ya deverdad he afondado mucho. no oys mí vos, y yo os oygo el eco, y oygo vuestra voz aunqueestoy en vn hoyo de dos bueltas, dixo zípacna en su hoyo, y allí estaba agachado, y gritaba a menudo. y entonzes los muchachos arrastraron, el palo y lo arrojaron con estruendo abaxo en el hoyo, y dixeron callen todos nadie hable síno oygamos quando grite, y ellos hablaban en secreto, entresí. y entonzes arrojaron elpalo enel hoyo ymquando diounavoz cayo alpalo y entonzes se alegraron, y dixeron oquebueno queesto quehemos hecho. ya murío y sí vbíera vívído nos vbíera hecho mucho mal porque se auía ya metido entrenosotros los cuatroçientos muchachos. ydixeron alegrandose; agora lo que haremos sera en estos tres días hazer nuestra chichi, y alos tresdías beberemos nosotros los cuatrozientos muchachos nuestra chichi. mañana veremos, y pasado mañana veremos, sívíenen acaso las hormigas en la tierra quando híeda, y se pudra, y entonzes sesentara nuestro corazon, y beberemos sín cuídado dixeron. y zípacna lo oya enhoyo quando lo hablaron los muchachos. y luego alsegundo día salieron de monton las hormigas, y andaban, y abundaban y llegaron de baxo d el palo, y muy ap vnas traían pelos otras traíanvnas dezípacna, y quando lo vieron los muchachos, dixeron, y aparezío aquel demonio. Mirad las hormigas víníeron, y se juntaron ytraen todas pelos, y vñas mírad loque hemos hecho. y zípacna estaba vivo y el se corto las vñas, y los cabellos de su cabeza, y con la voca se corto las vñas, y se las daba alas hormigas, y así pensaron queauía muerto, los cuatrozientos muchachos. y el terzero día empezo labebida y se emborracharon todos los muchachos. y estando todos borrachos los cuatroçientos muchachos, yano sentían. luego fue derribado el rancho sobre sus cabezas por zípacna, y todos fueron aporreados, y nívno, ni dos escaparon de los cuatrozientos muchachos, fueron muertos por el zípacna ho de vvcubcaquix. y así fueron las muertes delos cuatrozíentos muchachos y así se dijo queestos entraron en lugar de las síete cabríllas en el zielo quese llaman, moꜩ. esto es monton, porque de monton fueron muertos. y estoquízas sera mentíra. agora diremos como fue venzído zípacna, por a quellos dos muchachos hunahpu, y xbalanque.

07

Kab'raqan brought low

What follows is an account of how Zipacna was fought and killed, when he was overcome a second time by the two boys, Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque.

It sat heavy on the boys' hearts that Zipacna had killed the four hundred boys. His food was only fish and crab — that was all he hunted along the edges of the rivers, day after day. By day he went about looking for what he would eat; by night he carried mountains on his back.

So Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque made the likeness of a crab. Out of the leaf of the tree called ec they made its great claws; out of another, smaller leaf called pahac they made its lesser limbs; and its shell, and its underside too, they made of laxa — a flat slab of stone. They set it belly-up. Then they put it inside a cave, beneath a great mountain called Meawan — the place where he would be brought down.

Then the boys went out and came upon Zipacna at the river. "Where are you going, boy?" they asked him. "I am not going anywhere," Zipacna answered, "I am only looking for my food." "What is your food?" "Only fish, only crab — and I have found none. Since the day before yesterday I have eaten nothing; I can no longer bear the hunger," Zipacna told them.

Then Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque said to him: "There is a crab down in the ravine, and truly it is a very great one — how well you would eat it! We wanted to catch it ourselves, but it bit us, and we grew afraid of it, so we did not take hold of it."

"Have pity on me — come with me, take me there," Zipacna said. "We do not want to," they said. "Go by yourself; you will not lose the way. Follow the river upward, and you will come out right at it, beneath the great mountain, where it sounds, roaring, down in the ravine — just go straight there," Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque told him.

"Alas for me — have you truly not caught it yourselves?" said Zipacna. "Come, then — I will show you where there are many birds, where you may shoot with your blowguns; I alone know where they are." And there, in view of the boys, he went in beneath the stone slab.

Then they set out, Zipacna going along with them, and they came to the ravine. The crab lay there on its side, its shell glinting red — and there, beneath the ravine, was the boys' hidden device. "Very good," said Zipacna, rejoicing, for he was already eager to eat it, being faint with hunger. He tried to go in lying flat, and the crab kept climbing up and away from him. So he came back out, and the boys asked him: "Did you not catch it?" "No — I did not catch it; it very nearly got away from me, climbing on upward." "And truly, will you catch it? Do not make us come back for nothing," the boys said to him. "We too tried lying flat, and it bit us. It is best that you go in climbing upward," he was told.

So then he went in face-up, climbing; and once he had gone in, when only his knees still remained outside, the mountain crumbled and settled down quietly upon his chest, and he did not rise again. And Zipacna was turned to stone.

So Zipacna was brought down by the boys Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque — he who, it is told, made the mountains in ancient times, the eldest son of Vucub Caquix. Beneath the mountain called Meawan he was brought down; it was only by a kind of marvel that he was overcome.

The second one, he who exalted himself — now his account shall be told.

Translator’s note

Round 4 revision addressing the audit's remaining faithfulness flags. (1) Restored the crab's construction materials as named in the K'iche'/Spanish: the shell and underside made of "laxa," a flat slab of stone, and the small limbs from the leaf named "pahac" (both terms now given explicitly, not paraphrased away). (2) Restored the omitted dialogue exchange in which the boys tell Zipacna, after his first failed attempt, "And truly, will you catch it? Do not make us come back for nothing... we too tried lying flat, and it bit us. It is best that you go in climbing upward" — so the fatal upward-entry method is again the boys' device, not Zipacna's own trial-and-error. (3) Restored "in view of the boys he went in [beneath the stone slab]" rather than the vaguer "in front of." (4) Changed "a kind of trick" to "a kind of marvel," closer to the sense of nawal/milagro (miracle/wonder) than a trick or ruse. On resemblance: reworded the opening formula and the "his food was only fish and crab" sentence away from the Goetz & Morley cadence flagged in the audit ("Here now is the defeat and death of..."; "The only food of Zipacna was..."), rendering them independently and somewhat more plainly from the literal K'iche'/Spanish rather than reaching for another published translator's phrasing. Names (Zipacna, Hun Ahpu, Xbalanque, Vucub Caquix, Meawan) retained in the manuscript's own spelling throughout.

K’iche’ source text · p14

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph id p14 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

Are chic vchacatahic, v camic zipacna, ta xchac chic cumal ri e caib qaholab hun ahpu, xbalanque, are chic v yoe qui qux caholab ri omuch chi qaholab xecamic rumal zipacna xa car, xa tap chuꜩucuh chi tac a, xere chirechaah hu ta quih pa quih chivacatic ta chuꜩucuh recha. chacab cut chirecah huyub cate cut vhalvachixic hun nima tap cumal hun ahpu xbalanque are cut xquicoh ri v vach ec. ri mac ec qo patac quechelah are v xul tap xuxic pa hac chicut v coc eab xquicoho zelabah curi v va rachac tap rihouohic cate cut ta xquicoh vcoc chuxe pec, chuxe nima huyub; meauan vbi huyub xchacatah vi cate cut ta xeperi qaholab xquicu ri zipacna chiya, apa catbe vi at qahol xchax curi zipacna? mahabi quin be vi, xa vecha canuꜩucuh, ix qaholab qha curi zipacna. naquipa au echa? xa car, xa tap, xma qo chivi canu rico cabihir chincanah rechaxic, maui canuchih chic vaih xqha zipacna chiquech hun ahpu xbalanque huna relatap qo vla xe zivan quiꜩih chi ni ma tap caquihtala cha vechaah lo. xa cohvtio mi xrah cachapo cacaxibih quib rumal machibeon cachapa xe qhari hun ahpu xbalanque qui tocaba nu vach quibe ta ivaba ixqaholab xqha ri zipacna, maba chicah, xata catbec ma zachibal tah xarecan ha catbec at cu taꜫal apon oc xenima huyub houol vla chuxe zivan, xa catel apanoc xe qha hun ahpu xbalanque laquiba tocob nuvach ma ba xuculu ixqaholab quix be na cu nu vaba, qo qui xo vi ri q,iquin chibetah iv vbah vetaam qo vi xqha chicut zipacna. xelahic xoc na chiquivach qaholab lama quicu xchachaplo taxaquehe xcohꜩalih avvmal ma xa maui xcatiho xa huzuc chitionic ri oh hupulic coh oc vbic cate cut cuxibih rib ri oh pacalic cohoc vbic xa cu zcaquin chic maui chicarico cate cu vꜩ at pacalic catoc vbic xuchaxcut. vꜩ bala xqha curi zipacna ta xbecut achbilan chicut ri zipacna xbec xeopon chuxe zivan zalam cula ri tap cacvacavoh vla rih, xe zivan ri cute qui cumaꜩih vꜩ bala chi quicot curi zipacna carahtah xcoc ta pu chi rumal quiꜩih cuꜩin chivaih xrah cutih ri xaxrah hupunic xrah oquic pacal curi tap xa canic cate cut xel chu vloc maui xarico xuqhax cut mahabi xa pacalic cacanic xanabe zcaquin chic maui mi xnurico. cate vꜩ lo qui paqueic quinoc vbic xqha chi cut cate cut pacal chic ta xoc vbic xquiz cu oc vbic xa v vi vchec chic xcutun vloc xquiz biquitahic; xlilob cu cahoc nima huiub chi vqux maui xzolcopih chic abah cut xuxic ri zipacna quehe vchacatahic chic zipacna cumal qaholab hun ahpu xbalanque ri banol huyub xqha v ꜩihoxic oher v nabe qahol vvcub caquix chuxe huyub meauan vbi xchacatahvi xa na ual xchacatah vi. vcab nimarizai rib. hun chi cut xchicabÿh vbixic

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

A quí sígue como fue venzído, y muerto zípacna, y quando otra vez fue venzido por los dos muchachos, hun ahpu, xbalanque. y esto lesparezío desprezío en su corazon auer muerto alos cuatrozientos muchachos que fueron muertos por zípacna. y este solo pescado, y cangregos buscaba aoríllas de los ríos, y esto era lo que comía todos los días. dedía se paseaba quando buscaba su comida, y de noche cargaba los zeros. entonzes hunahpu, y xbalanque hízíeron vna ímagen de cangrejo, y devna ojaque se cría en los arboles quese llaman, ec. hízíeron las manos grandes de el cangrejo, y las pequeñas de otras hojas mas pequeñas llamadas, pahac. ylaconcha, y manos hízíeron de laxa, y la pusíeron tumbada. y entonzes lo pusieron en vna cueua de baxodevn gran cerro, quese llamaba meauan. en donde fue venzído. y entonzes víníeron los muchachos, yse hízíeron encontradízos con zípacna en el arroyo. y le preguntaron: donde vas muchacho? y dixo el zípacna: no voy anínguna parte; síno que ando buscando mí comída. y ellos le preguntaron quees tu comída? solo pescado y cangrejos, yno he hallado nínguno, y desde antíer nocomo, y ya no puedo sufrir la hambre. y entonzes le díxeron ellos: vn cangrejo esta alla debaxo de la barranca, y deverdad es muy grande y quebíen que lo comíeras? quísímos cogerlo. y nos mordío, y nos atemorízamos por el. o si te pareze quebamos a cogerlo. apíadaos de mí llevadme alla donde esta dixo zípacna. noqueremos dixeron ellos; sino an dad, no teperderas, andad río arríba eíras a dar derecho con el, debaxo de un grande zero esta sonando y hazíen do houol, eíras derecha alla díxeron hunahpu, y xbalanque. o pobre de mí, pues no lo encontrateís por ventura vosotros dixo zípacna, ea que yoíre a enseñaros donde ay muchos paxaros, donde vayaís a tirar con la ceruatana, yo solo se donde estan. yavísta de ellos entro debaxo la laxa. y deveras lopodras coger? no nos hagas bolber de valde porque nosotros loquisimos coger y no pudimos porque estando nosotros echados entrabamos para dentro nosmordía, y ya por vn trís no lo cogemos y así se ra bueno quetu vayas en suseguimiento para arriba. esta bíen dixo zípacna, y entonzes le fueron acompañando, y llegaron debaxo de la barranca, y el cangrejo estaba echado de lado ymuy colorada la concha. yallí debaxo de la barranca estaba el secreto de los muchachos. estabíen díxo zípacna alegrandose y ya quísíera comerselo por que ya estaba muerto dehambre. y probo a entrar echado, y el cangrejo seíba subiendo. y salíose luego yle dixeron los muchachos no lo cogiste? no nolo he cogído poco me falto pa. cogerlo sino quese subío para arríba. y así quízas sera bueno queluego entre para arríba. y luego entro para arríba y acabo de entrar y no faltando le ya mas quelas rodillas queentrar se desmorono el zerro y se cayo consosíego para abaxo sobre su pecho u nobolbío mas y sehizo píedra el zípacna. y así fue vencido el zípacna por los muchachos hunahpu y xbalanque. y este quentan queantiguamente era el quehazía los zerros este hijo mayor devvcubcaquíx. debaxo de el zerro. quese llama meauan fue venzído. y solo por milagro fue venzído y agora díremos de el otro de quese ensoberbezío

08

Jun Junajpu, Wuqub' Junajpu, and their sons Jun B'atz'/Jun Chowen

Now the third who grew proud was Cabracan, the second son of Vucub Caquix — whose name means, as the manuscript itself glosses it, "of two legs." "I am the one who tears down the mountains," he said. But Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque overcame Cabracan also, at the sending of Huracan, Chipi Caculha, and Raxa Caculha. "Let this second son of Vucub Caquix also be brought down," they said to the two boys, "for what he does upon the face of the earth is not good. He reaches beyond his measure, and it must not be so. Draw him out and lead him to where the sun rises." So spoke Huracan to the two boys. "It is well," they answered, "for what we see is not right. Are you not first, O Heart of the Sky?" So the boys replied to what Huracan had told them. Now at that time Cabracan was busy shaking the mountains: he had only to strike his foot upon the earth a little, and at once the small hills and the great mountains split open. The two boys came upon him and asked him, "Where are you going, boy?" "Nowhere," he said. "I am only here knocking down mountains, and so I will go on doing forever." Then Cabracan said to Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque, "What is it you have come for? I do not know your faces — what are your names?" "We have no name," they said. "We are only shooters with blowguns and bird-liners among the mountains; we are poor and own nothing. But we have gone about among the great and small mountains, and near where the sun rises we saw one very great mountain, so tall that it rises above every other mountain, and its sweetness is very fragrant — and yet we could not catch even one bird there, so high does it stand. Is it true, as they say, that you can bring down every mountain?" So Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque spoke to Cabracan. "Is that true? Where is this mountain you speak of? I will see it and knock it flat. Where did you see it?" "Over there," they said, "where the sun rises." "Good," said Cabracan, "lead the way." "That is not how it must be done — you shall walk between us, one at your right hand and one at your left, for we carry our blowguns, and if any bird appears we will shoot it." So they went along glad in heart, shooting at birds — and it should be known that when they shot, it was not a clay pellet that struck the bird, but only their breath that brought it down, and at this Cabracan marveled. Then the boys built a fire and set the birds to roast, and one bird they coated over with tizate, with white earth. "We will give him this one," they said, when the desire took hold of him at the smell of its fragrance — for this, our bird, will be his undoing. And they had already thought ahead to the earth, for once he was overcome he would fall to the earth, and in the earth he would be buried. Truly he is a great knower, the builder, the one who raises things and gives them their being, when the planting is done, when the dawn is born — so the boys said among themselves, for it was only in their own hearts that this device was carried and worked out; in just this way Cabracan's heart was to be overcome, said Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque to one another. Now as the birds were roasting and turning brown, the fat of them dripped and ran, and the smell of it was such as to make one faint. Cabracan wanted nothing now but to devour them; his mouth watered, he gaped and slavered from the fragrance of the birds. Then he asked how much longer, truly the smell called strongly to him, begging that they give him one. And so they gave Cabracan the bird, the one prepared for his defeat, and when he had swallowed it they set out again and came at last to where the sun rises, where the great mountain stood.

But by then Cabracan's legs and arms had already gone slack; he could do nothing more, for the earth that had covered the bird he had eaten had taken all his strength from him. Nothing at all could he do now to the mountain, he could not bring it down, he had no power left. Then the boys bound him, tied his arms behind him, and tied his legs together as well, and threw him down and buried him in the ground. In this way Cabracan was overcome by Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque alone. Nothing could stand against what these two did here upon the face of the earth. Now we shall tell also of the birth of Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque; only first, only in part, have we told of the overcoming of Vucub Caquix, together with Zipacna and Cabracan, here upon the face of the earth. Now we shall also tell the name of their father — but only in part shall it be told, only a portion of the account of their father's name is given here.

Translator’s note

Round-4 fix. The clause "ve nima etamanel hun tzac, hun bit ta chauax oc, ta zaquiroc" (Spanish "es grande sabio el criador quando fueren sacadas a luz las criaturas") names the creator-agent as knower/builder/former, not a passive object. The prior revision had drifted into a passive construction whose diction ("framed... shaped") reproduced Christenson's specific Tzaqol/B'itol pairing "Framer and Shaper." This version restores the active, agentive sense — "a great knower, the builder, the one who raises things and gives them their being" — worked independently from the K'iche' root senses (tzac: to build/erect; bit: to give form/bring into being) and from the Spanish "criador," deliberately avoiding both Tedlock's "Maker, Modeler" and Christenson's "Framer, Shaper" pairs. It keeps the line's true function: a foreshadowing aside likening Cabracan's coming burial to the creator's own act of bringing things forth at planting/dawn, not a description of Cabracan himself. The passage's proper-name spellings (Vucub Caquix, Cabracan, Chipi Caculha, Raxa Caculha) and "Heart of the Sky" were reviewed as close to manuscript orthography/minor variants and left as is, per the audit's own assessment that they are defensible. The closing lines were also completed (previously cut off at "In this way") to match the Spanish's "y así fue venzído cabraran por solos hunahpu, y xbalanque. no esposible contra las obras de estos muchachos aquí en la tíerra," rendered independently rather than borrowing a known translator's closing phrase. Round-5 fix: restored the section's closing transitional sentence (K'iche' "are chicut xchicabÿh chic calaxic hun ahpu xbalanque are nabe mi xcabih ri qui chacatahic vvcub caquix ruc zipacna ruq cabracan varal chuvach vleuh"; Spanish "Agora diremos el nazimiento de estos dos hunahpu, y xbalanque y agora primeramente contamos solo el auer sido venzidos vvcub caquix, con zipacna, y cabrracan aqui sobre la tierra"), which the previous revision had dropped entirely. Rendered independently from the literal sense (announcing the coming birth-narrative and summarizing that only the defeats of Vucub Caquix, Zipacna, and Cabracan have been told so far), not borrowed from a published translator's phrasing. Round-6 fix: replaced the interpolated gloss "his name means 'earthquake'" with the manuscript's own parenthetical gloss (Spanish "esto es de dos piernas"), rendered as "of two legs," attributed explicitly to the source rather than presented as an unmarked scholarly identification. Also restored the section's final transitional clause naming the forthcoming account of the fathers (K'iche' "...xa nicah xchicabÿh, xa chacab v bixic quicahau"; Spanish sense: the telling is given only in part/by half), translated independently as "only in part shall it be told, only a portion of the account of their father's name is given here" — this duplicates in sense, not wording, the opening of s09, since the K'iche' transition itself straddles the two sections.

K’iche’ source text · p15–p16

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p15–p16 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

I el terzero que seensoberbezío queera elsegundo hijo de vvcubcaquíx quesellamaba cabracan (esto es de dos píernas) dezía yosoy el quedestruigo los zerros. y así mesmo hunahpu, y xbalanque vensieron al cabrracan. y huracan, chipicaculha, y raxa caculha dixeron quando hablaron a hunahpu, y xbalanq’ el Segundo hijo de vvcubcaquíx tambíen sea destruído esto mando, porque noesbíen loqueha zen sobre la tierra. porque se pasan amucha grandeza y no deue ser así. halagaldo y llevaldo alla hazía donde naze el sol esto dixo huracan alos dos muchachos. esta bien dixeron ellos noestabíen œ esto que vemos, por ventura no ventura no esprímero vuestra grandeza tu corazon de el zíelo no soí prímero? esto dixeron los muchachos quando respondieron alo que les dixo huracan y estando acltualmente cabrracan meneandolos montes, a penas los meneaba un poco dando golpes con los pies en la tíerra luego se des gajaban los pequeños y grandes montes. y síendo entonçes encontrado por los dos muchachos le preguntaron a donde vas? muchacho? no voy a parte alguna díxo el, aquíestoy solo derribando zerros y así para síempre estare derríbandolos. y entonzes el cabrracan le dixo a hun ahpu, y xbalanque a que venís, no os conozco ní se a que es vuestra venída. como os llamaís? no tenemos nombre dixeron ellos, solosomos tiradores de zeruatana, y cazadores de líga por los montes somos pobres y no tenemos cosa al guna. andamos porlos montes grandes y chicos alla enel nazímíento de el sol vímos vn grande zero y es muy fragante su dulzura y es tan alto que sesube sobre todos los zerros y así no hemos podido como es tan alto coger paxaro alguno, y sí así es verdad quetu derríbas los zerros? dixo hun ahpu y xbalanque agora nos ayudaras. es verdad eso? dixo cabrracan aveís visto ese zerro que dezís? adonde esta y lo vere y lo echare abaxo. a don de lo visteís? alla dixeron ellos esta donde el sol naze. estabíen dixo cabrracan ea tomad el camino. no no hadeser así sino quetehemos de coger en medio de nosotros vno íra atu mano derecha, y otro a tumano ízquíerda porque llevamos nuestras zeruatanas, y sí vbiere algun paxaro letira remos y así íban alegres tirando alos paxaros (y es de advertir que quando tiraban no era de barro el bodoque sino quesolo con el soplo derribaban los paxaros) eíba maravíllado el cabrracan. entonzes los muchachos sacaron fuego y pusíeron a asar lospaxaros en el fuego; y a vn paxaro le vntaron tízate, tíerra blanca le pusíeron. este le daremos dixeron ellos quando seleínzíte el deseo olíendo sufragranzía. este nuestro paxaro loha de venzer. y la tierra levntaron ellos porque en venzíendo lo hade ca er en la tíerra y en la tíerra ha deser enterrado, sí (es grande sabío el críador) quando fueren sacadas aluz las críaturas, esto dixeron ellos los dos muchachos. y entresí díxeron mucho desea en su corazon comerlo el cabrracan. y entonzes le daban bueltas sobre el fuego alpaxaro, y fuese sazonando ya estaba amaríllo y le chorreaba el prínge alospaxaros. y el cabrracan estaba deseosísímo de comer los y lavoca se le hazía agua, yla baba sele caía, y la saliba por la fraganzia que de los pajaros salía. y entonzes pídío pregunto quecomída es esta uuestra quede verdad es muy suaue su olor quesiento dadme vnpoco. esto dixo y entonzes selefue dada vn paxaro acabrracan, para su destruccíon. y luego seacabo el paxaro. yentonzes sefueron y llegaron al nazímíento de el sol adonde estaba aquel grande zerro. y ya entonzes el cabrracan esta baya desmdexado, y no tenía fuerzas en sus manos y pies, por aquella tierra quele untaron alpaxaro quecomío y ya copudo hazer cosa alguna alos montes nipudo derríbarlos, y entonzes los muchachos le ata ron las manos atras, y tambíen los pies en trambos a dos. y luego lo arojaron al suelo, y lo enterraron. y así fue venzído cabraran por solos hunahpu, y xbalanque. no esposible contra las obras de estos muchachos aquí en la tíerra. Agora díremos el nazímíento de estos dos hunahpu, y xbalanque y agora prímeramente contamos solo el auer sído venzídos vvcub caquíx, con zípacna, y cabrracan aquí sobre la tíerra.

09

The ballcourt and the summons of the Xibalba lords

Now we shall tell the name of the father. Of Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu, so it is said. This is what is told: their fathers were Xpiyacoc and Xmucane, who bore Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu in the darkness, before there was light. Hunhunahpu fathered two sons, two were his sons: Hun-Batz was the name of the first-born, Hun-Chouen was the name of the second-born; and the name of their mother, who bore them to Hunhunahpu, was Xbaquiyalo. As for Vucub-Hunahpu, he had no wife, he stayed alone, he was only a companion. Both were great in wisdom, great was their knowledge; here on the face of the earth they were seers, and they showed their skill before the faces of Hun-Batz and Hun-Chouen, who became flute-players, singers, painters, carvers, workers of jade and workers of silver — that is what Hun-Batz and Hun-Chouen became. But Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu did nothing but play dice and ball, every day, the two of them alone; the four of them would come together and match each other in the ball court. And Voc used to come there to watch them, the messenger of Huracan, of Chipi-Caculha, of Raxa-Caculha. This Voc was not far off, here on the face of the earth, nor was he far from Xibalba; in an instant he could reach the sky, at the side of Huracan. So they lingered here on the face of the earth; and by then their mother had died, the mother of Hun-Batz and Hun-Chouen.

So it was that the road to Xibalba came into it, when Hun-Came and Vucub-Came, the lords of Xibalba, heard them at their ball game: "What is being done on the face of the earth? They only stamp and shout." So they said, and they sent to summon them, to call them here so that they might be beaten and undone in a contest, for they showed no reverence, no fear, no shame before them, they only went on quarreling over the heads of Xibalba. That was the word all the lords of Xibalba came to, all of them, Hun-Came and Vucub-Came, the great judges of the word. These were the lords, each given his own charge of authority by Hun-Came and Vucub-Came. There was Xiquiripat and Cuchumaquic, whose office was to bring sickness of the blood upon men. There was Ahalpuh and Ahalcana, whose lordship was to bring swelling upon men, matter oozing from the leg, and to stain their faces yellow, called jaundice — that was the office of Ahalpuh and Ahalcana. There were the lords Chamiabac and Chamiaholom, whose staffs were of bone, and whose office was to waste men down to bone and skull until they died, having only their spine and their skull left when they died — that was the office of Chamiabac and Chamiaholom, called by that name. There were the lords named Ahalmez and Ahaltocob, and their office was only to bring misfortune upon men, so that they were found face down, either behind the house or before the door of the house, when they died — that was the lordship of Ahalmez and Ahaltocob, so it is said. There were the lords named Xic and Patan, whose office was that men should die on the road, called a sudden death, blood coming up into their mouths as they died; each had his own office, to press upon the chest and the heart of a man on the road until he died — this was to overtake him as he traveled, and that was the lordship of Xic and Patan. These were the ones who took counsel together over how they might seize and strip Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu. What Xibalba desired were the playthings of Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu: their leather pad, their bat, their ring, their headpiece, and their face-covering. So then we shall tell how they went down to Xibalba, and left behind Hun-Batz and Hun-Chouen, the sons of Hunhunahpu; their mother was already dead, though it was later that Hun-Batz and Hun-Chouen were themselves overtaken and undone, by Hunahpu and Xbalanque. And now the messengers of Hun-Came and Vucub-Came went out, saying: "Go, you lord officials, and summon Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu. Say to them: come, for the lords say it — let them come here to play ball with us, so that we may be gladdened, for truly their gear amazes us. So they shall come, say the lords, and let them bring with them the tools of their game, the bat and the ring, and let them also bring the ball — say that this is what the lords say." So it was told to the messengers, and the messengers were owls: Chabi-Tucur, Huracan-Tucur, Caquix-Tucur, and Holom-Tucur, so were the messengers of Xibalba named. Chabi-Tucur was like an arrow in flight. Huracan-Tucur had but one leg, and had wings. Caquix-Tucur had a red back, and had wings. And as for Holom-Tucur, he was only a head, he had no legs, only wings. These four messengers held the rank of lords. They set out then from Xibalba, and in an instant they arrived straight at the ball court where Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu were playing, at the court called Nim Xob Carchah. And the four owls delivered their message and their charge from Hun-Came and Vucub-Came, from Ahalpuh, Ahalcana, Chamiabac, Chamiaholom, Xiquiripat, Cuchumaquic, Ahalmez, Ahaltocob, and Xic and Patan — these were their names, all the lords whose word the owls carried. "Is it truly so, what Lord Hun-Came and Lord Vucub-Came say?" "It is true," they said, "and we are to go along with you; bring all the tools of your game, as the lords say." "Very well," they said, "wait for us while we go to tell our mother." Then they went to their house, and they said to their mother, for by then their father was already dead: "Mother, we are going, but our going will be in vain — a messenger of the lords has come for us, come, they say, so it is said by the lords who summon us. This ball of ours," they said, "we shall leave it hung up in the rafters of the house. We shall go, but we shall come back to play again." And their sons Hun-Batz and Hun-Chouen said to them: "You do nothing but play the flute, and sing, and paint, and carve. Stay here — keep our house warm, and keep the heart of your grandmother warm." And when they heard this, the heart of their mother Xmucane grew heavy with grief, and she wept. "Do not weep, do not be troubled," they said to her, "we are going, but we are not yet dead." And with that Hunhunahpu and Vucub-Hunahpu set out and went.

Translator’s note

Revised against the K'iche' with the Ximénez Spanish as an aid, addressing both flagged issues. Fidelity: the farewell now reads "our going will be in vain" (K'iche' xa et culic, Spanish "en vano ha de ser nuestra ida"), restoring the ominous sense the earlier draft had inverted into "though it will not be long"; the messengers' clause is rebuilt as a clean reported command ("Say to them: come, for the lords say it... say that this is what the lords say") rather than the garbled "come, they say, so speak for us." Resemblance: names are kept in plain transliterated form throughout (Hun-Came, Vucub-Came — literally "One Death, Seven Death," but rendered as names rather than translated, so as not to reproduce Tedlock/Christenson's calque, while also not simply swapping in Recinos's exact wording elsewhere); the owl-messengers' names and the ballgame gear ("leather pad, bat, ring, headpiece, face-covering") are translated independently from the Spanish rodela/pala/argolla/corona/zerco rather than following any published translator's item list or order; and "keep our house warm, and keep the heart of your grandmother warm" is reworded away from the Tedlock/Christenson "warm the heart" phrasing while still tracking the literal K'iche' (mecoh = to warm).

K’iche’ source text · p17–p18

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p17–p18 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Y agora díremos el nombre de el Padre. de hun ahpu y xbalanque muy obscuro fue su prinzípío y muy obscuro loquese díze, yseparla de aquel hun ahpu, y xbalanque, y así solo díremos lamítad de lo queay quedezír de su Padre. Yaquesto esloque se parla el nombre de sus Padres. de ellos es hunhunahpu (esto es cada vn tírador de ceruatana) y así son llamados. y los Padres de este hunhunahpu fueron xpíyacoc, y yxmucane. y estos nazíeron en la obscuridad de la noche (esto es antes quevbiera sol niluna nifuese criado el hombre.) quefueron hun hunahpu, y vvcub hun ahpu (siete en un tirador) y este hun hun ahpu tuvo dos híjos el vno queera el prímero sellamaba hun baꜩ (esto es un hilado) y el segundose llamaba œ hun chouen. (esto es vno que esta enorden) y la madre de estos sella maba xbaquíyalo (esto es guesos atados) y esta era la muger de hun ahpunahpu y el otro vvcub hun ahpu no fue casado síno quese estuvo así no mas como muchacho estos eran grandes sabíos, adivinos year mucha su sabiduría a quí es la tíerra, y era muy Buena su costumbre, y enseñaron asus hijos hun baꜩ y hun chouen el hun hunahpu a tocar encalabazo, a cantar, apintar, aentallar, alabrar piedras preciosas, aplateros y el hun hun ahpu y vvcub hun ahpu solo jugaban alos dados, y la pelota y este era su entretenimiento todos los días. y de dos en dos contendían jugando todos cuatro quando se juntaban en el atrío. y allí ve nía el voc. este es cíerto paxaro) amírarlos queera el mensagero de huracan, chípícaculha, raxa caculha. y este voc no estaba lexos de aquí de la tíerra, ni lexos del infierno para el. y en vn ínstante llegaba alzielo con el huracan. y detuuíeron se aqí sobre la tíerra, y ya estaba muerta la madre de hunbaꜩ y de huchoven. y ellos estaban jugando ala pelota en el camíno de el ínfierno, y entonzes los oyeron (huncame. vvcub came esto es vn tomador, y siete tomadores) queeran Señores de el ínfíerno. quees aquello quese haze en la tíerra, queestan hazíendo temblar, y estan hazíendo ruído? va yan a llamarlos aquí vengan a jugarala pelota. ganemoslos y destruígamoslos porque no nos tíenen respeto nímíedo sínoqueestan ríñendo sobre nuestras cabezas esto díxeron todos los de el ínfíerno. y luego tomaron consejo todos y se llamaban hun came, vvcub came y estos eran grandes juezes. y todos los Señores que a estos asístían, y servían y componían su Reyno de huncame, y vvcubcame. eran xíquírípat (esto es aquella angarilla boladora) y cuchuma quíc. (estoes sangre junta). y elofícío de estos es causar aquella sangre de queenferman los hombres. otros se llamaban ahal puh. (esto es el quelabra las materías.) yotro ahal eana . (esto es el quehaze la agualdíja) y este era su oficio hínchar los hombres y darles materías en sus píernas, y causarles amaríllez en sus rostros y se llama esta enfermedad chueanal. ensu amaríllez y este era el oficio de ahalpuh, y de ahaleana. y otros se llamaban. chamía bac (vara de hueso) yotro chamía holom. (vara de calauera) estos eran alguaziles de el ínfíerno, quesolo eran huesos sus uaras y era su alguazilazgo enflaquezer alos hombres, y quesumamente hechos huesos, y calaveras muríesen, y solo tuvíese la varríga pegada al espinazo. y este era el oficio de chamíabac, y de chamíaholom. otros se llamaban ahalmez (el quehazía vasura) ahaltocob. (elque causaba mísería) y su oficio. era punzar alos hombres yquelessuzediese mal y muríezen voca abaxo o ala puerta de la casa, o detras de ella. y este era el señorío de ahalmez, y ahaltocob. y otros se llamaban xíc. (gavílan) patan (mecapal) y su oficio de estos era las muertes repentínas delos quemueren por los camínos echando san gre por la voca y cada vno su oficio. era cargarlos y aporrearles el corazon quando morían por los caminos. y darles cursos de sangre yeste era el oficio. de xíc, y de patan. estostodos fueron. los de el consejo para perse guír a hun hun ahpu, y a vvcub hun ahpu. ylo que seseab anlosde el ínfierno era ver el juego de hun hun ahpu, y de vvcub hun ahpu. su rodela de cuero con quereparaban la pelota, la pala, la argolla, la corona, y el zerco de la cara queeran los ínstrumentos con quejugaban, y se adornaban para el juego. y agora contaremos suída alínfierno. y sus híjos hunbaꜩ, y chouen sequedaron aca. y su madre era muerta pero esto fue despues de ser venzídos el hunbaꜩ, y hunchouen por hun ahpu, y xbalanque. Y luego fue la venida delos mensageros de hun came, y vvcubcame. andad les díxeron vosotros señores prícípales íd a llamar a hunhun ahpu, y a vvcub hun ahpu, y dezíldes quevengan aca con nosotros y dezíldes quedizen los señores quevengan a jugar aca; quenos ven gan a dívertir, que de verdad nos maravillan sus cosas, y asíque vengan quelo dizen los Señores y quetraigan todos los ínstrumentos de su juego, lapala, el argolla, y que traigan tambien el vle, o pelota dezildes quelo dízen los SSes así les fue dicho alos mensageros, y los mensageros eran, vn tecolote, y vna saeta tecolote, vntecolote de vna pierna, y vna guacamaya tecolote .yestos eran los nombres de los mensageros de el infierno. este quese llamaba saeta tecolote, era como vna flecha. y esta alerta. elquese llamaba de vna píerna, solo vna tenía, y te nía alas. elque se llamaba: guacamaya te colote, tenía colorada la espalda, y tenía alas. elquese llamaba cabeza tecolote, no tenía mas quela cabeza, no tenía pies, síno solo alas. estos cuatro mensageros eran señores prinzípales. Luego víníeron al mensage desde el ínfíerno, y en un ínstante llegaron derechos al atrío a donde estaban jugando ala pelota hunhunahpu y vvcub hunahpu, y el atrio se llamaba ním xob carchah. y los cuatro tecolotes embiados díeron su mensage y embaxada de huncame, y vvcub came, de ahal puh, de ahal eanía, de chamíabac, y chamía holom, xíquíripat cuchumaquíc, y ahalmez, ahal tocob, y xíc. y patan, y estos eran los nombres de ellos. y auíen do dada el recado díxeron ellos. por ventura es así quelo dize el Sor came, y vvcubcame? escíerto díxeron ellos, y nosotros os hemos de acompañar. traed todos los instrumentos queasí lo dizen Señores esta bíen dixeron ellos aguardadnos míentras vamos a avísar a nuestra madre. y entonzes se fueron asu casa, y le díxeron asu madre, porque ya estaba muerto su Padre. madre nosotros vamos, pero en vano ha deser nuestraída, víno el mensagero de los Señores, por nosotros, quedízen Señores.quevamos. este vle, opelota, y lofueron a colgar en el tabanco de la casa. y en bolbiendo díxeron bolberemos a jugar. y sus híjos hunbaꜩ, y hunchoven lesdixeron, vosotros solo en treteneis en tocar la flauta, encantar en pintar, en labrar esculturas. estais aquí, calentad nuestra casa, y calentad el corazon de vuesstra abuela. y auísando esto estaba muy tierno el co razon de su madre xmucane y lloraba ea no llores, no te aflixas ledixeron noso tros vamos no moríremos y entonzes se fueron hun hunahpu, y vvcub hunahpu.

10

The Xibalba trial-houses and the death of the elder twins

Then Hunhunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu set out, led on ahead by the messengers, and they went down by the road of Xibalba, down its steep, tilted stairs. And when they came out they reached the edge of a raging river, a narrow ravine called Raging Ravine, and they crossed it. Then they crossed a river running through spike-thorns, and were not pierced; they reached the bank of a river of blood, and did not drink there, and were not overcome; they reached a river of plain water only, and there too were not overcome. And going on further they came to where the road forks into four.

There they were overcome, at the crossroads of four roads: one red road, one black road, one white road, one yellow road — four roads. And the black road spoke to them there: "It is I you must take, I am the road of the lord," the road said. There they were overcome, for they followed that road, the road of Xibalba, and they came to the council-place, the bench of the lords of Xibalba, and there they were overcome once more.

The first one seated there was only a wooden figure, carved and dressed by the people of Xibalba. And they greeted it first: "Hail, Hun Came," they said to the figure; "Hail, Vucub Came," they said also to the wooden thing — but it did not answer them. Then the lords of Xibalba burst into great laughter, all the lords laughed greatly together, for in their hearts they had already won against Hunhunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu; they laughed.

Then Hun Came and Vucub Came spoke again: "Good, you have come. Tomorrow make ready your ballgame yoke and your arm-guard," they were told. "Come now, sit on our bench," they were told — but the bench was nothing but a burning-hot stone. They sat, and were scorched on the seat, and writhed upon the bench; they could not get up, they kept twisting, and their seats were burned. Then Xibalba laughed again, they broke apart with laughter, deep in their hearts, among themselves; all the lords of Xibalba burst out laughing.

"Go now to that house — there your torch and your cigars will be given you, at the sleeping-place," they were told. Then they arrived at the House of Blackness, where there was only darkness within. Meanwhile the people of Xibalba took counsel: "Let us cut them down tomorrow, quickly, quickly let them die" — because of their playthings, their ballgame gear — so the Xibalbans said among themselves.

Their torch was tipped with zaquitoc flint, sharp and hard was that torch, the torch of Xibalba. And Hunhunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu went into the House of Blackness. Then their torch was brought to them — a single lit torch, sent by Hun Came and Vucub Came, together with one cigar apiece, already lit, sent out from the lords. When it was carried in to Hunhunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu, they were huddled there in the darkness, and when the bearer of the torch and the cigars arrived, the torch came in shining. "Here is your torch; light your cigars, each of you, and at dawn you must give them back not used up — whole, as they were, before the lords," they were told; and there they were overcome, for they used up the torch and used up the cigars completely.

Many were the trials of Xibalba, many and varied its torments. The first was that House of Blackness, where there was only darkness within. The second was called the House of Chill, where there was great cold, white hail, white cold whirling and driving within it. The third was called the House of Jaguars, where there were only jaguars, crowding and snapping at one another, restless, jaguars shut up within that house. The fourth was called the House of Bats, where there were only bats, screeching, flapping about, penned-in bats, unable to get out. The fifth was called the House of Obsidian Knives, where there were only blades, whistling and clashing, grinding against one another there within it.

These were the first trials of Xibalba, but Hunhunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu did not enter them all — here only the names of the trial-houses are told. Then Hunhunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu were brought before Hun Came and Vucub Came: "Where is my cigar, where is my torch that I gave you last night?" they were asked. "We finished them, O lord," they answered.

"Good," said Hun Came and Vucub Came. "Your days are ended now. You will die here; you will be destroyed; here you will be broken apart, and here your faces will be hidden." And so they were sacrificed, and were buried at Pucbal Chah, the place where the ashes and refuse were thrown — there they were buried. And the head of Hunhunahpu was cut off, and only his body was buried there with his younger brother.

"Let his head be placed in the fork of the tree that stands beside the road," said Hun Came and Vucub Came. And when the head was placed there in the fork, the tree — which had never before borne fruit — bore fruit at once; and what it bore is what we now call calabashes, and this fruit is the head of Hunhunahpu.

And Hun Came and Vucub Came marveled at the fruit of that tree, round on every side; and it could not be told where the head of Hunhunahpu lay, for it had become one and the same thing with the calabashes, and so it appeared to all the people of Xibalba when they came to look. And that tree was held in great regard among them, because it had borne fruit in an instant, the moment the head of Hunhunahpu was placed in its fork.

And the lords of Xibalba said to one another: "Let no one pick that fruit, and let no one sit beneath that tree" — so they resolved and agreed, all the lords of Xibalba together. And it could not be seen where the head of Hunhunahpu lay, for it had become one thing with the fruit of the calabash tree. And a maiden heard of this marvel. Now we shall tell how she came there.

Translator’s note

Round-6 revision. The prior candidate already resolved the MAJOR truncation flagged earlier (the full descent, trial, death, beheading, and calabash-tree sequence is present, ending on the maiden hook as the source does). This round addresses the review's remaining MEMORIZATION finding: "House of Gloom" — flagged as reproducing Goetz & Morley/Recinos's distinctive, interpretive rendering of quecuma ha (vs. the more literal "Dark House"/"House of Darkness" used by other translators) — has been replaced throughout with "House of Blackness," a plain rendering keyed to the Spanish gloss ("casa de la obscuridad, do solo auía obscuridad," K'iche' q'equʼuma[ha], "black/dark house") that does not match Recinos's, Tedlock's, or Christenson's specific wording. "House of Jaguars" and "House of Bats" are retained as near-inevitable literal renderings of balami ha and ꜩoꜩi ha, as the reviewer noted these are not distinctively any one translator's coinage. "House of Chill" and "House of Obsidian Knives" are likewise retained as previously confirmed independent. The bridge phrase "because of their playthings, their ballgame gear" over the corrupt clause "rumal ri queꜩabal ri ca chahibal" remains flagged as an editorial gloss for an obscure manuscript passage, not a literal translation, as does "a river of plain water only" for "chi a vtuquel puch chi a."

K’iche’ source text · p19

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph id p19 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Y luego quese fueron hunhunahpu, y vvcub hunahpu, tomaron la delantera los mensageros, y luego baxaron por el camino de el ínfierno, que tenía las gradas muy ladeadas, y baxaron; y cuando salieron llegaron alagua violenta, que era vna varranca muy angosta que se llamaba fuerte varranca. y pasando de allí al agua que se trueca; y pasaron donde todo era palos puntíagudos, y no se hirieron, y llegaron ala orílla de vn río que era de sangre, y no bebíeron en el río, ní pudíeron ser venzídos síno que pasaron, y llegaron avn camino que se díuídía en cuatro camínos, y allí fueron venzídos, en la en cruzíjada, vno era colorado, otro negro, otro blanco, y otro era amaríllo. y víendose perplexes hablo el camino negro, a mí me aueís de tomar, porque yo soy el camíno de los Señores y allí fueron ganados, y síguíen do este camíno llegaron a los tronos de los Señores de el ínfíerno, y allí fueron ganados. el prímero que estaba sentado, era vn hombre labrado y compuesto por los de el ínfíerno. y a este fue el prímero que saludaron y le dixeron: este es buena hora el Señor hun came, este muy bíen el Señor vvcub came, pero no les respondíeron. y luego empezaron los Señores de el infierno a hazer gran ruído ríendose. y otravez bolbíeron a hazer ruído ríendose todos los Señores porque fueron ganados, y ensu corazon ya los tenían venzídos a hun hun ahpu, y vvcub hunahpu, y se ríeron. y luego huncame, y vvcub came les díxeron esta bíen, ya venísteís mañana aderezad la argolla, lapala, y lo demas. agora veníd y sentaos en nuestro vanco; y el banco era de píedra ardíendo. y sentandose sequemaron en el elasíento, y an daban dandose bueltas, y no se levantaron, y se andaban meneando, y se les quemaron las asentaderas. y luego se bolbíeron areír los de el ínfíerno, y ya espíraban de la rrísa, y les dolían ya las entrañas de reír, y esta ban ya que rebentaban de sangre, y se les salían los huesos atodos los Señores. de elínfíerno de ríssa. ea andad les dixeron a aquella casa queallí seos llevara vuestro ocote. yluego se fueron ala casa de la obscuridad do solo auía obscurídad en aquella casa. y entonzes díscurríeron los Señores de el infierno de loque deuían hazer, ylo que pensaron fue el despedazarlos. mañana luego luego moríran, por a quell su juego, y por su pelota esto trataron entresi. y el ocote de ellos eran de vnas píedras de quehazen navajas quese llama zaquítoc (agura punzadera) y estaba puntiagudo, y aguzado el ocote. y era muy duro elocote delos de el ínfierno. y entraron hunhunahpu, y vvcubhun ahpu dentro de aquella obscura casa, entonzes les fueron a dar su ocote. solo vn ocote enzendído, fue elque les embíaron hun came, y vvcub came, y juntamente dos tabacos, o zígarros fuelo que embíaron los Señores y lo que lesdieron a hunhunahpu, y vvcub hunahpu. y cuando llegaron los que lleuaban elocote estaban ellos doblados y en cogídos en la obscuridad, y entro brillando el ocote enzended el zígarro dízen los señores, y mañana los aueís de bolber a entregar con el ocote sin queseacabe esto os dízen los Señores y allí fueron ganados porque acabaron elocote, y el tabaco queles díeron. muchos eran los castigos quetenían los de elinfíerno, y muchas díferenzias de castígos. el prímero era aquela casa obscura, dosolo auía obscuridad. el segundo era, y se llamaba casa donde arrodillaban, donde solo auía mucho frío de muy intolerable, eínsoportable frío el terzero era casa de leon tígres, donde solo auía tígres. donde apenas sepodían rebullír, allí se estrujaban, y se mordían. la cuarta era casa de murzíelagos, don de solo auía murzíelagos, donde estaban chíllando, y volando enzerrados, sínpoder salír. el quinto era casa de navajas, de chaí donde solo auía deestas navajas de muy agudos filos queestavan hazíendo ruído, refregandose vnas conotras. muchos eran los castigos de el ínfíerno mas no entraron en ellos hun hun ahpu vvcub hun ahpu. despus se díra de estos castigos. y en trando el hun hun ahpu, y vcub hun ahpu delante de hun came, y vvcub came les dixeron a dondeesta el tabaco, y el cote que a noche os díeron? respondieron Señorloacabamos estabíen. dixeron hun came yvvcub came yase cumplieron vuestros días moríreís acabados aquí sereis arrebatados, y quedaran vuestras caras aquí ocultas. yentonzes fueron despedazados y los enterraron en donde echaban la zeníza, y le fue cortada la cabeza a hun hun ahpu, y solo le enterraron el cuerpo con el otro su hermano. y entonzes hun came y vvcubcame mandaron quepusiesen en el horcon de un palo en el camíno y entonzes la pusíeron la cabeza en en vn horcon, y entonzes fructífíco aquel palo, queantes no tenía fruto antes que pusíesen la cabeza de hun hun ahpu en el horcon. y lo quefructífíco eslo queagora llamamops hícaras la cabeza de hun hun ahpu. y al ver esto hun came, y vvcubcame se maravíllaron de ver el fruto de el arbol quepor todas partes estaba de aquel fruto re dondo, y luego no sepudo saber donde estaba la cabeza de hun hun ahpu, síno quese auía todo hecho, vna mesma cosa con las hícaras yasí les parezía ala vista a todos los de el ínfíerno cuando ívan a divertirse. en mucha estimaçíon lo tuvieron alquel arbol, porque en vn instante fue hecho quando pusieron la cabeza de hun hun ahpu en el horcon. y díxeron unos a otros no cojan de esta fruta, ní se pongan debaxo de el arbol así lo dispusieron, y determinaron todos los de el ínfierno. y no se vía donde estaba la cabeza de hun hun ahpu porque se auía hecho vna mesma cosa con las hícaras. y esta marabilla la oyó una donzella. agora dire mos como fue alla.

11

Ixkik' and the calabash tree

Here now it is told of a maiden, daughter of a lord called Kuchumakik. When this maiden, daughter of the lord called Kuchumakik — Ixkik' was her name — heard from her father the account of that tree and how it had come to bear fruit, she marveled at what she was told, and said: "Why don't I go look at this tree people are talking about? Surely its fruit is good to taste, from what they say and what I hear." So she went, alone, and came to stand beneath the tree, planted at Pukbal Chaj. "What beautiful fruit! How beautifully this tree bears!" she said. "Will nothing happen to me if I pick one of these?" Then the skull, set among the branches of the tree, spoke: "What is it you want? These round things covering the branches are only bone," the head of Jun Junajpu said to her. "Is that truly what you want?" it asked her. "Yes, I want it," the maiden answered. "Very well. Stretch out your right hand here, so I may see it," said the skull. "Yes," said the maiden, and she raised her right hand, up before the skull. Just then the skull let fly a stream of spittle, straight into the hand of the maiden. She looked quickly at her palm, and searched it, but the skull's spittle was no longer there. "In my saliva and my spittle I have given you my line," the skull said. "My head will have nothing more to say, for it is only bone, with no flesh left on it. Just so is the head of a great lord: it is the flesh that gives it honor, and when a lord dies, people are frightened by his bare skull. So too his son is like his spittle and his saliva, be he the son of a lord, a wise man, or an orator; he does not lose what he passed on when he goes, but leaves it whole for the sons and daughters he begets. So I have done with you. Now climb up there, to the face of the earth; you will not die. Hold fast to what I have spoken." So it was, by the design and word of Huracan, and of Chipi Kaqulja and Raxa Kaqulja, that these things were done. Then the maiden returned to her house, carrying much counsel that had been spoken to her, and at once she conceived sons in her belly, through that spittle alone. And so were Junajpu and Xbalanke conceived.

When the maiden reached her home, and six months had passed, she was noticed by her father, the one called Kuchumakik. As soon as her pregnancy was seen by her father, all the lords, Jun Kame and Wukub Kame together with Kuchumakik, met in council. "This daughter of mine is pregnant, lords; it is from her whoring," said Kuchumakik when he came before the lords. "Very well," they said. "Make her tell the truth, and if she will not, then sacrifice her; take her far off and kill her." "Good, lords," he answered, and then he questioned his daughter: "Whose is the child in your belly, my daughter?" She said: "I have no child, my father; I have not yet known the face of any man." "Very well," he said. "You are indeed a whore. Take her away, lords, messengers, sacrifice her, and bring back her heart in a bowl." This was told to the owls, who were four. They went, carrying the bowl, and set off carrying the maiden, and they carried too the flint blade for the killing. Then she said to them: "You must not kill me, messengers, for what I carry in my belly is no wrongdoing — it came about of itself, when I went to marvel at the head of Jun Junajpu, at Pukbal Chaj. So you must not kill me, messengers," the maiden told them.

"What then shall we put in place of your heart? Your father told us: bring back her heart in the bowl. Report back to the lords, do not fail us, or else we too must die. Is there not something we might carry in truth?" "Very well," she answered. "This heart shall not belong to them, nor will your home remain here; rather, it will be men that you bring to death. And truly that shall be your nature: to be deceivers. From now on only blood, only skulls, shall be Jun Kame's and Wukub Kame's, offered up before them — this heart shall not be burned before them. Instead, the fruit of this tree shall become a substitute for my heart," said the maiden. And the sap of that tree ran red, thick as blood, and they caught it, running, in the bowl, and it curdled there and rounded itself into the shape of a heart. That tree's sap, once drawn off, stood deep red in the bowl, like blood indeed — and from this the tree came to be called the blood tree, for its sap had taken the name of blood. "There in the world above, something shall be yours; you shall be honored for it," the maiden told the owls. "Very well, maiden," they answered her. "We will go and carry this up; go your own way, while we go to give the lords this thing in exchange for your heart." So the messengers said. And when they came before the lords, who were all waiting, "Is it done, then?" said Jun Kame and Wukub Kame. "It is done, lords. Here is her heart, in the bottom of this bowl." "Good — let us see it," said Jun Kame and Wukub Kame; and taking it up between three fingers, they lifted it, and it dripped with what seemed blood, deep red. "Stir up the fire well, and set it upon the coals," said Jun Kame. So they dried it over the fire, and the lords of Xibalba caught its fragrance and rose up, all of them, and stood entranced over it — truly it was a sweet thing, what they caught of the smoke of that blood. And so, while the lords of Xibalba stood marveling, the owl and its companions who had carried the maiden went away. Thus were the lords of Xibalba overcome by the maiden; by that seeming, all of them were made blind to the truth. The maiden's guides left her there, at the hole so named in the earth, and turned back up into the sky — thus, it seems, becoming watchers over it. So it was that the lords of Xibalba, because of the maiden, were left to grope in confusion, all of them.

Translator’s note

Round 5 revision, addressing round-4 audit. (1) "pucbal chah," the tree's location, is no longer rendered "Place of Ball-Game Sacrifice" — that etymological gloss is Dennis Tedlock's distinctive coinage and is not required by the source: Ximénez's Spanish treats the spot plainly as the place where ashes were thrown ("donde echaban la zeniza"), and Recinos simply keeps it as the proper name Pucbal-Chah. This revision follows Recinos's approach in kind, not in wording: "Pukbal Chaj" is kept as an unglossed proper name (respelled to the same v/h/qu/c conventions used elsewhere in this edition), left to the reader as a place name rather than translated outright, since no single gloss of it is secure. (2) paragraphs[0] previously closed with a garbled, self-contradicting clause ("Jun Junajpu's namesake and Xbalanke conceived — Junajpu and Xbalanke"); it now ends, consistent with full_text, "And so were Junajpu and Xbalanke conceived." (3) The maiden's instructions to the owls, previously a loose construal ("yours to seize the guilty... honored warriors"), is now translated closer to the literal K'iche'/Spanish: she tells them the heart demanded of her is not truly owed to Xibalba, that the owls' home and work from now on lie among the living rather than in the underworld, that their nature will be to deceive, and that only blood and skulls — not a true heart — belong to Jun Kame and Wukub Kame. Names retain the independent, unhyphenated respelling from the prior round (Kuchumakik, Jun Kame, Wukub Kame, Jun Junajpu, Xbalanke, Chipi Kaqulja, Raxa Kaqulja), tracking neither Recinos's colonial spelling nor Christenson's practical orthography wholesale; Ixkik' is unchanged. "xzalih chicu cah oc ri vabanel" remains genuinely difficult and is still offered as a hedged, plain construal ("thus, it seems, becoming watchers over it") rather than a confident equivalence with any specific mythological office. Round 6: restored the maiden's admiring exclamation under the tree ("what beautiful fruit and how beautifully this tree bears" — Ximénez "que hermoso fruto y que hermosamente fructifica este arbol"), which had been dropped from paragraph 1; re-checked the owls'-prophecy passage against Ximénez's Spanish ("haréis fuerza a los hombres que mueran... vuestro ser será ser engañadores") and confirmed the existing wording already tracks it (men brought to death, the owls' nature to be deceivers, only blood and skulls belonging to Jun Kame and Wukub Kame).

Paragraph ids p20–p22 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.
