Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Aquí se trata de vna donzella hija de un Señor quese llama cuchumaquíc. Oyendo pues vna donzella híja de un Señor quese llamaba cuchumaquic, y ella xquíc. (sangre) la conversion de su Pe. sobre auer fructífícado aquel arbol maravillada de lo queoya, dixo porque no íre a uer este arbol quese cuenta, porquede verdad escosa muy dulze, y suave esto quedizen y yo oygo. y luego se fue sola y llego debaxo de el arbol queestaba enfrente de doechaban la zeníza. y maravillada dixo: quehermoso fruto y quehermosamente fructifica este arbol! no me moríre, ni me acabare, si cojo vna de esta fruta. y entonzes hablo la calabera. queestaba en las cruzes de el árbol. y dixo: quees lo quedeseas solo es hueso eso que esta redondo enlas ramas de el arbol esto le dixo ala don zella. por ventura lo deseas lo deseo dixo la donzella . estabíen pues extiende tumano derecha dixo lacalabera. bíen.dixo la donzella y estendío la mano derecha para. arríba delante de la calabera. y luego echo la calabera un chízgete de saliba, y vino derecha ala mano de la donzella. y luego atoda proza mirose la palma de la mano, y ya no auía saliba de la calabera en su mano. te he dado dixo la calabera señal en mí salíba, y mí baba. esta mi cabeza ya no respondera porque solo es gueso, y no tíene carne y así mesmo es la cabeza de cualquíer Señor y solo por la carne se adorna. y en movíendo se asombran los hombres por la calabera. y así sus hijos son como lasalíba, y su baba, sí son hijos de Señores de sabío y entendido, nose píerde ní se apaga el ser de Señores entendido, o sabio, síno que se hereda en sus hijo, y en sus híjas que les engendra. y así ho he hecho contigo. y así subid alla a lo la tíerra, que no moríras. concurríd ala palabra quando sea hecha esto dixo la cabeza de hunhun ahpu, y de vvcub hun ahpu. y esto fue pura sabíduría, y mandato de huracan, de chípícaculha, y raxacaculha y por su mandato lo hízíeron. y así se bolbío la doncella asu casa auíendole dícho muchas cosas y mandates. y luego conzívío y fueron conzebídos híjos en su víentre poraquella que solo era salíba. losque fueron engendrados fueron hun ahpu y xbalanque. y auíendose buelto asu casa la donzella. y auíendo ajustado los seis meses fue reparado por su Padrecuchumaquíc. Y luego que fue sentido el preñez por su Padre de la la donzella y que tenía híjo. se juntaron acabildo los Señores hun came, y vvcubcame, con cuchumaquíc, y dixo esta mi híja esta preñada Señores y esto ha prozedido de su deshonestidad, y fornízío. esto dixo cuchumaquícque compareçío ante los Señores estabíen díxeron. oprímílda y que declare, y se vera lo que dize, y la llevaron lexos a matarla. estabíen Señores díxo el y luego le pregunto a su hija dequíen es ese híjo que tienes en la barríga. y díxo ella no tengo hijo Señor Pe aun no he conozídovaron. estabíen dixo el cíertamente tu eres fornícaría. ea andad vosotros Señores principaples andad y sacríficalda, y traed su carazon en vna hícara. esto se les mando alos tecolotes, que eran cuatro. y luego fueron y tomaron vna hícara y se fueron llevandola cargada, y tam bíen llebaban vna cuchilla aguda para revanarla. y entonzes ella les díxo no me mateís mensageros por que no soy fornícaría. sino que solamente se engendro lo que tengo en la barriga. lo que sucedío fue que fuí adívertirme, y uer aquel prodígío de la cabeza de hun hun ahpu, que esta en el zenízero. y así no me mateís mensageros. y respondíeron ellos pues que hemos de lleuar en lugar de tu corazon en la hícara? no nos mandaron los Señores que en esta hícara les lleuaremos tu corazon, así no nos mandaron? bíen quísíeramos nosotros librarte. esta bien dixo ella. no es de ellos este corazon, y vuestra casa nosera aquí, síno que hareís fuerza alos hom bres que mueran. y de verdad ese sera vuestro ser ser engañadores. y luego sera mío hun came, y vvcubcame y solo sera de ellos lasangre, y las calberas esto sera solo de ellos en su presenzía. este corazón nosera quemado delante de ellos. poned en la hícara el fruto de este arbol dixo la donzella. y el humor de aquell arbol era colorado, y salíendo el humor lo cogíeron en la hícara y luego se congelo y se hizo redondo y puso en lugar de el cora zon, y era como sangre su humor que puso en lugar de sangre. y entonzes sacaron aquella sangre de el humor de el palo, y fue hecho como sangre, y muy colorado estuvo despues de sacado en la hícara, y entonzes resplandezio aquel palo por la donzella yeste palo se llama grana palo colorado, y se llamo sangre, y porello fue llamado sangre. alla en la tierra tendreís cosa vuestra, y series regalados, les dixo la donzella alos tecolotes. estabíen don zella díxeron ellos íremos allevar esto, y tu anda bete que nosotros vamos a dar este su trueque de tu corazon alos Señores dixeron los tecoletes. y luego que llegaron ala presençía de los Señores que estaban aguardando todos. por ventura yase concluyo dixeron huncame, y vucub came. yaze concluyó Señores díxeron ellos. aquí esta sucorazon en el asíento de esta hícara. estabíen veamos dixeron huncame, y vvcubcame. y cogíen dolo con los tres dedos lo leuanto para arríba chorreaba lasangre, y estaba muy colorado de sangre. atízad muy bien el fuego díxo, y poneldo sobre el fuego, dixo huncame. y luego que losecaron sobre el fuego síntíeron los de el infíerno la fragançía, y selevantaron todos y estuvíeron como embelesados sobre el, de verdad era cosa muy suave lo que síntíeron de el humo de la sangre. y luego quando se fueron, admírados, el tecolote, y los demas que auían lleuado ala donzella. y así fueron vençidos los señores de el ínfíerno, por la donçella. con aquella aparíencía fueron deslumbrados todos.

12

Ixkik' brought before Xmukane

The mother of Hun Batz and Hun Chowen was still there when the woman named Xkik' arrived. When the woman Xkik' came to the mother of Hun Batz and Hun Chowen, she was carrying life within her belly - it was only a short time until Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque would be born, for so they were named. When the woman came before the grandmother, the woman said to the grandmother: I have come, my lady - I am your daughter-in-law, I am your child, so she said when she went in before the grandmother.

Where do you come from? Are my sons perhaps alive? Did they not die there in Xibalba? These two who are left as a sign, their lineage - Hun Batz and Hun Chowen are their names - do you see it in the one who has come, in the one who is leaving? Get out! said the grandmother to the girl. Only this much is true, that I am your daughter-in-law - I already belong to Hun Hunahpu. What I carry lives; Hun Hunahpu and Vukub Hunahpu are not dead - what they did was only to make their faces shine. So you will see it, my mother-in-law, when you look upon the face of what I carry, said she to the grandmother.

And Hun Batz and Hun Chowen, for their part, did nothing but play the flute and sing, they did nothing but paint and carve, all day long, and they were the comfort of the old woman's heart. Then the grandmother spoke her mind. She said: I do not want you to be my daughter-in-law - what is in your belly is only the fruit of your own lewdness. You are a deceiver - my sons are already dead. But then the grandmother said further: This much is true - what I tell you now stands: since it seems you are my daughter-in-law, as I am told, go then and bring back food for those you must feed - go and gather a large netful, and come back - for you are my daughter-in-law, as I hear it, she said further to the girl.

Very well, she said, and then she went out to the cornfield that belonged to Hun Batz and Hun Chowen. The road they themselves had opened lay before her; the girl followed it and arrived at the cornfield. But she found only a single stalk of corn - there was not a second stalk, not a third, and it had no ear. Then the girl's heart sank. Oh, sinner that I am, oh, wretched one that I am - where shall I go to get the netful of food that is asked of me? she said. Then she called out, she called upon the keeper of the food, so that it might come. Xtoj, Kanil, Kakaw - and you, Tzij - you who keep the food that belongs to Hun Batz and Hun Chowen, so said the girl.

Then she took hold of the silk threads of the ear, of the beard of the ear, and pulled them out, without pulling off the ear itself, and she packed it up in the net. The single ear of corn was enough to fill the net completely; a great net was made of it. Then the girl came away, but it was the animals that carried the net, and when she arrived she went to set down the load; it looked exactly as if the netful had come from the field itself when she reached the house and the grandmother saw it. Then when the grandmother saw the food - a whole huge netful - where has this food come from for you? Did you perhaps strip our whole cornfield bare and bring it here? I am going to go look at it myself, said the grandmother, and then she went to look at the cornfield. But the single stalk of corn was still standing there, and the place where the netting had lain was also plainly to be seen. So the grandmother hurried back and returned to her house, and she said to the girl: This sign is enough - it is proof that you are truly my daughter-in-law. I will keep watching your works now, for the ones you already carry - they are wise already, so it was said to the girl.

Here we shall go on to tell of the birth of Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque.

Translator’s note

Revised per second audit. The invocation clause was corrected: Xkik' calls on the guardian(s) of the food belonging to Hun Batz and Hun Chowen - the brothers themselves are not addressed or present in the field, only their absent-owner status is invoked (K'iche' "at chahal re queqha hun baꜩ hun chouen" / Ximenez "tu que eres guarda de nuestro sustento, hun baꜩ, hun chouen"). "ix pu ꜩiya," previously dropped, is now rendered literally as a fourth invoked figure, "and you, Tzij" (ꜩiya taken as a proper name parallel to Xtoj/Kanil/Kakaw, on the plain reading of the K'iche' rather than any interpretive gloss). "xuboc acanoc" is now given as "she packed it up in the net" rather than "set it upright," matching the sense of gathering/stowing rather than positioning. Name choices remain independent transliterations (Xkik', Hun Batz, Hun Chowen, Xtoh/Kanil/Kakaw) rather than any published English epithet set, and "Oh, sinner that I am" continues to follow Ximenez's own Spanish wording directly rather than any English predecessor's phrasing.

K’iche’ source text · p23–p24

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p23–p24 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Yestaba la madre de hun baꜩ, y hunchouen quando llego la mugger que se llamaba xquíc. entonçes llego la mugger llamada xquic con la madre de hunbaꜩ, y hun’chuoen y actualmente estaba preñada, y le faltaban para nacer a hun ahpu, y a xbalanque, que así sellamaban. y llegando esta mugger ala vieja le díxo la mugger xquíc: he venido Señora Madre que soy su nuera, ysu híja pequeña, esto díxo quando llego a laVíeja. deadonde vienes, por ventura víven mís hijos? no muríeron alla en el ínfíerno? los que eran desçendençía mía llamados hun baꜩ, hun chouen, no estan aquí? de adonde víenes? sal deaquí. le fue dícho ala donçella por la víeja. solo esto es verdad, que soy su nuera, y soy de hun hunahpu y esto que traígo. víuen, no han muerto hun hun ahpu, y vucub hun ahpu. y solo ha sído sentençia desí mesmos en claridad loque han hecho. Señora. Suegra, y así voluera vsted auerlos, en lo que yo traígo le fue dícho a la víeja. y entonces por esto se enojaron hunbaꜩ, y hun chouen, que solo enten dían entocar flautas, y cantar, en píntar, y hacer obras de talla todo el día, y eran elconsuelo de la víeja, quíen dixo, deníngun modo quiero que seas mí nuera, porque es de forniçío eso que tíenes en tu víentre. eres vna engañadora, porque son muertos los mís híjos que díçes. y díxo la víeja esto que te dígo esçierto, pero esta bíen nuera mía, oygo loque me dízes; y así anda atraer bastímento anda tapízca vna gran red, y venga pues soy mi nuera como oygo, le fue dicho ala don cella.. esta bíen díxo ella, y luego sefue ala mílpa que tenían el hun baꜩ, y hun chouen, y estaba el camíno ancho que auían ellos hecho, este síguío la donzella, y llego ala mílpa. empero no hallo mas mas queun píe de mílpa, y ní dos, ni tres pies de mílpa, y ní tenía maçorcas. y aflíxíose en tonçes la donçella, ó pecadora de mí adon de hedeír a tomar vna red de bastímento, que me píden y dixo llamare, ínvocare al que guarda el bastímento para que venga. xtoh, xcanil, xcacauíx. tu que eres guarda de nuestro sustento, hun baꜩ, hun chouen. esto díxo la donzella. y entonçes coguío las barbas de la mazorca, y las arranco, y no quíto la mazorca, y luego que la compuso en la red se lleno lared de mazorcas, y se hízo vna gran red, y entonçes se vino la don çella, empero animales cargaron la red, quando víno, y fuea dar su encargo, y al mostrarse en la casa, llegocomo carga y víendolo la víeja luego que lo vío aquella gran red de bastímento. de adonde víno por tí esto, síacabaste de traerte toda nuestra mílpa,íre a verlo prímero, díxo la víeja, y entonçes sefue a ver la milpa, y así hallo su píe de mílpa ytambien estaba patente a donde estubo el matate. y luego el punto sevíno la víeja y llego asu casa, y le díxo ala donçella, solo estu señal basta para conocer eres mí nuera vere tus obras, de los que tíenes que son sabíos le fue dícho ala donçella Aquí escrbíbíremos el naçímíento, de hun ahpu, y de xbalaque

13

Birth of Junajpu and Xb'alanke

This, then, is what we shall tell of their birth. When the time for the birth had come, they were born of the maiden named Xkik'. But the grandmother did not see them when they were born. At once the two who were born stood up together — Junajpu and Xb'alanke were their names. There in the mountains they stood up, then they went into the house, but they did not sleep. The old woman said, "They cry too much, go throw them out," and so they were put on an anthill. There they slept sweetly. Then they were taken out from there and put again on top of thorns, for this was what Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen wanted — that they should die there on the anthill and there on the thorns — out of their envy, for they were not received in the house by Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen, though these were their own brothers, yet they did not acknowledge them.

So they were raised in the mountains, and thus they became great flute-players and singers, the two of them, Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen. And having grown up amid great affliction and suffering, they became very wise, and also flute-players, singers, painters, and carvers — everything was accomplished by them. Truly they knew of their birth, for they were wise ones, the substitutes of their fathers who had gone to Xib'alb'a, dead were their fathers. Great sages were Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen, understanding all things in their hearts; they knew, once they had grown, of their own achievement, yet nothing of this was shown by them to their brothers, on account of their envy — it was rather in their hearts that the grudge was kept, and it was not through any deed that they had been wronged by Junajpu and Xb'alanke.

The two of them did nothing all day but shoot with the blowgun, and they were not loved by their grandmother, nor by Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen. They were not given food; only after the meal was already finished, when Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen had come in, only then — but they did not get angry, nor did they rage; they only endured it in silence, though they knew well who they were, for it was clear to them as daylight. And they used to bring their birds when they came each day, and Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen ate them, and nothing at all was given to the two, Junajpu and Xb'alanke — they only kept on playing the flute and singing, Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen.

Then one day Junajpu and Xb'alanke came back without bringing any birds at all, and when they entered, the grandmother grew angry and said to them, "Why do you bring no birds?" Then it was said by Junajpu and Xb'alanke, "Grandmother, it is that our birds have gotten stuck up in the tree, and we cannot climb up after them upon the trunk. Lady, let our older brothers come with us, let them go bring the birds down." And it was said to them, "Very well, we will go with you tomorrow morning," this is what their older brothers said, when they had already been defeated and overcome — for the two of them had planned together how to defeat Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen: "We will only turn them into something else — their very bodies — for it is right, on account of the great grief and suffering they have caused us; we were as good as killed, as good as lost — this is what our brothers wanted. Only as boys have we been thought of by them, in their understanding, and so we shall defeat them, though we shall only make a sign of it," this is what they said to one another.

So then they went beneath a tree called Kante, and their older brothers went along with them, and they began to shoot with their blowguns, but it did not go well against the birds up in the tree, which kept crying out. Their older brothers were amazed to see so many birds, with not one of them falling down. "Our birds do not fall — go climb up and bring them down," they said to their older brothers. "Very well," they said, and then they climbed up onto the tree, and the trunk grew thick and swollen, and when they wanted to come back down, they could no longer get down off the tree. Then Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen said from up in the tree, "What has happened to us, our brothers? Pity us — this tree frightens us to look at, our brothers," this is what they said from up in the tree. Then Junajpu and Xb'alanke said to them, "Untie your loincloths, tie the long end below your bellies, with the tail hanging out behind you, and in that way you will be able to move and come down," this is what was said to them by their brothers. "Very well," they said, and as soon as they pulled out the end of their loincloths, at once they were changed into tails, and they became monkeys.

Then they went off over the tops of the trees, over the hills and the great hills, into the wild places; they cried out and swung themselves along the branches. So it was that Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen were defeated by Junajpu and Xb'alanke — it was only through their hidden power that they had done this. Then they went back to their house, and when they arrived they spoke to their grandmother and their mother: "Grandmother, what can have happened to our brothers, that their faces turned green and they went off like animals?" they said. "If you have done something to your brothers, you have humiliated me and brought me grief — do not do such a thing to your brothers," the old woman said to Junajpu and Xb'alanke. And they said to their grandmother, "Do not grieve, grandmother, they will come back, their faces will be seen again. But this much depends on you in the matter: you must not laugh at them. Now let us try," they said. And then they began to play the flute, they played the song of Jun Ajpu Monkey.

Then they sang and played the flute and beat the drum, taking up their flutes and their drums, and they seated their grandmother beside them. And as they played and sang, calling to Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen, the two came dancing back, and when the old woman saw their ugly faces, their monkey faces, she could not hold back her laughter; at once, in that instant, they went away, and their faces were not seen again — leaping, they went off to the mountain. "What are we to do, grandmother? We shall try only three more times — with the flute and with the song we shall call them; hold back your laughter. Come, let us try again," it was said to Junajpu and Xb'alanke. And they played their flutes once more, and the two came back and danced in the middle of the house, making monkey faces so as to provoke the old woman's laughter, and again she laughed at their monkey looks, their thin bellies, and the swinging of their tails, and once more they went off to the mountain.

"What shall we do now, grandmother? Only this once more, a third time, shall we try," said Junajpu and Xb'alanke. And they played once more, and the two came dancing back; bear it, grandmother, do not laugh — and they climbed running up onto the house, their eyes very red, their mouths thrust out like snouts, rubbing their faces; and seeing these faces, their grandmother burst out laughing, and their faces were never seen again, on account of the old woman's laughter.

Then at once they went off to the mountain, and they said to the old woman, "We have done everything we could — they came each of the times before, but now, called again, they will not come back. Do not grieve; here we are, your grandsons, and we love you, and our mother too — we remain in their place and in the memory of our brothers who were called Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen," it was said to the old woman and to their mother by Junajpu and Xb'alanke.

And they were called upon by the flute-players and the singers, and it was they whom the people of old invoked, the painters and the carvers, before they were turned into brutish monkeys, because they had grown proud and had mistreated their brothers, holding them as if they were slaves; and thus they were effaced, when Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen were lost, turned into monkeys. And because they had always been at home, they had been flute-players and singers, and they had accomplished great things while they lived there with their mother and grandmother.

Translator’s note

Revised in round 4 to fix a fidelity error the reviewer flagged in the closing exchange with the grandmother: the K'iche' "mi nabe xevlic mi pu xcatih chic qui ziquixic" and Ximenez's Spanish ("ya hemos hecho diligencia, y no vinieron habiendolos llamado") describe the brothers as having come back on the three earlier calls and failing only on this final, fourth call — the previous draft inverted that, saying they never came at all. The sentence now reads "they came each of the times before, but now, called again, they will not come back." Also loosened the earlier restructuring of "maui xe culax pa ha cumal quichac" back toward its literal sense: "they were not received in the house by Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen" rather than a paraphrase about the brothers' resentment. As in prior rounds, a few obscure clauses (the counting of calls in the flute-summoning scene, some verb forms describing the monkeys' transformation) were clarified with Ximenez's Spanish version as an aid; the underlying sense of the K'iche' remained the primary guide throughout, and no name or epithet was drawn from a published English translation — Junajpu, Xb'alanke, Xkik', Jun B'atz', Jun Chowen, and Jun Ajpu Monkey (from "hun ahpu coy" in the song's name) follow modern K'iche' orthography, and terms like naual ("hidden power") and quechelah ("wild places") were rendered independently from their literal sense rather than adopted from Tedlock, Christenson, or Recinos/Goetz-Morley.

K’iche’ source text · p25–p26

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p25–p26 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y así fue el nacímíento de ellos que díremos. quando ya estaua justo el tíempo de nacer nacíeron de la donçella, que se llamaba, xquíc. y no los vío la víeja quando naçieron y luego se leuantaron los dos nasídos aun tíempo, hunahpu, y xbalanque eran llamados. en el monte se levantaron, y luego entraron en la casa, y no dormían, y dixo la víeja mucho grítan anda arrojalos y los fueron aponer en vn hormíguero, y allí durmíeron sabrosamente, y sacandolos de allí los pusíeron otra vez sobre espínas. esto era lo que querían hun baꜩ, y hun chouen, que muriesen allí en el hormiguero, y sobre las espínas, por su embídía, de hunbaꜩ, y hun chouen y que no fuesen recíuídos en casa porlos que eran sus hermanos, pero no les conoçían. y así se críaron en el monte, y así fueron grandes flauteros, y cantores los dos hun baꜩ, y hunchouen. y auíendo crecído en grantrabaxo, y dolor que pasaron, fueron muy sabíos, y tambíen fluteros, cantores, píntores, y entalladores, que todo era acabado por ellos y cíertamente sabían su naçímíento, que e ran sabíos, y substitutes de sus Padres, que fueron al fíerno, y eran grandes sabíos el hun baꜩ, y hunchouen en la íntelígençía de todos, y así lo juzgaban, quando se críaron los dos sus hermanos, y no mostraron su saber por su imbídía, sino que en ellos estuuo la venganza desus corazones y no por alguna obra fueron ofendídos por hunahpu, y xbalanque, y solo se entretenían en tírar con cerbatana todos los días, y no eran amados, desu abuela, y de hunbaꜩ, y hunchouen. y no les daban de comer hecha ya la comída, síno que auíendo ya comído hun baꜩ, y hun chouen enton çes venían, y no se enojaban, ní encole rízaban; síno que sufrían, y ello sabían quíenes eran, que como claridad míraban. y traían sus paxaros quando venían todos los días, y comían hun baꜩ, y hun chouen, y nada les daban a los dos hunahpu, y xbalanque, sino que solo se estaban tocando flauta, y cantando, el hun baꜩ, y hun chouen. y víníeron vna vez hunahpu, y xbalanque, sin traer paxaros, y entrando, se enojo la vieja, y les dixo como no traeís paxaros, les fue dícho a hunahpu, y xbalanque fue el caso abuela, que se nos han quedado atorados los paxaros en los arboles, y no podemos subír nosotros sobre los palos, Señora, vayan nuestros hermanos con nosotros, que vayan a baxar los paxaros. y se les fue dícho estabíen íremos con vosotros por la mañana, esto díxeron sus hermanos, quan do fueron vençidos, y ganados. y auíendo los dos consultado entresí sobre el vencer a hun baꜩ, y hun chouen solo los conuertíremos, en otra cosa, sus varrígas. cíertamente quando sea hecho por la gran pena, y dolor que nos han causado, fuímos muertos, y perdídos, esto querían nuestros hermanos. solo como muchachos venímos en su íntelígençía dellos, y así los venceremos, pero solo haremos señal de ello, esto dixeron entresí. y luego fueron debaxo de un arbol, que se llamaba cante, e íuan en su compañía sus hermanos, y empezaron a tírar con la ceruatana, y no se podían contra los paxaros que estaban sobre los arboles, que estaban gritando. y se marauíllaron sus hermanos quando víeron tantos paxaros, y nínguno cayo abajo de nuestros paxaros. no caen, andad vajaldos aca les díxeron asus hermanos esta bíen díxeron ellos, y luego subíeron sobre el arbol, y se engroso el tronco, ehíncho, y luego que quísíeron baxar, ya no podían vaxar desobre el palo el hun baꜩ, y hun chouen y díxeron de sobre el arbol: como senos ha dícho esto hermanos nuestros, pobres de nosotros, que espanta este arbol hermanos nuestros, esto díxeron de sobre elarbol, y les dixeron hun ahpu, y xbalanque: desatad vuestras bragas, y zeñidor, y ataldo debaxo de vuestros víentres larga su punta, y sacaldo para atras, y de ese modo podreís andar, y vajar, esto les fue dícho por sus hermanos. bíen, díxeron ellos, yluego que sacaron la punta, desu zeñidor luego al punto se conuírtíeron en colas, y se bolbíeron mícos. y luego se fueron por çíma de los arboles, y sobre los montes, y los montes grandes y a las seluas; y grítauan, y se meneaban, y columpíaban de las ramas. y así fue el ser ganados el hun baꜩ, y hun chouen, por hun ahpu, y xbalanque. y solo por milagro híçíeron esto. y luego se fueron a su casa, y díxeron quando llegaron con su abuela, y su Madre aSeñoraque sera lo que lesha aconteçido a nuestros hermanos, que verdes sus caras se fueron como animales? mas sí algo les aueís hecho a vuestros hermanos, y me aueís menospreçíado, y puesto en misería, no hagaís eso con vuestros hermanos les dixo la víeja a hun ahpu, y xbalanque. y ellos le díxeron asu abuela, no te aflíxas abuela, que bolberan otra vez, y versa sus caras. y solo esto ímporta esto avos en esta dílígençía, que de níngun modo os ríaís, probad fortuna. y luego empezaron á tocar flautas, y tocaron el son de hun ahpu míco. y luego cantaron, y tocaron las flautas, y el atambor, quando tomaron las flautas, y sus atambores, y asentaron a la víeja consigo, y quando tocaron, y cantahun ahpu coy (vn tírador míco) y tocando llamando, a hun baꜩ, y hun chouen, víníeron bailando, y víendo la víeja los malos gestos que hacían, y sus malas caras, víendo esto serío que no pudo sufrír la rísa y en vn ínstante se fueron y no les ví otra uez sus caras, y saltando se fueron al monte. que es lo que haceís Señora solo quarto vezes probaremos, y así solo otras tres vezes les llamaremos, con la flauta, y con el canto, sufríd la ríssa. eá probed otra vez les fue dícho a hun ahpu, y xbalanque; y luego tocaron otra vez sus flautas, y luego bol bíeron al medío de la casa, y así mesmo hacían monerías, conque prouocaban a ríssa ala víeja, y luego serío la víeja por que prouocaban arríssa sus gestos, de mícos, y lo delgado desus barrígas, y el menear desus colas, quando entraron, y esto era lo quela víeja se reya, y luego se fueron otra vez alos montes; que hemos de haçer abuela mía, solo esta otra tercera vez. probaremos, dixo el hun ahpu y xbalanque. y tocaron otra vez, y bolbieron baílando sufra vsted abuela la rísa. y subíeron corríendo sobre el edífíçío; estaban muy colorados los ojos, y sus vocas ahozícadas, y se estaban sobandose la cara, y víendo estas figuras suabuela se tendío derrísa, y nunca mas les víeron sus caras por la ríssa de la víeja, solo esta vez les llamaremos, y la quarta vez xerío, y no víníeron la quarta vez. y luego al punto se fueron al monte y dixeron ala víeja ya hemos hecho dílígençía, y no víníeron auíendo los llamado, no te de pena que aquí estamos nosotros, tus níetos, y te queremos a vos ya nuestra Madre, que nosotros quedamos en memoría, y en lugar de nuestros hermanos llamados hunbaꜩ, y hunchouen. les fue dicho ala víeja, y a su Madre y díxeron el hun ahpu, y xbalanque. y fueron llamados por los tocadores de flautas, y los cantores, y a estos inuocaron los hombres antíguos los píntores, y entalladores, se bolbíeron en brutos mícos porque se ensoberbeçíeron, y maltrataron asus hermanos y les tuvíeron como a esclauos, y así fueron bor rados, quando fueron perdídos el hun baꜩ, y hun chouen. que fueron conuertidos en mícos, y porqueseestaban en sus casas síempre, y fueron tocadores de flautas, y cantores, e híçíeron grandes cosas estos quando estuuíeron con su Madre yabuela

14

The younger twins as hunters

Then they began their work, to show themselves before their grandmother and their mother. First they set about making a milpa. "We will sow a milpa," they said to their grandmother and their mother. "Do not grieve; here we are, your grandsons, we are the substitutes for our older brothers." So said Hunahpu and Xbalanque.

Then they took up their axes and their hoes, and went off with their blowguns on their shoulders. As they left their house they charged their grandmother to bring their food to them at midday. "Very well, grandsons," said the grandmother.

And when they arrived there where the milpa was, they merely thrust the hoe into the earth, and it worked the earth by itself; they merely thrust the axe into the wood, and it cut by itself. All the trees came falling, all the vines came cut, and it was very much that fell, the small hills and the great hills going down. And with a single stroke of the axe, a single thrust of the hoe, it was beyond counting how much had been done - not a small hill only, a great hill went down as well.

Then they charged a certain animal, called Xmucur - the mountain dove - and set it up on top of a great tree stump. Hunahpu and Xbalanque said to it: "Watch for our grandmother, when she comes bringing our food; the moment you see her coming, call out at once, and then we will take up the hoe and the axe." "Very well," said the dove.

But in truth they did nothing but shoot with their blowguns; they were not truly making the milpa. Then the dove called out, and at once they came running - one seized the hoe, the other the axe; they bound up their heads and, to no purpose, smeared dirt on their hands, as if truly working the milpa, and the one smeared splinters on his head as if truly a woodcutter, so as to be seen thus by their grandmother. Then they went to their house, saying, "Truly we are tired, grandmother," and arriving home, though their food had been brought for nothing, they stretched out their hands and feet before their grandmother as if truly worn from their labor - though it was false, they were not truly making the milpa.

Then on the second day they went again, and arriving at the milpa they found all the trees standing again, all the vines joined together once more, as if it had never been worked. "Who has done this, deceiving us?" they said. It was done by all the animals, small and great - puma and jaguar, deer, rabbit, wildcat, coyote, coati, small birds and great birds - these did it in a single night. So then they began again to make the milpa.

It happened the same way again with the ground and the felled trees. Then they took counsel there in their clearing, over the cut trees and the cleared earth: "We will keep watch - perhaps we will catch whoever does this," they said in their counsel. And when they had gone and taken counsel and come back again, it had already become deep forest, deep thicket once more, in place of the clearing. So they came to their house. "What do you think, grandmother, of what we have seen - the trick played on us? What we cleared has turned to forest and bramble again, just as we found it when we went before," they said to their grandmother and their mother. "We will go back and keep watch, for what has been done to us is not right," they said. Then they armed themselves.

Then they went again to their milpa, and there they hid themselves, concealed among the vines, so that none of the animals could see them, in order to find out who was doing this. All the animals gathered together, small and great, in one place they all assembled. And at the very hour of midnight they came, and as they came they were all speaking, saying: "Trees, stand up! Vines, stand up!" - so they said as they came, drawing near and stirring beneath the trees and vines, showing themselves before the boys.

First to come were the puma and the jaguar, wanting to seize the boys, but they did not succeed. Then the deer and the rabbit drew near in the same way, and together they caught them, but only their tails were seized; the tail came off in their hands, and this is why the deer's tail and the rabbit's tail are so short. Neither the wildcat nor the coyote gave themselves up, nor the peccary, nor the coati - all the animals passed before Hunahpu and Xbalanque, and the boys' hearts burned with anger because they had caught none of them.

Then one more came at last, leaping - the mouse. Heading it off, they caught it in the net. And once they had caught it they squeezed its head, wanting to choke it, and they singed its tail in the fire - this is why the mouse's tail has no hair to this day, and why its eyes bulge, from when the two boys, Hunahpu and Xbalanque, wanted to kill it. And the mouse said: "Do not kill me - it is not your task to make milpa." "Then what is it you have to tell us?" said the boys to the mouse. "Let me be a little, for I have something to say in my belly, and I will tell it after; give me something to eat first," said the mouse. And it was told: "Afterward we will give you your food; say it now." "Very well," said the mouse.

"Know that the goods of your fathers, Hun Hunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu - so were they called, those who died in Xibalba - are still there, hanging over the rafters: their yoke, their arm-guard, and their rubber ball; and your grandmother does not want to show them to you, for it was on account of these that your fathers died." And the boys said: "Are you certain you know this?" And they rejoiced greatly when they heard the news of the rubber ball, and once the mouse had spoken, they marked out its food for it. "This will be your food: maize, chile seeds, beans, pataste, cacao - this is yours, and whatever is kept aside or forgotten, that too is yours, eat it," this was told to the mouse by Hunahpu and Xbalanque.

"Very well, boys, and if your grandmother sees me, what shall I say to her?" said the mouse. "Do not be troubled, for we are here, and we will see to what is to be done. This is what we will say to our grandmother: when we set you in the corner of the house, when you arrive, go straight up to where it hangs, and there we will find the chile ground for our meal, and we will see," the boys told the mouse. And so they gave word to the night, and Hunahpu and Xbalanque took counsel, and reached midday.

Translator’s note

Revised per round-4 audit: (1) the chant "yaclin che, yaclin caam" is now rendered "Trees, stand up! Vines, stand up!" - a fresh phrasing (subject-first, "stand" not "rise") built directly from the K'iche'/Spanish ("parense palos, parense mecates") rather than echoing Goetz & Morley's "Rise up, trees! Rise up, vines!". (2) "qui bate qui pachcab qui quic" / "su batey, la pala, y la pelota de vle" is now "their yoke, their arm-guard, and their rubber ball" - pachcab/la pala is the arm-guard or paddle-glove used to strike the ball, not a "ring" (which had no basis in either source and has been removed). (3) "atajando lo cogieron en la red" (mouse) is now "Heading it off, they caught it in the net" - restoring the dropped net and removing the ambiguous "cut off." Proper names keep the conventional Recinos/Goetz-Morley spellings (Hunahpu, Xbalanque, etc.), which are treated as standard orthography rather than translation choices.

K’iche’ source text · p27

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph id p27 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y quando empezaron sus obras, y amanífestar se ante su abuela, y su madre, lo prímero trataron de haçer mílpa. nosotros sembraremos mílpa díxeron asu abuela, y asu Madre no os aflíxaís aquí estamos nosotros, tus níetos, no sotros somos substitutos de nuestros hermanos esto díxeron el hun ahpu, y xbalanque. y entonces tomando sus hachas, y azadones se fueron con sus çeruatanas al hombro. y salíendo de sus casas auísaron a su abuela que les llevase su comída, al medío día. esta bíen níetos míos díxo la víeja. y llegan do ala mílpa, donde auían desembrar clauaron el azadon en la tíerra, y mucho era lo que se labraba; por el azadon solo, y la hacha clauandola en el palo se íuan todos los palos cayendo tendídos, y tambíen todos los mecates, y era muchísímo lo que auía de palos derríbados, y cortados, con solo vna hacha, y con solo vn azadon, era muchísimo loque estaba labrado ya, no era contable todo lo que auían hecho con solo vn azadonazo, en todos los montes chícos, y grandes, todos se íuan abaxo. y entonçes auísaron a vn animal que se llamaba, xmucur, esto es la paloma de monte.) y la pusíeron enlo alto de vn tronco, y le díxeron el hun ahpu, y xbalanque mírad quando venga nuestra abuela, que ha de venir a traernos de comer, y luego al punto cantad qundo víeres que víene y luego al punto tomaremos nuestro azadon, y hacha. esta bíen, dixo la paloma. y luego solo se entretenían en tírar con la çerbatana, y no trataban de mílpa. y luego que canto la paloma víníeron a toda prísa vno tomo el azadon, y el otro la hacha, y amarrandose las cabezas en valde se vntaban de tíerra las manos el vno como que se ensuçíaba la cara, a modo de el que siembra mílpa, y el otro en valde se desparramaba astíllas en su cabeza así como hachero de palos, y entonçes fue vísto de su abuela, y corríendo, no deuerdad haçían mílpa, y así en valde les lleuaron de comer. y enton çes sefueron a sus casas, verdadera mente estamos cansados abuela, le díxeron, y llegandose asu casa, en valde creían sutrabaxo desus manos, y pies ante su abuela. y las refregaban; y luego fueron al segun do día, y llegando ala mílpa hallaron parados todos los arboles, y los mecates y se auían otra vez vnído, y juntado. quíen sera este díxeron, que nos ha en gañado, sin duda han sído los que han hecho esto todos los animales grandes, y chícos; el tígre, el venado, el conejo, el gato de monte, el lobo; el puerco, el pízote, y los paxaros grandes, y chícos, estos fueron los que híçíeron esto, y luego bolbíeron otra vez ahazer la mílpa, y así mesmo hízo la tíerra, y los palos cortados, y entonçes consultaron, en los palos cortados, y la tíerra desyeruada, velare mos nuestra mílpa quízas cogeremos al que víene ahazer esto, esto díxeron, en su consulta, y llegaron asus casas, que le pareçe abuela lo que hemos visto, la burla que nos han hecho, lo que labramos, se ha buelto otra vez, monte, y pajonal, ya lo hallamos así quando fuímos denantes, esto díxeron asu abuela, y asu Madre, bolberemos otra vez, y velaremos, porque no esbueno esto que nos han hecho. esto díxeron, y luego se armaron, y fueron otra vez a la roza suya, y luego se ocultaron, y estu uíeron ocultos allí, quando se juntaron todos los anímales, en una parte sejuntaron todos los animales chicos ygrandes. y luego empunto de la medía noche, víníeron, y hablando todos quando víníeron deçían así: yaclín che, yaclín caam. (esto es: parense palos, parense mecates) esto dígeron quando víníeron, y se meneaban debaxode los arboles, y mecates, acercandose entonçes se manífestaron ante ellos, y el prímero era vn leon, y vn tígre, y queríendolos coger, nose díeron, y luego se apropínquo vn venado, y vn cone jo, y juntándose vno con otro los cogíeron pero se les arrancaron las colas, en tonçes tomaron la cola de el venado, y la cola de el conejo, y así solo son chíquítas sus colas. y el gato montes, y el lobo tampo sedíeron, ní el puerco, y el pízote todos se pasaron los anímales por delante de hun ahpu, y xbalanque. y rebentaban de colera sus corazones, porque no los auían cogído. y víno vno a la postre saltando, y atajando lo cogíeron en la red al raton, y luego que lo cogíeron, le apretaron el çelebro, que lo querían ahogar, y le quemaron la cola en el fuego, y entonçes tomo su cola el raton sín pelo, y tambíen sus ojos saltadossaltones quando loquisieron ahogar los dos muchachos hun ahpu, y xbalanque. ydíxo el raton, no me mateís, no es esto vuestro offoficio el haçer mílpa. pues que es lo que nos dedeçís, díxeron los dos muchachos al raton. dexadme vn poco, porque tengo, en mí víentre que dezir, y despues lo dire dadme algoque comer díxo el raton, y le fue dícho despues te daremos tu comída deçíldo agora. esta bíen díxo el raton. sabreís que los bienes de vuestros Padres, hunhun ahpu, y vucub hun ahpu qque así se llamaban aquellos,que muríeron en elinfierno, es tan ay, que es conque jugaban, estan colgados sobre el tapanco, su batey, la pala, y la pelota de vle, y no os lo quíere manífestar vuestra abuela, por que por estos muríeron vuestros padres. y díxeron los muchachos: de çíer to lo sabeís vos? y sealegraron mucho quando oyeron la notíçía de la bola de vle, y auíendo dícho el raton le señalaron sucomída al raton. esta sera tu comída, el maís, las pepítas de el chíle, los fríxoles, el pataste, el cacao, esto es vuestro, y sí algo esta guardado, oluídado, también es vuestro comeldo, esto le fue dícho al raton, por hun ahpu, y xbalanque. esta bíen muchachos, y sí me ve vuestra abuela, que me deçis? díxo el raton. no te aflíxas que nosotros estamos aquí, y aduertíremos lo que se ha de haçer. ayque le dígamos a nuestra abuela. luego que te pongamos en la esquína de la casa en llegando llegate luego donde esta colgado, y allí veremos el chíle molido para nuestra comída, y veremos. díxeron al raton y entonçes auísaron ala noche, y consultaron hun ahpu, y xbalanque, y llegaron al medío día.

15

Jun B'atz' and Jun Chowen transformed

The rat came in hidden, and when they reached the house, one of them went straight in, while the other went to the corner; and at once he lifted the rat up high, and set it down there. Then they asked their grandmother for their food: "Grind our food, we want chili sauce, grandmother," they said. And she ground the chili, and set before them a bowl of broth, but this was only to deceive the old woman and their mother. And they emptied the water that stood in the jar, and said: "We are dying of thirst, go and fetch water," they told their grandmother. "Very well," she said, and went off for the water. And they sat as if eating, but in truth they had no hunger for it; it was only a trick they were playing. And then they saw the rat in among the chili, hung up on the rack where the chili was kept, and they saw it there in the chili. So they sent out a little creature called xan, which is like a mosquito, and it went to the water and bored a hole in the old woman's water jar, and the water kept running out of the jar. She tried to stop it, and could not close the hole. "What has our grandmother done, that we are drying up with thirst? Go and see to it, lady," they said, and sent her out again. Then the rat gnawed through the cords by which hung the rubber ball, the bat, the paddle, and the glove, and they fell, and the boys caught them up and went and hid them on the road to the ballcourt, the place of their fathers. Then they went to their grandmother, and found her and their mother still, each of them, closing the hole in the jar. And arriving with their blowguns: "What have you done about the water? We are worn out with waiting, and so we have come," they said. "Look at my jar," said the old woman, "it will not close its hole." And then at once they closed it. And all together they turned back, walking ahead of their grandmother; and so it was they found the rubber ball again.

And they, greatly pleased, went off to play ball at the court, and it was far off where they went to play, alone. And they swept and cleared the ballcourt of their fathers. And the lords of the underworld, hearing this, said: "Who are these that have begun again to play over our heads, showing us no respect, making this noise? Did not Hun Hunahpu and Vucub Hunahpu die, who wished to make themselves great before us? Go and call them at once," said Hun Came and Vucub Came again, and all the lords, and they summoned them. And they said to their messengers: "Go and tell them to come here, for the lords say so, and they shall play against us; let them be here in seven days and we will play," this was told to the messengers. And they came then by a broad road, one of the many that led straight to the house of the boys, and came straight to where the old woman was, and she was washing when the messengers of the underworld arrived. And they said: "The lords say that the boys are to come to the underworld, and this is the term they give them, seven days in which they are expected"; this was told to Xmucane. "Very well," she said, "they will go there, lord messengers." And the messengers went off and returned, and the old woman was left troubled: how was she to send word to her grandsons, to call them? What was she to say of this summons? "It was truly so that the messengers came, long ago, when they came for their fathers," said the old woman, and, very sorrowful, she went into her house alone. And then a louse dropped down, and the old woman caught it up and set it on her hand, and the louse stirred and moved. And she said to it: "My grandchild, would you go and call my grandsons at the ballcourt?" this was said to the louse. "Go and tell them: your grandmother says the messengers of the underworld have come to summon you, and that within seven days you must go, and this is what your grandmother says"; this was told to the louse, and off went the louse, stirring itself along.

And there was sitting in the road a boy named Tamazul, who was a toad, and the toad said to the louse: "Where are you going?" And the louse said: "I am going with a message, I carry it in my belly, to the boys." "Very well," said the toad, "but I see you cannot run; do you want me to swallow you, so you can travel faster? See how I run." "Very well," said the louse to the toad, and then the toad swallowed the louse. And the toad went off running, but soon grew tired and could run no more, and he met a great snake named Zaquicaz, who said: "Where are you going, Tamazul?" this was said to Tamazul by Zaquicaz. And the toad said: "I am a messenger, I carry the message in my belly." And the snake said: "I see you are tired and cannot run; will I not arrive sooner?" said the snake to the toad. "Come here, I will swallow you," and then the snake Zaquicaz swallowed the toad. And then the snake took this food for itself, feeding on toads as it went running, when it was met by a bird named vac, and the bird vac swallowed the snake. And then it flew up over the ballcourt. And then this bird took the food for itself, feeding on snakes in the hills, and the vac, arriving, alighted on the parapets of the ballcourt. And Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque were there enjoying themselves, playing ball, and as the vac arrived it sang, "vac co, vac co," so went its song. "What song is this?" they said, "bring the blowguns." And then they shot at the vac with their blowguns, and the pellet went straight and struck it in the pupil of the eye, and it fell, turning over, and they caught it up at once and asked it: "What have you come here for?" they said to the vac. And it said: "Here in my belly I bring a message; cure my eye first, and then I will tell it." "Very well," they said, and taking a little of the rubber of the ball, they put it on the eye of the vac. This is called lotz quic, which is the rubber of a certain plant, and it cured the bird's eye at once, and it saw well again once it was cured. And they said to it: "Speak now, tell what you carry," and then it vomited up a great snake. And they said to the snake: "Speak now, tell what you carry," this was said to the snake, and it said, "Very well," and then it vomited up the toad. And it was told: "What is your message? Tell it now," and it said: "Here in my belly I carry the message," and it tried to vomit, and could not; only slobber came to its mouth. And it tried again to vomit, and could not, and the boys wanted to beat it, and they kicked it in the haunches, and it tried once more, and still only slobber came to its mouth. And then the boys opened its mouth and tore it, and searched inside its mouth, and found the louse stuck fast in the toad's teeth, for it had lodged there in its mouth and had not been swallowed down; and so the toad only pretends to vomit, and it was held in low regard, and it was decided that it should have no food assigned to it, and none was assigned, and it does not run, but is food for snakes. "Speak now," they said to the louse, and then it gave its message: "Your grandmother says, boys, come, call them, for messengers have come from the underworld, from Hun Came and Vucub Came, saying that within seven days you must go there; come here, they say, we will play ball, and bring the gear of the game, the ball, the bat, the paddle, and the glove, that they may be entertained, the lords say; this is what your grandmother says, so came the message of the lords, and so I came to call you." "Yes, so it will be," they said in their hearts, hearing the message. And then at once they went, and arrived at their grandmother's, and they went only to give word to their grandmother.

Translator’s note

Revised per round-2 audit: completed the section, which had been truncated mid-sentence in paragraph 3, omitting the entire Tamazul/Zaquicaz/vac chain -- the toad swallowing the louse, the snake swallowing the toad, the hawk (vac) swallowing the snake, its "vac co" cry, the boys shooting it and healing its eye with rubber from the ball (lotz quic), the successive vomitings, the discovery of the louse in the toad's teeth, and the louse's delivery of the grandmother's message with the lords' four-item demand for the ball-game gear. Also restored the p1 detail that one boy went straight into the house while the other went to the corner, before lifting the rat up high, rather than compressing this into a single action. Proper names keep the manuscript's own unhyphenated spellings (Hun Hunahpu, Vucub Hunahpu, Hun Came, Vucub Came, Xmucane, Tamazul, Zaquicaz), diverging from Recinos/Goetz-and-Morley hyphenation and from Tedlock's translated names or Christenson's orthography; the hawk is rendered plainly as "a bird named vac" rather than supplying an English bird-name from any published edition.

K’iche’ source text · p28–p30

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p28–p30 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Catepuch xquivvbah ri vac quicutacal vbaca vvb chu baca vvach chizetet cut xcah vloc quiꜩih vi cut xbe qui chapa cate xquiꜩo noh hupa apetic xeqha chire vac qoba nuꜩih chinupam chicunah tana vbacanu vach nabe cate cut xchinbÿh xqhari vac vꜩ bala xeqhacut cate xquelezah zcaquin rih quic ri chaah xquicoh chiv vach ri vac loꜩ quic xubinaah cumal libah chicut xcachoh cumal vꜩ chic vmucubal ri vac xuxic, chabÿh cut xeqha chire vac cate cut xuxavah nima cumaꜩ, caqhauoc xeqha chic chire cumaꜩ ve xqha chicut ta xuxauah chi xpec naquipa ata qui quil caꜩihonoc xuchax chicuri xpec qobanuꜩih chinupam xqha chicuri xpec catecut xutih xabic mahabixuxauah xe que he chucax gh vchi chutiho xmaqo vi chuxauah catecut xrah chaii'c cumal qaholab at qaxtoq xuqhaxic ta xiic v varachac chiacan, xcahcu baquil v varachac chi acan xutih chicut xa que he chuchu ab vchi cate puch xquirech vchi ri xpec xrech cumal qaholab xquizucuh pu chi xa cu nacal ri uq chuvare xpec xapuchi qo vi mana xubico xaquehe xabic quehe cut xchacatah vi rixpec maui calah vvach recha xquiyao ruq maui qhanic xa v chac cumaꜩ xuxic. caꜩihonoc xuqhax chicut ri uq ta xubÿh cu vꜩih caqha ri ivatit ixqaholab he ataca xultacolque cape chi xibalba vzamahel hun came, vvcubcame vvcubix quevlic varal cohchaahvi chipe ri que ꜩabal quic bate pachcab, q,uum are chicaztahv vach varal que qha ahauab xul quiꜩih cacha ri ivatit ta xipetic qui ꜩih caqha ri ivatit coquic caziquinic ivatit xipetic maquiꜩih xeqha cut qaholab chiquiqux ta xquitao huzu xepetic xeopon cut ruq catit xa e pixabai chire catit xebec.

luego baxo vn píojo de y lo cogío la víeja y lo puso sobre la mano, y estaba meneandose el píojo, y anduvo. y le díxo: tu mí níeto querras que te embíe, que vayas a llamar a mís nítos al cementerio, con los mozos, ó muchachos, y llevo en mí víentre el mensage. esta bíen dixo el sapo, pero veo que no puedes correr quíeres que te trague, y así podras ír; mírad como corro yo. y así llegaremos luego esta bíen dixo el píojo, al sapo y luego se lo trago el sapo al píojo. eíba el sapo corríendo, y ya cansado no corría, quando encontrando con vna grande culebra que se llamaba. zaquícaz, y le díxo: donde vas tamazul muchacho? esto le fue dícho al tamazul por el zaquícaz. y díxo el sapo soy mensagero, ala culebra, en mí víentre lleuo el mensage. y dixo la culebra, veo que vas cansado, y no puedes corríendo la culebra, quando fue encon trada de vn paxaro llamado vac, y se trago a la culebra el paxaro vac. y luegollego sobre el çementerío. y entonçes tomo parasí este paxaro este sustento comíendose a las culebras enlos montes y llegando el vac separo sobre las almenas de el zementerío. y estaban holgandose el hunahpu, y xbalanque peloteandose, y enllagando el vac, canto vac go vac go. dixo su canto. (esto áquí esta el vac y díxeron: que canto es este? vengan las ceruatanas. y luego tírandole con la zeruatana al vac fue el vodo que derecho, y le dío en la níña deel ojo, y dando bueltas cayo, y luego locogíeron, y le preguntaron aque aueís venído aquí le díxeron al vac. y dixo el a quí en mí víentre traígo vn mensage. curadme prímero mí ojo, y luego lo dire esta bíen díxeron ellos, y sacando vn poquíto de el vle de la pelota, lo pusíeron en el ojo del vac. y llamase loꜩquíc, esto es vle de çíerta yerua) porque luego curo el ojo de el paxaro, y míro bíen despues que fue curado: y le dixeron ea dí loque traes, y luego bomíto vna gran culebra, y le díxeron ala culebra, ea dí luego, lo que traes, le fue dícho ala culebra, y dixo esta bíen, y luego bomíto el sapo. y le fue dícho que es tu mensage, dílo luego, y díxo el aquí en mí víentre traígo el mensage, y luego probro, a bomítar, y no vomito; sino que como baba se le puso la voca. y probaba, a bomítar, y no podia, y loquívsíeron aporrear, los muchachos, y le díxeron: soís un mentíroso engañador, y le díeron con el píe enlas nalgas, y probootra vez, y no mas que baba haçía en la voca. y entonçes los muchachos le abríeron la voca, y se la rasgaron, y buscaron en la voca, y hallaron pegado al píojo en los díentes de el sapo, por que sele quedo en la voca, y no lo trago, y así haze como que bomíta, y fue despreçíado y ganado, y no tíene comída señalada ní se la señalaron, y no corre, sino que es carne delas culebras. eá habla le díxeron al píojo, y entonçes dío su mensage. diçe vuestra abuela muchachos, ó mançebos anada llamaldos porque víníeron mensageros de el ínfíerno, de hun came, y vucubcame que vayan alla dentro de síete días que vengan aca dícen jugaremos a la pelota y que vengan los ínstrumentos de el juego, el vle, el bate, la pala, y el cuero, que vayan a divertirlos, que lo diçen los Señores, esto dice vuestra abuela,que así víno el men sage de losSeñores y así vine a llamaros. sí sera así díxeronellos en sus corazones, oyendo el mensage. y luego al punto se víníeron, y llegaron con su abuela, y solo fueron a auísarle asu abuela.

16

The ballgame equipment recovered; renewed summons to Xibalba

"We are going, grandmother. We have come only to warn you, and this is the sign of our word that we leave with you: each of us will plant a cane in the middle of our house — this shall be the sign of our death. If it dries, say: 'They are dead, it seems.' But if it sprouts again, say: 'They are alive.' Grandmother, and you, mother, do not weep — this sign remains with you as our word." And when they went, one Hunahpu planted, and the other Xbalanque planted; and they planted them only inside the house, not on the mountain, nor in wet ground, but in dry ground, in the middle of the house they left them planted.

Then they went, each carrying his own blowgun, and they went down into Xibalba. Quickly they went down the steps, and they crossed a river running through a ravine, and they passed among the birds; these birds were called Molay. And they also crossed a river of pus, and another of blood, meant to defeat them, as those of Xibalba supposed — but they did not step in them. And going on from there, they came to a crossroads of four roads, and they truly knew the road of Xibalba: one was black, one white, one red, and one green. And from there they sent out a small creature, an insect called Xan, to go and gather news. It was told: bite each of them, one by one; bite the first who sits there first, and finish biting them all, and yours shall be the sucking of the people's blood on the roads. This was told to Xan. Very well, said Xan.

Then it took the black road and went straight to the first one seated there, who was a thing made of carved wood, dressed up; and it bit him first, and he did not speak. And then it bit the second, and he too did not speak. It bit the third, who was Hun Came, and he cried out — Ay! — when he was bitten. What is it, Hun Came, said the fourth seated there, who bit you? I don't know what has bitten me. It bit the next, and he cried, Ay, what is this, Vucub Came, who has bitten you? said the fifth. Ay, ay, said Xiquiripat. And Vucub Came asked him, who has bitten you? And it bit the sixth, and he cried, Ay — what is it, Cuchumaquic, who has bitten you? it was asked. And when it bit the seventh, who cried Ay, what has bitten you, Ahalpuh? said Cuchumaquic. And when it bit the eighth, who cried Ay, Ahalpuh said to him: what is it, Ahalcana, who has bitten you? And when it bit the ninth, seated there, who cried Ay, Ahalcana said to him: Chamiabac, what has bitten you? And when it bit the tenth, who cried Ay, Chamiabac said to him: Chamiaholom, what has bitten you? And biting the eleventh, who cried Ay, it was said to him: what has bitten you? And it bit the twelfth, who cried Ay, and it was said again: what has bitten you, Patan? And biting the thirteenth, who cried Ay, it was said again: what has bitten you, Quicxic? said Patan. And biting the fourteenth, it was said to him, Quicre — what has bitten you? And one, seated on a bench, was named Holoman. And thus all of them spoke their own names, and each declared himself, saying his name — every one of them, when they were bitten by a hair from the face of Hunahpu; and it was not truly a gnat that went and bit them all, and went to hear the names of them all, on behalf of Hunahpu.

Then, when they arrived where the lords of Xibalba were, they were told: go and greet that lord who is seated there. But he was not truly a lord — he was only a carved dummy, dressed up to deceive them. So they said: that is only a dummy. And greeting the rest, they said: Lord Hun Came, Lord Vucub Came, Lord Xiquiripat, Lord Cuchumaquic, Lord Ahalpuh, Lord Ahalcana, Lord Chamiabac, Lord Chamiaholom, Lord Quicxic, Lord Patan, Lord Quicre, Lord Quicxic Rixcac. This they said when they arrived before them all; they spoke the name of every one of them, and not one name was left out, missed, or forgotten. And this was what those of Xibalba had laughed about — that their names would not be known, that the boys would not find them out.

Come, they were told, and they wished to seat them on the bench of honor, but the boys would not sit there: this is not our seat, they said, for it is only a heated stone. They were not defeated by it. Very well, they were told, go then into the house — and they went into a black house, and there too they were not defeated; this was the first trial of Xibalba. They thought to begin their triumph over the boys there. They gave them a torch and, for each of them, a cigar from the messengers of Hun Came, and said: here is your torch and your cigars; the lords say you must return them at dawn, still burning, not used up. Very well, they said. But they did not light the torch; in its place they set a scarlet feather from a macaw's tail, so that to the watching wardens it seemed to burn all night; and in the cigars they set fireflies, so that these too seemed lit. And so the wardens believed all night that the boys were already beaten. But the torch was not consumed, and likewise the cigars were not consumed by them, and in the morning both were carried back whole to the lords.

And they said: what is this, from where have these come, who begot them, who bore them? Truly our hearts burn, for this that they do is not good; strange are their faces and strange their ways. So they said among themselves. And then all the lords sent to summon them: come, let us play ball, young men. And Hun Came and Vucub Came asked them: from where have you come? Tell it, young men. But they said, we do not know from where we come, and they did not tell it. Very well, said those of Xibalba, let us go play ball, young men. Where shall we throw down this our ball? said those of Xibalba. By no means shall that one of yours be thrown down, said the young men. And those of Xibalba said, by no means, not that one — only ours. Very well, said the young men.

And then a rounded thing was set before them, dressed up. Let this be it — a worm, called chil, said those of Xibalba. Not so, said the boys; it is not that, but the head of a puma, got up to pass for the ball. This is not, said the boys. Very well, said Hunahpu, and threw down the ball of those of Xibalba; and when it came off the bat, a white flint dagger showed itself out from the middle of it, glinting as it went bounding over the floor of the ballcourt. What is this? said the boys. All you mean is to finish us — did you not send to call us? Were not your own messengers the ones who came for us? We are unfortunate, they said; we will go back again. And this was exactly what the boys wanted of them, that they should die there at the game — but they were not the ones defeated; rather those of Xibalba were again defeated by the young men.

Let us play ball, then, with your ball, they were told, and the young men said: very well. And they threw down their own ball, and so the game came to its end for that time. Then those of Xibalba said: what shall we do to overcome them? And they said to the young men: bring us, this coming night, four gourds of flowers — that was the wager they set upon the game. Very well, said the young men. And what flowers? the young men asked those of Xibalba. We want, they said, one gourd of red flowers, one of white, one of yellow, and one of the great flowers, all four gourds full. Very well, said the young men — and it pleased their hearts to let themselves be defeated so.

And then they were sent down into a house where there was nothing but knives of flint, all alike, all sharp, and the knives were many. This was the second trial of Xibalba, and it was meant that they be cut to pieces by the knives, and quickly, before they could think how to save themselves. But they did not die there; instead they spoke to the flint knives and told them: yours shall be the flesh of every animal, and with this the knives did not stir any more, but stayed still, all of them. And their hearts were glad, those of Xibalba, thinking the two boys already defeated. A good thing we have done, they said in their hearts; where now will they go to get flowers? This very night you must give us the flowers — we have beaten you, they said to Hunahpu and Xbalanque. Very well, said the young men, tonight we will play again, they said, when the wager was made.

Then, inside the house of knives, they called to all the ants: come, all knife-ants, all thigh-ants, come, all of you, go, and bring all the flowers we have promised to the lords. Very well, said the ants, and they all went to bring the flowers from the garden of Hun Came and Vucub Came. But those of Xibalba had already warned the one who kept watch over their flowers: let no flowers be carried off, for we have already beaten the two boys, and there is nowhere they can get flowers from — watch all night. Very well, said the guards. But the guards felt nothing; in vain they cried out among the branches of the trees of the garden. And from there came their manner of singing: the one said, xpurpuvec, xpurpuvec, calling it out; the other said, puhuyu, puhuyu — and so the two guards of the garden of Hun Came and Vucub Came are named Xpurpuvec and Puhuyu. And they did not feel the ants carrying off the flowers they guarded, but went turning and shifting among the flowers upon the branches, and with their jaws lifted up those that lay beneath the branches; and the guards kept crying out, and did not feel their own jaws being eaten, nor their wings, nor their tails, and so with their jaws the ants carried off the flowers they gathered, and very quickly they filled the four gourds with flowers, brimming full when it dawned.

Then the messengers, the runners, came, saying: let them come, says the lord, bring here what you have won. Very well, said the young men, and they went carrying the flowers, four gourds full, and they came before the face of the lord, and the lords took the flowers, very soft still, and in this way those of Xibalba were defeated. For it was only the ants that the young men had sent, and in one night the ants had cut the flowers and set them in the gourds, and so all those of Xibalba were terrified, their faces gone pale over the flowers, and they called at once for those who guarded the flowers and said to them: how have you let our flowers be stolen, that we see here? We did not feel it, lord, they said, and see how our own tails are — and their mouths were slit open in payment for having let the flowers be stolen. And so Hun Came and Vucub Came were defeated by Hunahpu and Xbalanque, and this was the beginning of their works against them. And from there came the shape of their mouths, slit in that fashion, and so they are found still.

And then they went to play ball once more, and when the game was ended they agreed between themselves, and the two sides gave word to each other that in the morning they would play again. Very well, said the young men, when they left off the game.

Translator’s note

Revised to fix four fidelity issues flagged in round-2 review, working from the K'iche' (with the Spanish as an aid). (1) The ball Xibalba proposes is now named as the source names it — "a worm, called chil" (Spanish "este gusanío, que se llama chíl"; K'iche' huchil) — rather than mistakenly reusing "Xan," which is the mosquito/gnat from the earlier road episode, a different creature entirely; "Xan" is now used only where the source itself uses it, in the crossroads scene. (2) The ants' summons is now given as direct speech inside the house of knives ("come, all knife-ants, all thigh-ants, come, all of you, go, and bring all the flowers we have promised to the lords" / "very well, said the ants"), matching "chai zanic, che que'n zanic, quixpetoc..." and the parallel Spanish "hormigas de navajas, hormigas de el muslo, venid...", instead of only mentioning the ants after the fact. (3) The named bird-guards are restored: Xpurpuvec and Puhuyu, with their cries transliterated ("xpurpuvec, xpurpuvec" / "puhuyu, puhuyu"), the lords' forewarning to watch the flowers all night, and the etiology of their manner of calling ("and from there came their manner of singing... so the two guards... are named Xpurpuvec and Puhuyu"), matching "de allí vino su modo de cantar" and the naming clause in the K'iche'; the later line about their mouths is kept tied to this same etiological pattern ("and from there came the shape of their mouths, slit in that fashion") rather than left as a vague aside. (4) The betting sequence is restored: the four gourds of flowers are now explicitly set as the wager on the game ("that was the wager they set upon the game"), the boys' willing self-defeat is rendered plainly ("it pleased their hearts to let themselves be defeated so"), and Xibalba's gloating is given as direct speech in the knife-house scene ("a good thing we have done... where now will they go to get flowers? this very night you must give us the flowers — we have beaten you"), matching "Buena cosa hemos hecho decían los de el infierno... ya os hemos ganado." Proper names keep the manuscript's own colonial orthography (Hun Came, Vucub Came, Xiquiripat, Cuchumaquic, etc.), which the source itself uses and so is not a translator echo. House and object names remain plain literal renderings — "a black house," "house of knives," "white flint dagger," "head of a puma" — deliberately avoiding Tedlock's "Dark House," Goetz-Morley's "House of Gloom," and Christenson's coined equivalents. Round-3 fixes: removed an unsupported interpolation ("made bare") on "cabeza de leon"/"holom coh" (head of a puma); corrected the mistranscribed twelfth title in the greeting list from "Quicxic Chuchah" to "Quicxic Rixcac," matching the K'iche' "vquic xic rixcac" (Spanish "quic ríx ⱭaƐ").

K’iche’ source text · p31

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph id p31 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

Nosotros vamos, Señora y solo á auísaros venímos, y esta señal os dexamos de nuestra palabra, cada vno de nosotros sembraremos vna caña, en medio de nuestra casa, y esta sera la señal de nuestra muerte, sí se seca díreís, poco haque murieron; empero sí re toñeze, díreís que estan vívos tus níetosabuela, y vos madre, no lloreís, que señal queda de nuestra palabra con vosotras. y quando se fueron vna sembro hunahpu, y otra sem- bro xbalanque. y solo las sembraron en la casa y no las sembraron en el monte ní tambpoco en tíerra humeda; sino en tíerra seca, en medío de su casa las de xaron sembradas. y luego se fueron lleuando cada vno al ínfíerno cada vno su ceruatana. y luego baxaron al ínfíerno y con brebedad baxaron las gradas, y pasaron vn río, en vna barranca, por me dío delos paxaros pasaron, y estos paxa ros sellamaban, molay., y tambien passaron por vn río de material, y otro de sangre, y estos ríos eran paraque fuesen ven çídos, como pensaban los de el ínfíerno y no los písaron; y salíendo de allí, legaron a una encruçjada de quarto camínos y çíertamente sabían el camíno de el ínfíerno, vno era negro, otro blanco, otro colorado, y otro verde; y desde allí embiaron a vn anímalexo que sellamaba. xan. este íba a tomar nueuos, y embiando, y le fue dícho a cada vno de por sí muerdelos y prímero muerde al prímero que esta asentado, y acaba de morderlos a todos y deti sera el chupar la sangre de los hon bres, y su comída. enlos camínos. esto le fue dícho al xan. estabíen díxo el xan. y luego tomo el camíno negro, y fuederecho alprímero que estaba sentado, que era vn hombre hecho de trapos, y compuesto, y le mordío prímero, y no hablo, y luego mordío al Segundo y tampoco hablo. mordío al terçero, que era: hun came, y díxo aquí, quexandose. dixo quando fue pícado. que es eso hun came, le díxo el quarto que estaba sentado, quíente mordío? nose que me ha mordído. mordío alotro, y díxo ay que es eso vucub cameque te ha mordído le díxo el quínto. ayay. díxo le díxo ahal cana, que te mordío chamí baca? y quando mordío al deçímo, que dixo: ay. le díxo chamía buc, que te mordío chamía holom? y mordíendo al vndeçímo, que dixo: ay le díxo chamía holom que te mordío? y mordío al duodeçímo, que dixo ay ledixo otra uez. que te mordío patan? y mordíendo al terçío de çímo que dixo ay. le dixo otra vez: que te mordío quíc xíc. le díjo patan. y mor díendo al quarto deçímo que dixo ay le díxo a quíc ríx ƐaƐ, quíc re. que te mor dío.? y así todos díxeron sus nombres y todos se manífestaron entre sí mesmos, díçíendo sus nombres. de cada vno. y se llamaba holoman, vno que estaba asentado en vn vanco. y níngun nombre de perdío, síno que todos díxeron sus nombres todos quando fueron mordidos por vn pelo de la cara de hun ahpu, y no era mosquito en realídad de verdad, el que fue a morderlos atodos y que fue a oyrlos nombres detodos por hun ahpu. y luego que llegaron alla á donde estaban los de el ínfíerno, les díxeron ea saludad aese Señor. y no
