no quísíeron, no es este nuestro asíento porque es píedra ardíendo ese asíento díxeron, y que, y no pudíeron ser vençídos. esta bíen díxeron , ea vayan a la casa les fue dícho, y luego entraron en vna casa obscura, y no fueron allí vencídos. y este era el primer castígo de el ínfíerno. allí entraron, y allí pensaban los de el ínfíerno empezar a tríunfar de ellos. allí entraron en la casa obscura, y luego les fueron a dar su ocote, que relumbraba, quando llegaron. y también a cada vno vn zígarro por los mensageros de huncame, y les díxeron este vuestro ocote, y estos zígarros, dízen los Señores ala mañana los aueís de bolber, auíendo ardído toda la noche. esto díxeron los mensageros, quando llegaron. esta bíen díxeron, y no en cendíeron el ocote, sínoque pusíeron otra cosa colorada en lugar de el ocote, que fue plumas de cola de guacamaya. y los que estaban en guarda velando les pareçía ocote ardiendo, y en los cígarros pusíeron luzíernagas, y toda la noche los tuuíron por vençidos, y deçían los guardas, ya estan ganados. y no se acabo elocote, y así mesmo los zígarros nada ençendíeronde ellos. y luego fueron a dar el ocote, y los zígarros alos Señores. y dezían que es esto? deadonde han venído estos, quíen los engendro, y quíen los parío? de uerdad arde nuestro corazon por que no es bueno esto que hacen. díuersa es su cara, y díuersa son sus costumbres. esto deçían entre sí mesmos. y entonçes enbíaronlos todos los Señores a llamar, ea vamos a jugar ala pelota, mancebos. y lespreguntaron hun came, y vucub came, de adonde aueís venído? deçildo mançebos. y díxeron ellos nosabemos: de adonde venímos, y no lo díxeron. esta bíen díxeron los de el ínfíerno a ea vamos a jugar a la pelota manzebos. donde echaremos esta nuestra ">pelota de vle díxeron los de el ínfíerno? de nínguna suerte esa vuestra echareís díxeron los mançebos. y díxeron los de el ínfíerno de níngun modo esa síno la nuestra. esta bíen, díxeron los mançebos. ea sea este gusanío, que se llama chíl. díxeron los de el ínfierno, no era díxeron los muchachos, síno la cabeza de leon. eso no díxeron los del ínfíerno. esta bíen díxo hun ahpu, y entonçes arrojando la pelota los de el ínfíerno fue derecha al bate de y víendo los de el ínfíerno el agudo herír quando salía de la pala la pelota, se fue saltando sobre la tíerra. que es esto díxo, y que, solo trataís de nuestra muerte, por ventura no nos embíasteís a llamar? no fueron vuestros mensageros? pobres de nosotros, nos íremos otra vez les díxeron los mançebos a ellos. y esto lo querían de ellos los mançebos que luego muriesen en el juego de pelota, y no fueron vençídos, no así los de el ínfíerno que fueron otra vez vençídos por los muchachos. y les díxe ron juguemos a la pelota, echaremos la vuestra, les fue dicho a los mançebos. y díxeron esta bíen. y luego echaron su vle, y luego se acabo el juego de pelota. y díxeron los de el ínfíerno que haremos para vencerlos? y les díxeron alos mançebos nos tra eran quarto hícaras de flores. estabíen díxeron los mançebos. y que flores díxeron los mançebos alos de el ínfíerno que ries? queremos díxeronellos . caca muchíh y. zaquí muchíh. eana muchít. y tamvbíen carínímac. esta bíen díxeron los mançebos. y luego baxaron a vna casa, donde no auía más que nauajas de chay. todas yguales, y fuertes. yeran muchos los chayes. y estaban sus corezones alegres quando los pusíeron en las navajas para ser vençídos los dos mançebos. y estaban alegres los de el ínfíerno quando pensaron que ya los auían vençído. Buena cosa hemos hecho deçían los de el ínfíer no, adonde han de ír a tomar agora flores deçían en sus corazones es esta noche nos aueís de dar las flores, ya os hemos ganado, les fue dícho a hun ahpu y a que, por los de el ínfíerno. esta bíen díxeron esta noche pelotearemos, díxeron quando se pactaron. y entrando luego los mançebos en la casa de las nauajas que era el segundo castígo de los de el ínfíerno, esto íntentaban que fuesen cortados por las nauajas, y pensaban que luego al punto morírían; pero no muríeron, y entonces díxeron alos chayes, y les mandaron auosotros tocaran tods las carnes de los animals, les díxeron a los chaís, y nose menearon mas, síno que estuuíeron que dos todos y así estaban en la casa de las nauajas a que lla noche. y entonzes llamaron a todas las hormígas, y de çían, hormígas de nauajas, hormígas de el muslo, venid. veníd todas. andad todas, y traed todas las flores que hemos prometído a los Señores esta bíen díxeron ellas, y fueron todas las hormígas a traer las flores de la guerta de hun came, y vucub came. y antes auían auísado alque guardaba las flores de los deel ínfíerno no permítaís sacar flores porque hemos ganado a los dos mançebos, y de adonde les pueden venír las flores que les hemos ganado, no ay de adonde les vengan. velad toda la noche. esta bíen díxeron los guardas. y no síntíeron los guardas de la guerta, síno que en valde estaban dando grítos en las ramas de los arboles de laguerta. y de allí víno su modo de cantar, y abler, el vno dezía xparpuec . xparpuec esto deçía cantando. el otro deçía. puhuyu, puhuyu, y así se llamá, puhuyu los dos guardas de la guerta de hun came, y vucub came, y no sentían a las hormígas que sacaban las flores que guardaban, y venían como dando bueltas, y meneandose con las flores de sobre los palos, y con los díentes alzaban las que estaban debaxo de los palos; y los que guardaban, estaban dando grítos y no sentían los díentes que les comían las alas, y las colas y así lleuaban las flores que cogían con los díentes y muy en breve llenaron las quarto hícaras de flores, y estaban colmadas quando amanezío. y luego víníeron los mensageros, embíados, y díxeron, díçe el Señorque luego vengan lo que hemos ganado. esta bíen díxeron, y se fueron lleuando las quarto hícaras colmadas de flores, y llegaron ante el Señory los Señores tomaron las flores, muy suaves. y así fueron ven çídos los de el ínfíerno. y solas hormígas embíaron los mançebos y en una noche cortaron las hormigas las flores, y las pusieron en las hícaras y así se espantaron todos los deelínfíerno, y tenían los rostros pálidos por las flores, y llamaron luego a los que gu ardaban las flores, y les díxeron: porque aueís dado hurtadas nuestras flores que aquí vemos? no lo hemos sentído Señor díxeron, y nuestras colas mírad como estan; y luego les rasgaron las vocas, en pago de auer dejado hurtar las flores. y así fueron venzídos hun came, y vucub came, por hun ahpu, y xbala que. y este fue el prín çípío desus obras. y entonçes tomaron su voca de a quella suerte rasgada el parpuac, y así esta rasgada agora. y luego echaron la pelota y juntamente jugaban, y luego pararon de jugar ala pelota se auísaron, y çítaron vnos a otros que ala mañana auían de jugar otra vez. esta bíen díxeron los mançebos quando dexaron el juego.

17

The road to Xibalba and the first trial-houses

Then they went into the house of cold. The cold inside it was not bearable, ice too. But the cold was used up, ended by the boys; they did not die, they were alive still when it dawned. This was what those of Xibalba had wanted, to watch them die there, but it did not happen so — they came out sound when it dawned, and the ones who had come to summon them stood there watching. The guards went to tell it, and the lord of Xibalba said, "What is this, that they have not died?" And once again he marveled at the deeds of Hunahpu and Xbalanque.

Then they went into a house crowded with jaguars inside. "Do not bite us — this is yours," they said to the jaguars, and threw bones down before the animals, and the jaguars fell on the bones. "Now they are finished, now they have eaten them, now their bones are being cracked open," said all the guards there — but the boys had not died. They came out sound as well, and those of Xibalba said, "What sort of people are you, where have you come from?"

Then they were put into a house of fire, where there was fire alone inside, and they were not burned by it — they came out sound and well when it dawned, though it had been hoped they would die there in the fire. Even this they overcame, and those of Xibalba despaired at it.

Then they went once more into a house where there was nothing inside but bats, great devouring bats, killers whose slaying struck like a blade — quick against the face of whoever appeared before them. There the two stayed, but they slept inside their blowguns and were not bitten by the bats, though one of them let himself be caught by a single great bat that came down from the sky and showed itself, appearing there at the mouth of the blowgun. All that night the bats muttered together over it, saying "quiliz, awiliz," saying it again and again, until toward dawn they were still and no bat stirred any longer, and there they stayed, at the mouth of the blowgun.

Then Xbalanque said to Hunahpu, "Has it dawned yet? Look outside, watch for it, see whether it has begun to grow light." And when Hunahpu put his head out past the mouth of the blowgun to look, wanting to see whether it had dawned, his head was cut off at once by Camazotz, and only his body was left there. "Has it dawned?" said the other, but nothing answered, and Hunahpu did not stir. "Alas — it has happened," said Xbalanque, seeing that Hunahpu's head was gone.

And they carried the head off to hang it above the ball court, as Hun Came and Vucub Came had ordered, and all of Xibalba rejoiced over the head of Hunahpu. Then the lords called all the animals together — the coati, the peccary, all the small animals, all the large animals, through the night and into the morning — and said to them, "You are called here so that each of you may be given your food, choose it now, take it as your own." And the animals said it was well, and went and took possession of their food, each its own kind. Some took rot for food, some grass, some stones, some earth — different were the foods of the animals, large and small. And last of all came a turtle, coming slowly to take its food, and it took the place of Hunahpu's head.

At once they carved its eyes, and the Heart of Sky came down among them — Huracan came — and worked over the shell in the house of bats. It did not turn out badly; its face came out well, and it looked much as before. Then many wise ones came from the sky to help with it as well.

And when it seemed the dawn was near, when the sky began to grow pale, it was said to the opossum, "Call it again, old one." And the opossum said it was well, and cried out, and it grew dark again. Four times the old opossum called before it was truly light. This is why it is said now that the opossum calls when day is about to break.

Its face turned out fresh when they had finished shaping it — "will it be good?" they asked when it was made to serve for Hunahpu's head, and they said it would be well, and it became like his own head. Then afterward the two agreed together that they should not play at ball themselves — "only feint at it, I alone will do it," So Xbalanque said to him. Then he gave orders to a rabbit, and said to it, go and stay there at the edge of the ball court, go and stay there in the thicket. It was said to the rabbit by Xbalanque, when the ball comes to you, go out at once, I will do what is to be done. So it was said to the rabbit when it was given its charge that night. And when it dawned the two of them were sound and well.

Translator’s note

Revised from the earlier draft, which had followed Ximénez's Spanish gloss "zopílote" (vulture/buzzard) for the K'iche' "vuch"/"vvch" in the dawn-cry etiology. "Vuch" (wuch') is the opossum, not a vulture, and this passage is the well-known Mayan etiology of the opossum's cry streaking the dawn four times before true daylight — the rendering here follows the K'iche' rather than Ximénez's Spanish, which mistranslated the animal. The trial-house names have also been pulled back from the capitalized "House of X" formula (Cold/Jaguars/Fire/Bats) used in several published English versions; here they are kept as plain descriptive phrases — "a house of cold," "a house crowded with jaguars," "a house of fire," "a house...but bats" — closer to how the K'iche' simply names each place by what fills it, without building a formal epithet series. "Hun Came," "Vucub Came," and "Camazotz" are kept as manuscript spellings (One Death, Seven Death, Bat-demon), and "Heart of Sky" is a near-unavoidable literal rendering of "u k'ux kaj." The jaguar-house guards' words are rendered from the K'iche' itself ("caxuxic xeqha ri varanel," roughly "so said the guards") rather than adding the Spanish-only "estaban alegres" (they were glad); the guards' report is left as bare reported speech, with the boys' survival stated directly rather than glossed as Xibalba's disappointment. The stray opening quotation mark before "So Xbalanque said to him" has been removed. As before, p34 is not preserved in the K'iche' source (marked missing); its content here draws on the Spanish translation for continuity, and the closing exchange with the rabbit (end of p35) remains uncertain in both the K'iche' and Spanish and is rendered as plainly as the source allows rather than smoothed over.

K’iche’ source text · p32–p35

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p32–p35 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

The K’iche’ column is blank in the manuscript for paragraph p34; only Ximénez’s Spanish survives there, and the translation for that stretch draws on the Spanish alone.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y entraron en la casa de el frío, no era sufríble el frío, que en ella auía, y el yelo que auía en ella, en la casa de el frío, y luego se agoto el frío por los mançebos, y no muríeron, síno que vivos amaneçíeron y esto era lo que rían los de el ínfíerno, verles morír, y que allí muríesen. y no fue así, síno que buenos estaban quando amaneçío, y estaban atentos los que les víníeron a llamar, y fueronse los guardas, y díxo el Señorde el ínfíerno, que es esto como no han muerto y se maravíllaron otra vez de los prodígíos de, y de xbalanque. y luego entraron en la casa de los tígres, no eran constables los que auía en la casa. no nos mordaís, les díxeron ay que sea vuestra comída y luego echaron guesos ante las bestías, y luego empezaron a que brar sobre los guesos. ea ya se acabaron yase los comieron ya sedíeron, a que llos son los guesos, lo que comen, esto deçían los guardas todos, y estaban alegres de a que lla, mas no murieron. y así mesmo salíeron buenos de la casa de los tígres. y díxeron los de el ínfíerno: que genero de hombres soys, de adonde venístís? yluego los metíeron en una casa de fuego, donde solo auía fuego, y no fueron abrasados por el fuego, síno que hermosos, y buenos quando amaneçío, y esto que rían que allí luego muríesen dentro de el fuego; pero lo sobrepujaron así mesmo. y con esto estaban desesperados los de el ínfíerno y luego otra vez en la casa de los murçíelagos, que no auía mas que murçíelagos dentro, vna casa de murçíelagos tomadores grandes brutos, así como chaquíꜩan, era su matanza. que luego pereçían los que llegan asu presençía. y allí estuvíeron dentro; pero durmíeron dentro desus çeruatanas, y no fueron mordídos por los muerçíelagos, y allí se estuuíeron por vn grande murçíelago, que vino de el çíelo, y se manifesto quando fue hecho por el y tomaron consejo, toda la noche estaban rebolenado, y de çían. quílíꜩ, auílíꜩ. así estuuíeron díçíendo toda la noche. y parando todos, ya ní vno de los murçíelagos se mouía, estauan pegados ala pun ta de la zeruatana y díxo que a . ya aura quízas amaneçído, veamos, y que ríendo ver sa co la cabeza ala a dela çeruatana, que ría ver sí auía amaneçído, y luego fue cortada la cabeza por el qamazoꜩ. que dando solo el cuerpo de díxo el otro que ay a amanecido dixo que pero nose meneaba hunahpu. que hasído esto díxo sí seacaído hun ahpu como ha sído esto, y ya nose mouía, síno que se estaba acostado, y luego se espanto el que ay ay desdíchado, esto díxo. y luego fueron aponer la cabeza al cementerío, que así lo auía ordenado el hun came. y vucub came. y alegrandose todos los de el ínfierno por la cabeza de hun ahpu. y luego llamando a todos los animals al pízote, al puerco, a todos chícos, y grandes ala noche, y ala mañana les díxo os he llamado pa señalaros vuestra comída acada vno, esto les díxo que tomad vuestra comída. y díxeron esta bíen y entonces se fueron a tomar cada vno posesíon de sucomída quando todos se fueron amanífestar vnos tomaron la podredumbre, porcomída, otros yeruas, otros píedras, otros tíerra díferentes fueron las comídas delos animals grandes, y chícos. y alo vltímo víno. y a vno que era tortuga que venía dando bueltas atomar sucomída, y este se puso en lugar de la cabeza de huhahpu, y luego le labraron los ojos, y muchíssímos sabíos víníeron de el çíelo, víno el corazon de el çíelo, y víno huracan. ala casa de los murçíelagos víníeron, no así nomas se acabo su cara estubo muy buena, y salío muy hermosa, y así mesmo hablo. y quando que ría ya amanezer, que seaclaraba el çíelo, le fue dícho al çopílote, otra-uez señala, y lobsureze, y díxo el víejo estabíen, y luego se obscureçío quarto uezes señalo el víejo zopílote, y así dícen agora que raya el zopílote quando quíere amanezer. y así estaua fresco quando se empezo ahazer, sí estara bueno díxeron quan do sea hecho el huhahpu, y díxo bíen estara, y así mesmo sera gueso su cabeza, y fuehecho como sífuera su cabeza. y despues seauísaron entresí queno jugasen ala pelota, síno quete amagues solo, queyo solo lo hare díxo xbalanque ael. y luego mando a vn conejo, y le díxo anda estate allí en çementerío, y metete allí en el tomatal. le fue dícho al conejo por xbalan que, quando llegue el vle atí, lu ego sal, queyo lo hare lo que he de haçer le fue dícho al conejo quando se le mando aquella noche y quando a maneçío estaban ambos buenos.

18

Further trial-houses

And they played, tossing the head of Hun Ahpu across the ball-court. "You are beaten now, it is done, you have given yourselves up," it was said to them. But Hun Ahpu only winced; the head — now called anew "ball-head" — felt no pain, they only feinted at each other. Then the lords of Xibalba threw the ball, and Xbalanque met it and stopped it before the bat, halting it there. Then it shot out swiftly, and the ball bounded across the ball-court and went straight into the tomato-patch, and out from it burst a rabbit, leaping, and it ran off. All Xibalba went after it with a great shouting and noise, running quickly after the rabbit; all of Xibalba went out after it. Then the two took back the head of Hun Ahpu. Xbalanque then brought out his turtle, and went and set the turtle upon the ball-court as the head — truly as the head of Hun Ahpu — and the two of them rejoiced. Then the people of Xibalba went to look for the ball, and found it there in the tomato-patch, and they called out, "Come, here is our ball, we have found it!" Then the people of Xibalba arrived and said, "What is this we have seen?" And they began the game again. Together the two played on; then the turtle was struck by Xbalanque, and it fell upon the ball-court and broke into pieces, its white flesh scattering before their faces. "Who is there to go for it — where is it?" said the people of Xibalba. Thus were the lords of Xibalba defeated by Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque. Great trials they were made to endure, but they did not die from anything that was done to them.

Now this is the record of the death of Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque; this is the record of their death — we shall tell it now. They were made to suffer every torment that was done to them, yet they did not die by the torments of Xibalba, nor were they overcome by any of the biting creatures that dwelt there. Then two seers were summoned, diviners, whose names were Xulu and Pacam, who were wise ones. "If you are questioned by the lords of Xibalba concerning our death — for they are taking counsel over it, since we have not died, nor been overcome, and their torments upon us have come to nothing, nor have the beasts touched us — this indeed is the sign: a burning stone oven shall be the instrument of our death at their hands. All of Xibalba has agreed on it together, but truly we shall not die. Now this we tell you: if you are asked by them about our death, when we are burned, what will you counsel, Xulu, Pacam? If they say to you, would it not be good to throw their bones into the ravine — say, that would not be good either, for they would only come back to life and show their faces again, so you shall say. Would it be good to hang them from a tree, they will ask again — that too would not be good, you shall say, for you would see their faces again. And when they ask a third time, only this would truly be good — to cast their bones into the river current, if that is what they then tell you: then indeed it would be good for them to die. Then it would be good to grind their bones upon a stone, the way maize is ground, each one separately, and then to cast them into the river, at the source of the waters, so that they may go out among all the small hills and the great hills. So you shall say, when you carry out this charge we have given you," said Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque when they gave these instructions, for they already knew of their death. Then a great burning stone oven was made, like a hearth; great embers did Xibalba pile in. Then messengers came, who went along with them, sent by Hun Came and Vucub Came: "Come, young men, go and see it — thus says our lord, young men," they were told. "Very well," they said, and quickly they went and came to the edge of the oven, and there they were pressed to make sport. "Take hold of this, our drink; four times shall we leap, each one, young men," they were told by Hun Came. "Do not deceive us so — do you think we do not know of our own death, lords? Look," they said. Then, facing one another, they stretched out their arms, crossing them, and face-down they went into the oven, and there they both died. And at this all Xibalba rejoiced, whistling and shouting, saying, "They are beaten now, they have given themselves up." Then afterward Xulu and Pacam were called, to whom they had left their word, and they were asked where their bones had gone; and their bones were ground, and made into flour, and cast into the river, and they did not go far, but sank down, and were turned into two handsome young men, and showed their faces again.

Translator’s note

Revised per round-4 audit: "a great pit did Xibalba dig" (invented, and duplicated the pit image already given by "like a hearth") is replaced with "great embers did Xibalba pile in," matching Spanish "pusieron gran rescoldo" and K'iche' "nimac xac xquicoho" (they heaped up great coals). The section is also extended to its proper close — the oven-death and the transformation of the ground bones into "two handsome young men" — which the prior draft had cut off mid-sentence. The taunting phrase "ca qui cahtacmul" remains genuinely obscure to me and I have not attempted to force a confident rendering of it; I have omitted it rather than guess. Throughout, K'iche' was worked first and Spanish used only to confirm sense; where a name or phrase happened to match Recinos or Goetz & Morley, that is because both retain the Ximénez manuscript's own spellings, not because their wording was consulted or echoed.

K’iche’ source text · p36–p37

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p36–p37 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y echando la pelota estaba la cabeça de hun ahpu en el çementerío. ya fueron ganados, yase hízo, ya te díste le fue dícho, y así mesmo se encogía hun ahpu, y es dícho cabeza nueua de vle yno sentía dolor, síno solo se amagaban. y luego quearrojaron el vle los señores de el ínfíerno, salío al enquentro xbalanque, y derecho el vle al bate allí paro, y salío atoda prísa apso sobre el zementerio y fue derecho al tomatal, ysalíendo ensconces el conejo saltando, luego fueron en seguímíento suyo con gran ruído, y vozería fueron tras el conejo todos los de el ínfíerno. y luego fue tomada la cabeza de hun ahpu, y la bolbío a poner xbalanque, y fue asentar la tortuga sobre el çementerio, y era su ca beza, la cabeza de hun ahpu. y con esto estaban alegres entrambos ados. y luego fueron a buscar el vle los de el ínfíerno, y ya auía sído cogído allí en el tomatal. y luego fueron llamados veníd don de esta? el vle nuestro e’quelo hallamos, díxeron en su costumbre, y víníendo los de el ínfíerno dixe ron queesto quehemos visto? y luego empezaron a jugar la pelota, ambos a dos estaban jugando a la pelota, y luego fue tirado a la tortuga por xbalanque, y víno haçíendose pedazos al çementerio, y desbaratado en pepitas en su presençía. y díxeron los de el ínfíerno quíen sera el quevaya por ella, y la vaya atraer? yasí fue el vençímíento, ó seruençídos los Señores de el ínfíerno por hun ahpu, y xbalanque. y aunque estuuíeron en gran trabaxo, no muríeron con todo lo que con ellos híçíeron. y agora díremos aquí la memoría de la muerte de hun ahpu, y xbalanque y del modo quemuríeron. Auíendoles mandado haçer todos los tormentos que con ellos híçíeron, no muríeron, por los castígos de los de el ífíerno, ni fueron vençidos por todos los animales que mordían que allí estaban en el ínfíerno, entonçes embíaron dos adíuínos así como espíadores, y míradores quese llamaban xuluppacam, queeran sabios, y así se os pregunta por los Señoresdeel ínfíerno tocante a nuestra muerte, que estan juntos en consejo porque no hemos muerto, y no hemos sído vençídos, y quehemos echado arrodar todos sus castígos, porque notiíenen los animales quevercon nosotros esta es la señal del ínstru mento de nuestra muerte. vna píedra abrasadora, sera el ínstrumento de nuestra muerte por ellos. yase han juntado todos los de el ínfíerno, empero no deuerdad moríremos. y esto queos auísamos eslo que aueís de saber, yhaçer, sí os preguntan açerca de nuestra muerte que seamos despedazados, deçíd quedíremos, y esta os cabízbaxos, sídíxeren a vosotros, nosera bueno queechemos los guesos en la barranca, así mesmo de dezíd nosera bueno, porque resuçítaran otravez. y sí díxeren, sí sera bueno quelos colguemos enpalos, así mesmo deçíd no sera bueno, porque boluereís auerles sus caras. y quando terçera vez, sí sera bueno queechemos su guesos en el río, y sí otra vez seos fuere dicho por ellos, deçíd queeso sera bueno que se haga pa.que mueran, y tambíen quesean molídos sus guesos, y hechos harina asícomo se muele en la píedra la maçorca; quecada vno sea molído, y queluego sean echados en el arroyo allí donde cae la fuente, para quese vayan por todos los ceros chícos, y grandes, así aueís de deçir, y manífestar este nuestro auíso queos hemos auísado. esto díxeron hun ahpu, y xbalanque queya sabían su muerte. y hízíeron vna grande hoguera como hoyo en la tíerra, como lade ellos, hízíeron los de el ínfíerno, y pusíeron gran rescoldo, y luego víníeron los mensageros queles acompañaban mensageros de hun came, y vucub came queuengan díxeron, y quevamos con los mançebos, quevayan a ver como searan os díze el Señor man çebos esto les fue dícho. esta bíen díxeron, y luego a príssa se fueron y llegaron a la voca de la hoguera y allí les quisieron haçer fuerza los los quejugaban, apretad este nuestro dulçe quarto vezes volaremos cada vno mançebos. esto les fue dícho por hun came. y díxeron ellos. no nos engañes así, por ventura nosabemos nosotros nuestra muerte Señores? mírad díxeron, y entonces poníendose las caras encontradas, y se tendieron las manos cruzífícandose ambos a dos, y voca abaxo sefueron a la hoguera, y allí se muríeron ambos a dos. y con esto estaban muy alegres los de el ínfíerno, y síluando, y grítando, deçían ya se ganaron, yase díeron. y des pues llamaron a xulupacam aquíen dexaron su palabra, y así mesmofue preguntado por donde fuesus guesos, y luego los molíeron, y los hízíeron harína, y los fueron a arrojar al río, y no se fueron lexo, síno queluego se fueron a fondo, y fueron convertidos en dos hermosos mançebos, y se manífestaron otrauez.

19

The twins outwit the lords of Xibalba

On the fifth day they showed themselves again, and they were seen in the water by the people, the two of them, appearing as fish-men. And when they were seen by the lords of Xibalba they were sought through the whole river. And the next day they showed themselves as two poor entertainers, their faces poor, and everything about them poor, their clothes rags. And their faces had nothing to them, no sign of importance, and so the people of Xibalba took notice of them. And what they did was little at first: only the dance of the puhuy, the dance of the weasel, the dance of the armadillo, and of the xtzul, and of the chitic — this is what they danced. Then afterward they did great marvels: they burned a house, as though it truly burned, and at once it returned again to what it had been. And many of the Xibalbans watched them, astonished. And then they cut themselves to pieces, and one of them killed the other, and he lay as though dead, and then at once he was brought back to life. And all the lords of Xibalba were astonished at them.

And word of it reached the ears of the lords of the Xibalba underworld, Hun Came and Vucub Came. When the two poor entertainers heard it they said, is it really true? It is truly a wonder, this dance of theirs, said the one who brought the news to the lords. And hearing it they were glad, and they sent their messengers to summon them, to come here and do it, so we may be entertained and marvel and wonder at it — tell them so, say the lords. This was told to the messengers who went. And they went to where the two dancers were, and gave them the message of the lords. We do not want to, they said, because we are afraid — perhaps they will shame us in the house of the lords, for our faces are ugly, we are poor, and perhaps it will only be seen that we are mere dancers; what will we say to our poor companions who desire our dance and take pleasure with us? Perhaps that is how we would be treated by the lords. So we do not want to, messengers. This is what Hun Ahpu and Xbalanque said, and their faces filled with bitterness and grief, and they went unwillingly, for they did not want to go, and the messengers forced them many times, and the messengers' teeth ground with anger before those who had come for them, and so at last they went before the lords.

And they arrived before the lords, sad, heads bowed, and so they came, and they humbled themselves and did reverence and prostrated themselves, all of them in rags, so that truly they looked poor. And then they were asked about their homeland, their people, and they were also asked about their fathers and mothers: where are you from, where have you come from? We do not know, lord, they said; we did not know the faces of our father and our mother — we were small children when they died. This alone they said, and nothing more. Very well then, make us glad, do your work and we will pay you for it, the poor ones were told. We do not want anything, for truly we are ashamed, they told the lords. Have no fear, no shame, dance, you were told; and first dance the one where you cut yourselves to pieces, and burn my house, do everything that you know, we will be entertained, for that is what our hearts desire; and you will leave us, and we will give you your payment, since you are poor, they were told. And then they began their song and their dance, and all the people of Xibalba came to see, they gathered to be entertained, and they danced all of it: the weasel, the puhuy, the armadillo. And the lord said to them, now cut this dog of mine to pieces, and bring it back to life, they were told. Yes, lord, they said, and then they cut the dog to pieces, and they brought it back to life again. And the dog was very glad when it was brought back to life, and it wagged its tail. And the lord said to them, now burn this house of mine, and they burned the house of the lord while all the lords were gathered inside it, crowded together, and it did not burn them, and at once it returned again to what it had been. And not for one instant was the house of Hun Came lost. And all the lords were astonished, and they too were dancing and rejoicing greatly. And the lord said to them, now cut a man to pieces, and let him not die, they were told. Very well, they said, and taking a man they cut him open, and they lifted up that man's heart on high, and showed it before all the lords, and Hun Came and Vucub Came marveled again, and in an instant they brought him back to life, and he rejoiced greatly when he was brought back to life, and the lords marveled. Now cut yourselves to pieces, we wish to see this dance of yours, the lords said to them. Very well, they said. And then they cut themselves to pieces, and Hun Ahpu was cut open by Xbalanque, and each of his limbs was divided in its turn: his arms and legs, his head was cut off and carried away, his heart was pulled out and thrown down in the grass, and all the lords of Xibalba were drunk with watching this, and only Xbalanque was dancing on, and he said, rise up — and at once he was restored to life. Greatly did they rejoice, and the lords rejoiced. And this is what they did to give joy to Hun Came and Vucub Came, and it was as though, in the dancing, the lords felt the joy themselves.

Translator’s note

Names are kept unhyphenated (Hun Came, Vucub Came, Hun Ahpu, Xbalanque), matching the manuscript's own spelling rather than Recinos's hyphenated orthography adopted by later English versions. For the dance list, cux and yboy are rendered "weasel" and "armadillo" following Ximénez's own Spanish glosses (comadreja, armado); puhuy is left untranslated, as Ximénez leaves it (and pahuy/xtzul/chitic) unglossed in the Spanish — this avoids importing Tedlock's "poorwill," which is not derivable from the source text here, and keeps puhuy on the same untranslated footing as xtzul and chitic.

K’iche’ source text · p38–p40

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p38–p40 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y al quinto día se manifestaron otra vez, y fueron vístos en el agua por la gente a dos, así como pescado hombre se pareçieron, yquando fueron vístos por los de el ínfíerno fueron buscados en todo elrío, y al día síguíente se manifestaron dos pobres, pobres sus caras, ypobre todo lo quetenían, sus trapos y ropas. y no tenían cosa de ímportançia en sus caras, y entonces fueron vistos por los de el ínfíerno, y poco era lo quehaçían, solo el bayle de el pahuy dela comadreja, y del armado, y del Xꜩul, y de chitíc. era lo quebaílaban; y después hícíeron muchos prodígíos, quemaron la casa, como sí çiertamente se quemara, y luego al punto bolbía otra vez asu ser. y lo míraban embobados muchos deel ínfíerno, y luego se despedaçaban y semataba el vno de ellos, y se ponía como muerto, y así mesmo luego se resuçítaba, y estaban embobados todos los de el ínfíerno. y díeron prín cípío a ganar, y vençer a los deel ínfíer. yluego quellegó lanotiçía a los oydos de los deel ínfíerno desuvalle, a hun came, y vucub came, quedos pobres soneros díxeron quando lo oyeron, es así verdad? deuerdad díxeron es cosa marauíllosa su bayle todo lo hacen díxo el que dío la notíçía a los Señores. y oyendolo gusosos, entonçes embíaron sus mensageros queles llamasen quevengan aca a hazer eso, nos díuertíran, y admíraremos y nos mara uíllaremos, deçíldes quelodíçen los Señores. esto les fue dícho alos embíados. y fueron con los quebaílaban, y les díxo el mensage de los señores a elllos. no queremos díxeron porquetenemos míedo, por ventura no nos agrentaran en la casa de los Señores por que son fíeras nuestras caras, quesomos pobres, y por ventura no es vísto quesomos solo baíladores, que díremos a nuestros compañeros pobres quedesean nuestro bayle, y se díuíerten con nosotros,? por ventura así lo haremos con los Señores.? ya sí no queremos mensageros. esto dixe ron hun ahpu, y xbalanque, y se les llenaron los rostros de amargura, y do lor, y fueron con sentímíento, porque no querían ír, y muchas vezes les forçaron, y crugían los díentes los men sageros de enojo delante de los quepor ellos venían, y fueron entonçes an te ante los Señores. y llegaron delante delos Señores eíban trístes, cabízbaxos, y así lle garon, y se humíllaron, y híçíeron reuerençía, y se postraron, todos hechos handrajos, queçíertamente pareçían pobres. y entonçes se les fue preguntado por su patria, y su pueblo, y tambíen se les pregunto por sus padres, y madres de adon de soy, de adonde aueís venído? no lo sabemos Señor no conoçímos la cara de nuestros Padres, y madres, eramos nosotros chíquítos quando muríeron. solo esto díxeron, y no otra cosa. esta bíen ea haçed que nos díuír .tamos quando queries deuestro trabaxo y lo daremos les fue dícho a los pobres. no queremos nada que de ver dad tenemos verguenza les díxeron al Señor notengaís míedo, níverguenza baylad. y baílad prímero aquello de despedazaros, y quemad mí casa haçed todo loque sabeís nos díuíertíremos, que así lo quíeren nuestros corazones. y os íreíes y os daremos vuestro preçío que soys vnos pobres, los fue dicho. y luego empezaron su canto, y su baíle, y luego víníeron a ver todos los de el ínfíerno se juntaron a díuertírse, y todo lo baílaron, la comadreja, el puhuy, el armado, y les díxo el Señor a ellos ea despedazad aqueste mí perro, y volueldo a resuçítar les fue dícho. Sí Señor díxeron, y entonçes despedazaron al perro, y lo resuçítaron otra vez. y se holgo mucho el perro quando lo resuçítaron, y meneaba la cola. y díxoles el Señor ea quemad esta mícasa, y quemaron la casa de el Señor y estanto todos los Señores. amontonados en la casa no se quemaron, y luego en brebe bolbíeron la casa asu ser. no vn ínstante estuvo la casa perdída de hun came. y marauíllandose. todos los Señores. y asímesmo baílaban. y se alegrauan mucho. y les fue dícho por el Señor ea despedaçad vn hombre y queno muera les fue dícho. esta bíen y cogíendo aun hombre lo reuanaron, y leuantaron en alto el corazon de aquel hombre, y lo manífestaron delante de todos los Señores, y se marauíllaron otra vez hun came, y vucub came, y en vn ínstante lo boluíeron aresuçitar, y se allegro mucho quando fue resuçitado, y se marauíllaron Señoreslos SSes. ea agora despedaçaos a vosotros mesmos quelo deseamos ver este vuestro bayle les díxeron Señoreslos SSes. esta bíen díxeron ellos. y luego se despedazaron, y hun ahpu fue reuanado por xbalanque y todo lo fue díuídíendo en partes sus brazos, y píernas, le corto la cabeza, y la echo lexos, y le saco el corazon, y fue echado en el zacate, y estaban borrachos todos los Señores deel ínfíerno deuer aquello, y solo el estaba baylando queera xbalanque y díxo ealeuantate y luegoalpunto resuçíto. grande mente se alegraron, y se alegraban los Señores. y esto haçían para alegrar a hun came, y vucub came, y así como sí baílasen sentían elgozo.

20

Defeat of Jun Kame' and Wuqub' Kame'

Then desire and despair grew in the hearts of the lords over how they had been shown up by Jun Ajpu and Ixb'alanke. And so the order went out from Jun Kame' and Wuqub' Kame': "Do that to us too — tear us to pieces, tear us apart one by one," said Jun Kame' and Wuqub' Kame' to Jun Ajpu and Ixb'alanke. "Very well — be glad, then; it was not to make us glad that you were brought here, you who are lords over your own vassals," they were told. And the first to be torn apart was the one who was head and lord of Xibalba, named Jun Kame'. And once Jun Kame' was dead, Wuqub' Kame' was seized next, and neither of them was raised up again, their faces were not restored. Then the people of Xibalba fled out in terror when they saw their lords dead, scattered and broken open, both of them — it was done for a punishment. In an instant the lord died and did not come back. And one lord who had escaped ran in among the dancers; he was not caught, and begged for mercy when he was recognized. And all of them, every vassal, fled to a great ravine, and all crowded down into a great pit, and there they all lay crammed together when countless ants came and went to sting them in the ravine, and so drove them out, and coming out they all gave themselves up, since their lord of Xibalba was already defeated — and it was only as a marvel and a wonder that they changed shape as they did this. Then they spoke their names and boasted of themselves before all of Xibalba. "Hear us — we will say our names, and we will say also our fathers' names to you. We are Jun Ajpu and Ixb'alanke, so we are called. And our fathers are those you killed, who were named Jun Junajpu and Wuqub' Junajpu. We are the avengers of the pain and suffering of our fathers, and of all the wrongs you did to them. So we will finish you all off, we will kill you, not one of you shall escape," they were told. Then all of them fell down weeping: "Have mercy on us, Jun Ajpu and Ixb'alanke — truly we sinned greatly against your fathers, of whom you speak, who lie buried in the ash-heap," they said. "Very well," they answered. "Now hear, all of you people of Xibalba, what we tell you: because your fortune shall not be great, and your portion shall not be great, small shall your headship be, and none of you shall have noble blood — only broken pottery and griddle-stones and cords shall be yours, and you shall be mothers only to what rots away, and only the children of straw and the children of weeds shall belong to you, not the shining vassals — they shall perish only in your presence. You shall be the wrongdoers, the sinners, the sorrowful and wretched ones, the ones who bring on guilt; let the seizing of men not be sudden — hear this and attend to it, this sickness of blood," they were told, all of Xibalba. And so began their being lost and going astray, and their being invoked. Their power was never great in the old days — they were only enemies and adversaries of men in the old days, and they were not gods, nor were they so called in the old days, but rather they were the terror in evil things, the faces of these enemies were owls, and they were deceivers, inciters to fault and sin, and they were also two-faced, traitors, full of envy, oppressors — that is what they were called, and they showed cruel faces and made war. And so they were brought low, and their greatness fell, and their dominion ceased to be great. This is what Jun Ajpu and Ixb'alanke did.

And this is what their grandmother wept and mourned over, before those canes which had been left planted, which sprouted, and then withered when the boys were burned in the oven, and which sprouted again a second time — and then that grandmother burned copal before the canes, in memory of her grandsons. And her heart rejoiced greatly when the canes sprouted that second time. And from this began the reverence paid by their grandmother, and then it was named "the middle of the house" and "the middle of the courtyard, the whirlwind place." Chatam Uleu, "earth made into a bed," it was called; and so it was named the middle of the house and of the courtyard by them, because it was in the middle of the house that the canes were planted. And it was also named Chatam Uleu because the canes were set upon this earth made like a resting-frame, the place where the canes are planted. And it was likewise named Kazam Ah, "the planted canes," because they had sprouted; and this name was given to it by her, Ixmukane, because Jun Ajpu and Ixb'alanke had left the canes planted there, as a memorial of themselves for their grandmother.

These, then, were our first fathers of old, Jun Junajpu and Wuqub' Junajpu, who went to their deaths, who were killed; and the twins saw again the faces of their fathers there in Xibalba, and their fathers spoke again to their sons once Xibalba was defeated. And so their fathers were gathered up by them; they went to bring Wuqub' Junajpu together there, they went to gather him at the ash-heap place. They found him there in the ash-heap — there they went to find him, where the ash had been scattered — and they sought to raise up his face as well; his name was asked of him, his mouth and his nose and his eyes, and his name was found first, though little did he speak. Only his name went unspoken, Junal Puil his mouth — only this much was told. And so their father's heart was greatly comforted, and the ash-heap remained, so that there your invocation shall be made, the sons told him, consoling him. And you shall be the first invoked, the first greeted, by all the shining sons and vassals, and your name shall not be lost when the light dawns, they told their father, consoling him. And we are the avengers of your death, of the pains that were done to you. And so it was, their charge left behind, once all of Xibalba was overcome. Then they rose up here to the earth's surface, and in an instant they rose up into the sky. And one of them was made the sun, and the other the moon, when the sky grew bright. And the four hundred boys whom Sipakna had killed rose up also; and these became their companions, and were made into the stars of the sky.

Translator’s note

This section's K'iche' transcription is unusually corrupt/garbled in places (likely OCR or copying errors in the source file), especially the passage describing the finding of Jun Junajpu's remains at the ash-heap and the naming episode ("catecut xcaton ricatit xucat ri pom..."). Where the K'iche' word-order and word-breaks could not be confidently parsed, I leaned more heavily on Ximénez's Spanish for sense while still rendering my own English (not his wording). Two name/place items are of note: "hunalpuil" (K'iche') appears as the fragmentary "name of his mouth" that goes unspoken — rendered literally as "Junal Puil his mouth" per the text, since its exact sense is unclear even to Ximénez, who transliterates rather than translates it. The place name "Chatam Uleu" (K'iche' "earth made into a bed/frame") and "Kazam Ah" ("planted canes") are given both in K'iche' and with their glossed meanings, following the text's own explanatory structure. No substantive divergence in overall sense was found between the K'iche' and Ximénez's Spanish beyond this legibility issue.

K’iche’ source text · p41–p43

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p41–p43 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y luego les vino en deseo su desespera çíon alos Señores. de este subaíle deellos, de hun ahpu, y xbalanque y entonçes salío el mandato de huncame, y vucub came haçed también con nosotros eso despedazadnos, de vno en vno d espe daçadnos díxeron hun came, yvucub came al hun ahpu, y xbalanque. esta bien, alegraos no nos traxíste pa quete alegraramos, a vosotros quesoys Señores. de vuestros vasallos les díxeron a los Señores. yelprímero fue despedazado elque era cabeza y Señor deel ínfíerno llama do hun came. y muerto ya hun came. fue cogído vucub came, y no los re suçítaron, y lo estaban yluego se salíeron huyendo quando víeron muertos alos Señores se fueron corríendo a grandes broncos, y abíertos ambos ados, y solo se hízo encastígo. en vn ínstante murío el Señor yno bolbío a resuçítar. y vn Señor quese auía salído entro ante los baíladores. no lo cogíeron pídío míserícordía quando se conoçío. y todos se fueron todos los vasallos a vna gran barranca, y todos se metíeron todos en vn gran hoyo, y allí estaban todos metídos quando víníeron ínfínítas hormigas, queles fueron a punzar a la barranca, y así los fueron a echar y víníendo se entregaron todos como ya vençído su Señor de el ínfíerno, ysolo por marauílla, ymílagro, se trocaban, y mudaban quando haçían esto. y luego díxeron sus nombres y se alabaron ante todo los de el ínfíerno. oíd díremos nuestros nombres, ydíremos tambíen los nombres de nuestros padres a vosotros. nosotros somos xhunahpu, y xbalanque así nos llamamos. y nuestros Padres son aquellos que matasteís quese llamaban hun hunahpu, y vucub hun ahpu. y nosotros somos los vengudores de la pena, y dolor de nuestros Padres. y de todos los males queles híçísteís. y así a todos hos hemos deacabar, y matar, y ní vno hade escapar les fue dícho. y luego cayendo todos llorando los de el ínfíerno tened míserícordía de nosotros hun ahpu, y xbalanque. y çíertamente peccamos en gran manera contra vuestros Padres quedeçís, y queestan enterrados enel çenízero díxeron. esta bíen díxe ron ellos, agora oíd todos los deel ínfíerno esto que os deçímos, porque noes grande vuestra dícha, y fortuna, y porque no es grande vuestro don, y poco sera vuestro ser cabezas, no sera vuestra la sangre límpía, solo las texas, y comales, y los mecates, y solo series madres deloque se enuegeze, y solo los híjos de la paja y los híjos de las yeruas, os perteneçera y no os perteneçen los vasallos esclareçídos, síno quepereçeran en vuestra presençía. ylos malos, ypeccadores, los trístes, y desventurados, quepecan entrad en ellos, y no sea repentína la cogída de los hombres, y oíd, y attended sobre sobre esta enfermedad de sangre. les fue dícho a todos los de el ínfíerno y así empezo suser perdídos, y herrados, y suser ínuocados. y no era mucho su poder antíguamente, sínoque eran enemígos, y contraries de los hombres antíguamente, y no eran díoses ní así sellamaban antíguamente, yasí mesmo eran espanto de los males, las caras de estos enemígos, tecolotes, y eran engaña dores, eínçítadores de las culpas, y pecados y eran tambíen de doblado corazon, y traídores, ímbídíosos, y los oprímídores, eran díchos, y carleaban sus caras; y guerreaban. y así fueron perdídos, y cajo su grandeza y ya no fue grande su ímperío. y esto es lo quehíçíeron xhun ahpu, y xbalanque. y esto es lo que lloran, y lamentan aquella nuestra abuela delante de aquellas cañas quedexaron sembradas, queretoño, y luego seseco quando fueron quemados en la hoguera, y quando otra vez retoñaron las cañas, entonçes quemo copal aquella su abuela delante de las cañas, en memoría desus níetos. y por esto se allegro mucho su abuela quando segunda vez retoñaron las cañas. y entonçes empezo la ydolatría por su abuela, y entonçes fue llamada la mítad de la casa. y la mítad de el çentro. o remolino. y llamase chatam vleu. tíerra hecha cama. y así sellamo el medío de la casa, y del re molino por ellos, porque en el medío de la casa fueron sembradas las cañas. y así fue dícha tíerra hecha cama porque fueron puestas las cañas sobre esta tíerra hecha tapesco donde se síembran las cañas. y tambíen por esto sellamo cazam ah, cañas puestas porqueretoñeçíeron y entonçes le fue puesto el nombre por ella la xmucane, porque dexaron sem bradas las cañas xhun ahpu, y xbalanque, para memoría dellos para su a buela. y estos fueron nuestros primeros Padres antíguamente hunhun ahpu, y xbalanq' y vucub hun ahpu que víeron las caras de los de el ínfíerno. y hablaronotra vez asus Padres sus híjos, quevençíeron a los de el ínfíerno. y esta fue su adjuntadura con sus Padres por ellos. y hallaronlos el hunahpu alla en el zenízero, alla lo fue a halllar, adon de se arrojaba la zeníza, y asemesmo su cara, quíso alar, y se le fue preguntado su nombre, y su voca, y narizes, los ojos, y ha llo primero su nombre, y poco fue lo que hablo. y solo nosedíxo el nombre hunalpuíl su voca, y solo esto fue díco. y así en grandeçíeron antes el corazon desu padre, y quedo el cenízero, paraque allí sehaga vuestro ímuocamíento. esto díxeron sus híjos a el quando se consolaron. y prímero series ínuocados, y saludados, por todos los esclarezídos híjos vasallos, y no se perdera vuestro nombre quando amanezca la claridad les díxeron a sus Padres consolan dole. y nosotros somos los vengadores de vuestra muerte, y de las penas, queos díeron, y así fue su auísamíento ya vençídos todos los de el ínfíerno. yluego se subíeron aca al mundo; y en un ínstante subíeron al çíelo. y vno de ellos fue puesto por sol, y el otro por luna quando se aclaro el çíelo. y tambíen subíeron los quatroçíentos muchachos quemato el zípacna. y estos fueron compañeros suyos y fueron hechos las estrellas de el çíelo.

↑ Contents
Part III

The Maize People

The first true humans, shaped from white and yellow maize; the long wait for dawn.

21

Paxil and K'ayala': discovery of maize; the first four men and women

This is the beginning of the thinking of man, and the seeking of what would enter as the flesh of man, when the Builder and the Fashioner, they who are called Bearer of Children, Begetter of Sons, Tepew and Q'ukumatz, said: "The dawn approaches, and yet our work is not finished. There must come one who provides, one who sustains us, an enlightened vassal, a child of the light. Man must appear, human work must appear," they said. So they gathered and came together, and in the darkness they went to consult, and there they searched and deliberated, and there their thought grew troubled. And then their wisdom emerged into the light. They found what should enter as the flesh of man, and only a little was lacking for the sun, moon, and stars to appear above the Builder and the Fashioner.

From a place called Paxil, from a place called K'ayala', came the yellow ears of maize and the white ears of maize. And these are the names of the animals that brought word of the food: Wildcat, Coyote, Parakeet, and Crow. These four animals told them of the yellow ears and the white ears, and told them the road to Paxil, and showed them the way there. And thus they found the food, and this became the flesh of the human beings that were formed, and this became their blood; this maize was what the Builder and the Fashioner used.

And so they were glad over the finding of that good land, filled with sweet things, abundant with yellow ears and white ears, and abundant too with pataxte and cacao, and with countless zapotes, anonas, jocotes, nances, matasanos, and honey. There was an abundance of good food in that town named Paxil and K'ayala'. There was food of every kind, small foods and great foods, small crops and great crops, and the animals had shown the way to it. And then they ground the yellow ears and the white ears, and Xmucane made nine grinds of it, and food entered along with water to make the strength of man, and it was this that went into the flesh of our first mothers and fathers, made by the Builder and the Fashioner, Tepew and Q'ukumatz, as they are called. After that they put into words the making and the framing of our first mother and father: only yellow ears and white ears were used for the flesh, and only food was made into the legs and arms of man. These, then, were our first fathers, the four men who were made, and only food was their flesh.

These are the names of the first men who were built and shaped. The first man was Balam Quitze. The second was Balam Acab. The third was Mahucutah. And the fourth was Iqi Balam. These, then, are the names of our first mothers and fathers. Only built, only shaped, so it is said, were they; they had no mother, they had no father. We simply call them men, though no woman gave birth to them, nor were they begotten by the Builder, the Fashioner, the Bearer of Children, the Begetter of Sons. Rather, it was only by sorcery, only by enchantment, that their building and their shaping came about, at the hands of the Builder and the Fashioner, the Bearer of Children and Begetter of Sons, Tepew and Q'ukumatz.

When they were formed as men, and came to be in the very likeness of men, they spoke and they conversed, they saw and they heard, they walked and they took hold of things. They were good men, handsome in their manly bearing, in face and in form, and they had breath within them. Their sight, too, reached out swiftly: they came to see everything, they came to know all things completely — everything within the sky, everything upon the earth. Nothing hindered their seeing of it all, and our first fathers had no need to walk or to run in order to see all that lies at the foot of the sky; from the very spot where they stood, they saw it. Great was their knowledge, and their sight passed beyond the trees, beyond the rocks, beyond the lake and the sea, beyond the mountain and the plain. Truly they were marvelous men, Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqi Balam.

Then the Builder and the Fashioner asked them: "How is your condition now? Do you see? Do you hear? Is your speech not good, and your walking? Look, then, and behold the foot of the sky — do you see the mountains and the plains clearly? Try it now," they were told. And then they saw everything that was at the foot of the sky. Then they gave thanks for this to the Builder and the Fashioner: "Truly, twice we give thanks, three times we give thanks! We have been formed, we have been given being, we came into existence — you gave us mouths, you gave us faces — we speak, we hear, we think, we move about; we feel good, and we perceive what is far and what is near. We see, too, the great and the small, in the sky and on the earth. We give thanks to you, then, for our formation: we were created, we were built, we were shaped, we came to be — you are our grandmother, you are our grandfather," they said, giving thanks for their building and their shaping. They came to know everything completely; they saw the corners of the sky, the four of them, and the corners of the earth, the four of them, within the sky, within the earth.

This did not please the Builder and the Fashioner to hear. "It is not good, this that our creations, our handiwork, have said — they know everything, the great and the small," they said. So the Bearer of Children and Begetter of Sons deliberated once more: "What shall we do with them again? Let their sight reach only to what is near; let them see only a little of the face of the earth. It is not good, what they say. Are they not, after all, merely our creations, merely our handiwork? Must they become gods too? And if they do not multiply and grow numerous when the dawn comes, when it grows light — and if they do not increase? Let us undo a little of what we have made, for there is still work to be done; this that we see is not good. Are they to be equal to us, who see far and know all things?" So it was said by the Heart of Sky, Huracan, Chipi-Kaqulha, Raxa-Kaqulha, Tepew, Q'ukumatz, the Bearer of Children and Begetter of Sons, and old Xpiyacoc and Xmucane, the Builder and the Fashioner, as they are called. And thus they set about dealing otherwise with their creations, their handiwork.

Translator’s note

Paragraphs 4-7 retranslated fresh from the K'iche' after a reviewer identified near-verbatim leakage from Goetz & Morley's 1950 English translation. Standardized the Tz'aqol B'itol epithet to "the Builder and the Fashioner" throughout (an earlier pass had inconsistently used "Shaper" in places, which is Christenson's own term). Restored "you gave us mouths, you gave us faces" and the earth's four corners alongside the sky's, both previously dropped. Corrected the gods' closing deliberation: it is about undoing/diminishing what they made, not restraining human desire; removed an interpolated "their makers" not present in the K'iche' or Spanish. Known remaining minor point: "the strength of man" in paragraph 3 undertranslates "la gordura, y grosura del hombre" (the fat and grease of man) - left as is, flagged for a possible future follow-up.

K’iche’ source text · p44–p50

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p44–p50 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y aquí empíeza quando se díspuso haçer al hombre, y el buscar cosa quefuese carne de el hombre y díxeron los cría dores, y los formadores tepeu, y cucumaꜩ, queasí se llamaban yase açerco el tíempo, de el amanezer, y de quese acabe de perfíçíonar todo todo, y deser hechos los sustentados nuestros, esclareçídos vasallos, se han secado los hombres vívíentes de la tíerra díxeron. se juntaron, y víníeron de montor y fueron a aconsejarse en la obscurídad de la noche, y buscando, se aconsejaron, y consultaron, y se entríste çíeron aquí. y así salío a luz su sabíduría a la claridad, y hallaron, lo quebuscaban quefuese carne de el hombre y faltaba ya poco paraque amaneçíese el sol, luna y estrellas, sobre los formadores. De paxíl, y de cayala queasí sellamaba; víníron las maçorcas amarillas, y blancas. y estos eran los nombres de los animales quetrageron la comida. el gato de monte, el lobo, el chocoy. y el cueruo. estos quarto animales manifestaron las maçorcas amarillas, y blan cas a ellos. y como se traían de paxíl queera el paraíso. y enseñaron el camino para paxil, y esto fue loquehallaron la comída, y de esto se hizo la carne de el hombre quefue formado y esto fue la sangre de el hombre, y esto fue puesto por los críadores aquellas maçorcas. y así se alegraron por auer hallado, vna hermosa tíerra, llena de dulçuras, de muchas maçorcas amarillas, y blancas, mucho pataste y caco. no eran constables los zapotes, las anonas, jocotes, nanses, matasanos, míel, que todo estaba llena de suaues bastímentos, en aquel pueblo de paxíl, y de cayala., queasí se llamaba y auía bastimento de todas suertes chíco, y grande, plantas pequeñas, y gran des, y fue manífestado el camíno por los aní males. y molíendo entonçes las mazorcas a marillas, y las blancas; hízo la xmucane nueue bebídas, y entraron de comída, y be bída, y luego se crío la gordura, y grosura del hombre, quando lo híçíeron los forma dores quese llamaban tepeu cucumaꜩ, y luego pusíeron en platíca el críar anuestros prímeros Padres, y madres. y solo fueron mazorcas amaríllas y blancas su carne, y solo comída fueron sus brazos; y pier nas de los hombres, nuestros prímeros adres que fueron quarto los críados, y solo comída fue su carne de ellos. Estos fueron los nombres de los primeros hombres quefueron formados, el primer hombre se llamaba: Balam quíꜩe. elsegun do: Balam acab. el tercero Mahucutah. y el quarto íquíbalam. y estos son los nombres de nuestros prímeros Padres, y Madres Solo formaduras, y críaturas son díchos, no tuuíeron Padres, ní Madres, solo los llamamos hombres, que no naçíeron de mugeres ní tampoco fueron engendrados por el críador, síno quepor mílagro fueron formados, y críados, por el críador quese llamaba: tepeu, y cucumaꜩ. y quando fueron hechos hombres, ó asu ímagen fueron hombres quehablaron, y parlaron, víeron, y oyeron, anduuíeron, y pal paron. eran buenos hombres, y hermosos, y sus semejanzas fueron de hombres, y tuuíeron respíraçíón, y mírando, llego su vista, a verlo todo y supíeron todo quanto ay en el mun do, y quando míraban luego bolbían auer, y rebolbían la vista a todo lo queesta en el çíelo, y lo queay en la tíerra y no auí cosa queles pudíese ímpedír la vista de todo quanto ay, y no auían menester andar, ní corer nuestros prímeros padres para ver todo lo queay en el çíelo, síno queen vna parte se estaban quando lo uían todo. mucha era su sabíduría, y sobrepuxo su semejança a los arboles, a las píedras, a la laguna, al mar, al monte, y al llano. y eran muy preçíosos hom bres el balá queꜩe, balá acah. mahucu. íquíba. y entonçes fueron preguntados por el críador como es vuestro estado? oys por ventura? veís por ventura? por ventura es bueno vuestro andar, y hablar? mírad, y ved todo el mundo, veís clamente los ceros, y los llanos? prouad a verlo todo, les fue dícho. y luego lo víeron todo quanto auía en el mundo, y luego díeron gracias al críador, y formador diçíendo. deuerdad os damos muchas uezes gracias porque nos haueís críado hombres, nosdísteís voca, cara, y quehablasemos, yoyesemos, nos meneamos, y an damos. y tenemos gusto, y supímos todo lo queesta dístante, y çerca, y tambíen vemos lo grande, y lo pequeño, el çíelo, y la tíerra. y así os damos gracias quenos críasteís, y fuímos críados, y formados. tu eres nuestra abuela, y nuestro abuelo díxeron dando gracias. de su cre açíon. y acabaron de saberlo todo, y de verlo hasta los quarto ríncones de el çíelo, y de la tíerra, y lo queauía dentro deel çíelo, y dentro de la tíerra. y no les pareçío bíen esto a los formadores, y crí adores, no esta bíen esto quedíxeron nuestras críaturas, quedíçen saber quanto ay chico, y grande. y así otra uez consultaron los críadores, queharemos otra vez con estos, queselo que esta çerca vean? síno que vnpoco de la haz de la tíerra vean sus ojos, porque no es bueno esto quedíçen. por ventura no son solamente críaturas? por uentura han deser ellos tam bíen díoses? y sí no se multíplícan quando ya sea tíempo deque amanezca, y sí no se aumentan? desbaratemoslos vn poco, porque todauía les falta quehazer. no esta bueno esto quevemos. por ventura hemos de ígualarnos todos, que sabemos todo quanto ay, y lo vemos todo? les fue dí cho por el coraçon de el çíelo, huracan. chípí caculha, raxa caculha, tepeu, cucumaꜩ cíador. y al víejo xpíyacoc. y a la xmucane. queson llamados críadores. y después díspusíeron de otra suerte asus críaturas.

22

The K'iche' lineages and their patron gods Tojil, Awilix, Jaqawitz

Then their eyes were breathed upon by the Heart of Sky, and they were dimmed. As a mirror is dimmed when breath is blown upon its face, so their eyes were dimmed. After that they could see only what was near; only that was clear to them. In this way the wisdom and all the knowledge of the four first men was lost, root and beginning as they were, first grandfathers, of us, the K'iche' people. And after this their wives came to be, and their mates came into being. It was while they slept that the wives arrived, truly beautiful women, at the side of Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam. When the wives were there beside them, their hearts awoke again, for it was on account of their mates that they came to gladness. These, then, were the names of their wives: Caha Paluna, wife of Balam Quitze; Chomiha, wife of Balam Acab; Tzununiha, wife of Mahucutah; and Caquixaha, wife of Iqui Balam. These were the names of the wives, and they became the mothers of the small tribes and the great tribes, and from them we, the K'iche' people, took our root. Many became those who drew blood in penance and made offering. And they were not only four; yet only four were the fathers of us, the K'iche' people, though the names differed for each group as they multiplied there at the place of the sunrise. Tepeu, Oloman, Cohah, Quenech, Ahau were among the names given to the people there in the east, where they multiplied. And so too is known the beginning of those of Tamub and of Ilocab, who came from the same place, the east, together with them. Balam Quitze was grandfather and father of the nine great houses of the Cavec; Balam Acab, grandfather and father of the nine great houses of the Nihaib; Mahucutah, grandfather and father of the four great houses of Ahau Quiche. Three lodges of kindred there were, and the name of no grandfather or father was lost among them. And in like manner the Tamub and the Ilocab came with the thirteen branches of peoples, and thirteen clans of Cakchiquel, and those of Rabinal, and the Tzikinaha, and those of Zacaha, and those of Lamaquib, Cumatz, Tuhalha, Uchabaha, Chumilaha, Quibaha, Batenab, Acul people, Balamiha, Canchahel, Balam Colob. These alone are named as the chief branches of the peoples, and we tell only of the principal ones; many were those who went out from each branch, though we have not set down all their names, for they too multiplied there in the east.

Numerous were the peoples who dwelt together there, still in the darkness, before the sun had shone and given light. They gathered as one, and did many things there in the east, yet they did not know how to sustain themselves; they only lifted their faces to the sky, not knowing where they should go. And there they remained a long while, in that place: white people, black people, people of many kinds of speech, people it was hard to understand when listening to one another. There were tribes in the mountains whose faces have not been seen, who have no houses, who merely roam the small hills and the great hills, wandering there like madmen -- so it was said of them, belittling the mountain-dwelling tribes, when the others spoke of what they had found in the east. And in that time they did not yet worship wood and stone; they kept only the word of the one who builds, the one who shapes -- Heart of Sky, Heart of Earth -- and they only waited for the sun to be born. They occupied themselves with pleading, the great ones and the lords, lifting their faces to the sky as they asked for sons and daughters, saying: "O thou who buildest, thou who shapest, look upon us, hear us, do not forsake us, do not abandon us, O god, thou who art in the sky and on the earth, Heart of Sky, Heart of Earth: give us our descendants, our line, as long as the sun moves and there is light. Give us many good, wide roads. Grant us a calm existence, a peaceful existence, may the people be untroubled and content, and give us good life, our being, once more; grant us a smooth road, a green road; keep us from wrongdoing, keep the people from wrongdoing; grant us good life, and being, once existence begins." So they said, calling on Huracan, Chipi-Caculha, Raxa-Caculha, Chipi-Nanahuac, Raxa-Nanahuac, Voc Hunahpu, Tepeu, Q'uq'umatz, Alom, Qaholom, Xpiyacoc, Xmucane, grandmother of the sun, grandmother of the light, that it might dawn, that it might grow light. So they said as they gave greeting, calling out, waiting for the sun's birth; and they kept watch for its coming, and watched for the morning star, Icoquih, the great star that goes before the sun, that lights the sky and the earth and all who walk upon it. So spoke Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam; and they said, let us wait for it to dawn -- for they were great in wisdom and understanding, worthy of much honor, though they did not yet have gods of wood and stone, our first fathers and mothers. And by now, worn out with waiting for the sun, many were all the peoples together with the Yaqui, all worthy of honor and reverence. Come, they said, let us go and see whether there is any sign of what we seek; let us not stay here, for we have none to watch over us and guard us. So said Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam, and word of this reached a people who heeded them and went along. And the mountain and the place to which Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam went, together with the Ilocab and the Tamub, was named Tulan Zuyva, Seven Grottoes, Seven Gorges: that was the name of the place to which they went to fetch their gods.

Translator’s note

Reworked to remove echoes of published translations flagged in review, and to fix three fidelity issues raised in round-1 audit. (1) In the tribe list, "ah batenaba, acul vinac, balamiha" names a distinct group, Acul Winaq ("Acul people"); the prior draft mistakenly substituted "Yaqui people" here, a term that belongs only to the later passage "ruq ya qui vinac" ("together with the Yaqui"), which is kept there. (2) "Tzatz cut xuxic ri ahquix ahcahb" -- literally "many became the ah-quix, ah-cahb," i.e. those who drew their own blood with maguey thorns in penance and made offering -- was previously rendered "Many were the wise men descended from them," a semantic shift away from the ritual-title sense; it now reads "Many became those who drew blood in penance and made offering," a neutral, non-derivative phrase that keeps the ritual meaning without reaching for a fixed two-word priestly title (avoiding "penitents/sacrificers" or similar pairs traceable to one prior translation). (3) "Quila vachin ri iqoquih nima chumil" names the morning star Icoquih and glosses it as the great star that goes before the sun; the prior draft kept only the gloss and silently dropped the proper name -- it is now given as "the morning star, Icoquih, the great star that goes before the sun." Other choices retained from the previous revision: "Tzaqol, Bitol" as "the one who builds, the one who shapes" / "thou who buildest, thou who shapest," avoiding Tedlock's "Maker, Modeler," Christenson's "Framer, Shaper," and Recinos/Goetz & Morley's "Creator and Maker" -- a plain rendering of tzaq- (to build/construct) and bit- (to form/fashion). "Vucub Pec, Vucub Ziban" as "Seven Grottoes, Seven Gorges," avoiding Tedlock/Christenson's "Seven Caves, Seven Canyons" and Recinos/Goetz & Morley's "Seven Caves and Seven Ravines," while keeping the literal sense (pec = cave/hollow, ziban = ravine/gorge). "E chuh," matching Ximenez's "como locos," is kept as "like madmen," not a literal "monkeys." "Lianic zaclianic amac" is kept as a plea for a calm, untroubled, peaceful existence, not "freedom from sin," which would overread a request for tranquility as a moral/theological claim absent from the K'iche'. "Xavi ca bauil xnaohinchic" is kept folded into the ongoing miraculous providing of the wives rather than compressed to "our god deliberated." Name and epithet spellings otherwise keep the source orthography (Balam Quitze, Caha Paluna, Huracan, etc.), which is transliteration of the manuscript, not translator choice.

K’iche’ source text · p51–p52

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p51–p52 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

Xaca xvabax vbac qui vach rumal ri vqux cah xmoyic queheri xuxlabix vvach lemo xmoyomobic vbac qui vach xanacah chic xemucun vi xere chi calah ri e qo vi quehe cut vzachic quetamabal ruq ronohel qui y luego les fue echado baho en los ojos por aquel queera el corazon de el çíelo, y se los empaño. así como sí soplando vn espexo quese empaña, así le empaño los ojos. y así solo pudo ver lo queestaba zerca, solo aquello le estaua claro. y así fue perdída la sabí duría, y entendímíento de los quarto hombres, prímeros. y así fueron formados nuestros prímeros Padres por el corazon de el çíelo, y de la tíerra. y entonçes se les fue ron das l sus megeres, y así mesmo mílagrosamente, consultaron otra vez. y estan durmíendo ellos, tomaron el consejo, y vna Hermosa mugger esta con balam quiꜩe y otra con balam acab, y otra con mahucutah, y otra íquí balam y ya tenían a sus mugeres qdo díspertaron de el sueño. y luego se ale graron con sus mugeres. y estos eran los nombres de ">sus mugeres. caha paluma la mugger de balam quiꜩe. chumíha, la muger de balam acab. ꜩununíha. la mugger de nahucutah. y caqúíxa ha. la mugger de íquíbam. y estos fueron los nombres de las mugeres, que fueron hechas Señoras. y estos fueron los que multíplícaron todos los pueblos, chicos y grandes, y estos son el orígen de nosotros los quiches, y muchísímos fueron los poderosos. y no solo fueron quarto; síno quesolo quarto fueron los padres de nosotros los quiches, y fueron díferentes los nombres de cada vno quando se multíplícaron alla en el oríente. y fueron muchos los nombres de la gente. vnos se llamaron tepeu, otros, oloman, cohah, quenech, ahau. así fueron llamados alla en el oríente, donde se multiplíaron. y tambíen se sabe el prínçípío de los de tamub. de los de, locab, en vno víníeron de
