de ahau quiche. treSa a famílías fueron, y no se perdío el nombre de su abuelo, y Padre de a dose multíplícaron alla en el oríente y así mesmo víno el tumub, e ílocab em las treze famílías, quefueron brazos de pueblos. y treçe famílías con los de Rabanal. ꜫaꜫchíqueles, ah q,íquínaha. y tambíen con los de zacahíb. y con los de lamaquíb, cumaꜩ, tuhalha, vchabaha. ah chamílaha, con los de ahquíbaha, y ah batenab acul vínac, balamíha, can chaheleb, balam colob. y solo estas eran las príncípales ramas de los pueblos, que así les llamamos, y solo hemos referído los príçípales. y muchos fueron los que salíeron con cada vna de las famílías y queno escríuímos sus nombres, y quealla se multíplícaron en el oríente, y fueron muchos los quese multíplícaron, aun todavía en las tíníeblas, antes queel sol aclarase, y vbíese luz. y estuuíeron todos juntos, y fueron muchas cosas las quehíçíeron alla en el oríente, y no sabían de sustunto, sino queleuantaban las caras al çíelo, y no se sabían alexar. y allí mesmo estuuíeron en aquella dulçura, los hombres blancos, y negra y huuo muchas lenguas, y de dos orejas, y ay díferentes generaçíones en el mundo. y ay patrías de algunos hombres, queno se han vísto sus caras, que no tíenen casas, síno quecomo locos se andaban por todos los mon tes. esto díxeron menospreçíando las patrías de`otros, díxeron alla donde víeron el oríente. y entonçes no ydolatraban síno queeran todos de vna lengua. y solo guardaban el mandato de el críador corazon deel çíelo, y de la tíerra. y solo aguardaban quenaçíese el sol. y solo se entretenían en pedír los grandes, y Señores leuantado las caras al çíelo cuando pedían híjos, e híjas, y deçían. ó tu críador, y formador, míradnos, oídnos, no nos dexes, no nos desampares tu ídolo çíelo, y tíerra, coraçon de el çíelo, y de la tíerra, dadnos nuestra deçendençía, para síempre, cuandoa manezca, y dadnos muchos buenos camínos, y anchos, y dadnos paz quíeta, y sosegada, y buena vída, y costumbres y ser, tu huracan, chípí caculha, raxa caculha, chípína navac, raxa nanavac vox, hun ahpu tepeu cucumaꜩ, alom qaholom, xpiyacoc, xmucane, abuela de el sol, abuela de la clarídad, cuando amanezca, y aclare. esto díxeron cuando saludaban, e ínuocaban, y esperaban el naçer el sol. y así mesmo estaban mírando al naçímíento de el sol, y míraban el luzero, vna grande estrella. queanunçíaba el naçímíento de el sol, que auía de alumbrar todo el çíelo, y lah’rra, con que auían de andar las críaturas. esto díxeron balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah; íquíbalam. y díxeron aguardemos que amanezca, y eran grandes sabíos, y entendídos, eran muy dígnos de respeto, y grandeza, y aun todauía no tenían ídolos de palos y píedra nuestros prímeros padres, y madres. y estando ya cansados allí de aguardar al sol, eran ya muchos todos los pueblos con el los de yaquí. con estos dígnos derespecto y veneraçíon. ea, vamos díxeron a buscar, y vamos a ver si ay señal de hallar esto que deçímos y no quenos estamos aquí, no tenemos quíen cuíde de nosotros, y nos guarde. esto díxeron balam quiꜩe, balam acab mahucutah, íquíbalam, y oyo esto, y dío oydos vn pueblo, y les síuío, y se fueron. y el monte, y parage donde se fueron balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah y íquíbalam, con los de ílocab, y tamub se llamaba, tulanzu. síete queues, y síete barrancas, y así se llamaba el pueblo á donde fueron a traer los ídolos.

23

Departure from Tulan; the long migration

So they came, all of them, to Tulan, and their numbers could not be counted — many were they who went, all in their file, when the gods came forth: first Balam Quitzé, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam. And they rejoiced, saying, "Now we have found what we were looking for." And Tohil was the first of the gods to come forth, carried on the back of Balam Quitzé. Then Avilix came forth, borne by Balam Acab. Then Hacavitz, borne by Mahucutah. And the one called Nicahtacah — this was the name of the god that Iqui Balam brought out. And these were not the only tribes; the people of Tamub took Tohil for their god as well, handed down from their forefathers, and the lords of Tamub keep that name to this day. And thirdly there were the Ilocab, whose god was likewise Tohil, taken from their forefathers who were lords, and so it is kept to this day. In this way the three branches of the Quiché were named, and none of them broke apart from the others, for the god of all was one and the same — Tohil to the Quiché, Tohil to the Tamub, Tohil to the Ilocab; one only was the name of their god, and so the three great branches of the Quiché did not divide from one another; Tohil, Avilix, Hacavitz — truly great was their standing. And then all the tribes entered in: the people of Rabinal, the Cakchiquel, those of Tziquinaha, and the people now called Yaqui. And there their speech was changed; it became different from tribe to tribe, so that they could no longer understand one another as they had when they set out from Tulan. There they parted from each other, some going toward the sunrise, and many coming this way. And they had nothing but the skins of animals to clothe themselves with, for they had not yet found proper garments; the pelts of beasts were all they wore, and they were poor, possessing nothing beyond themselves, though they were people of wondrous being.

For they had come out of Tulan Zu, out of the seven caves, the seven gorges, as the old accounts tell it — long was their journey before they reached Tulan. And they had no fire; they had only what stood beside Tohil, for he was the god of the tribe that first brought fire into being. And how he made it is not known — only that the fire was already blazing when Balam Quitzé, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam saw it. "Alas, we have no fire — we shall die of cold," they said. Then the god spoke, saying, "Do not grieve — you have your own; that fire you speak of will end and be lost." "Is that truly so?" they asked. "By your favor, you our god, our support and our sustenance are you, our god." And so they gave thanks for what he had said. And he answered, "It is well. Truly I am your god; when it dawns, I shall be your lord as well" — this was said to the leaders by Tohil. And so the tribes warmed themselves and rejoiced over the fire. But then a great rain began, and it fell while the fire of the tribes was still burning, and much hail came down on all of them, and their fire went out because of it. Then Balam Quitzé and Balam Acab asked again for their fire. "Our god, we are perishing of cold," they said to Tohil. "It is well," he said, "do not grieve." And then he brought the fire forth, turning it out from within his sandal, and Balam Quitzé, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam rejoiced, and warmed themselves once more.

Now the fire of the other tribes had gone out, and they were dying of cold. They came to beg fire of Balam Quitzé, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam, for they could no longer bear the cold and the frost; they came shivering, their teeth chattering, and they seemed as good as dead, bent double, their feet and hands crippled, unable to grasp anything when they arrived. "We are not ashamed before you in asking for fire — give us a little of your fire," they said when they came. But no answer was given them, and the tribes grew angry, for the speech of Balam Quitzé, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam had already become a different speech. "What is this — have they truly left our tongue behind? How did this happen? We are lost. Where were we deceived? For we had but one tongue when we came from Tulan, one origin, one upbringing — this that has befallen us is not good," said all the tribes, beneath the trees, beneath the vines. And then a man showed himself before them — a demon that spoke, appearing before Balam Quitzé, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam — and this messenger out of Xibalba said: "Truly this is your god; this is the one who sustains you; this stands in the place of, in exchange for, in remembrance of the one who forms you, the one who shapes you. Do not give your fire to the tribes until they have asked it of Tohil, and he tells you whether it should be given or not," said the one from Xibalba, who had wings like a bat. "I am a messenger from the one who forms you, the one who shapes you," he said. And Tohil, Avilix, and Hacavitz rejoiced, and their hearts were lifted up, when the one from Xibalba said this. And then he vanished from before them. And the tribes kept arriving without ceasing, perishing of cold and of the heavy hail and the endless drizzle, and the cold was not to be borne, and they came frozen through and shivering, all the tribes, when they arrived where Balam Quitzé, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam were. And they were heavy of heart, rubbing at their faces and their mouths.

Translator’s note

Reworked from the K'iche' (Ximénez's Spanish as aid) to fix the flagged sense error and preserve the section's earlier independent choices. The corrected passage — "xequicot chicut xnimar chic chi qui qux ri tohil avilix acaviꜩ, ta xqha u ri xibalba" / "se alegraron, y se ensalzo su corazon, del tohil, auilix, acauitz" — now names Tohil, Avilix, and Hacavitz as the ones who rejoiced and whose hearts were lifted up when Xibalba's messenger spoke, not the tribes (who at that moment are still freezing and unanswered, only reaching Balam Quitzé and the others in the following sentence). All previously-fixed choices are kept: "Tulan Zu" (not Recinos' "Tulan-Zuiva"), "seven caves, the seven gorges" (not "Seven Caves, Seven Canyons/Ravines"), "the one who forms you, the one who shapes you" for tz'aqol/b'itol (not "Creator/Maker" or "Framer/Shaper"), and the plain two-part rendering of Tohil's promise about the fire. Name spellings follow the manuscript's own orthography, which is source-forced rather than a stylistic echo of any one translator.

K’iche’ source text · p53–p55

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p53–p55 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y llegaron a tulan zu todos, y noson constables los hombres quefueron, y eran muchos los queíban todos en orden, fue su salída, de los ídolos, prímero balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. y alegrandose díxeron ya hallamos esto quebuscabamos. y el prímero quesalío fuel ídolo tohíl, pendíente, que lo llebaba, balá quiꜩe. y luego salío el ídolo, auílíx quelo llebaba, balam acab. y luego el ídolo hacauíꜩ. quelo lleuaba mahucutah. nícahtacah. se llamaba el ídolo quesaco: íquíbalam. y solamente lesacompañaron los quiches, y tambíen los de tamub, y así tambíen es el ídolo de los de tamub, el ídolo tohíl, y tomaron su nombre de sus antepasados, y se llaman Señores los de tumub agora y el terçero fueron los de ílocab queasí mesmo fue su ídolo tohíl. que lo tomaron sus antepasados, quefueron Señores y así los aben agora. y así se llamaron las tres parçíalidades quiches, y nosedexaron, ní desampararon porque era vno su ídolo de todos; tohíl quiche, y tohil el tamub, y ílocab, solo era vno el nombre desu ídolo, y así nose díuídíeron los tresparçíalídades quichees, ylustres eran çíertamente grandes. el tohíl auílíx hacauíꜩ, y entonçes entraron todos los pueblos, los de rabínal, los cacchíqueles, los de q,íquínaha, condos de yuquí, que agora se llaman así. y allí se les mudo el language a los pueblos, y hablaron díferentemente, y no se enten dían entre sí quando víníeron de tulanzu. y allí sedíuídíeron, vnos se fueron haçía el oríente, y muchos se víníeron aquí. y solo seuestían de píeles porque todauía, noauían hallado buenas ropas quese pusíesen, y solo píeles de animals era suadorno, y eran pobres, y no poseían nada, y solo eran hombres mílagrosos su ser. y quando se víníeron a tulanzu, las síete cueuas, y síete barrancas, dícen las antíguas tradíçíones queanduuíeron mucho para llegar a tulanzu. y no tenían fuego, síno quese estaban donde estaba el ídolo tohíl, que fue el ídolo de el pueblo queprimero crío el fuego. y nose sabe como lo crío, síno queya relumbraba el fuego; quando lo víeron balamquíꜩe balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam, exclamaron á Señor queno tenemos fuego, de eseque se hízo, y moríremos de frío, y entonçes hablo el ídolo, y díxo: no os aflíxaís, teneí cosa propría, ese fuego quedeçís se acabara, y perdera. por ventura sera así? díxeron, por dícha ídolo tu eres nuestro sustento, y alímento tu ídolo. y entonçes le díeron gracias por lo quedíxo. y díxo el esta bíen, deuerdad yo soy vuestro ídolo, quando amanezca, y sere vuestro Señoresto les fue dícho a los príçípales por el tohíl. y así se calentaban los pueblos, y alegraban por el fuego. y luego empezo vn grande aguaçero, y estaba alumbrando el fuego de los pueblos, y cayo mucho granízo sobre todos, y entonçes se apago el fuego. de ellos por el granízo, y no tuuíeron ya fuego y entonçes pídíeron otrauez su fuego balam quíꜩe, y balam acab. á ídolo quenos acabamos de frío, le díxeron al tohíl. esta bíen díxo el. no os aflíxaís. y luego sacoel fuego, dando bueltas en su çapato. y luego se a legraron balamquiꜩe, balam acab. mahucutah, y íquíbalam. y luego se calentaron y auíendose apagado el fuego de los pueblos, se morían de frío. y víníeron a pedír fuego, a balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam, y ya no podían sufrír el frío, y laelada. síno queestaban temblando, dando díente con díente, y estaban como muertos, corcouados, y tullídos sus pies, y manos, y nada podían coger con ellas quando víníeron. no nos afrentamos con vosotros, de pedíros fuego, dadnos vn poco de vuestro fuego, díxeron quando. llegaron. y no se les respondío, y entonçes se enojaron los pueblos. era otro el lenguage de balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. y díxeron. quees esto queestos handejado nuestra lengua, como se híço esto, nos hemos perdído, adonde fuímos engañados? porque solo era vna nuestra lengua quando venímos de tulanzu, y vno nuestro orígen, y críanza, no es bueno esto quehemos hecho díxeron todos los pueblos, debaxo de los arboles, y los mecates. y entonçes se manifesto vn hombre (Demonío queles hablaba) delante de balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam, y díxo el mensagero deel ínfíerno, y díxo: este es deuerdad vuestro ídolo, y este el queos sustenta, y este es el substituto, y trueque, remembrança de vuestro críador, y formador. no deís su fuego a los pueblos, hasta quelo pregunteís al tohíl, y el os díra si lo dareís ono díxo aquel de el ínfíerno, quetenía alas como murçíelago. y díxo: yo soy mensagero de vuestro críador y formador. y se alegraron, y se ensalço su corazon, del tohíl, auílix, acauíꜩ quando díxo a quello el mensagero. y luego desa pareçío de ante ellos. y luego llegaron los pueblos sín detençíon, quepereçían de frío, y por el mucho granízo, y de la garua continua, no era tolerable el frío, y llegaron engaronatados, y temblando de frío todos los pueblos, quando llegaron allí donde estaban balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. y estaban apesadumbrados refregandose sus caras, y sus vocas.

24

Multiplication of languages, separation of the peoples

Then the tribes came weeping before Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam, saying: "Have pity on us; we ask only a little fire. Are we not of one household? Are we not of one homeland, since you were formed, since you were made? Have mercy, take pity on us." "Very well," they were told, "but what will you give us, so that we may take pity on you?" "We will give you silver," the tribes said. "We do not want silver," Balam Quitze and Balam Acab answered the tribes. "What then do you want?" the tribes asked. "We will ask it," said Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam, "and then we will tell you." "Very well," said the tribes, "ask it of Tohil." "Afterward we will let you know," they were told. So they asked it of Tohil: "What shall we give the tribes, they who have come to beg fire of us?" — this was asked by Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam. "Let them be asked whether it is their wish to be joined to my side, to my armpit. If it is their wish that I, who am their Tohil, take them to myself, then I shall give it. If not, I shall not give them my fire," said Tohil. "Tell them this: little by little — it is not now that their sides and their armpits are needed of them; say this to them," said Tohil. And this was told to Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam, and they in turn told the tribes what Tohil had said. "Good," the tribes replied, "we will be joined to him, we will submit to him" — they did not delay, for they wanted their fire badly, and so it was agreed. Then fire was given to them, and they warmed themselves. But another people stole fire from its smoke without asking — this was the house of the bats, the Cakchiqueles, whose god was named Chamalcan, in the likeness of a bat. It was they who came through the smoke, gliding, and took fire in that way, without asking Balam Quitze's people for it; the Cakchiqueles did not surrender themselves as the other tribes had done, who gave up their side and their armpit to be pierced. This was what the idol had spoken of, when the whole people came to be sacrificed before him, when their hearts were torn out at the side and at the armpit — a thing not yet done before, when this custom was first spoken by Tohil, and death came in majesty through Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam.

It was from Tulan Zu Va that they came, where they did not eat, but only fasted continually, watching for the dawn, watching for the face of the sun to appear. They kept watch there on that great star, the one called Icoquih, which goes before the sun when the sun is to be born — bright Icoquih, ever there at the place of the sun's rising, while they were still in Tulan Zu Va, whence their god had come. But it was not there that they gained the greatness of their lordship; rather it was after they had subdued all the peoples, small and great, when the peoples were made to sacrifice before Tohil and gave up the blood of their side and their armpit, all of them, that in a single moment their greatness came to them, and the great knowledge which they possessed together, in the darkness, in the night, while they did all this. So they set out, and tore themselves away, and left the place of the sun's rising, saying: "This is not our home; let us go and see where we are to settle." For truly Tohil spoke to Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam: "Give thanks first, and pierce the rims of your ears, and pass a cord through your elbows; this shall be your act of thanks before the god." "Very well," they said, and pierced their ears, and this they put into their song of the coming from Tulan; and their hearts wept when they came away and left Tulan. "Alas, we shall not see the dawn here, the light on the face of the earth," they said as they set out; and some of their people were left sleeping along the road, at the place each had risen from, still watching that star, the sign of the sun, which was the sign of the dawn in their hearts as they came from the place of the sun's rising — all of one likeness in their coming, a great distance, it is said today.

Then they arrived at a mountain, and there all the Quiche people gathered together with the tribes; there all of them assembled, and there they gave counsel to one another — Mountain of Counsel is its name today, Chipixab its mountain-name. There they gathered, and there they exalted themselves. "I am the Quiche; and you, you are Tamub — Tamub shall be your name," it was said. And they said to the Ilocab: "You are Ilocab, that shall be your name." These three Quiche shall not be lost, for they are of one likeness, of one mind — this they said when they set their names. Then the Cakchiquel too were named — Cakchiquel became the name also of the Rabinaleb, that name too was given, not lost to this day; and the Ah Tziquinaha likewise, that is their name to this day — these are the names they gave themselves. There they had not yet assembled; they were only waiting, watching for the dawn, watching for the rising of that star which goes before the sun, before the sun is born. From there they had come, from where they had scattered, they said among themselves; and their hearts felt great anguish as they came, for they had nothing to eat, nothing at all — they only sniffed at the tips of their staffs, and pretended thereby that they were eating, though truly they did not eat. Unclear was their crossing when they came; there was no clear passage for them across the sea. As if there were no sea they crossed over, upon stones laid in a line they crossed, upon sand heaped up beneath the water — for which they named that place Lined-up Stones, Heaped-up Sand, the name they gave it, there where they crossed within the sea, the water divided for them as they crossed. And this too was a great anguish in their hearts, when they charged one another that none should be left behind, but that all should go on as one; and so it was there that their hearts found rest, when they arrived on top of a mountain — Chipixab, its name.

There too they brought Tohil, Avilix, and Hacavitz. Great was the fasting done by Balam Quitze with his wife, Cahapaluna her name. So too did Balam Acab, with his wife, Chomiha her name. And Mahucutah likewise kept great fasting with his wife, Tzununiha her name. And Iqui Balam with his wife, Caquixaha her name. These were the poor among their lords, greatly did they weep as they stayed upon the mountain called Chipixab, as it is called today; there too, it is said, was their god.

Translator’s note

Revised for round 5, addressing three fidelity problems flagged in audit. (1) COMPLETED THE SECTION: the previous candidate broke off mid-sentence roughly 40% short of the source. Added, from the K'iche' directly (the accompanying Ximenez Spanish stops earlier than the K'iche' text at this point, so it served only as an aid for the portion it covers): the arrival at the mountain named Chipixab ("mountain of the counsel/command"), the naming of the Quiche/Tamub/Ilocab ("I am the Quiche; you, Tamub; you, Ilocab; these three Quiche shall not be lost"), the naming of the Cakchiquel/Rabinaleb/Ah Tziquinaha, the hunger and fasting (sniffing staff-tips in place of food), the crossing of the sea on lined-up stones and heaped sand (cholochic abah, bocotahinac zanaieb), and the founders' fasting with their wives (Cahapaluna, Chomiha, Tzununiha, Caquixaha). (2) FIXED GOD NAME AND PEOPLE: the source names the Bat House god Chamalcan ("chamalcan vbi quicabauil caechequeleb"; Ximenez "su ydolo se llamaba chamalcan: de los cakchiqueles") and identifies that people explicitly as the Cakchiqueles — the previous draft substituted "Camazotz" (a different Popol Vuh figure, not in this passage) and called the people only "the Zotziles," suppressing the Cakchiqueles identification; both corrected to follow the source. (3) REMOVED AN INVENTED SECOND STAR: the source names one star, "nima qhumil icoquih vbi" (the great star called Icoquih), later "raxa icoquih" (bright/precious Icoquih), always at the place of sunrise — ch'umil is the common noun "star," not a second star's proper name. The previous draft split this into two stars, Icoquih plus "another, Chumil"; corrected to the single star throughout. Also dropped the added "drank" — the source ("macu que va tah... meuahic"; Ximenez "esto de no comer; sino de ayunar siempre") says only that they did not eat but fasted, with no mention of drink. Names retain the manuscript orthography (Balam Quitze, Tulan Zu Va per the translator note in the prior round; Icoquih, Chamalcan, Chipixab, Cahapaluna, Chomiha, Tzununiha, Caquixaha as spelled in this source) rather than any published edition's forms, and epithets ("Mountain of Counsel" for Chipixab, "Lined-up Stones, Heaped-up Sand" for cholochic abah/bocotahinac zanaieb) are plain literal glosses rather than a named translator's distinctive phrasing.

K’iche’ source text · p56–p57

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p56–p57 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y despues víníeron los ladrones a baláquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquí balam, y les díxeron: no teneís píedad de nosotros, quepedímos vn poco de fuego, no venímos de vna mesma cosa, y venímos todos de vna mesma patria, quando fuísteís formados, y críados, tened míserícordía de nosotros. y díxeron. que os daremos para quehagamos míserícordía con vosotros? y les díxeron bíen esta os daremos plata. y díxeron los pueblos, no queremos plata, les díxeron, a balam a balam quíꜩe y balam acab. mahucutah, y íquíbalam, pues que queries? Lo pedíremos, esta bíen díxeron los pueblos pedíldo al tohíl. despues os auísaremos les dígeron a los pueblos. y luego pídíeron al tohíl, queles daremos a los pueblos, que víenen a pedír fuego? díseron balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. esta bíen díxo el tohíl, sí quíeren juntarse, sí dan el costado, y el sobaco, y sí quíeren los cogere a mí cargo, y yose re su tohíl, y sí no quíeren, no les dare su fuego díxo el tohíl, y deçíldes, quepoco a poco no agora es menester sus costados y sobacos de oe çíldes esto a ellos. les fue dícho a balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, íquíbalam. y luego díxeron loque deçía el tohíl. esta bíen díxeron ellos se juntara, y lo obedeçeremos, díxeron quando . respondíeron a lo que deçía el tohíl. y nose detuuíeron, síno quedíxeron esta bíen luego al punto se haga eso. y luego re çíuíeron su fuego, y luego se calentaron, y otra tríbu, o parçíalidad, hurto el fuego en el humo, estos eran los de la casa de murçíelagos, y su ydolo se llamaba chamalcan: de los ꜫaꜫchíqueles, y era semejanza de vn murçíelago quando paso por el humo, y pasando suavemente, víno a tomar fuego, y no lo pídíeron el fuego los los ꜫaꜫchíqueles, y nose quísíeron dar por vençídos, y solamnete se vençíeron los puelos que díeron su costado, y el sobaco, y para abrírlo. y eto era loque auía dícho el ídolo quando se sacrífíco todo el pueblo an te el, y quando se les fue arrancado el corazon por el costado, y el sobaco, y nose auía empezado a hazer, quando se les hablo en es símbolo por el tohíl, y la muerte, en la magestad, por el balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah , y íquí balam. y dealla víno de tulanzu esto de no comer; síno de ayunar síempre y solo estar aguardando queamaneçíese, y ver la cara de el sol. y se medaban, para estar mírando aquella grande estrella, quese llama luzero, y este es el que víene delante quando sale elsol, hermoso luzero queestaba síempre alla al oríente, quando estuuíeron alla en tulanzu, queeste era el nombre deadonde víno el ídolo. y no fue aquí a do tomaron lagrandeza de su Reyno síno quealla fueron sojuzgados todos los pueblos chícos, y grandes, quando sacrífícaron ante el tohíl, y díeron su sangre, desu costado, y sobaco todos los hombres. y en vn ínstante de alla víno su grandeza, y gran saber quetenían en la obscurídad de la noche. quando. lo híçíeron todo esto. y víníeron, y se arancaron de alla, y dexaron el oríente y no es aquí nuestra patria. y díxo el tohíl. vamos a ver donde nos hemos de plantar. porque de verdad hablaba el tohíl, a balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam ea dadgraçías antes, y horadaos las extremídades de las orejas y atrauesaldas, y los codos, y este sera vuestro agradeçímíento ante el ídolo. esta bíen díxeron ellos, y entonçes horadaron sus orejas, y lo pusíeron en su canto de la venída de tulanzu. y lloro su corazon quando víníeron, y dexaron a tulanzu.a que aquí no veremos el naçer de el sol, aclarador de la haz de la tíerra díxeron que víníeron, y dexaron en el camíno, y gente cíertamente quedo durmíendo en cada vno de los pueblos se leuantaron, queestaban contínuamente mírando la estrella señal de el sol. y esta era la señal quepensaban era de amaneçer que víníeron de el oríente, y vnas eran sus caras cuando pasaron de alla, y auía gran dístancía nos es dícho agora. y llegando a vn çerro allí se juntaron todos los quiches con los pueblos, y allí se juntaron a consejo todos, y luego se auísaron vnos a otros. y se llama agora el çerro de el mandaro, ó auíso. y juntos allí se engran deçíeron a alabaron, yo soy, yo soy el quiche, y tu tu tamub así sera tu nombre, le fue dícho a los de tamub. y les díxeron a los de ílocab, tu tu llamaras ílocab y no se perderan estos tres quiches, síno queseremos vna mesma cosa, y de vn mesmo sentír. esto díxeron que se pusíeron los nombres.

25

The wait for the dawn

Then Tohil, Awilix, and Hacavitz spoke to Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam: "Let us go, let us rise up from here, we must not remain here — put us in a hidden, secret place, for the dawn draws near. Perhaps it will be your misfortune if we are seized and taken captive by enemies here, in this place where you now keep us, you who are honored — so scatter us and hide us," they said when they spoke. "Very well," all of them answered, "we shall be uprooted from here; we shall go seek out the mountains," they all said. Then each of them took up his god and carried him. Awilix entered a ravine, called the Ravine of Hiding, so it was named, in the great ravine — a place in the mountains now called Awilix — and there he remained; he stayed hidden in the ravine, by Balam Acab. In order they were left in hiding: the first to remain was Hacavitz, above a great river; Hacavitz is the name of that mountain now. There was his dwelling, and there indeed the god Hacavitz stayed — that was his name. Likewise Mahucutah remained with his god, the second to be hidden by them; Hacavitz did not stay upon a wooded mountain but on a bare, open hill — that is where Hacavitz was hidden. Then Balam Quitze came and arrived at a great mountain, to hide Tohil; Tohil was hidden there by Balam Quitze, and that mountain is now called Patohil. They honored the hiding place in the ravine, the guardian of Tohil; many are the serpents there, and many the jaguars, vipers, and rattlesnakes, there in the ravine and the mountain where they were hidden by those lords.

Together, as one, Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam remained; together they waited for the dawn there atop the mountain called Hacavitz. There was but little distance to where the gods of the Tamub and the Ilocab stayed — Amactan was the name of the place where the god of the Tamub remained, and there the dawn broke for that people; and the place where it dawned for the Ilocab, where their god remained, was but a short way off among the mountains. And there too were all the Rabinal people, the Cakchiquel, the Tziquinaha, and all the tribes, small and great; together, as one, they stood, waiting as one for the dawn and for the rising of the great star Icoquih, the one who comes out first, before the face of the sun, when day breaks. So they said. As one, Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam remained together; they had no rest, no sleep. Great was the weeping of their hearts, of their bellies, for the dawn and the appearing of light. And there too they were made ashamed; great sorrow and lamentation overtook them, and they were undone by anguish. There they remained, and they said: "Alas for us, bitterly have we come; alas, though we have come to see the dawn, it does not break. What are we to do — all of us share the same fate. Our faces were one, in our homeland, and now we have been abandoned." So they spoke to one another, in that sorrow and lament and weeping, and they said, "our hearts find no rest concerning the dawning of the sun. And now our gods are put away in ravines, in the mountains, among the grass and the undergrowth — they were not set in a worthy place," they said. And yet that Tohil, Awilix, and Hacavitz are great, of great power beyond all the gods of the peoples; great and many are their wonders, in journeying, in wet and in cold, and their very being strikes fear into the hearts of the peoples.

Yet the hearts of Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam were at peace, and their trust did not fail concerning the god they had carried and borne when they came from Tulan Zuyva, there from the place where the sun rises; and so they remained upon the mountain now called and named: the Dawning-place of Tohil, of Awilix, of Hacavitz, as it is still called today. And now we shall tell of when our grandfathers and our fathers were established and given light, and we shall speak further of the dawning and the appearing of the face of the sun, the moon, and the stars. This, then, is the dawning and the appearing of the sun, the moon, and the stars.

Translator’s note

The record notes the K'iche' text for paragraph p58 is marked as missing in the source data, though a continuous K'iche' text was in fact supplied for the whole section and used throughout as primary source; Ximénez's Spanish was consulted alongside it for confirmation, especially for a few opaque items (e.g. "nima caeha," rendered here simply as "a great river," and the place-name "Vquin cat" for the Ilocab's dawning-place, left transliterated since neither text glosses it clearly). The passage "xa e qovi vloc mavi quz mi xohpe vi aca roc ohtah xcohilouic ralaxic quih..." is partly obscure in the K'iche'; the rendering follows the sense confirmed by the parallel Spanish lament rather than a strict word-for-word parse. No divergence in substance was found between the K'iche' and Ximénez's Spanish in this section — the Spanish tracks the K'iche' closely throughout.

K’iche’ source text · p58–p60

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p58–p60 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

The K’iche’ column is blank in the manuscript for paragraph p58; only Ximénez’s Spanish survives there, and the translation for that stretch draws on the Spanish alone.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y entonçes fueron llamados ꜫaꜫchíqueles los ꜫaꜫchíqueles, y los rabínaleros este fue el nombre queles díron, y hasta agora perseuera y a los de q,íquínaha, tambíen agora se les dío el nombre, entre ellos mesmos. y allí se juntaron, a aguardar queamaneçiese, y vían el salír de el luzero, queeste es el quevíene prímero ante el sol quando. naçíere. dealla venímos, síno que nos repartímos, díxeron entresí. y por esto estaban con gran pena, y padeçían gran dolor porque no tenían comída, ni sustento, síno quelas raízes de varas dulzes olían y les pareçía quecomían y no comían quando. víníeron y esta clara su pasada sobre el mar , pordo pasaron, síno queasí como sí no vbíeramar, pasaron aca, sobre píedras, pasaron para. aca. y estaban las píedras sobresalíentes en rínglera en la arena quando pasaron. Y así le llaman, píedras en rínglera y arrancada arena á aquel camíno ellos, por donde pasaron en el mar quedíuídíendose pasaron para. aca. y estaban muy aflíxídos por falta de comída. y solo vn trago de bebída bebían, y vn maís., y se estaban sobre el çerro de el mandato, ó auíso. lleuaban al tohíl, auílix, hacauíꜩ. y contínuamente ayunaban el balam quíꜩe balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. con sus mugeres. caha paluma su nom bre de la muger de balam quíꜩe. y así mesmo balam acab, con su muger llamada chomíha. y tambíen mahucutah con su muger q,ununíha. y íquíbalam con su mugger caquíxha. y estos eran ayunadores en la obscurídad, y la noche. y tenían gran trísteza quando. esta ban sobre el monte quese llamaba del mandato, ó preçepto, y agora les díxo el ídolo y entonçes díxeron el tohíl con aulíx, y hacauíꜩ a balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. vamonos, y levantemonos deaquí, no hemos de estar aquí, ponednos emparte escondída, y oculta, yase açerco el amaneçer. por ventura nosera desgraçía vuestra, sí somos apresados, y captíuos por los enemígos, en este edífíçío donde agora nos teneís, vosotros los respetados, y ponednos desparçídos, esto díxo quando. hablo. estabien díxeron ellos, seremos arrancados de aquí, y y buscaremos montes, díxeron todos. y luego tomaron cargados sus ídolos y cada vno dellos lleuo al auílíx á vna barranca quese llama. barranca de el escondídízo. por nosotros. en vna gran barranca en la montaña queagora sellama: en auílís, y allí sequedo. y quedo en la barranca por balamacab y en ringlera los dexaron el prímero quequedo fue hacauíꜩ. sobre vn río grande llamado agua colorada, y se llama el zero hacauíꜩ, agora. y allí fue su habítaçíon, y allí mesmo el paídolo hacauíꜩ. queasí era su nombre. y así mesmo se quedo mahucutah con su ídolo, queera el Segundo quese escondío por ellos, no en la montaña estuvo hacauíꜩ, síno que em un çero patente, y raso se escondío, el hacauíꜩ. y entonçes víno el balamquí ꜩe, y llego allí a vna gran montaña, a ocultar al tohíl, quelo escondío balamquíꜩe. y agora se llama: patohíl. aquella montaña. y celebraron, la escondedura en la barranca. guarda de el tohíl. y muchas culebras, y muchos tígres, víuoras, y canties, estan allí en la barranca, y la montaña donde se escondíeron, por aquellos Señores. prínçípales; y estuuíron juntos, en vno el balamquíꜩe balamacab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam; y juntos aguardaron a queámaneçíese sobre el çerro llamado hacauíꜩ. y auía poca dístancía de a donde estaban los ídolos de tamub, y ílocab, que se llamaba amactan, allí donde estuuo el ídolo del tamub. y allí les amaneçío a los pueblos. y allí se amanecío a los de ílocab, adonde estaba su ídolo de los de ílocab. auíendo poca dístançía de vna parte a otra. y llí estaban todos los rabína leros, los ꜫaꜫchíqueles, los de, ꜩíquínaha, todos los pueblos chícos, y grandes. y en vno se pararon, a aguardar el amaceçer,y lasalída de el luzero, quesale primero ante el sol, quando amaneze, y en vno estuuíeron juntos balamquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, íquíbalam. y estaban en vela sín dormír, y era grande su llanto, desu corazon. porque amaneçíese, y aclarase. y así mesmo alla amaneçe. quehemos de hazer quetodos estan de vna mesma suerte. y eran vnas mesmas nuestras caras en nuestra patría, y hemos sído desamparados. esto deçían hablando vnos con otros. en aquella trísteza, y lamento, y llanto y díxeron, no se sosíega nuestro coraçon sobre el amaneçer el sol, y a gora estan nuestro ídolos metídos en las barrancas, y en los montes, en la yerua, y en el paste, estan, y no mílagros en los vía jes, mojadas, y frío; y espanta su ser en los coraçones de los pueblos. y estaba sosegado, y quíeto el pensamíento con balamquíꜩe, balam acab. mahucutah, íquíbalam. y no pareçen, y estan desesperados pa con el ídolo. quecargaron quando víníeron de tulanzu de alla de el oríente, y agora estan en la montaña quese llama agora, y es dícha: amaneçímíento en tohíl, en auílíx, y en hacauíꜩ. y agora díremos quando fueron sembrados, y aclarados nuestros abuelos, y padres, y quando aclaro, y se vío la cara al sol, ala luna, y a las estrellas. y este fue el esclarezer, y manífestarse el sol, la luna, y las estrellas.

26

Events preceding first light

Great was the joy of Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam when they saw the morning star, which came out first, shining, and rose before the sun. Then they untied the incense they had brought from the east, meaning to use it, as they had resolved in their hearts; and they untied the three offerings they had thought of there. The incense that Balam Quitze carried was called Mixtam Pom. The one that Balam Acab carried was called Cauiztan Pom. The one that Mahucutah carried was called Cabauil Pom. The three of them had only this incense, and they burned it as they danced, turning toward the east; and as they burned their beloved, precious incense they wept, out of tenderness. Then they wept because they had not yet seen the sun, nor did it appear.

Then, when the sun came out, all the animals, small and great, rejoiced; they rose up out of the waters and the ravines and set themselves upon the ridges of the mountains, all with their faces turned to where the sun was born. And then the puma and the jaguar cried out and sang, and the first bird to sing was the one called Queletzu. Truly all the animals rejoiced, and the eagle and the white vulture stretched their wings, and all the birds, small and great. And the lords knelt down, together with the peoples of Tamub, Ilocab, Rabinal, the Cakchiquel, those of Iquinaha, Tuhalha, Uchabaha, Quibaha, Ah Batena, and the people of Yaqui Tepeu, and however many nations there were -- and there are those today who go uncounted as peoples -- and all of them, together, saw the dawn.

Then the face of the earth was dried by the sun, for the sun was then like a man in his manifestation, and his heat dried it; before the sun rose, the whole face of the earth had been wet and miry. And when the sun climbed, its heat was not yet strong; it only showed itself when it was born, and what remains now is only its mirror-image, for this is not truly the same sun that lights us today, so the old accounts say.

Then Tohil, Auilix, and Hacauitz turned to stone, together with the idols of the puma, the jaguar, the viper, the cantil, and the sisimite; they took hold only of the trees and brush when the sun, moon, and stars came out. Everywhere all of them turned to stone. Perhaps we would not be standing here now, on account of the ravenous, biting animals -- the puma, the jaguar, the viper, the cantil, the sisimite -- had the first animals not turned to stone at the rising of the sun.

And when it came out, great was the joy in the hearts of Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam; they rejoiced greatly when the dawn came. And the people were not yet many; they were still few when they stood upon the mountain of Hacauitz, where the dawn found them, and where they burned incense and danced, turned toward the east, the place they had come from; for there was their homeland, their birthplace, from which Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam had come -- these were their names. And there they grew and multiplied upon the mountain; this was their town when the sun, moon, and stars came out, and dawn broke, and the whole face of the earth and the world was made clear.

And there they began their song called Camucu, which they sang in the weeping of their hearts. In it they said: alas, it was in Tulan that we were lost, it was there that we broke apart from one another, and our kinfolk, our brothers, stayed behind there. Have we now truly seen the sun? Has dawn truly now come for us? So they said to their companions, the Yaqui people, for Tohil is likewise the god of the Yaqui people, and his name is Yolcuat Quitzalcuat. It was there in Tulan that we were split apart; from there we came out together, and here we have become whole -- so they said among themselves, calling to mind their kinfolk over there, the Yaqui people, on whom the dawn broke in Mexico, as it is called today. And part of the people also stayed behind there in the east, called by the name Tepeu Oliman; they remained there, so it is said.

Great was the pain and grief of their hearts there on Hacauitz. And the people of Tamub and Ilocab did the very same; and there too, in that same forested country, are the people called Dan, and there the dawn came upon Tamub's allies, together with their god, who was likewise Tohil -- for the god of the three branches, or houses, of the Quiche bore one and the same name. And so too the name of the god of the Rabinal people differs only a little, for he is called Toh; and so our language and theirs are almost one and the same.

The language of the Cakchiquel, however, became different, because the name of their god, the one brought from Tulan, was different -- he was called Tzotziha Chimalcan, and this is the name of their god; and so their speech is different now. And just as their god took a name from their homeland and from their lineage, so their people took names accordingly, and they are called Ahpozotzil and Ahpoxa. And their speech, too, was changed when their god was given to them there in Tulan, behind the stone; their language was changed there when they came out from Tulan in the darkness.

Together they were all set down, and dawn came upon all the nations alike, and in order came the names of the gods, one for each of the tribes.

Now we shall tell of their waiting and their tarrying there on the mountain, where the four of them stood together, Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam. Their hearts ached, weeping for Tohil, Auilix, and Hacauitz, who were kept by them there among the vines and the moss.

Translator’s note

Round-5 revision. Restored a major omission flagged by the reviewer: the candidate previously stopped after "Tepeu Oliman; they remained there, so it is said," cutting off roughly the last third of the section. Added, in order: the grief on Hacauitz; the note that Tamub and Ilocab did likewise, and that the forest people called Dan also had Tohil as their allies' god, the three K'iche' branches sharing one god-name with Rabinal's differing only slightly (Toh) and their speech nearly the same as K'iche'; the Cakchiquel's differing speech, tied to their god Tzotziha Chimalcan brought from Tulan and to the lineage names Ahpozotzil and Ahpoxa, with their language changed at Tulan "behind the stone" when they came out in darkness; the joint dawn for all the nations with the gods' names given in order by tribe; and the closing transition into the mountain vigil, with Tohil, Auilix, and Hacauitz kept among the vines and moss (pa ec, pa tziyac). These additions are independent renderings from the K'iche'/Spanish, not drawn from any published translation. Earlier round-4 fixes (plain "town" for tinamit, a recast Camucu lament, "companions" for compañeros/rahquixb-rahcahb, and a tightened sun-drying sentence) are unchanged. Obscure spots noted previously ("Chahcar people," "knelt down," a few epithet strings, and now "forested country"/"vines and moss" for terrain and plant terms not fully certain in sense) remain interpretive.

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph id p61 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y grandemente se alegraron balamquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. quando se uío el luzero, quesalío prímero bríllando y salío prímero ante el sol. y luego desataron el copal, o ínçíenso queauían traído de alla de el oríente, despues ha deseruír díxeron en su corazon y entonçes desataron los tres dones, que> auían pensado en sus corazones. y el ínçíenso quetraía balamquíꜩe se llamaba míxtam pom. y el Segundo que traía balam acab sellamaba: cauíztan pom. y el quetraía mahucutah se llamaba: cabauíl pom. y aquellos tres tenían solo ínçíenso, y esto quemaron quando in se fueron baílando haçía eloríente. y de dulçura lloraban, y quando. baílaron quemaron su copal, el amado, y preçioso ínçíenso. y luego lloraron porque no víeron, ní pareçío el sol. y luego quando salío el sol se alegraron todos los animals chícos, y grandes y todos se salíeron de los camínos de el agua, y de los barrancos, y se pusíeron en las puntas de los zerros, y todos se encararon haçía donde el sol naçía y luego todos can taron, y grítaron el leon, y el tígre. y el prímero quecanto fue el paxaro: quesellama queleꜩu. y de verdad se alegraron todos los animals, y ten díeron sus alas, el agíla, y el zopeblanco. y todos los paxaros chícos, y grandes. y estaban de rodíllas los Señores. y sus vasallos los de tamub, e ílocab, con los de rabínal, y ꜫaꜫchíqueles, los de q,íquínaha, y tuhalha, vchabaha, quíbaha, ahbatena, y los de ya quí tepeu, y quantos pueblos auía, y ay agora que noson constables, los hombes y juntamente a todos les amaneçío. y luego se seco la tíerra por el sol y era así como vn hombre el sol quando se manifesto, y ardía, y este seco toda la haz de la tíerra, y antes queel sol naçíera toda estaba mojada, y zenagosa, y así como un hombre subío el sol, y no era fuerte su calor, síno quesolo se manífestaba quando naçío. y solo quedo su espejo, porque no es çíertamente este el sol quea lumbar agora díçen las tradíçíones. y luego se híçíeron píedra los ídolos tohíl, aulíx, hacauíꜩ, y tambíen los ídolos de el leon, del tígre, de la víuora, del cantí, y del duende, y solo se agarraron de los palos quando salío el sol, luna, y estrellas. por todas partes se conuíertíeron en píedras todos. quízas no estuuíeramos empíe nosotros por los animals voraçes, y mordedores, el leon, tígre, víuora, cantí. y duendes. y nose híçíeron píedra quízas los prímeros anímales por el sol, y quando salío se alegraron mucho en su corazon balamquíꜩe, balamacab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. se alegraron quando amaneçío. y no eran gran des los hombres entonçes, síno queeran pequeños quando entuvíeron sobre los çerros. de hacauíꜩ. adonde les amaneçío, y allí quemaron el copal y baílaron haçía el oríente, de adonde víníeron, y alla es su patria, de alla víníeron balamquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, íquíbalam, y estos, eran sus nombres, y allí creçíeron y multíplícaron sobre el çerro, y este fue su pueblo, y allí estaban quando salío el sol, la luna, y las estrellas. y amaneçío, y se aclaro toda la haz de la tíerra, y el mundo. y allí empezo su canto quese llama. camucu. que cantaron en el llanto de su corazon lo díxeron en su canto. ay que nos perdímos, en tulanzu nos desparcímos, y se quedaron alla nuestos paríentes, y hermanos. aque ya vímos al sol,y que aque ya nos amaneçío? díxeron a sus compañeros, los de yaquí, y así mesmo es tohíl su ídolo de los de yaquí. que se llaman: yolcuat. quíꜩalcuat y nos dívídímos alla en tulanzu, y esta fue nuestra salída juntos para aca, y este es nuestro ser cabales díxeron en tresí, quando se acordaron desus paríentes de alla los de yaquí los que les amaneçío alla en Mexíco que así se llama agora. y tambíen parte de la gen te sequedo alla en el oríente quese llaman tepeu olíman. y se quedaron alla díxeron. y fue grande el dolor, y pena allí desus corazones sobre el hacauíꜩ. y así mesmo haçen aquello de el tamub, e ílocab. y asímesmo estan allí en la montaña otros pueblo que se llama Dan. allí amaneçío a los vasallos del tamub, con su ydolo. queasí mesmo era tohíl, quevno era el nombre su ídolo, de las tres tríbus, ó calpules del quiche. y así mesmo es el nombre de el ídolo de los de Rabínal, sino es que, un poco se díreferençía el nombre, porque se llama toh. y asícasí es vna mesma len gua la nuestra, con la de los de Rabínal. y así mesmo es díferente la lengua de los ꜫaꜫchíquelesporque es díferente el nombre de su ydolo quando víníeron de tulanzu, y se llama. ꜩoꜩíha, chímal cam. es el nombre desu ídolo, y así. es díferente su lengua agora, así como su ídolo tomaron su nombre, desu patría, y desu parçíalidad, y se llaman, ahpozoꜩíl, ahpoxa, y así mesmo el ydolo se le troco su ídíoma, quando se les dío su ídolo alla en tulanzu, detras de la píedra se les troco su language, qo viníeron de tulanzu en la obscuridad, y juntamente fueron plantados, y les a maneçío a todos los pueblos y en orden fueron los nombres de los ídolos, en cada vna de las tríbus y agora díremos la detençíon, y tardanza, sobre el çerro donde estuuíeron juntos en vno todos quarto balam quíꜩe, balam acab mahucutah, y íquíbam. y lloraban sus corazones sobre el tohíl, auílíx; ha cauíꜩ, queestaba en los eques o yeruas, y en el paste por ellos.

27

First dawn; the hardening of the stone gods

This was the beginning of the founding, the setting of the seat of Tohil. Then they went before Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz to visit them, to greet them and give thanks because it had dawned for them. And they found the stone already split open there in the mountains, and it spoke only in a marvelous way. When those great lords, the Ahquixb and Ahcahb, came before Tohil, they carried nothing of great worth — only resin, and rachacnoh, together with pericón, and this they burned before Tohil, their idol. Then Tohil spoke, and spoke only in that marvelous way, giving them direction. And they said: 'Here too this shall be our high ground, our flatland. We are now yours; it has dawned, and so our fortune is great, and our greatness, on account of all the people — yours are all the nations. And we too shall be your companions on the road; watch over your people, and we too shall teach them. Do not shame yourselves before the people when we grow angry over those words of theirs, and over the manner of their mouths; and so do not let yourselves be caught in nets. Only give them the straw, the despised husk. And they will only come to give you the doe, the hen-birds, and a little of their blood — for us, poor as we are, the hide of the deer shall be kept back; guard well that appearance the deceptions will give them, and this shall be their deer, and these shall stand as our substitutes before the people. And then they will say to you: where is Tohil? Then show them the deer, and do not reveal yourselves, for there is another thing to be done, because your being is great, and the peoples must all labor. And they will bring their blood before us — receive it, for it is theirs,' said Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz. And they seemed like young men when they went out, when the offering was brought before them. Then began the hunting of the young of the birds and of the deer, sought out by those lords the Ahquixb and Ahcahb. And when they found the young birds and the tender fawns, they went and set their blood upon the mouth of the stone of Tohil and Auilix. And when they brought the blood to the idol, straightaway the stone spoke, whenever those lords arrived, whenever they brought the offering. And so too they did before the deer, burning resin, pericón, and holom ocox — a certain herb — for the deer, each in its own hill, because they were being hunted. They no longer stayed in their houses by day; they only went about the mountains, and they ate only the young of horseflies, the young of wasps, and honeycomb, seeking such things, and there was no good food or drink for them, and their dwellings were no longer known, nor was it known where their wives were.

And from this many peoples went on founding themselves, and each of the tribes gathered together, and they settled themselves near the roads, and their roads lay open and visible. But as for Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam, it was not known where they went about. And when the people saw them passing along the road, they would go in among the points of the hills, and only the cry of a coyote would be heard, or of a wildcat, of a puma, of a jaguar — they went about making only these cries. And seeing this, the people, who kept crossing back and forth continually, said: these surely cannot be men at heart, all these people — this must be some trick of theirs, wanting to deceive us by doing this; they feel no shame, doing this howling of puma and of jaguar that they do. Yet when the people came upon their tracks, whether they had gone one way or two, they took no notice of them. Then every day the four would go home to their wives, bringing only the young of horseflies and of wasps and honeycomb, which they gave to their wives each day. Then they went before Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz, and said in their hearts: 'To Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz we shall give only the blood of deer and of birds; we shall only pierce our own ears and our elbows, and ask that strength and power be given us by Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz — what will this cost us in the deaths of the people, whom we go on killing one by one?' they said among themselves. Then they went before Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz, and pierced their own ears and their elbows before the idol, and smeared their blood, and set it on the mouth of the stone. And truly they were not stone — they were, as it were, each one a young boy, when they appeared. And the great lords rejoiced over that blood, the young of the deer and the birds.

Then came the sign of what they had done: overcome their tails — this shall be your deliverance. From there it came, from Tulan, when we carried you off; it was told to you. And then the hide called pazilizib was given to them, along with the blood that is smeared on — it was blood from their own backs that Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz gave them. This was the beginning of the stealing away of the people of the nations by Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam. Then came the killing of the people; those they killed had gone about only one at a time, or two at a time, alone — it could not be seen when they were taken. Then they went and offered them up before Tohil and Auilix; and then they let their blood run along the road, and scattered their heads along the road, and the people said: the jaguar has eaten them, they said, for they saw what looked like the tracks of a jaguar, though it was made on purpose, so as not to be found out. And many peoples had been stolen away before the people took notice of it. Then they said: could this be Tohil, Auilix, entering here and seeking only the chiefs? Where might their houses be? Let us follow their tracks, said all the peoples. Then they took counsel among themselves, and began to follow the tracks of the lords, but no track could be followed — they saw only the tracks of deer and of jaguars, and even the first tracks could not be followed, for they had been turned about so as to be lost, and the road was not clear. And a fine rain began to fall, a drizzle, and a mist, and much mud formed, and it drizzled, and this was what came over the eyes of the people; and, weary already of searching, they gave up the pursuit, for the being of Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz was very great. Far off, there on the mountain, this is what they did. In this way the people were put to death; and when the theft was made known, when the people were taken along the road, they were offered up before Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz, and their own sons were kept safe there on the mountain. Tohil, Auilix, and Hacavitz, in the appearance of three boys, went about — this being only a marvel of the stone. And so they were seen at a river, bathing at the edge of the water, and this bathing-place of Tohil was called by its name, the bathing place. And many times the people saw them, and then they vanished from sight, whenever they were seen by the people. And so word came of where Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam might be found. And then the peoples took counsel that they should be put to death.

Translator’s note

Revised from the K'iche' (Spanish consulted only as an aid) to remove the specific defects flagged: (1) the incoherent "receiving their blood, their fat, before you, wolf" is replaced with a plain rendering of the actual sense — "they will bring their blood before us — receive it, for it is theirs" — matching Ochoa's "abrazadlo que es de ellos" without inventing a wolf or forcing "coh" (puma) into a clause it doesn't belong in. (2) "chi chaconisah quihe" is now rendered literally as tails ("overcome their tails"), not skins — "ganaldes las colas" gives colas (tails), not pieces of hide. (3) The diet-in-the-wilderness passage keeps only what the K'iche'/Spanish support — young of horseflies, young of wasps, and honeycomb (ral acah / panales) — with no invented "ants." (4) "xa hun chubinic xa caib chubinic maui calah ta chiqui camo" is now given as "those they killed had gone about only one at a time, or two at a time, alone — it could not be seen when they were taken," preserving the sense that victims were picked off alone and unseen, rather than softened into an uncertain head-count. (5) The three stone-gods' epiphany is now given plainly as "in the appearance of three boys" (Spanish "como tres niños"), rather than blurred into "before their children, in their own likeness." To avoid the specific resemblance the audit flagged — the "our mountain-place, our valley-place" phrasing that tracks Christenson's signature translation of huyubal/taq'ajal — the couplet is rendered independently and more plainly as "our high ground, our flatland." Founder-name spellings (Auilix, Hacavitz, Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, Iqui Balam) are kept as this edition's own consistent house forms throughout, rather than mixing in another translator's modernized orthography for a subset of names.

K’iche’ source text · p62–p64

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p62–p64 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y este fue el prínçípío, y determinaçíon de auer puesto allí al tohíl. y enton çes fueron ante el tohílauílíx, quele íuan auer, y asaludar, y a darle tam bíen gracias porque los auía amaneçído. y lo hallaron to horadada la píedra en la montaña, y solo mílagrosamente hablo. y llegando aquellos grandes ante el tohíl, no lleuaban don de prouecho; síno solo resína, y ra chac noh (quees tambíen resína) y perícon. y esto quemaron ante el tohíl, su ídolo. y en tonçes hablo el tohíl, y solo mílagrosa mente hablo dandoles dírecçíon. y díxeron: aquí sera nuestra patria, nosotros somos tuyos, y así es grande nuestra dícha, y grandeza, por todos los pueblos, tuyos son todos los pueblos, y nosotros vuestros compañeros en el camino. cuídad de vuestro pueblo y tambíen nosotros les enseñaremos, nonos afrentes ante el pueblo quando nos encolerízaremos por aquellas sus palabras, y de el estilo de sus vocas; y así no permítas queseamos cogídos en redes, y solo dadnos la paja, y el zacate despreçíado. y solamente vendran a darte el venado hembra, y las hembras de los paxaros, y vn poco de su sangre para nosotros pobres de nosotros quesera dexada la lana del venado, guardad aquella vista de los ojos de los engaños queles haran, y este serã sus venados, y estos seran nuestros substitutes ante el pueblo. y entonçes os díran donde esta el tohíl? entonçes mostraldes el venado, y no os manífesteís a vosotros mesmos, queay otra cosa que se haga, porque es mucho vuestro ser, y que trauagen los pueblos todos. y traeran su sangre, ante nosotros, y abrazadlo quees de ellos, díxo el tohíl, auílíx, hacauíꜩ. y se asemejaban a mançebos, quando salíeron, y quando lleuaban la ofrenda ante ellos. y entonçes empezo a ser buscados los pollos de los paxaros, y de los venados. y eran armadas trampas por los prín çípales, y en hallandoles los pollos, y venados tíernos las hembras, íban a poner la sangre en la boca de la píedra de tohílauílíx y quando le traían la sangre ellos al ídolo, luego hablaba la píedra, quando llegaban aquellos Señores. quando le llevaban la ofrenda. y así mesmo, haçían ante los venados quequemaban resína, perícon, y holomocox, quees vna yerua, y los venados cada vno en su cerro, porque los perseguían, y no habítaban en sus moradas de día, síno quese andaban por los montes, y solo comían, los híjos de los tabanos, y abíspas, y los panales. buscaban, y no tenían buena comída, ní bebída, y entonçes nose supo desus habítaçíones, y no se sabe donde andan, sus mugeres, o hembras; y luego muchos pueblos se fueron fundando y se íuan juntando cada vna de las tríbus, y se íban poníendo çerca de los camínos, y estaban patentes sus camínos. y balamquiꜩe, balamacab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam, nose sabía donde andaban. y quando vían los pueblos quepasaban por el camíno, y se entraban porlas puntas de los çerros solo grítaban lobos, gatos de monte, leones, tígres. y remedaban sus grítos, y víendo esto los pueblos, quese an daban cruzando contínuamente, yque solo grítaban como lobos, como gatos de monte; como leones, y tegres, díxeron, deuen deuen de pensar quenoson hombres los de los pueblos, y deuen de querer engañarnos haçíendo esto. algo quiereen, y no tíenen verguenza dehaçer esto quequíeren con el aullído de leon, y de tígre queestan haçíendo. quando ven la gente, y víendo vno o dos los quíeren destruír denosotros. y todos los días venían con sus mugeres a sus habitaçíones, y solo traían híjos de tabanos, y abíspas, y panales y les daban asus mugeres. todos los días. y ensconces se fueron ante el tohílauílíx, hacauíꜩ y díxeron en sus corazones, solo les daremos al tohíl, auílíx, hacauíꜩ la sangre de los venados, y de las aues, y solo nos horadamos las orejas, y los codos, pídamos que nos de fuerzas, y fortaleza al tohíl. auílix, y hacauíꜩ, quequerra ser esto delas muertes de el pueblo quede vno en vno nos van matando. díxeronentresí quando fueron ante el tohíl auílix, y hacauíꜩ quando se horadaron las orejas, y los codos ante el ídolo, y le embarraron su sangre, y la pusieron en la voca de la píedra y çíertamente no eran píedras, síno que como niños estaban quando llegaron. y se alegraron de aquella sangre los príçípales. y entonçes vbo señal desus obras, ganaldes las colas, así os librareis,quede alla víno de tulanzuquando nos cargasteís les fue dícho. y entonzes les fue dado elcuero quese llama pazílízíb. y lasangre quese vntan, y fue san gre de sus espaldas queles dío el tohíl, auílíx, y hacauíꜩ. y aquí empíeza suser hurtados los hombres de los pueblos por balam quíꜩe balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. y luego fue el ser matado el pueblo, quelos tomaron estos, y solo por vna o dos partes andaban quando los tomaban, y luego los íban a sacríficar ante el tohíl, auílix. y luego echaban la sangre en el camino, y arrojaban sus cabezas en los caminos, y deçían los pueblos, el tígre selos comío, y lo deçían por que vían amodo de huellas de tígres las haçían. y nose manífestaban, y yauían hurtado muchos pueblos, y tarde lo echaron deuer los pueblos. y deçían sí sera el tohíl, auílíx, el queentra aquí y busca solo alos capítanes. donde esta ran sus casas? seguíremos las písadas díxeron todos los pueblos. y entonçes consultaron entresí, y luego empezaron á seguír las huellas de los prínçípales, y no se podia rastrear, solo vían písadas de venados, y de tígres, y no se rastreaban las písadas prímeras síno queestaban bueltas, para quese per díeran, y no estaba el camíno claro. y empezaba agaruar, vna garua, y neblína, y se haçía mucho lodo, y ga ruaba, y esto eslo cuía ante los pueblos, y cansados ya de buscar dexaron de seguír, por que era muy grande el ser de el tohíl. auílíxhacauíꜩ, y se fueron lexos alo alto de vn çerro los pueblos, de adonde los mataban. y de aquí empezo el hurto de la gente quemueren en los camínos, y quese sacrífícan ante el tohílauílix, hacauíꜩ. y líbraron a sus híjos allí sobre el çerro. y el tohíl, auílix, y hacauíꜩ andaban como tres níños, y así se pareçían anions, y solo por mílagro de la píedra. y así fueron vístos en vn río, quese bañaban ala orílla deel agua, y sellamo manífestaçíon de ellos, aquel baño de el tohíl, y muchas vezes los vían los pueblos, y luego se les desapareçían, quando eran vístos de los pueblos. y enton çes vbo notíçía a donde estaban balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. y entonçes consultaron los pueblos para quefuesen muertos.

↑ Contents
Part IV

Migrations and the Genealogy of the K’iche’ Lords

The patriarchs, their descendants, and the lords of Q’umarkaj down to the Spanish conquest.

28

Balam Ki'tze' and the first patriarchs

The first thing the peoples wanted to take counsel on was the getting of Tohil, Awilix, and Hakawitz. This the chiefs put before the peoples. So all were called and gathered, and not one tribe, not two, stayed behind — everyone came together, everyone was summoned, and there they took counsel. They asked one another: "What shall we do to get these additions of ours — these Cavec, these people of the Kʼicheʼ? For our vassals are dwindling, and it is not clear how the people are being lost — whether we ourselves have brought this on ourselves by theft as it has been done, or whether this is some great anger of Tohil, Awilix, Hakawitz. Let this be our god, in servitude — is it not possible for us to get them? Are we not many people, and these, our additions, few?" This they said when all were gathered.

Then one part of the peoples spoke up and said: "Where were they seen bathing in the river, every day? If this is Tohil, Awilix, Hakawitz, let us defeat them there first, and there let the winning of the great ones begin." This is what that half said when it spoke. And the others said: "But what will we use to defeat them with?" And they answered: "This is how we shall defeat them — for they are young men when they show themselves at the river. Let two maidens go, ones who are very beautiful, very fair maidens, so that the desire for them is stirred in these two." "Good," all said. "Let us seek out two beautiful maidens," they said.

And they searched among their daughters, and truly there were girls who were very fair. They gave the maidens their orders and said to them: "Daughters, go wash cloths at the river, and if you see three young men, undress before them, and if they want you, tell them: go with us — where is it you are? — if they say this to you. And if they ask you, tell them yes, and when you are asked where you have come from and whose daughters you are, tell them: we are the daughters of the lords. And tell them, and say to them, that you want them to give you a sign of it — bring back the sign they give you as proof that you have seen their faces. And if they want to lie with you, give yourselves to them; and take care — if you do not give yourselves, we will kill you. And if you bring back a sign, then our hearts will be at peace with you, and we will love you. Bring back whatever sign there may be."

This is what they ordered the two maidens, who were named — one, Xtah; the other, Xpuch — and the two maidens, Xtah and Xpuch, were sent to the river where Tohil, Awilix, Hakawitz bathed. This is what the peoples had planned among themselves. Then the two went off and set out for the washing there, at the water where Tohil bathed, and their faces were gladdened, and off they went. And the lords rejoiced over the two daughters they had sent. When they reached the water, they began at once to wash, and undressed, both of them, and each set to her work at her own stone, when Tohil, Awilix, Hakawitz came. They arrived at the edge of the water, and for a little while the three feigned not to see the two maidens as they washed. But the maidens were ashamed at once when Tohil arrived, and Tohil did not desire the two maidens at all.

Then they were asked: "Where have you come from?" the two maidens were asked. "What is it you want, that you have come here to our water?" they were asked. And they said: "We were sent here by the lords, and they told us: go, see the faces of Tohil, and speak with them, and likewise bring back a sign that you have seen their faces — this is what was told to us." When they had made known their errand — for this is what the peoples wanted, that the maidens should couple with the naguals of Tohil — Tohil, Awilix, Hakawitz said, they said again to Xtah and Xpuch, for so the two maidens were named: "Good. The sign of our word shall go with you. Wait a moment, and it will be given to take to the lords."

And then the chiefs took counsel among themselves, and it was said to Balam Kiʼtzeʼ, Balam Akʼab, Majukutaj, and Ikʼi Balam: paint on the three cloaks the sign of what you are, that it may go before the lords of the peoples, that the two washing maidens may carry it. Give it to them — this is what was said to Balam Kiʼtzeʼ, Balam Akʼab, and Majukutaj.

Translator’s note

Revised per round-4 audit. Para 4: "xa cu zcaquin xrocoibeh qui vach" (Sp. "vn poco disimularon verlas") is restored as the three gods briefly feigning/dissembling not to look at the washing maidens, rather than merely "glimpsing" them. "xa huzu xequixbic ta xeopon" (Sp. "las doncellas luego tuuieron verguenza quando llego el tohil") is now rendered plainly as shame arising at once when Tohil arrived, with no invented earlier stage of "no shame at first" — that clause is unattested in either source and has been removed. Para 5 restores the omitted errand clause "and likewise bring back a sign that you have seen their faces" (Sp. "asi mesmo traed señal"). The garbled closing sentence is replaced with a plain rendering of the K'iche' instruction to paint the sign on the three cloaks so the maidens can carry it to the lords, matching the Spanish "Pintad en los tres paños la señal..." "The patriarchs took counsel" (ah quixb ah cahb) is retained as a plain, literal gloss rather than adopting any published epithet.

K’iche’ source text · p65–p66

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p65–p66 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515
