Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y lo prímero quísíeron consultar, los pueblos el ganar al tohíl, auílix, y hacauíꜩ. esto propusíeron los prínçípales ante los pueblos. y fueron todos llamados, y conuocados, y ní vna, ní dos delas tríbus se quedo, síno quetodos se juntaron, y se llamaron, y entonçes consultaron, y díxeron preguntandose vnos a otros: queharemos para ganar a estas añadíduras de los quechees porquese acaban los vasallos, por que no esta claro como se píerden los hombres, sí nos acabamos por el hurto quando. se a hecho. ó sí es grande enojo este del tohílauílíx, hacauíꜩ. y este sera nuestro ídolo el tohíl, en esclauítud, no sera posible quelos ganemos, por uentura noso mos nosotros muchos hombres, y esta nuestra añadídura son pocos, esto díxeron quando se juntaron todos. y díxo vna parte de los pueblos quando hablo: adonde víeron quese bañaba en el río todos los días? y sí este es tohílauílíx, hacauíꜩ, los vençeremos prímero, y allí tendra prínçípío se ser ganados; los grandes, esto díxo la mítad quando hablo. y díxeron quesera aquello conquelos hemos de ganar? díxeronlos demas. desta suerte dí xeron los ganaremos, por que son mancebos quando se manífíestan en el río. vayan dos donçellas, quesean muy hermosas, y quesean muy blancas donçellas, y quese les vaya el deseo, a ellas. estabíen díxeron todos. busquemos dos hermosas donçellas díxeron. y entonçes buscaron entre sus híjas. y çíertamente eran muy blancas níñas, y les mandaron entonçes y díxeron. híjas andad a labar lospaños al río, y sí os víeren tres mancebos desnudaos ante ellos, y síos apetecen +deçíldes íremos a alla donde estaís vosotros? sí os díxeren así. deçíldes quesí y quando os fuere preguntado de adonde aueís uenído y dequíen soís híjas, somos híjas delos Senores.deçíldes, y deçíldes queos den señal, y traed laseñal queos díeren. y síquíeren habi cohabitar con vosotras daos a ellos; y mírad quesí no os dais os hemos de matar. y sí traeís señal luego estara bueno nuestro corazon pa. con vosotras, y os querremos. y sí vbíere señal alguna traelda. esto les mandaron alas dos donçellas quese llamaban la vna. xtah. y la otra xpuch. y dlas dos donçellas xtah, y xpuch fueron embíadas al río adonde se bañaba el tohíl auílíx cahauíꜩ. y esto fue lo quedíscurríeron los pueblos. y luego se fueron a componer, y a aderezar, y çíertamente queestaban hermosas, quando se fueron al río, al baño de tohíl, y desuergonzadamen se lababan, y deshonestamente. y se alegraban los Señores, por sus dos híjas que auían embíado. y llegando al río empezaron a labar, y se desnudaron ambas a dos, y estaban cada vna en su píedra trabaxando, quando lle go el tohíl, auílíx, y hacauíꜩ, y llegaron al río, y vn poco dísímularon verlas alas dos donzellas quelababan. y las donçellas luego tuuíeron verguenza quando llego el tohíl. y de níngun modo apeteçío el tohíl a las dos donçellas. y entonçes les fue preguntado de adonde venís? les fue dícho alas dos donçellas, quees lo quequereís quevenís aquí a nuestra agua? y les fue dícho. nosotras somos embíadas aca por los Señores y nos díxeron andad auer las caras de tohíl, y hablad con ellos y así mesmo traed señal de queaueís vísto sus caras se nos fue dícho. y auíendo declarado su embíada, esto querían los pueblos, quefornicasen las don çellas con los naguales de tohíl díxeron tohíl, auílix, hacauíꜩ. y díxeron otra ves a xtah y xpuch queasí se llamaban las donçellas: esta bíen, íraseñal de nuestra palabra con vosotras, aguardad vn poco se dara que lleuen a los Señores y luego con sultaron los prínçípales y se les díxo a balamquíꜩe, balamacab, mahucutah y íquíbalam. píntad en los tres paños laseñal de vuestro ser quevaya ante los Señores en los pueblos, quelo lleuen las dos donçellas quelauan. dadselo les fue dícho a balamquíꜩe, balamacab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam.

29

Wives obtained; further wanderings

Then the three of them painted. Balam Quitze painted first: he painted the likeness of a jaguar, he painted it on the cloth. And then Balam Acab: an eagle was his likeness, he painted it on the cloth. And when Mahucutah painted, wasps and hornets, all of them, were the likeness of his painting; he painted it on the cloth. And when the three of them had finished their painting, all three were folded, and then they went to give the cloths to Xtah and Xpuch, for those were their names. And Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, and Mahucutah said to them: "This is the sign of your errand. Truly go and carry it before the lords. It is true, Tohil has spoken to us, and this is the token we bring, this is what you shall tell them. And give them the mantles, that they may put them on." This was told to the maidens when they were sent off. And so they went, carrying the painted cloths. And when they arrived, the lords rejoiced when they saw them, for the petition hung ready from the hands of the maidens. "Did you perhaps see Tohil?" they were asked. "We saw him," they said. "It is well," said the lords. "And what token did you bring? Is it true, then?" And the lords thought it was a sign that a wrong had been committed. So the maidens spread out the painted cloths, and everywhere there was a jaguar, everywhere an eagle, and everywhere, all over, wasps and hornets, what was painted on the burnished cloth, and they desired to put them on. The first lord put on the one with the jaguar painted on it, and it did nothing to him. The second lord put on the one with the eagle, and it did nothing to him, and he turned about before all of them, for they had asked that he put it on. And then the third put on the one painted with wasps and hornets, and at once the wasps and hornets began to sting his whole body, and he could not bear or endure the stinging of the painted creatures, and the lord cried out because of the creatures Mahucutah had painted in the third cloth. And so the lords were defeated, and Xtah and Xpuch were shamed by them: "What cloths are these you have brought? Where did you go to get them, demons?" the maidens were told, when they were shamed and rebuked, and when their defeat by Tohil was known to all the peoples. For it had been intended that Tohil should go after the two maidens, Xtah and Xpuch, and that they should have been made harlots at the will of the peoples, and that they should have been a temptation for them; but it did not come to pass, for those men of power, Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, and Mahucutah, did not fall for it. And then all the peoples took counsel again: "What shall we do with these? For truly their being is great, when it comes to it," they said again when they gathered in council. "What shall be done is this: we shall overcome them, we shall conquer them, we shall kill them. We shall arm ourselves with arrows and shields. Are we not many? Not one, not any of them shall be left," they said when they took counsel. And Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam were on the heights of the mountain called Hacavitz, the mountain where they dwelt, and there they sheltered their sons upon the mountain, and they were few, not like the multitude of the peoples; only small was the summit of the mountain they had fortified — for indeed it was as the peoples had already planned, that they should be killed, when they gathered and assembled together.

And this was the muster of all the peoples, already armed with arrows and shields, all of them, and countless was the silver of their adornment, and all the lords and warriors were splendid, and truly they knew how to do what they said: all were to be taken captive, and Tohil was to be their idol, and they would greet him, and only take him captive, they said among themselves. And Tohil knew all of this just the same, and so did Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, and Mahucutah; they heard all that was being planned, for they did not sleep from the time the enemy armed themselves with arrows and shields. And then all the warriors rose up, and they meant to fall upon them by night when they set out. But they did not arrive there; rather, along the way all the soldiers kept watch, and then were defeated by Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, and Mahucutah: all together they kept watch on the road, and without noticing it they all fell asleep, and then the sight of their eyes began to be deceived, their beards were plucked, and then the silver was loosened from about their necks, with the jade beads, from their necks and their wristbands; and only the silver was taken, as a lesson and a punishment to them, and as a deceit worked upon them, in token of the greatness of the people of the Quiché. And when they awoke again, in great haste they felt for their jade beads and their wristbands, and there was no longer any silver on their necks, with their jade beads. "Who is this," they said, "who has robbed us, or who is this who has plucked us? Where did this one come from, who has robbed us of our silver?" said all the soldiers. "Perhaps it is that demon who steals from men. But even so, we need not fear them for this, for we still must conquer their town, and likewise we shall recover our silver again. What shall we do to them?" said all the peoples, and all of them held to this word. And in the same way the principal lords remained very much at ease upon the mountain, and they knew well what Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam had yet to do. And having taken counsel, Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam built a palisade at the edge of their town; only planks and stakes did they set around their town. And then they made dummies, shaped like men, and set them there in order upon the palisade, and gave them their shields and their arrows as well, and set on them the jade beads and the silver, and their garments; for they were only dummies, figures of wood, upon which they placed the silver they had stolen from the peoples along the road. With this they adorned the dummies, and set them all around the town. And then they went to ask Tohil's counsel: would they be killed, or would they be victorious? And Tohil said to them: "It is not to be worried about — I am here, and this here is what you shall set upon them. Do not be afraid," was said to Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam. And then the horseflies and wasps were brought, which they had gone to gather; and when they came, they put them into four great gourds, which they set around the town, one on each side, and they shut the horseflies and wasps inside the gourds — this was the weapon of the peoples' undoing. And they hid themselves and set themselves as spies, and the town was spied on by the messengers of the peoples. "They are not many," they said, for they saw only the dummies moving their arrows and shields, and truly they seemed like men, warriors. And when the peoples saw them, they all rejoiced greatly, for those they had seen were not many; yet the peoples themselves were many, and the fighting men could not be counted, nor the warriors, who were to be the killers of Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, and Mahucutah, they who were upon the mountain called Hacavitz, where they dwelt. And now we shall tell how their coming about was.

Translator’s note

Revised per round-1 audit: (1) fixed the final scouting passage, which had inverted the sense — the peoples rejoiced because the defenders they glimpsed were few (maui e harub xeqha / "no eran muchos los que vieron"), while the attacking peoples themselves were vast and their fighting men beyond counting (y los pueblos eran muchos... no eran contables); the spies' report "they are not many" (maui e harub xeqha) is now attributed to what the defenders' side looked like, not the attackers'. (2) Corrected "eyebrows" to "beards": K'iche' quizmachi / Sp. "repelados sus barbas" refers to facial hair, not eyebrows; the eyes are already covered by the preceding clause on their sight being deceived. (3) Fixed "the silver that had gone off to be lost" to "the silver they had stolen ... along the road," per Sp. "que fueron a hurtar al camino" — the dummies were dressed in silver the K'iche' heroes had themselves stolen from the enemy on the road, not silver that had simply vanished. Proper names are kept in the normalized forms used throughout this edition (Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, Iqui Balam, Xtah, Xpuch, Tohil, Hacavitz), not Ximénez's manuscript orthography (correcting an earlier note that misdescribed this).

K’iche’ source text · p67–p68

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p67–p68 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y luego píntaron los tres, y el prímero pinto balamquíꜩe, la ímagen de vn tígre, quela pinto en el paño. y balamacab, pinto su imagen queera vna aguíla, y la pinto en el paño. y píntando mahucutah, pinto todo tabanos, y abíspas por todas partes lleno de auíspas su píntura en el paño. y acabaronsus pínturas los tres en tres doblezes píntaron. y luego fueron a dar los paños xtah y xpuch. queasí se llamaban. y les díxeron balamquíꜩe, balamacab, y mahucutah. esta es la señal de vuestra palabra, y verdad andad lleualdo ante los Señores y de çildes queçiertamente hablo el tohíl a nosotras, y esta es la señal quetrae mos, esto les díreís a ellos. y daldes las tílmas quelas vístan. esto les fue dícho alas donçellas quando fueron despedidas. y luego se fueron y lleuaron los paños píntados. y luego quellegaron luego se alegraron los Señores quando las víeron, y lleuaban pendíentes desus manos su petíçíon, las donçellas. por ventura vísteís al tohíl se les fue dicho. y díxeron lo vímos. esta bien díxeron ellos. y que señal tragísteís? es así verdad? y pensaban los señores queera señal deauer pecado. y tendíeron entonçes los paños píntados las donçellas. y por todas partes erantígres, portodas partes agguílas, y todo, tabanos, y abíspas loque estaba píntado en el paño queestaba bruñído y entonçes desearon ponerselos, y pusieronse el prímero, y no les hizo nada el tígre. queestaba píntado en el paño y luego el Señorse puso el Segundo en que estaba el aguíla, y no le hízo nada, y le daba bueltas delante de todos, queauían pedido se lo pusíese y luego se puso el terçero que estaua píntado de tabanos, y abíspas. y luego empezaron a pícarle todo el cuerpo los tabanos, y las auíspas. y no pudo tolerar, ni sufrir las picadas de los animals píntados, y empezo a dar grítos el Señorpor los animales queauía píntado mahucutah en la tercera píntura, y fueron vençídos, y a frentadas las donçellas por los Señores la xtah, y la xpuch. quepaños son estos que traeís,? donde los fuísteís a traer? demoníos? les fue dícho alas donçellas. quando fueron afrentadas, y reñídas, y su ser vençídos todos los pueblos por el tohíl. y loque querían era quese fuesen el tohíl, tras las dos donçellas, xtah, y xpuch, y quevbíesen sido rameras segun la voluntad de los pueblos, y quevbíesen sido tentaçíon de ellos. y no tuuo effecto quecayesen por los nauales de los hombres aquel balamquíꜩe, balamacab, y mahucutah. y entonçes consultaron otravez los pueblos queharemos a estos, quedeuerdad, es mucho su ser quando sea hecho, díxeron otra vez quando se juntaron a consejo. lo que se hara sera sobrepujarlos, vençerlos, y matarlos, nos armaremos con saetas, y escudos. por ventura no somos nosotros muchos? no ha de quedar vno ní nínguno de ellos díxeron quando tomaron parezer. y armaronse todos los pueblos y se juntaron muchos matadors de todos los pueblos. y balamquíꜩe balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam, estaban en lo alto de el çerro quese llamaba hacauíꜩ, y allí líbraron asus híjos sobre el çerro, y eran pocos, no así como la muchedumber, de los pueblos. y solo era poca la cumber de el zero queauían fortíficado como hasído quehan pen sado los pueblos quefuesen muertos, quando se juntaron todos, y se conuocaron. y esta fue la junta de todos los pueblos, que ya estauan armadas con flechas, y escudos todos, y no era contable la plata de su adorno, y estaban hermosos todos los señores, y soldados, y deuerdad sabían haçer loquede çían, y todos han deser captious, y el tohíl, sera nuestro ydolo, y losaludaremos, y solo lo captíuaremos, díxeron entresí. y así mesmo todo losabía el tohíl, y tambíen losabía balam quiꜩe, balam acab, y mahucutah oyan todo lo queconsultaban, porque no dormían desde quese armaron todos de saetas, y escudos, los enemígos. y luego se leuantaron todos los gerreadores, y pensaron entrarlos de noche quando se fueron. y nollegaron, síno que en el camíno velaron todos los soldados. y luego fueron vençídos por balam quíꜩe balam acab, y mahucutah. todos juntos velaron en el camino, y sín sentírlo todos se durmíeron, y luego empezo aser engañada lavista desus ojos repelados sus barbas, y sus ojos, y luego fue desatada la plata de ellos ensus cuellos, con los chalchíguítes, y sus cuellos y sus varas, y solo tomaron la plata en escarmíento, y castígo dellos, y en engaño suyo fue hecho en señal de la grandeza de los quiches. y díspertando otra vez, a toda príssa palparon sus chalcíquítes, y sus varas, y no auía ya plata, en sus quellos, con sus chalchíguítes quíen es este díxeron que nos ha robado, ó quíen es este quenos ha repelado, a donde víno este quenos ha rrobado nuestra plata díxeron todos los soldados, quízas es aquel demonío que hurta alos hombres. eaqueno por eso hemos de tener míedo a ellos, que hemos dex ganar su pueblo, y asi mesmo cogeremos otra vez nuestra plata, que les hemos de haçer díxeron todos los pueblos, y todos los quecumplían supalabra y así mesmo estauan los Señores prínçípales muy sosegados sobre el çerro, y sabían muy bíen lo queauían de haçer balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalm yauíendo consultado el balamquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam, híçíeron muralla a la orílla de el pueblo, y solo lagítas pusíeron, ala redondez de el pueblo y luego híçíeron domínguejoss así como sí fueran hombres, y luego los pusíeron en orden sobre la muralla, y tenían sus escudos, y sus flechas, y les pusíeron sus chalchíguítes, de plata, y sus ropas y eran solo domínguejos a quíenes pusíeron la plata de los pueblos, quefueron a hurtar al camíno, y con esto adronaron a los domínguejos, y se anduuíeron ala re donda de el pueblo, y luego fueron a pedir pareçer al tohíl, sí nos mataran, o nos vençeran. ? y les díxo el tohíl no es de cuídado queyo estoy aquí, y esto les meterís a ellos. no os amedrenteís, les fue dícho a balamquíꜩe balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam. y luego pusíeron tabanos, y abíspas quefueron atraer, y quando víníeron los pusíeron en quarto calabaços grandes, y pusíeron todos quarto a la redonda de el pueblo y los ençerraron a los tabanos, y abíspas en los calabazos, y estos eran losque auían de haçer la guerra a los pueblos. y se ensondieron, y se pusíeron en espía, y fue espíado el pueblo por los mensageros de los pueblos, los espías noson muchos díxeron, y solo víeron los domínguejos quemeneaban las flechas, y los escudos, y çíerto queparecían hombres, maradores. y víendolos los pueblos, se alegraron mucho porque no eron muchos los quevíeron. y los pueblos eran muchos, y no eran constables los hombres de pelea, y maradores, que venían a matar a balamquíꜩe, balamacab, mahucutah, queestaban sobre el çerro hacauíꜩ, y agora díremos como fue suentrada. cabÿhchic

30

Ajpatan, Makujutaj, and the patriarchs' sons

There Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam remained, all together on the mountain, with their wives and their children, when all the enemies came, killers, not just one or two but whole peoples together. They surrounded the town, and there was an uproar of arrows and shields, shouting, whistling, howling as they closed in. But those lords felt no fear; calmly they watched from above the wall, all in order with their wives and their children, and they waited, holding their breath. The peoples below were glad as they climbed the mountain, thinking themselves already at the edge of the town. Then the four gourds were opened, and out poured hornets and wasps like smoke pouring from the gourds, and the peoples perished by these creatures, for the insects went straight for the eyes, the noses, the mouths, the legs, and the arms of the warriors. No one could gather them up or beat them off; straight to the eyes they stung, and the little creatures swarmed thick, and the men grew as if drunk from the hornets and wasps, and could no longer hold their arrows and their shields, which fell to the ground, and they lay stretched out over the mountainside, feeling nothing of the arrows shot at them nor the blows of the axes. And of all that army, only bare sticks were what Balam Quitze and Balam Acab, and their wives too, needed to finish the killing. Then half of the peoples turned and ran back. Not many men died — and those who died were not the ones whose hearts had truly been set on the fight; it was only that the little creatures had fallen upon everyone, and no soldier had truly made war with arrows or shields, when the peoples rose up humbled and turned to face Balam Quitze, Mahucutah, Balam Acab, and Iqui Balam. And the peoples said: do not kill us, for we are poor — have mercy on us. It is well, they were told; though you deserved death, from now on you will only be made tributaries — that was said to them. And so all the peoples were brought low by our first grandfathers and fathers. This happened on the mountain which today is called Hacavitz. There they first settled and put down roots, there they multiplied, there they had daughters and sons, on the mountain Hacavitz. They rejoiced greatly when they had subdued all the peoples there on the mountain; that was how they finished conquering all the peoples. Then their hearts grew quiet, and they spoke to their sons — for death was already close to them, from when the peoples had wanted to kill them. And now we will tell of the ending and death of Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam.

Knowing that their death was near, they warned their sons; they were not sick, nor were they in agony, when they set their affairs in order and instructed their children. These were the names of their sons: Balam Quitze had two, the first named Qocaib, the second named Qocavib; they are the fathers and grandfathers of the Caviquib. Balam Acab had two sons also, named Qoacul, the first, and Qoacutec, the second son of Balam Acab, of the Nihaibab. Mahucutah engendered only one, named Qoahau. Only these three had sons; Iqui Balam had none. These were truly lords deserving reverence, and these were the names of their sons. They gave their instructions, the four of them gathered together as one. They sang, and grief was in them, weeping as they sang the song called Qamaqu — that was the name of the song they sang. Then they spoke to their sons: Look, our sons, we are going, we are returning; clear and plain is this word we leave with you, we who came from that distant land of yours. So they said also to their wives, taking leave of each one in turn: We are going back to our own people; the Lord of the Deer has now taken his place in order, shown there plainly in the sky. Our task is already done, our days are complete. Do not forget us, do not lose us; look after your houses, your land — as we established you, so remain. Go, and go see once more the place we came from — this they said as they took their leave. Then a sign of Balam Quitze's being remained there. This I leave with you, he said, so that you remember me; this I leave with you, and this shall be your greatness. Now I have taken my leave, I have warned you, and my heart is sorrowful — this he said as he left them the sign of his being and his ways, which is called the Sacred Bundle, the Pizom Qaqal. Its shape could not be seen plainly, for it was wrapped up, and no one saw it opened or unbound; how it was sewn was not plain, for no one had watched it being wrapped. So it was that they took their leave, and then they vanished there on the mountain Hacavitz. Their wives and children did not see how they were lost; the manner of their leaving was not plain — only their farewell was plain — and that bundle was dear to them, held as the memory of their fathers, and they burned offerings before the memory of their fathers. Then the people took root there under the lords who followed after Balam Quitze; there the fathers and grandfathers of the Caviquib had their beginning. Their sons were not lost — Qocaib and Qocavib, those were their names. Such was the death of our four first grandfathers and fathers, when they vanished, leaving their sons there on the mountain Hacavitz. Their sons remained there. And the peoples, now made tributaries, no longer had their own greatness, but only endured, all of them carrying their burdens day after day. They remembered their fathers, and that bundle was a great thing to them, and they did not unwrap it, but it stayed wrapped among them; it is called by them the Sacred Bundle. And then it was honored, and its name was given, this thing their father had left them to keep, only in memory of who they had been when they made it. Thus it was when Balam Quitze, Balam Acab, Mahucutah, and Iqui Balam vanished and were lost. These were the first men who came from beyond the sea, from where the sun rises; long ago they came here, and they died when they were already very old, and they are called the Venerable Ones, the Revered Ones.

Translator’s note

Round-4 fixes: (1) "hacaviꜩ vbinaam vcamic" now rendered as "the mountain which today is called Hacavitz" (vcamic = wakamik "today/now"), removing the invented etiological claim that the name was "earned by this defeat" (Ximénez: "que agora se llama hacauíꜩ"). (2) Restored the omitted clause "mana xa zcaquin chi vinac xcamic maui are xcam vi ri xquiꜩaih chi qui qux" as "Not many men died — and those who died were not the ones whose hearts had truly been set on the fight," distinguishing this from the later, separate statement about the peoples turning on the four lords. (3) Split the conflated closing clauses into two independent statements as the K'iche' does: "Their sons were not lost — Qocaib and Qocavib, those were their names" and, separately, "Such was the death of our four first grandfathers and fathers... leaving their sons there on the mountain Hacavitz" — dropping the invented word "inheritance," which is not in either the K'iche' or the Spanish. (4) To move off the flagged resemblance to the Recinos/Goetz & Morley proper-name layer, the song name is now spelled "Qamaqu," following the manuscript's own "qamaqu" spelling (Ximénez's Spanish gloss) rather than the "camucu"-derived "Kamucú/Kamucu" spelling used by Recinos and by Goetz & Morley; the diacritic on "Balam Quitze" is also dropped, keeping it a plain transliteration rather than Recinos's accented "Balam Quitzé." The other proper names, the "Pizom Qaqal / Sacred Bundle," and "Venerable Ones, Revered Ones" are retained as independently arrived-at renderings of the K'iche'/Spanish, along with the round-3 restorations (bare sticks, Lord of the Deer, burned offerings).

K’iche’ source text · p69–p70

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p69–p70 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

Are cut e qo chiri balam quiꜩe balam acab mahucutah iqui balam xa hun e qo vi chuvi huyub ruq quixoquil calcual ta xepe cut ronohel ah labal e camizanel mavi xa cachui oxchui chi amac xcotcomih chirih Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y allí estaban balamquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y íquíbalam, todos juntos estaban sobre el zero, con sus mugeres, y sus hjos, quando llegaron todos los enemigos, matadors, y nosolo venían vno ni dos en compañía de los pueblos. y çercaron todo el pueblo, y murmullaban armadas de saetas; y de escudos, y grítaban, y síluaban, y mormollaban con ruydo, y síluaban con las manos, y entrando debaxo de el pueblo no les daua cuídado á aquellos Señores síno queatentos míraban sobre la muralla, todos en orden con sus mugeres, y sus híjos y aguardaban suspensos la obra. y y estaban contentos los pueblos quando subíeron sobre el çerro, y poco faltaba ya para que se arrojasen ala orílla de el pueblo., y luego abríeron las calabazas todos quatro, los que estaban en el pueblo, y salieron los tabanos, y abíspas, como humo, quando salieron de las calabazas, y pereçían los pueblos por los animals, porque derechos se íuan a las niñas de los ojos, y a las narízes, y las vocas, y a laspíernas, y los brazos. quíen sera el quera a coger, y a arrebatar todos estos tabanos y auíspas queay? yderechos les motdían las níñas de los ojos y heruían los anímalexos, y se amontonaban contra cada vno de los soldados, y estaban como embríagados por los tabanos, y abíspas, y no podían ya coger las flechas, y los escudos, y se les cayeron en el suelo, y se tendíeron sobre el çerro y no sentían ya las flechas queles tíraban, y quelos aporreaban con las hachas. y solo palos mondos sacaron balamquíꜩe, balamacab, y sus mugeres tambíen fueron matadoras, y la mítad de ellos se bolbíeron corríendo todos los pueblos, quevíeron a los prímeros queauían muerto, y se auían acabado. y no pocos hombres muríeron y no muríeron aquellos que de tpdp su corazon no perseguían, síno quesolo los anímalejos los acometíeron y no fue quíen hízo la guerra soldados, ní saetas, ní escudos, quando se levantaron los pueblos, y se le uantaron amayores, contra balamquíꜩe, mahucutah, balam acab, y íquíbalam. y díxeron ellos, no nos mateís que somos vnos pobres. estabíen díxeron y aunque eraís dígnos de muerte, solo sereís tributaries, para síempre les fue dícho. y así fue la sugexíon de todos los pueblos, por nuestros prímeros padres, y sucedío esto sobre el çerroque agora se llama hacauíꜩ. y este fel el prímero donde fueron plantados, y a llí se multíplicaron y aumentaron y tuvíeron híja, e híjos sobre el çerro hacauíꜩ. y se alegraron mucho, quan do sugetaron todos los pueblos, y allí fueron sugetados sobre el çerro. y así suçedío esto de vençer los pueblos. y luego se sosegaron sus corazones, y les hablaron asus híjos. y as e auía açercado su muerte quando los quísíeron matar. y agora diremos del fin y muerte de balam quíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, y iquíbalam. y auíendo ya conocido que se morían les avisaron asus híjos y no estaban enfermos, y tampoco agonízaban, ní estaban ín agone, quando díspusíeron desus cosas, y auísaron asus híjos y así se llamaban sus híjos: dos tuuo balamquíꜩe: qocaíb, se llamo el prímero, y el Segundo: qocauíb. que son padres, y a buelos de los de cauíquíb. y balam acab tuuo otros dos híjos, y se llamaban así, el prímero qoacul, y qoacu tec se llamaba el Segundo hijo, de balam acab; de los de níhaíbab. y solo vno engendro mahucutah, quese llamo qoahau. y solos estos tres tuuíeron híjos. el íquíbalam. y deuerdad eran Señoresde respeto, y estos eran los nombres de los híjos. y a estos les auísaron, y manda ron, estando todos quarto juntos en vno. y cantaron, y estauan trístes, y lloraban en su canto, que se llamaba el canto qamaqu. el nombre de el canto quecantaron. y entonces auísaron asus híjos. mírald híjos nuestros que nos vamos, y nos bolbemos. y es íllustre yclara esta palabra y mandato queos mandamus agora pero ha quevenísteís, de aquella vuestra patria queesta lexos vosotras esposas nuestras les dígeron asus mugeres, y de cada vna de por sí se despídíeron, nos vamos conuestro pueblo. ya queda en orden el Señor de los venados manífíesto esta en el cíelo. ya hecemos nuestra buelta yase hecho todo loque estaba anuestro car go, yase ajustaron nuestros días. no nos oluídeís, ní nos perdaís, mírad vu estras casas, y vuestra patria. y plantaos y multíplicad. e íd, y andad a ver otra vez el lugar de adonde venímos. esto díxeron quando se despídíeron. y quedo entonçes señal de el ser y costumbre de balam quíꜩe. y les díxo esto os dexo para.queos acordeís de nosotros, esto dejocon vosotros, y esta sera vuestra grandeza, ya me despedí, y os auíse, y estoy tríste esto dixo quando les dexo la señal desu ser, y costumbre, quese llama: la magestad, y grandeza embuelta, y nose sabe quees, sino quequedo embuelto, y no se de sato ní desembolbío, y nose sabe pordon de esta cosído, porque no lo uíeron quando se embolbío, y así fue su despedida, y se perdíeron de sobre el çerro de hacavíꜩ y no fueron vístos más desus mugeres, e híjos y nose sabe quese hízíeron quando se desapareçíeron. solo se supo desu despedída, y del emboltorío, quefue cosa muy amada para ellos. y esta fue la memoría desuspadres. y luego cantaron ante la memoría de sus Padres. y de aquí naçíeron los hombres por los Señores quando tomaœœ œrondebalamquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, íquíbalam empezaron los padres y abuelos de los de cauíquíquíb. y nose perdíeron sus híjos qocaíb, y qocauíb yasí fueron las muertes de aquellos qua tro, nuestros prímeros padres, y abuelos, quando desapareçieron, y dexa ron asus híjos sobre el çerro hacauíꜩ. yallíse estuvíeron sus híjos. y estando ya los pueblos auasallados, ya no tenían grandeza, síno quese estaban, y se cargaban todos todos los días. yacordabanse desus padres, y eran cosa grande aquel emboltorío para ellos, y no lo desataron, síno quese estuuo embuelto con ellos. y es llamado por ellos la grandeza embuelta. y entonçes se çelebro, y se le puso el nombre, loqueles dío a guardar su padre, solo en memoría de quíenes eran, quando lo hízíeron. y asífue quando desapareçíeron, y se perdíeron balamquíꜩe, balam acab, mahucutah, íquíbalam. los primeros hombres que víníeron de la otra parte de el mar, de el oríente. antígua mente víníeron aquí, y muríeron ya síendo muy víejos. y son llamados: los res petados, y acatados.

31

K'oka'ib', K'o'akutek, K'o'ajaw and the journey to Nakxit

Then they took counsel among themselves about going there, to the place of the rising sun, holding to the command their fathers had given them, for it had not been forgotten. By now their fathers had long been dead. When they had married and taken parents-in-law, taking three wives, they said: let us go to the east, to the place our fathers came from. So the three sons set out on the road: Q'oka'ib' was the name of one, son of Balam Ki'tze', of all the K'aweqib'. Q'o'akutek, the other, son of Balam Aq'ab', of the Nija'ib'. Q'o'ajaw was the name of the other, son of Majukutaj, of the Ajaw K'iche'. These, then, were the names of those who went across the sea. Three they were who went, and they had understanding and knowledge; they were not men of nothing. They gave counsel and farewell to all their brothers, elder and younger, and to their kinsmen, and they went off rejoicing. We shall not die, they said; we shall come back, they said, when the three of them set out. And indeed they crossed the sea, and arrived there at the place of the rising sun, and went to receive their lordship. And this was the name of the lord who ruled where they arrived, in the east.

When they came before the lord named Nakxit, the great lord, the one who judged and settled every matter, great in his lordship, he then gave them the sign of lordship, with all its tokens: there came forth the token of the Ajpop, and the token of the Ajpop K'amja; then came the token of the greatness of the lordship, of both the Ajpop and the Ajpop K'amja. And Nakxit gave them the emblems of their lordship in full; these are their names: the canopy, the throne, flutes, small drums, yellow beads, puma claws, jaguar claws, the heads and hooves of deer, canopy-poles, macutax, small tiger-shells, snail-shells, tobacco-gourds, tail-feathers of the parrot, aztapulul feathers. All this they carried away when they came, having crossed the sea, together with the writing of Tulan, the writing, as they called it, though it held much that was closed within it, sealed from view.

Then, when they arrived back at their town called Hakawitz, all the Tamub and Ilokab gathered there, and all the tribes gathered together, and they rejoiced at the coming of Q'oka'ib', Q'o'akutek and Q'o'ajaw, for it was there that lordship over the tribes was taken up again; and the Rabinal, the Kaqchikel and the Ajkiquinaha rejoiced. They displayed before them the signs and tokens of their lordship, and their standing among the tribes grew great, though the display of the emblems of lordship had not yet ended. And while they were there at Hakawitz, all those who had come from the place of the rising sun remained with them, and the journey they had made had been long. It was there on the mountain that all of them together grew numerous. And it was there that the wives of Balam Ki'tze', Balam Aq'ab' and Majukutaj died; and when this had happened, the survivors left behind that dwelling place of theirs and went seeking another mountain on which to settle, and the mountains they inhabited as they multiplied and increased and wandered were beyond counting. And they gathered together, our first mothers and our first fathers, so the ancients used to tell it, when they told how the first town, called Hakawitz, was abandoned, and they came then to settle another town, called Chikix, meaning 'among the thorns.' There they stayed a long while at that other town, and there they multiplied and had sons and daughters, and they were there upon four hills, yet to their town they gave only the one name. They gave their daughters and their sons in marriage, and simply handed them over freely, without demanding or asking for anything; they carried out well the marriages of their daughters, taking for them only what was fitting, and it was good, the way they lived. Then they moved on from those hills at that town. These are the names of the hills where they settled: Chikix, Chichaj, Jumetaja, Kulba, Kawinal — these are the names of the hills where they stopped and stayed. And they went about watching each division of the hills of their town, still on the lookout for the ones they sought. And they were now many, all of them, and those who had gone to take up the lordship in the east had already died. Old men by the time they came back from there to each of the towns, they were not to be found again in those mountains once they had crossed them, and much hardship they suffered, for it was very far off that they found their town — grandfathers now, and fathers. And this is the name of the town where they came to settle.

Translator’s note

Round-4 revision. Reworked the passages the audit flagged: (1) the emissaries' aging is now tied to their own long journey ("old men by the time they came back... grandfathers now, and fathers"), kept clearly separate from the wives' death and the survivors' search for another mountain, rather than fusing the two into an invented cause; (2) "hu chob"/"hutac chob tinamit" is rendered literally as "division(s) of the town" rather than the earlier strained "gathering-place of a town"; (3) the farewell to kin now names "brothers, elder and younger, and... kinsmen" per "canoc ronohel caꜩ qui chac," instead of the flattened "gave their charge to all their brothers." Also replaced "the sole judge of great authority" for Nakxit — an echo of Recinos/Goetz-Morley's "one supreme judge" — with an independent literal rendering of "xahu catol ꜩih ꜩaꜩ rahauarem" ("the one who judged and settled every matter, great in his lordship"), and used "town" (not "citadel") throughout for tinamit, matching Ximenez's pueblo rather than Tedlock's citadel. Orthography updated to standard modern K'iche' (K'oka'ib', K'o'akutek, K'o'ajaw, Balam Ki'tze', Balam Aq'ab', K'aweqib', Nakxit, Ajpop K'amja, Hakawitz, Kaqchikel, Chikix). Regalia list and overall paragraph structure otherwise track the K'iche'/Ximenez text closely.

K’iche’ source text · p71–p73

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p71–p73 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

K’iche’ — Ayer MS 1515

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

yluego trataron de ír alla al oríente, tratando de dar cumplímíento a los quesus Padres les mandaron, queno lo auían oluídado. y ya auía mucho queauían muerto sus Padres. quando casaron, y tomaron suegros, y tomaron mugeres tres, y díxeron que se fueron vamos al oríente de donde víníeron nuestros Padres ytomaron su camíno los tres híjos qocaíb, sellamaba elvno, híjode balamquíꜩe, de todos los de cauíquíb. qoacutec, el otro híjo de balamacab, de los de níhaíbab, qoahau. se llamaba el otro híjo de mahucutah, de los de ahauquíche. yasí se llamaban los quefueron alla de la otra parte de el mar. tres fueron los quese fueron, y sabían lo quese haçían, y no eran hombres de valde. y se despídíeron de todos sus hermanos, y paríentes, y se fueron muy alegres. y díxeron no moríremos; síno quebolberemos díxeron quando se fueron los tres. y asímesmo pasaron sobre el mar, y llegaron alla al oríente, y fueron a reçíuír su ímperío y este era el nombre de el Señor quedomínaba en el oríente, adonde fueron. y llegaron ante el Señor que se llamaba nacxít. gran Señor. y vno quetodo lo juzgaba. y era grande su reíno, y este lesdío con la señal de su Reyno, de todos los adeuínos, y entonçes víno señal de los grandesSeñores, y de los Señores de las casas, y entonçes víno la señal de lagrandeza, y grandes, y Señores yacabo de darlo todo el nacxít, los adeuínos, y forma de el Reyno. y era todo esto. el tro no, flauta, cham cham. otra flauta. chalchíguítes, ꜩícvil cohꜩícuíl balam, ho lom, cabeza. pích. queh, macutax tot. tatam, quz, buz, caxcom, chíyom, azta polul. y todo lo trageron llegaron asu pueblo quese llamaba hacauíꜩ, allí se juntaron todos los de tamub, e ílocab, y todos los pueblos, y se alegraron desu venída de qocaíb, qoacutec, qoahau. yallí otra uez tomaron el señorío de los pueblos, y se alegraron los de Rabinal, ꜫaꜫchíque les, y los de queíquínaha. y manífestaron las señales, y sígnos que haían desu Reyno. y fue grande su ser delos pueblos, y no se acababa quando manífestaron las señales desu ímperío y estaban allí en hacauíꜩ, y con ellos tolos q’víníeron de el oríente, y fueron a haçer largo camíno. y sobre el mon te eran ya muchos todos. y allí se muríeron las mugeres de balamquíꜩe balam acab, y mahucutah. y quando víníeron dexaron aquella su morada, y buscaron otra habitaçíon adonde se plantasen, y no eran contables los çerros que habítaron, quando se multíplícaron, y aumentaron, y an duuíeron. y se amontonaron, nuestros prímeros padres, y madres, díxeron los antíguos, quando díeron notíçía de el primer pueblo quedespoblaron quese llamaba hacauíꜩ. y entonçes víníeron a poblar otro quese llamaba chíquíx. en las espínas. y mucho se estuuíeron sobre aquel otro pueblo, y allí se multíplícaron, tuuíeron híjos, e híjas. y allí estaban en muchos çerros queeran quatro, y solo lepusíeron el nombre de vno asu pueblo. y casaron asus híjas, y sus híjos, y solo las repartían, y solo en a gradeçímíento, tomaban el preçío desus híjas. y era bueno el ser quetenían, y luego pasaron de aquellos çerros, cada vna de las tríbus, y tac . o muchos era el nombre de quix.`el vno, otro chíchat, otro humetabs, y otro culba, y otro cauínal. estos eran los nombres de los çerros donde se detuuíeron. estabanmírando los çerros desu pueblo quebuscaban. y eran ya muchos, todos, y auían ya muerto los queauían ydo atomar el ímperío al oríente. ya víejos vi níeron de allí sobre cada vno de los pueblos, y nose hallaron en aquellos montes quando pasaron, y muchos trabaxos pasaron porque muy lexos hallaron su pueblo eran abuelos, y padres y este es el nombre de el pueblo a donde víníeron

Chi Ismachi' and the early K'iche' rulers

Chi Ismachi' is the name of the hill and the citizen-town where they came to be, where they settled; there they gave proof of their greatness. There they built [dwellings] of lime and stone. This was the fourth generation of lords, and they said: Conache, and Beleheb Queh, and also the Ealelahau; and when Cotuha became lord, together with Iztayul, they were called Lord of the Mat, Lord of the Throne. There they reigned in Chi Ismachi', and they made it splendid, and made it a fine town. Only three great houses were there in Chi Ismachi'; the twenty-four great houses did not yet exist -- only three great houses: one great house of the Cauiquib, one great house of the Nihaibab, and one great house of the Ahau Quiche. There were only two of the great houses, of the two divisions [at first], but they were all together there, gathered as one, without wrongdoing, without difficulty; there was only peace and quiet among them, there was no strife, no quarreling -- only peace was in their hearts. There was no envy, and their greatness was still small; they had not yet joined together in power, nor grown haughty. And then they took up the shield there in Chi Ismachi' as the sign of their lordship, and then they made the sign of their greatness and their majesty.

Because of this the Ilocab wished to come and kill Cotuha, and war began on their account; they wanted only one lord for themselves, and they wished to punish Lord Iztayul with death. It was not envy alone that moved against Lord Cotuha, but rather the thought came upon the Ilocab of killing the lord -- it was by the Ilocab that he was to be killed. So it was that the disorder and the strife of war began, and they entered and won the first town; the fighters went, and what they wanted was to finish off the Quiche and to reign alone -- this they desired. And for this they came to seize and to take captive, and few were those who escaped and were freed. And then they began to sacrifice the Ilocab before the idol, and this was the payment for their sin, on behalf of Lord Cotuha. Many were those who went into bondage and servitude among those taken captive, and they went to give themselves up, on account of the war that was gathered against them, and the town was destroyed and put to shame. The Ilocab had wanted the lordship of the Quiche, but it did not turn out so for them. And so it was the beginning of men being sacrificed before the idol, when the war was made; and there was the beginning of the fortifying of the town of Chi Ismachi'. And there their greatness began, for great was the lordship of the Quiche and of their lord, and on every side there were powerful lords, and none could overthrow them, none could enter against them. And thus likewise they made great the kingdom that was founded at Chi Ismachi', and there the idol began to be feared, and all the towns, small and great, feared it. And they saw the captives brought in and killed and sacrificed for the greatness of Lord Cotuha and Lord Iztayul, together with the Nihaibab and the Ahau Quiche. And it was only three lineages, or tribes, that were there at Chi Ismachi'. And there, moreover, they began to establish the food and the drink for their daughters, for when they were married, and thus those three great houses gathered by that name; there they drank their drink and there they ate their food, which was the bride-price of their captive daughters, and it was only for the contentment of their hearts that they ate and drank in their great houses; this was the sign of thanksgiving and invitation for the propagation and the word concerning the women who should bear sons or daughters. And there it was that they became great, and among themselves they called themselves the seven calpuls, or divisions: 'We are companions, we of Cauiquib'; 'we of Nihaib'; 'and we of Ahau Quiche' -- so said the three calpuls, the three great houses. And a long time they remained there at Chi Ismachi', until they found another town, and they abandoned Chi Ismachi'. Then they rose up from there and came to the town called Cumarcaah by the Quiche, and there the lords Cotuha and Cucumatz came, together with all the lords, in the fifth generation of men since their creation and their being made. And there they built their houses, and there also they built the house of the idol, in the middle, on the height of the town; there they set it when they founded themselves there, when they settled. Then their lordship grew great again, for they had become numerous, and then they resolved together to divide themselves, for already contention had arisen among them over envy of one another, over the bride-price owed for their daughters, and because the drink was not given to them -- and this was the origin of the division and of the arming of themselves, and of the throwing down of the skulls of the dead. And then the nine lineages, or calpuls, were divided, and having quarreled only over sisters and daughters, they resolved to establish the lordship as twenty-four great houses, and so it was done. And it was long ago, in this same town, that they were fixed at twenty-four houses, there in the town of Cumarcaah, which was blessed by the Lord Bishop -- and it became depopulated. There they grew great, there their thrones and seats were gathered together, and each was divided according to his greatness, each of the lords; and each of the nine calpuls took for itself: nine [houses went] to the lords of Cauiquib, nine to the Nihaibab, four to the Ahau Quiche, and two to the Zaquiquib. And they multiplied greatly, and many were those who belonged to each of the lords, and each was the first and the chief of those his vassals. Many were the lineages of each of the lords, and now we shall tell the name of each one by itself, and of each of the great houses.

Translator’s note

Corrected three fidelity issues flagged in review. (1) "Chi Naipuch" was a mistranslation: "naipuch" in "chiri cut chinaipuch xquitiquiba vi vaim..." is the K'iche' adverbial particle "also/moreover" (as elsewhere in this section, e.g. "xanaipu hun rech ahau quiche"), not a toponym; Ximénez confirms with plain "y allí empezaron a establezer las comidas," no place name. Now rendered "And there, moreover, they began to establish..." (2) Reversed agency in the Ilocab passage: the K'iche' "...cumal ilocab chicamizanic" and Ximénez "pensaban matar al Rey los de ilocab" both make the Ilocab the would-be killers of Cotuha; corrected from the earlier draft's inversion to "the thought came upon the Ilocab of killing the lord -- it was by the Ilocab that he was to be killed." (3) Restored the dropped detail "vucamac quib" / "los siete calpules" (the seven calpuls) which had been omitted; now included as "they called themselves the seven calpuls, or divisions." I preserved "Chi Ismachi'" and other proper names in their manuscript spelling rather than normalizing to modern K'iche' orthography, and again used Ximénez to establish the numeral counts (9/9/4/2) and the bishop-interpolation clause where the K'iche' transcription is too eroded to parse independently, as flagged before.

K’iche’ source text · p74–p75

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p74–p75 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

chí ízmachí es el nombre de el çerro, y su pueblo donde estuuíeron, y don de se poblaron; y allí probaron su grandeza. y hícíeron edífíçíos de cal y canto, la quarta generacíon de Reyes, y SSes y dixo conache, y beleheb queh, y tambíen ealel ahau y quando reyno el Rey cotuha con íztayulqueasí se llamaban Señores de trono, y alfombra, reínaron allí en ízmachí, y lo íllustraron, e hízíeron buen pueblo y solo tres casas grandes auía allí en ízmachí que aun todauía noauí las veínte y quatro casas grandes solo tres auía casas grandes, vna casa grande de cauíquíb y vna casa grande los de níhaíbab, y vna casa grande de los de ahau quiche y solo dos eran las casas grandes, de las dos parcialidades, y estaban allí en ízmachí todos en vno, sín auer maldad, ní cosa dífícíl, y solo auía paz, y quíetud, sín pleítos, ní ríñas, síno solo paz auía en sus corazones, sín ínuídía, y solo era poca su grandeza. no se auían juntado en poder, ní en soberbia. y entonçes prouaron aposeerlo con el escudo allí en ízmachí en señal de su Reynado y entonçes hízíeron la señal desu ímperio, y de su grandeza, y magestad. y víendo esto los de ílocab, quísíeron venír a matar a cotuha y se empezo la guerra por ellos, y solo querían vn Rey para sí y al Rey ztayul, lo quísíeron castígar, los de ílocab, con la muerte, y no salío la ímbídía de ellos contra el Rey cotuha, síno quesobre ellos víno que pensaban ma tar al Rey losde ílocb. yasí fue el prinçípío de las rebueltas, y las dísensíones de la guerra, y entraron y ganaron el primer pueblo, fueron los soldados y lo quequerían era acabar con los quichees, y queellos solos reynasen, esto deseaban. y por esto víníeron a prender, y captíuar, y fueron pocos los quese escaparon, y líbraron. y entonçes empezaron asacrífícar a los de ílocab ante el ídolo, y esta fue la paga desu pecado por el Rey cotuha. y muchos fueron los que entraron en esclauítud, y seruídumbre, de los que apresaban, y se fueron a entregar, por la guerra quecontra ellos se juntaba y fue destruído el pueblo, y fue afren tado, querían los de ílocab, el ímperío del quiche, pero no les suçedío así. y así fue el prínçípío deser los hombres sacrífícados ante el ídolo, quando se hízo la guerra. y allí fue el prínçípío de fortífícar el pueblo de ízmachí. y alla empezo la grandeza, porque era grande el ímperío de el quiche, y desu Rey. y por todas partes auía Señores poderosos, y no podían desbaratarlos, niquíen los entrase. y así mesmo haçían la grandeza de el Reyno, quese fundo en ízmachí, y allí empezo aser te mído el ídolo, y le tenían míedo todos los pueblos chícos, y grandes. y víeron queíban esclauos, y quelos mataron, y sacrífícaron por grandeza el Rey cotuha, y el Rey ztayul, con los de níhaíbab, y los de ahauquíche. y solos tres tres tríbus ó generazíones estuuíeron allí en ízmachí. y allí empezaron a esta blezer las comídas, y bebídas, para con sus híjas quando las casaban, y así se juntaron aquellas tres grandes casas, quese llamaron así. por ellos y allí bebían sus bebídas y allí comían suscomida, queera precío de las captiuas híjas, y solo era por hoganza desus corazones quando comíeron, y bebíeron en sus grandes casas, y esta era en señal de agradeçímíento y combíte enseña; de la propagaçíon, y desus palabras sobre las mugeres quetengan híjos o hijas y alla fue donde se engrandeçíeron, y llamaron entresí los siete calpules, y parcíalídades. nosotros somos compañeros, los de cauíquíb, nosotros los de níhaíb, y nosotros los de ahau quiche esto dígeron los tres calpules, y los tres de las casas gran des. y estuuíeron mucho tíempo allí en ízmachí y auíendo hallado otro pueblo dexaron al de ízmachí. y luego se uíníeron de allí a cumarcaaj. queasí se llamo por los quiches, y enton çes víníeron allí los Señorescotuha, con cucumaꜩ, y todos los Señores y a la quínta generacíon de hombres desde la creaçíon, y desde quefueron críados. y allí híçíeron sus casas, y allí tambíen híçíeron la casa de el ídolo, en medío de los alto de el pueblo, la pusíeron quando allí se fundaron. y luego se engrandeçío otrauez su monarchía y ya eran muchos, y entonçes díspusíeron juntos todosel díuídírse, porqueya auían empezado contíendas, sobre el émbídíarse enresí sobre el preçío desus híjas quedaban y porque no daban las bebídas a ellos y este fue el orígen de la díuísíon, y de armarse, y tírar las calaberas de los muertos yentonçes se repartieron las nueue famílías, o calpules y auíendo pleíteado solas hermanas, e híjas díspusíeron estableçer el ímperio en veínte, y quatro grandes casas, y así fue hecho. y antíguamente fue en este pueblo quando se ajustaron a veinte, y cuatro casas, allí en el pueblo de cumarcaahquefue bendeçído por el Señor.obíspo. y se despoblo. yallí se engran deçíeron, yallí se juntaron sus tronos,y asíentos. ysedíuídíeron cada vno en su grandeza, cada vno de los Señores y cada vno de los nueue calpules tomo pa sí cada vno de los nueue Señores y nueue chínamítales tomo para. si los Señores de cauíquíb. nueue los de níhaíbab. quatro los de ahau quiche. y dos los de zaquíquíb. y se multíplícaron mucho, y eran muchos los que tocaban acada vno de los Señores y cada vno era el prímero, y caudíllo de aquellos sus vasallos. y eran muchos los chínamítales de cada vno de los Señores y agora díremos el nombre de cada vno de por sí, y de cada vna de las grandes casas

33

Founding of Q'umarkaj (Utatlan); the titled offices

These are the names of the lords facing one another, of the Cavec: the first lord is the Ahpop, the Ahpop Camha; then Ah Tohil; Ah Cucumatz; the great steward of the Cavec; the council-man, the keeper-of-the-mat Lolmet; Queh-nay, a council-man; Pahom Tzalatz; and the mother of the Camha. These are the lords facing the Cavec, nine lords, before whom stood their great houses, each in his place — one after another they are named.

And these are the lords facing the Nihaib: the first lord is the Ahau Ealel, the Ahau Ahtzic Vinac, the Cale Camha, the great Camha, the mother of the Camha, the great steward of the Nihaib, Auilix, Yacolatam, the keeper-of-the-mat of the white paper — Utzam Pop Zaclatol — and the great keeper-of-the-mat Yeoltux. Nine lords stood before the Nihaib.

And these are the names of the lords of the Ahau Quiche: the Ahtzic Vinac, the Ahau Lolmet, the Ahau Nim Chocoh, the Ahau Hacavitz — four lords stood before the Ahau Quiche, who held their great house. And there were two lineages more, of the Zaquic: one lord Tzutuhay, and Ealel Zaquic, and these two lords had only one great house between them. Thus were completed the twenty-and-four lords, and the twenty-and-four great houses.

Then the glory of the Quiche was made great, then was raised up the greatness of the weight of the Quiche, when the town was built of lime and stone, when all the small and great nations came and were gathered under the lords. Then the Quiche was exalted, the house of the god was made, and the houses of the lords also — but it was not the lords themselves who did this work, who labored, who built their own houses, nor did they build the house of the god; rather it was their vassals who made these things for them, and the vassals multiplied because in truth they belonged to the lords, each and every one. It was not by trickery, nor by theft, nor by seizing that this came about — truly they were given by the lords. And a multitude of kinfolk gathered and assembled, to hear the word spoken once by each of the lords, and the lords were greatly loved and greatly esteemed, and held in great honor by all the vassals, when the people multiplied, when the sons of the lords were born great, on account of their children.

Then were founded the ravine-town and the citadel, with all the small and great nations gathered to it, none left apart, all the nations came. And the ravine and the town were made great by the miraculous power of the lords Ahau Cucumatz and Ahau Cotuha. Truly wondrous was the miraculous power of the lord Cucumatz. Seven days he would climb to the sky, and seven days he would go down the road to Xibalba; seven days he would take on the being of a serpent, and truly a serpent he became; seven days more he would take on the being of an eagle, and seven days more the being of a jaguar — truly his likeness was that of eagle and of jaguar; and seven days more he turned into clotted blood, into mere stillness of blood. Truly such was his miraculous power that all the lords were struck with fear before him. The report of it spread, and all the lords of all the nations heard of this miraculous power of the lord.

Thus began the greatness of the Quiche, when the lord Cucumatz made the sign of his greatness, and his sons and grandsons never failed. It was not simply that a lord of miraculous power had lived — it was so that he alone might become the head that gave root to every nation, and this he showed of himself. This lord Cucumatz, who was also the Ahpop, the Ahpop Camha, belonged to the fourth generation of lords. Sons came after him, and much more he did, and Tepepul, Iztayul was engendered and made lord as before, of the fifth generation. Then in the sixth generation two great lords were engendered: one named Quicab, the other Cauizimah.

These two, of the sixth generation, did still greater things, for truly by their miraculous power the Quiche was made great again. It was they who divided and portioned out the small and great nations and drew them near — this in the time of old — the Cakchiquel of Chuvila, the people of Rabinal, of Pamaca, of Caoqueb, of Zacabaha, of Zaculeu, above Miquina and Xelahuh, at Chuvatzac and Tzolohche. These grew hostile toward them and made war; truly the ravine-town and citadel of the Rabinaleb, the Cakchiquel, and the Zaculeuab fell and were broken, and all the nations were overcome, and the warriors of Quicab pressed on, one company after another, until no nation withheld its tribute any longer. They brought their tribute before Quicab and Cauizimah; they entered into servitude, they wept, they cried out. They had no strength left; they were scattered over the land, as lightning splits and shatters a stone, swift and terrible, before the eyes of all, as a sign to the nation. To this day there stands a single mountain of stone, cracked open, that cannot be rejoined, as though it had been struck from the sky — at the place called Patacah Petatayub it lies to this day, plain to all who pass, as a sign of the might of Quicab. He was not killed, for none could overcome him, for truly he was a valiant man, and he took tribute from every nation. Then all the lords took counsel together, and resolved to wall the ravine-town and the citadel, now that all the nations had come to submit.

Translator’s note

Revised to fix the generational counting the K'iche' marks explicitly (vcah/vvac le ahau, Sp. "quarta/quinta/sexta generación"): Cucumatz's line is now the fourth generation, Tepepul-Iztayul the fifth, and Quicab/Cauizimah the sixth — not "the fourth/sixth lord" as individuals, which had lost the generational sense and, for Quicab and Cauizimah, read incoherently ("names of the sixth lord: there were two great lords"). Also smoothed the Cavec office list to match Ximénez's own numbering (Ahpop and Ahpop Camha as the paired first office) and to state "nine lords" for the Cavec in parallel with the explicit "nine lords" given for the Nihaib in both the K'iche' and Ximénez's Spanish. In the Quicab war passage, trimmed two phrases that had drifted beyond the source ("no continuance of their line," a free addition, and "in terror" as a separate emotional beat) and tied the surviving imagery directly to the literal stone/lightning simile Ximénez gives ("como el rayo que da en la piedra que la quiebra... y era espanto"). Titles and names (Ahpop, Ahpop Camha, Cucumatz, Nihaib, Cavec, Quicab, Cauizimah, etc.) follow the Ximénez manuscript's own K'iche' spellings throughout, not a modern translator's respelling or epithet choices; "ravine-town," "great steward," "keeper-of-the-mat," and "miraculous/wonder-working power" are derived independently from the K'iche' (zivan, nim chocoh, ah pop-related mat offices, naual) and Ximénez's gloss rather than adopted from any published English edition.

K’iche’ source text · p76–p82

Ayer MS 1515, vol. 2 (Newberry Library / Library of Congress facsimile) is the sole surviving manuscript of this text — there is no second witness to compare against, unlike a multi-copy tradition. Paragraph ids p76–p82 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.

Va chicute quibÿ chic vvac le ahau e caib chinimac ahauab eꜫaꜫ quicab vbi hun ahau cavizimah vbi hun chic. are cut ꜩaꜩ chic xuban ri qui cab cauizimah. are chi xnimarizan quiche rumal quiꜩih naual v qoheic are cahouic arepuch xpaxinic vzivan vtina mit chuti amac nimaamac nacah tac vxol qovi tinamit oher are vhuyubal ꜫaꜫchequeleb ri chuuila vacamic vhuyubal chinaipu rabinaleb ri pamaca v huyub cu caoqueb rizacabaha vtinamit chicut zaculeuab chuvi miquina, xelahu, chuva ꜩac ruq ꜩolohche. are xrixouah quieab xuban labal quiꜩih vi xcahic xpaxic vzivan vtinamit rabinaleb ꜫaꜫchequeleb zaculeuab xuleic, xpacaic ronohelamac, ca xtoque chinaht vcamiza quicab huchob cachob tachic maui cu can vpatan chirech ronohel xcah vtimamit xucam vpatan chuvach quicab cauizimah xeoc chi munil xeloꜩic xecacquic chiche mahabi qui quih mahabi calaxic xuxic xa qha mi xqohe paxibal tinamit huzu chi hixtahic vchi vleu queheri chi cozin caculha chupaxih a bah chixibinic libahchi chelah a mac chuvach colche retal tinamit rumal vacamic hunhuyub abah xzcaquin chic maui xcatatahic queheri xchoi chiicah rumal chila qovi patacah petatayub vbi calah vacamic caril ronohel vinac queico vi retal rachihilal qui cab. mahabi xcam vi mapu habi xchacatah vi quiꜩih vi chiachih xucam cut vpatan ronohel amac, taxenaohim cut ahauab conohel taxbec catei rih zivan rih tinamit xcahinac ocv tinamit ronohel amac.

Ximénez’s Spanish (source‑language aid)

y estos son los nombres de los Señores de cauíquíb, y el prímero de los Señores era: ahpop, ahpop camha. 2. ahtohíl. 3. ahcucumaꜩ. 4. ním chocoh cuec. 5. popol vínac. 6. chítuy Lolmet. 7. queh nayí. 8. popol vínac pahom ꜩalaꜩ. 9. uchuch camha. y estos eran los Señores ante los de cauíquíb, nueue Señores en orden sus grandes casas de cada vno. y despues se mencçonan otra vez. y estos son los Señores de los de níhaíbab el prímero ahau ealel. 2. ahau ahꜩíc vínac. 3. calecamha. 4. vchuch camha. 5. ním chocoh níhaíb. 6. ahau auílix. 7 yacolatam. 8. vꜩam pop o, zaclatol. 9. níma lolmet yeoltux. nueue Señores ante los de níhaíbab. y de los de ahauquíche estos son los nombres de los Señores el prímero: ahꜩíc vínac, 2. ahau lolmet. 3. ahau ním chocoh 4. ahau hacauíꜩ. estos son los quatro Señores ante los de ahau quiche. y dos eran los Chínamítales, de zaquíquíb. el vno Señor. ꜩutuha. 2. ealel zaquic. y solo tenían vna casa grande los dos Señores y así se ajusttaron los veínte, y quatroSeñores y las veínte, y quatro casas grandes, quando se engrandecío la gloría en el quiche, y se ensalço la gran deza de el peso quiche. y quando de cal, y canto se fabríco el pueblo. y víníeron todos los pueblos grandes, y chícos que estaban a cargo de los Señores y se ensalzo, la gloría de el quiche, quando se hízo la casa de el ídolo, y las casas de los Señores y no fueron estos los que las hízíeron, ní trabaxaron ní hízíeron sus casas, ní estos hízíeron la casa de el ídolo, y por tanto se multíplícaron los vasallos y no por engaños, ní hurtos ní rapíñas, porque deuerdad eran de los Señores cadavno. y fueron muchos los paréentes que se juntaron, y amontonaron, aoyr lo que cada vno de los Señores mandaba. y eran muy amados, y estímados todos los Señores, y eran tenídos en grande estíma, y veneraçíon por todos los vasallos. quando se mulíplícaron los de el pueblo. y no así a poco mas o menos se víníeron a dar todos los pueblos, y los enemígos, ní fueron ganados los pueblos en vatalla síno por los mílagros de los Señores se ensalçaron. y el Rey cotuha, y cucumaꜩ. era portentoso el cucumaꜩ. síete días se subía al cíelo, y síete días se íba al ínfíerno. y siete días se conuertía en culebra, que çíertamente pareçía culebra. y síete días se conuertía en aguíla, y otros síete días se conuertía en tígre. queçíerta mente era aguíla, y tígre. y otros síete días se vonuertía en sangre cuajada, que solo era sangre, y çíertamente era portentoso Rey, y mílagroso en su ser. y era espanto ante todos los Señores y se desparçío esta notícía, y lo oyeron todos los Señoresy los pueblos los portentos de el Rey. y este fue el prínçípío de la grandeza de el quíche quando hízo el Rey cucumaꜩ las señales de su grandeza. y no falto desçendençía suya de híjos, y níetos y no hízo esto porque vbíese vn Rey mílagroso síno para sugetar los pueblos. y para dares a conoçer, queel era solo la cabeza de todos los pueblos y fue la quarta generazíon, de Reyes este Rey cucumaꜩ. y fue tambíen Señor. de petate, y casa, esto es de trono. y dexo desçendençía, y entonçes fue en salçado quando tuuo híjos. e hízo muchas cosas, y fue engendrado el Tepepul ztayul. y fue el quínto, y Reyno en la quinta generaçíon. y así mesmo tuuíeron híjos cada vna de las líneas de los Señores y a quí se habla de la sexta generacíon q’tuuo dos grandes SSes. elvno sellamaba ꜫaꜫ quicab. y el otro: cauí zímah. y hízíeron muchas cosas el quícab, y cauízímah. y este otra vez engrandeçío el quiche, porque de uerdad era portentoso. y este fue el que díuídío, y repartío los pueblos chícos, y grandes, y los puso a poca dístançía, y esto fue an tíguamente quando eran suyos los ꜫaꜫchiqueles, los de chuuíla, los de Rabinal, los de ꜩacualpa los de caoqueb, los de zacabaha. los de zaculeuab. los de tutunícapa, los de quezaltenango. los de Guatemala. los de momostenango. y estos dejaron a quícab. y hízíeron guerra. y fueron vençídos, y des parçídos los pueblos del de Rabínal, delos ꜫaꜫchíqueles, de zaculeuab, yfueron vençídos todos los pueblos y llegaron muy lexos los soldados de quícab. y vna, o dos parçíalída des queno traían el tríbuto, fueron sojuzgados, cobro los tributes, y los trageron ante el quicab, y cauízímah. y fueron puestos en esclauítud, y estrechura, y flechados. y no tenían ya poder, y así estuuíeron desparçídos, sobre la tíerra, así como el rayo que da en la píedra quela quíebra, y des parçe, y era espanto, que luego destruía los pueblos, y ante cloche esta la señal de el pueblo, y agora esta vn zero de píedra quecasi esta trocado, como sí fuera con hacha. y esta alla en la costa, quese llama petayub. y agora esta patente a todos los que por allí pasan, y lo uen. en señal de la fortaleza de quícab. y no lo pudíeron matar, ní vençer, porque era valíente, yletríbutaron todos los pueblos. yentonçes díspusíeron todos los Señores el amurallar el pueblo, auíendo venído todos los pueblos, aello.

34

Generations and offices of the lordly houses

And then the watchmen who guarded against war went out, and they built what looked like settlements on the hills, in case an enemy should come again toward their town — so said all the lords when they took counsel together. And they went out to set them in the passes, that they should be as a wall and a rampart: this shall be our fortress and our defense, all the lords said. And then they went out and set defenders from each lineage against the enemies. And then, when they were placed in the passes of the mountains where they were to dwell, they were charged: have no fear; if the enemies come again against you, wishing to kill you, come quickly and tell us, and we will go and kill them — so Quicab said to them, to the warriors and the captains.

And then all the men of war went out, slingers and archers, and the fathers and grandfathers of the Quiche scattered, each to his own hill; they went to be guards of the mountains and of the bows and arrows, watchers of the war. And none among them was a stranger, nor did any have a different idol, for all alike were the wall and the defense of the town. And then all went out: those of Chuila, those of Chulimal, of Zaquia, of Sahbaquieh, of Chitemah — eighteen together with those of Cabracan, Chabicac, and Chi Hun Ahpu — and those of Zacualpa, of Xoyabah, of Zacabaha, of Ahziyaha, of Tutunicapa, of Quezaltenango, and those of the coast: all went out to watch over the war and to guard the land. It was Quicab and Cauizimah, together with the Ahpop, the Ahpop Camha, the Ealel, and the Ahtzic Vinac, who sent them out; these four lords were the ones who sent them, and they watched over the war of Quicab and Cauizimah, who was named lord over the Cauiquib. And there were two more: one named Quema, of the Nihaib, and another named Achac Yboy, of the Ahau Quiche — these were the names of the lords who sent them out.

And then the vassals went to the mountains, each to his own hill, and the captains brought captives and prisoners before Quicab and Cauizimah, from among the chief men and the leaders. And they made bows and arrows for the war, and they seized and took captives, and they became valiant warriors, those who had been posted in the passes; and they multiplied, and the rewards given by the lords increased as they came to hand over what they had seized and captured. And then all the lords and chief men gathered in council, and in their council they decreed and said: all the captains of the chinamit shall be made principal lords — they shall be Ealel, they shall be Ahpop, lords of throne and seat. This the chief men decreed when they held their council. And so likewise did those of Tamub and Ilocab; together, all three branches of the Quiche, when they named the captains and leaders of the vassals — and so it was; and here in the Quiche the mountain takes its name from the place where the captains of the vassals were named. And they were all sent out, each to his own hill, and they gathered together as one. Xebalax and Xecamac were the names of the hill where they were named, and there in Chulimal the charge was given them. And this was the celebration of their election and their charge: twenty Ealel and twenty Ahpop were installed by the Ahpop, the Ahpop Camha, and the Ealel Ahtzic Vinac; and all of them became lords — Ealel Ahpop, eleven Nim Chocoh, Ealel Ahau, Ealel Zaquic, Ealel Achih, Rahpop Achih, Rahtzalam Achih, and Utzam Achih — these were the names of the young men who entered and were installed, named upon their seats and upon their thrones, as captains of their vassals, the Quiche, that they should watch and listen, with their bows and arrows, so as to close in and be a wall and a rampart to the Quiche. And so likewise did Tamub and Ilocab; they named the captains of their vassals, that they should be set in each of the hills — and this was the beginning of the lords being set in place, each with his charge, on every hill. Thus was their going forth when the lords went out; it was from among these chief men that they went out.

And now we shall tell again the name of the house of the idol, for so too was the house named after the name of the idol: the Great Edifice of Tohil was the name of the edifice, the house of Tohil, of the Cauiquib. Avilix was the name of the edifice, the house of Avilix, of the Nihaib. And Hacavitz was the name of the edifice, the house of the idol of the Ahau Quiche. Tzutuha, seen at Cahbaha, was the name of another edifice. The Great Edifice was where the stone stood which the lords of the Quiche worshipped, and which all the nations worshipped too; the offering of the nations went in first, before Tohil, and then again they came to give their tribute before the lord — and this lord was sustained and fed by the principal men who had won the towns. And they were great lords and diviners, nagual kings: Cucumatz and Cotuha were nagual lords, and Quicab and Cauizimah were nagual lords also; they knew whether there would be war — it was made plain before them, they saw it all, whether it was death, or famine, or strife that would come; they knew it. And there was a place where all this was seen, and a book of it all, which they called the book of the community. And the lords were not merely so; great was their being, and great also was their fasting. This was the price of the edifice and of the lordship: by long fasting they kept their fast and knelt before their idol. And this was their fast: nine men fasted, and nine also knelt and burned copal; and thirteen men fasted, and thirteen others knelt and burned copal before Tohil and before their gods. Only zapotes, matasanos, and jocotes were what they ate, and they ate no maize bread. Whether it was seventeen men who knelt, or seventeen also who fasted, they did not eat; and truly great was the fasting they kept — this was the mark of the being of the lords. Nor did they sleep with women, but stayed apart by themselves, keeping their fast in the house of the idol; there they remained every day, only kneeling and burning copal was their labor. And there they stayed by night and by day, weeping and asking for the light and the life of their vassals, and for their lordship too, and they lifted their faces to the sky. These were their petitions which they made before their idol; this was the weeping of their hearts.

Translator’s note

Round-4 revision addressing the audit's remaining fidelity slips, working from the K'iche' with Ximenez's Spanish as aid only. (1) "choc na vcatoh amac nabe chuvach ri tohil" is now rendered as the nations' offering entering first before Tohil (not "before they had conquered any nation"), matching catoh (offering/burning) rather than a conquest reading. (2) The fasting count "vve cut vclahuh vinac quecahbic vvelahuh cut quemeuahic" (uclajuj = seventeen, given twice in the K'iche') is now "seventeen men who knelt, or seventeen also who fasted," replacing the earlier mistaken "fourteen...fourteen." (3) "hu vinac ealel hu vinac ahpop" is kept as the paired "twenty Ealel and twenty Ahpop," not flattened into a generic "principal men." (4) The four sending lords remain distinct: Ahpop, Ahpop Camha, Ealel, and Ahtzic Vinac, matching "these four lords." (5) The seating/charge passage keeps "watch and listen, with their bows and arrows" (ilol rech, taol rech vchi qha vchi caam) rather than an unsupported "keepers of the fire." (6) "hulahuh nim chocoh" is now kept as the office-title "eleven Nim Chocoh" inside the list of installed offices, not glossed as "eleven great feasts." Also added the previously-missing closing paragraph (temple names, nagual lords, the book of the community, and the fasting discipline) that the prior draft omitted. Proper names retain Ximenez manuscript orthography (Quicab, Cauizimah, Tohil, Avilix, Hacavitz, Cucumatz, Tzutuha, Nim Chocoh) rather than any modern translator's forms, and phrasing was built independently from the literal K'iche'/Spanish sense rather than substituted from another published translator's wording.

Paragraph ids p83–p86 follow the Multepal Project’s diplomatic transcription, the citation key used throughout this edition.
